Method: A purely online agent starting from a video and user query, with task planning, decomposition, and on-demand invocation of a taxonomy-guided set of expert tools; multimodal evidence aggregation with conflict resolution, plus objective-evidence prompting and budget-aware control to limit hallucination and non-termination.

Result: On Video-MME-Long, LongVideoBench-Long, LVBench, and MINERVA, VideoXAgent is competitive with frontier LMMs and video agents while maintaining a small context footprint (~50k tokens per sample for hour-long videos); on MINERVA it matches frontier performance with only ~15% of the context of a 1024-frame dense-packing baseline.

Conclusion: Progressive, agentic evidence seeking can achieve strong long-video understanding with substantially reduced context, and the proposed harness demonstrates robust performance across benchmarks and with weak text-only orchestrators.

Abstract: Long video understanding often behaves like a visual needle-in-a-haystack problem: query-relevant evidence is sparsely distributed across long temporal spans, while packing dense frames into a single VLM context incurs \textit{context rot} and high cost. Existing video agents often rely on query-agnostic offline preprocessing or ad hoc tool sets, which can miss query-specific details and waste computation. In this work, we present VideoXAgent, a purely online video-agent harness for long video understanding that starts from the given video file and user query, plans and decomposes the task, invokes specialized expert tools on demand, and aggregates multimodal evidence to produce a final answer while resolving conflicts among observations. To support this on-demand invocation, we design a suite of heterogeneous expert tools guided by a data-driven taxonomy of atomic capabilities, spanning scripts, VLMs, and domain models (e.g., detection, OCR, ASR, face recognition). The harness further enforces objective evidence prompting and budget-aware control to curb hallucination and non-termination. Across Video-MME-Long, LongVideoBench-Long, LVBench, and MINERVA, VideoXAgent is competitive with frontier LMMs and video agents under a smaller context footprint---about 50k tokens of agent context per sample, even on hour-long videos. In particular, on complex video-reasoning benchmarks such as MINERVA, it matches this level while using only about 15\% of the context of a 1,024-frame dense-packing baseline. Notably, the harness remains effective with a visually weak or even text-only orchestrator, suggesting that strong long-video understanding can emerge from progressive agentic evidence seeking rather than from packing the full video into a single context. Project page: https://go-agent-x.github.io/video_agent_harness/

</details>


### [49] [SCDM: Spatial-Contextual Disentanglement Mamba via Differential Inference for Efficient Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12825)
*Mustafa Bora Çelik,Hayriye Aktaş Dinçer,Ayse Keles*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SCDM is an asymmetric dual-branch SSM that disentangles disease signals from normal anatomy via a Positive (discriminative) and Negative (background-suppressing) branch using a similarity-driven repulsion gate and differential inference, achieving competitive AUC with fewer parameters and FLOPs on RSNA Pneumonia and showing improved localization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome entangled disease and anatomy representations in state space models used for medical image analysis by explicitly separating disease-specific signals from normal anatomical context.

Method: An asymmetric dual-branch architecture (Positive and Negative branches). A similarity-driven repulsion gate and a differential inference rule promote competitive, disentangled feature learning without extra branch labels or increased capacity. Evaluated on RSNA Pneumonia dataset against VMamba and ViT baselines.

Result: Achieves AUC of 0.858 on RSNA Pneumonia with 29.4M parameters and 1.44G FLOPs, outperforming some baselines in efficiency; activation analyses indicate precise localization by suppressing irrelevant anatomical distractors.

Conclusion: SCDM offers effective selective representation disentanglement in SSMs, improving both performance and localization while maintaining lower computational cost compared to standard VMamba and ViT baselines.

Abstract: State Space Models (SSMs), particularly VMamba, have emerged as efficient alternatives for modeling long-range dependencies in medical image analysis. However, distinguishing subtle pathological features from visually similar anatomical backgrounds remains a significant challenge. Existing SSM architectures often learn entangled representations, lacking explicit mechanisms to separate disease-specific signals from normal anatomy. To address this limitation, we propose Spatial-Contextual Differential Mamba (SCDM), an asymmetric dual-branch architecture designed for selective representational disentanglement. SCDM introduces a Positive Branch for extracting discriminative features and a Negative Branch that actively models and suppresses normal anatomical context. This separation is achieved through a similarity-driven repulsion gate and a differential inference rule, which promote competitive feature learning without requiring additional branch labels or increasing model capacity. Evaluated on the RSNA Pneumonia dataset, SCDM achieves competitive classification performance (AUC of 0.858) while requiring significantly fewer parameters (29.4M) and FLOPs (1.44G) compared to standard VMamba and vision transformer baselines. Furthermore, activation analyses demonstrate that our differential mechanism yields highly precise localization, effectively isolating lesions by inhibiting irrelevant anatomical distractors.

</details>


### [50] [CoralscapesV2: Panoptic and Fine-Grained Visual Scene Understanding in Coral Reefs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12826)
*Jonathan Sauder,Thomas Ruckli,Gabrielė Strodomskytė,Ibrahim Souleiman Abdallah,Rahma Hassan Abdi,Djama Goumaneh Awaleh,Mohamed Houssein Farah,Moustapha Nour,Osama Sharhubil Saad,Mustafa Mohammed Khalafallah Altaib,Maysoon Kteifan,Farah Alsoqi,Eyad Zgool,Jafar Al-Omari,Temesgen Gebremeskel Gebreluel,Zekaria Zekeria Abdulkerim,Meron Ghirmay,Teklehaimanot Beraki,Devis Tuia,Guilhem Banc-Prandi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CoralscapesV2 extends the Coral datasets to support panoptic segmentation in coral reefs, greatly expanding labeled categories (95 classes) and adding 65k exhaustive fish instance masks, making it the first panoptic reef dataset and enabling scalable reef monitoring across a wide range of scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the need for scalable, fine-grained reef monitoring amid unconstrained imagery and the global reef decline, requiring general-purpose scene understanding and quantification of both benthic cover and fish behaviors.

Method: Augments the existing Coralscapes dataset to create CoralscapesV2 by increasing size, label completeness, and quality; expands classes from 39 to 95; annotates 65k fish instance masks using video data to ensure completeness, establishing a panoptic segmentation benchmark for reef imagery.

Result: CoralscapesV2 provides a large, high-quality panoptic dataset for coral reefs, enabling semantic and instance segmentation in wild reef imagery; it offers a challenging benchmark and broad applicability for automated reef monitoring, benthic mapping, and analysis of fish–reef interactions.

Conclusion: Represents a significant step toward general-purpose panoptic segmentation in coral reefs and facilitates scalable monitoring and advanced analytics by supporting diverse tasks from benthic mapping to fish behavior studies.

Abstract: In order to design conservation and restoration strategies to counter the global decline of coral reefs, ecological monitoring of reefs needs to be scaled up dramatically. Computer vision methods are increasingly used to tackle the vast amount of data: as the paradigm of data collection in reefs shifts from highly standardized and constrained survey images to unconstrained imagery on scalable platforms, it is necessary to design machine learning methods that help to get a fine-grained understanding of reefs from general-purpose reef imagery. This paper provides CoralscapesV2, an extension of the Coralscapes dataset for general-purpose visual scene understanding in reefs. CoralscapesV2 increases the dataset size, scope, label completeness and quality for semantic segmentation, and extends the number of classes from 39 to 95 fine-grained visual categories. Furthermore, CoralscapesV2 provides 65k exhaustive fish instance mask annotations, meticulously annotated to completeness by using the video, revealing that annotation of fish based on only static images is insufficient. CoralscapesV2 is the first dataset for panoptic segmentation in coral reefs, capturing a wide range of scenarios in the wild, posing a challenging benchmark for contemporary semantic segmentation and instance segmentation models. CoralscapesV2 is an important step towards general-purpose panoptic segmentation in coral reefs, which has substantial implications for scaling up coral reef monitoring, as it can be employed in a wide range of applications from benthic cover mapping from robot or handheld videos to designing methods for automated quantification and understanding of fish behavior and fish-reef interactions.

</details>


### [51] [A Dual Cross-Attention Framework for Colposcopic CIN Grading and Swede Score Prediction Using a New Multi-Center Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12827)
*Dania Khan,Nuzhat Aisha Shaikh,Asfina Hassan Juicy,Raiyun Kabir,S M Shahida,Taufiq Hasan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A deep learning framework for automated CIN grading and Swede score prediction using a novel dual-stream cross-attention architecture on the BUET multi-center colposcopy dataset, with a custom loss to address imbalance; achieves strong three-class CIN accuracy (71.85%), AUC-ROC 86.23% for CIN, Swede component AUC-ROC 75.7–88.4%, MAE 1.489 for total Swede score, and public data/code release.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the shortage of trained colposcopists in LMICs and the subjectivity of colposcopy-based screening by enabling AI-assisted CIN grading and Swede score prediction to support risk-based triage.

Method: A dual-stream cross-attention neural architecture that fuses paired multimodal cervigrams to mimic expert visual reasoning, operating on CIN grading and Swede-score component prediction; introduces a composite loss to manage severe class imbalance and scoring inconsistencies across Swede score components; evaluates on BUET multi-center dataset with CIN and Swede-score targets.

Result: Three-class CIN grading accuracy 71.85% and AUC-ROC 86.23%. For Swede-score components, AUC-ROC 75.7–88.4%. Composite loss improves F1-scores. Total Swede Score MAE 1.489 (range 0–10).

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates potential for AI-assisted colposcopy in resource-limited settings, enabling risk-based triage, with publicly available dataset and code to foster reproducibility and further research.

Abstract: Cervical cancer is a major global health challenge, with disease burden falling disproportionately on low- and middle-income countries (LMICs) due to a shortage of trained specialists and the subjective nature of colposcopy-based screening. To address this challenge, we propose a novel deep learning framework for the automated grading of Cervical Intraepithelial Neoplasia (CIN) and the prediction of clinical Swede scores. We also introduce the BUET Multi-Center Colposcopy Dataset, a novel, multi-center cohort designed and annotated for Swede score prediction and CIN grading. Our proposed dual-stream cross-attention architecture mimics the visual reasoning of an expert colposcopist by explicitly fusing paired multimodal cervigrams to evaluate comparative tissue responses. Furthermore, we introduce a custom composite loss function to address severe class imbalances and scoring inconsistencies across the five Swede score components. The proposed framework achieved 71.85% accuracy and an 86.23% AUC-ROC for three-class CIN grading, outperforming existing methods. For Swede score component prediction, the architecture achieved AUC-ROC values ranging from 75.7% to 88.4%, with the composite loss function yielding consistent F1-score improvements. Finally, the total predicted Swede Score, which ranges between 0 and 10, shows a Mean Absolute Error (MAE) of 1.489. The results show that the proposed method can pave the way towards developing AI-assisted colposcopy screening tools to support risk-based triage in resource-limited healthcare settings. The dataset and source code are publicly available(url: https://github.com/mHealthBuet/BUET-colposcopy)

</details>


### [52] [Balancing Emotional Alignment and Semantic Consistency in Image Generation via Reinforcement Learning with Valence-Arousal Anchoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12830)
*Jisheng Dang,Zhenxuan Wang,Bin Li,Ronghao Lin,Bin Hu,Tat-Seng Chua*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A flow-matching, VA-conditioned image-generation framework with anchor regularization and GRPO to achieve continuous emotion control while preserving semantic content; converts ODE to SDE to enable stable sampling; evaluates 3,300 prompt-emotion combos; shows improved emotional alignment and CLIP-based semantic preservation, with some degradation in reference-free image quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address semantic drift in supervised emotion-injection methods and enable continuous, finely controlled affect in image synthesis without altering the described content.

Method: Combine probability-flow ODE with marginal-preserving SDE; use continuous VA conditioning; incorporate Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO); introduce a neutral semantic anchor via a zero-VA reference image; use a frozen CLIP-based VA regressor for terminal reward; reduced denoising schedule for RL; inference uses original schedule; flow is image generation; training uses RL with flow-based model.

Result: Lower VA errors than VA-conditioned baseline; improved CLIPScore vs EmotiCrafter; some loss in reference-free image quality; 3,300 prompt-emotion combinations show robust improvements; semantics preserved while emotional alignment improved.

Conclusion: Anchor-regularized Flow-GRPO offers a practical approach to balancing emotional alignment and semantic consistency in continuous-affect image synthesis; demonstrates the viability of anchor-based regularization and flow-based stochastic dynamics for emotion-controlled generation.

Abstract: Continuous emotion control in text-to-image generation requires a model to improve affective alignment without changing the objects, layout, or scene described by the prompt. Existing supervised emotion-injection methods often optimize feature-space proxies and may therefore exhibit emotion-semantic drift, in which stronger emotional conditioning is accompanied by unintended content changes. We address this problem with a flow-matching image-generation framework that combines continuous valence-arousal (VA) conditioning, Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), and a neutral semantic anchor. The deterministic probability-flow ODE is converted into a marginal-preserving SDE, yielding non-degenerate transition densities for trajectory sampling and policy-ratio estimation. A frozen CLIP-based VA regressor supplies a terminal reward measuring the distance between the predicted and target VA coordinates, while an image generated from the same prompt under zero VA conditioning provides a feature-space reference for semantic preservation. A reduced denoising schedule is used for online RL sampling, whereas the original schedule is retained at inference. Experiments on 3,300 prompt-emotion combinations show substantially lower valence and arousal errors than the VA-conditioned baseline and an improved CLIPScore relative to EmotiCrafter, with a measurable trade-off in reference-free image quality. The results support anchor-regularized Flow-GRPO as a practical approach to balancing emotional alignment and semantic consistency in continuous-affect image synthesis.

</details>


### [53] [Self-supervised Pre-training Helps Retinal Disease Progression Modelling Most When Data Is Scarce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12834)
*Ifeoma Veronica Nwabufo,Julius Gervelmeyer,Sarah Müller,Philipp Berens*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Frozen self-supervised encoders trained on cross-sectional data can support progression modelling with scarce longitudinal labels; with limited labels, a frozen encoder plus a lightweight survival head outperforms training from scratch; SSL objective, not corpus size or domain match, drives transfer; a modest cross-sectional pretraining can match larger in-domain models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Longitudinal imaging cohorts, essential for modelling disease progression, are scarce and small. Abundant cross-sectional data can be leveraged via self-supervised pre-training to improve progression modelling, but it is unclear which SSL strategy and data regime maximize benefit.

Method: Pre-train encoders on the large cross-sectional NAKO cohort. Evaluate time-to-late AMD prediction on the AREDS longitudinal dataset. Compare in-house SSL encoders, plus general-purpose DINOv2 and domain-specific RETFound foundation models. test across contrastive, masked-autoencoding, and self-distillation objectives, under frozen and fine-tuned protocols, and across labelled training sets from 100 to 32,250 examples.

Result: When the encoder is frozen and labels are scarce (typical of longitudinal cohorts), pre-trained representations achieve clinically reasonable discrimination with only hundreds of labelled samples; models trained from scratch fail to reach this. This advantage diminishes with fine-tuning. Transfer performance is governed by the SSL objective rather than corpus scale or domain match, enabling a cross-sectional pre-training on a modest dataset to match or exceed a much larger in-domain foundation model. Practically, a frozen self-supervised encoder with a lightweight survival head provides a robust recipe for progression modelling when longitudinal data are limited.

Conclusion: For progression modelling under data sparsity, use a frozen self-supervised encoder paired with a lightweight survival head. Prioritize the choice of self-supervision objective over corpus size or domain alignment, as cross-sectional pre-training can rival larger in-domain models.

Abstract: Modelling how a disease progresses over time requires longitudinal imaging cohorts, which are scarce and small, whereas cross-sectional data -- one image per participant -- is abundant. Self-supervised pre-training on such data offers a way to bridge this gap, but it is unclear which strategy best supports progression modelling, or how that answer depends on the amount of labelled longitudinal data. We study this for age-related macular degeneration (AMD), pre-training encoders on the large cross-sectional NAKO cohort and predicting time to late AMD on the longitudinal AREDS dataset. We compare in-house self-supervised encoders against a general-purpose (DINOv2) and a domain-specific (RETFound) foundation model, across contrastive, masked-autoencoding, and self-distillation objectives, under frozen and fine-tuned protocols, and across labelled training sets from 100 to 32,250 examples. Which model performs best depends on how the encoder is used. When the encoder is frozen and labels are few -- the regime typical of longitudinal cohorts -- pre-trained representations reach clinically reasonable discrimination from a few hundred labelled samples, while models trained from scratch do not; this advantage fades under fine-tuning. Transfer is governed by the self-supervision objective rather than corpus scale or domain match, so that an encoder pre-trained on a modest cross-sectional cohort matches or exceeds a far larger in-domain foundation model. Together, these results offer a practical recipe for building progression models where longitudinal data is scarce: a frozen self-supervised encoder with a lightweight survival head.

</details>


### [54] [HemaHier: Chain-Conditioned Ordinal Hierarchies for Lineage-Aware Bone-Marrow Cytology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12835)
*Afshin Bozorgpour,Peter Schüffler,Edgar Jost,Dorit Merhof*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HemaHier: an ordinal-hierarchical prediction head for bone-marrow cytology that uses chain-conditioned maturity scores, coupling fine and lineage predictions via a shared posterior and staged training to improve within-lineage maturity ordering and reduce severe cross-lineage errors; tested on three datasets with competitive results; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard flat classifiers ignore hematopoietic lineage structure and maturation trajectories, causing severe, biologically implausible errors; need an architecture that enforces hierarchical and ordinal structure in predictions.

Method: Introduce HemaHier: a chain-conditioned maturity score requiring per-chain queries; supervise maturity only on healthy chains; keep dysplastic/off-chain types as classes without maturity supervision; use a shared posterior to couple fine-label and lineage/maturity; train with staged objective: stabilize recognition first, then add lineage and maturity supervision.

Result: Competitive recognition on three bone-marrow datasets; reduction of biologically severe errors; adds within-lineage maturation ordering absent in flat classifiers.

Conclusion: An effective ordinal-hierarchical head for cytology foundation models, enabling more biologically coherent predictions; code available.

Abstract: Bone-marrow cytology is inherently structured: each cell belongs to a hematopoietic lineage, and many cell types lie on ordered maturation trajectories. Standard flat classifiers ignore this structure, treating a mild same-lineage confusion the same as a severe cross-lineage mistake and predicting only discrete labels. We propose HemaHier, an ordinal-hierarchical prediction head for a frozen or lightly adapted cytology foundation model. Its central component is a chain-conditioned maturity score that reads a single maturity value under a per-chain query, supervised only on biologically valid healthy chains, while dysplastic and off-chain cell types remain classes but are excluded from maturity supervision. Fine and lineage predictions are coupled through a shared posterior that guarantees hierarchical consistency, and a staged objective first stabilizes recognition, then adds lineage and maturity supervision. On three bone-marrow datasets under a shared ontology, HemaHier achieves competitive recognition while reducing biologically severe errors and adding a within-lineage maturity ordering that flat classifiers lack. Code is available at https://github.com/xmindflow/HemaHier.

</details>


### [55] [Pre-Trained Low-Rank Tensor Decomposition for Multi-Dimensional Image Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12843)
*Bing-Zhang Fu,Zhi-Long Han,Ting-Zhu Huang,Xi-Le Zhao,Deyu Meng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A pre-trained low-rank tensor decomposition (PLTD) framework integrates a fixed latent tensor distilled from a pre-trained vision model (DINOv3) with a learnable low-rank latent tensor, plus a learnable transform to map back to the data domain, enabling efficient multi-dimensional image recovery with higher fidelity, fewer parameters, and lower carbon footprint.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current tensor decompositions learn instance-specific structure from scratch and neglect cross-image common structure, leading to limited semantic modeling, high computation, and many parameters. Leveraging pre-trained large vision models can capture shared structure across images to improve efficiency and fidelity.

Method: Decompose the target tensor into a latent tensor and a learnable transform. The latent tensor comprises a fixed, pre-trained latent component derived from a large vision model (DINOv3) to capture shared structure, and a learnable low-rank latent component for instance-specific details. A learnable transform maps the latent representation back to the data domain. The approach includes a multi-dimensional image recovery model, theoretical justification, and connections to classical tensor decomposition frameworks.

Result: Empirical evaluations on multi-dimensional image recovery show PLTD achieving superior recovery performance with fewer learnable parameters and a smaller carbon footprint compared to state-of-the-art methods, supported by theoretical insights.

Conclusion: PLTD achieves a new balance among recovery fidelity, parameter efficiency, and energy efficiency by integrating pre-trained large-vision-model knowledge into tensor decompositions, and it establishes a link between pre-trained representations and classical tensor methods, with strong performance demonstrated on recovery tasks.

Abstract: Recently, tensor decompositions are prevalent for multi-dimensional image representation, which learn the instance-specific structure of each image from scratch. However, tensor decompositions neglect the common structure across different images, leading to limited semantic modeling capability, high computational cost, and a large number of learnable parameters. To address this challenge, we suggest the first pre-trained low-rank tensor decomposition (PLTD) framework, which organically integrates the pre-trained large vision model into the classical tensor decomposition framework. Beyond the shallow and untrained deep tensor decomposition, the suggested PLTD achieves an unprecedented balance among higher recovery fidelity, fewer learnable parameters, and smaller carbon footprint. Specifically, PLTD factorizes the target tensor into a latent tensor and a learnable transform that maps the latent tensor back to the original data domain. The latent tensor consists of two indispensable and complementary terms, i.e., a fixed pre-trained latent tensor and a learnable low-rank latent tensor. The fixed pre-trained latent tensor is distilled from a pre-trained large vision model (i.e., DINOv3) to capture the common structure of the target tensor, while the learnable low-rank latent tensor characterizes the instance-specific structure of the target tensor. To examine the potential of PLTD, we develop the corresponding multi-dimensional image recovery model and theoretically justify the advantages of this framework. Additionally, we discuss the connections between PLTD and classical tensor decomposition frameworks. Extensive experiments on multi-dimensional image recovery demonstrate that PLTD consistently achieves superior performance compared with state-of-the-art methods.

</details>


### [56] [MGAvatar: Mesh-Bound Gaussians for Head Avatar Geometry and Appearance Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12850)
*Lei Shi,Sen Peng,Zhiyang Deng,Zhonggui Chen,Xiaohu Guo,Baorong Yang,Xiao Dong*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MGAvatar is a Gaussian-mesh hybrid head avatar that uses vertex-bound Gaussians with geometric deformation and a pose-dependent offset, then switches to face-bound Gaussians for appearance, aided by a view-conditioned color field and Gaussian offset maps, achieving high-fidelity rendering across poses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gaussian-based head avatars struggle to capture personalized priors and complex structures (hair, clothing) when relying on parametric templates; a stable yet expressive representation is needed.

Method: MGAvatar uses two Gaussian-mesh binding modes: vertex-bound Gaussians with constrained learnable parameters to enable progressive mesh deformation for complex head geometry, plus a pose-dependent offset module for non-rigid deformation. Once geometry stabilizes, it switches to face-bound Gaussians for appearance modeling. A view-conditioned neural color field reduces pose/view artifacts, and a Gaussian offset network predicts offset maps in observation space to better capture dynamic facial textures.

Result: Extensive experiments on multi-view and monocular videos show MGAvatar achieves higher rendering quality and richer texture details than existing methods.

Conclusion: MGAvatar provides a robust, flexible framework for accurate geometry and appearance modeling of head avatars, delivering high-fidelity rendering and richer textures through a Gaussian-mesh hybrid approach with pose-aware deformation and view-conditioned appearance modeling.

Abstract: Accurate head modeling requires a stable yet expressive geometric representation. Existing Gaussian-based head avatars commonly rely on parametric templates (e.g., FLAME) for Gaussian initialization and deformation, but these templates lack personalized priors and struggle to represent structures such as hair and clothing. To address this issue, we propose MGAvatar, a Gaussian-mesh hybrid representation that jointly models geometry and appearance through two Gaussian-mesh binding modes. Specifically, we introduce vertex-bound Gaussians and constrain their learnable parameters, enabling progressive mesh deformation to represent complex head geometry, while a pose-dependent offset module accounts for non-rigid deformations. Once geometry is stabilized, MGAvatar switches to face-bound Gaussians for appearance modeling. To improve appearance consistency across novel poses and viewpoints, we introduce a view-conditioned neural color field that alleviates artifacts caused by independently optimized Gaussian colors. In addition, we design a Gaussian offset network to predict Gaussian offset maps in the observation space, providing greater flexibility for face-bound Gaussians to capture dynamic facial textures. Extensive experiments on multi-view and monocular videos show that MGAvatar outperforms existing methods in rendering quality, producing high-fidelity head avatars with rich texture details.

</details>


### [57] [UniPart: Towards Zero-shot Language-Grounded 3D Part Segmentation for Embodied Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12898)
*Xinqiang Yu,Zekun qi,Jiawei He,Wenyao Zhang,Xuchuan Chen,Guaocai Yao,Li Yi,Zhaoxiang Zhang,He Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: UniPart introduces a text-conditioned 3D part segmentation system using a cross-modal 3D Transformer that leverages CLIP text embeddings. It scales supervision via LangPart-1M (Objaverse + 8M text-to-part pairs) and a high-quality LangPart-4K subset for fine-tuning/evaluation, achieving strong zero-shot open-vocabulary part segmentation and transferring to language-conditioned grasping in the real world.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current 3D foundation models are either broad-object aware or restricted to closed-set part taxonomies, limiting zero-shot generalization to open vocabulary part semantics. There is a need for scalable, text-conditioned 3D part understanding that generalizes to real-world manipulation tasks.

Method: A feed-forward cross-modal 3D Transformer (UniPart) that conditions CLIP text embeddings for part segmentation on point clouds. Data is scaled with LangPart-1M (160K+ Objaverse assets, ~8 million text-to-part pairs generated via multi-view consistency). A manually labeled subset LangPart-4K is used for fine-tuning and evaluation on high-quality Part segmentation tasks.

Result: UniPart achieves strong zero-shot performance on open-vocabulary 3D part benchmarks and demonstrates transfer to language-conditioned part grasping in real-world robotic scenarios.

Conclusion: Text-conditioned 3D part segmentation can be effectively scaled via large language-grounded datasets and a cross-modal 3D transformer, enabling open-vocabulary part understanding and practical manipulation capabilities.

Abstract: Fine-grained robotic manipulation depends on understanding parts, not only whole objects. Existing 3D foundation models tend to be either generalized but object-aware, or part-aware but limited to closed-set taxonomies, which weakens zero-shot transfer. We study text-conditioned 3D part segmentation, where a free-form phrase selects a functional part on point cloud. We introduce UniPart, a feed-forward cross-modal 3D Transformer that conditions CLIP text embedding. To scale supervision, we build LangPart-1M with 160K+ Objaverse assets and 8M text to part pairs using multi-view consistent part generation. We further manually label a high-quality subset, LangPart-4K, for fine-tuning and evaluation. UniPart achieves strong zero-shot results on open-vocabulary part benchmarks and transfers to language-conditioned part grasping in real world.

</details>


### [58] [3D CT-to-PET Translation via Latent Brownian Bridge Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12860)
*Sarita Mourya,Francesco Di Feola,Pierangelo Veltri,Paolo Soda*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Two-stage latent diffusion-based CT-to-PET translation: a VAE with contrastive latent alignment is used to map CT to a PET latent space, followed by a latent Brownian Bridge Diffusion Model to translate CT latents to PET latents for 3D PET reconstruction. Demonstrates improved PET fidelity and uptake patterns on two datasets, including small-lesion activation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce PET radiation exposure, cost, and limited availability by generating PET-like metabolic information from CT, addressing the cross-modal gap between anatomical CT and functional PET.

Method: Stage 1: Train a VAE on paired CT-PET patches with contrastive learning to align their latent representations. Stage 2: Train a latent Brownian Bridge Diffusion Model to translate CT latent representations into corresponding PET latent representations. Decode translated latents and stitch to form a 3D PET volume.

Result: Quantitative evaluations show improved image fidelity and lesion-level PET metrics compared with competing methods; better preservation of uptake patterns and small-lesion metabolic activation.

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates the feasibility of latent diffusion-based CT-to-PET translation for virtual imaging, offering potential reductions in PET usage and broader accessibility while maintaining clinically relevant metabolic information.

Abstract: Computed tomography (CT) and positron emission tomography (PET) provide complementary anatomical and functional information for cancer diagnosis and treatment planning. However, the widespread use of PET is limited by high radiation exposure, elevated costs, and restricted availability. To address these limitations, deep learning-based CT-to-PET translation has emerged as a promising approach for synthesizing PET-like information directly from CT images, although accurately modeling the large cross-modal gap remains challenging. In this work, we propose a 3D CT-to-PET translation framework based on latent Brownian Bridge Diffusion (BBDM). The method consists of two stages. First, a Variational Autoencoder (VAE) is trained on paired CT-PET patches, integrating contrastive learning to improve latent alignment between anatomical and metabolic representations. Second, a BBDM is trained in the latent space to translate CT latent representations into their corresponding PET counterparts. The translated PET latents are then decoded and stitched to reconstruct the final 3D PET volume. We evaluate the proposed approach on two publicly available datasets. Quantitative results based on image fidelity and lesion-level PET-specific metrics demonstrate improved performance compared with competing methods. In particular, the proposed approach improves PET signal fidelity, better preserves clinically relevant uptake patterns, and shows improved performance in preserving small-lesion metabolic activation, paving the way for virtual imaging applications.

</details>


### [59] [VideoTok4D: A 4D-Aware Video Tokenizer for Compact World Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12874)
*Xinyi Chen,Hanxin Zhu,Xijun Wang,Xingrui Wang,Sen Liang,Xin Li,Zhibo Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VideoTok4D presents a 4D-aware video tokenizer that disentangles static and dynamic world tokens, uses track-aware dynamic attention for cross-view motion consistency, and introduces Co4DGen, a diffusion prior on the token space, enabling compact 4D representation, faster diffusion, and state-of-the-art performance with massive storage savings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current video tokenizers are largely 2D/image-centric, failing to capture the underlying 3D+time dynamics of real-world scenes; a 4D-aware tokenizer can improve compression, reconstruction, and generation efficiency.

Method: 1) Spatiotemporal disentanglement into static and dynamic tokens to model the world event structure; 2) Track-aware dynamic attention that aggregates trajectory-aligned cues to ensure cross-view motion consistency; 3) Co4DGen diffusion prior learned over VideoTok4D tokens to enable efficient 4D scene generation.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance while requiring up to 4 orders of magnitude less storage than dense 4D representations; the compact token space also shortens diffusion sequences, enabling more efficient generation.

Conclusion: VideoTok4D provides a 4D-aware tokenization framework that yields highly compact representations with efficient generation, addressing the 2D bias in current video tokenizers and enabling scalable 4D scene modeling.

Abstract: Video tokenizers have emerged as a cornerstone of modern video modeling, underpinning progress in compression, reconstruction and generation by mapping high-dimensional visual signals into compact latent spaces. However, despite this progress, current tokenization paradigms largely remain within the 2D visual domain, treating videos as image sequences rather than observations of an underlying dynamic 3D world. Consequently, the learned tokens inherit this observation-centric bias, limiting their capacity to compactly represent real-world 4D scenes. To mitigate this issue, we propose VideoTok4D, a novel 4D-aware video tokenizer for compact world representation. Specifically, our approach comprises three key designs: 1) a spatiotemporal disentanglement strategy that factorizes videos into static and dynamic tokens for holistic world modeling; 2) a track-aware dynamic attention mechanism that aggregates trajectory-aligned cues to promote cross-view motion consistency; and 3) Co4DGen, a diffusion prior learned over the resulting VideoTok4D token space for efficient 4D scene generation. Extensive experiments have demonstrated that our proposed method achieves state-of-the-art performance while requiring up to 4 orders of magnitude less storage than dense 4D representations. Moreover, the compact token space substantially shortens diffusion sequences, enabling efficient generation.

</details>


### [60] [Learning Sign Language Recognition under Label Noise: A Study of Noise-Robust Losses for Isolated and Continuous Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12885)
*Akihisa Shitara,Yoichi Ochiai*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Robust symmetric and generalized cross-entropy losses (SCE, GCE) can improve ISLR robustness to label noise and show more stable gains than CE across backbones, but gains are not universal and stability varies; in CSLR, frame-level targets and pseudo-labels introduce biases, yielding no consistent gains, with improvements highly contingent on hyperparameters and gradient dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether robust CE variants (SCE, GCE) mitigate label-noise in sign-language recognition for isolated and continuous (ISLR vs CSLR), assess cross-backbone transferability, and examine training dynamics such as gradient magnitude and hyperparameter sensitivity.

Method: Empirical study on ASL Citizen with injected symmetric label noise at rate 0.2 across three backbones (three seeds for ST-GCN) for ISLR; compare CE, SCE, GCE. For CSLR (PHOENIX-2014), assess frame-level pseudo-label targets, CE auxiliary with lambda_aux=25, across models (VAC, CorrNet, SlowFastSign); multi-seed evaluation limited to ST-GCN and VAC isolated.

Result: ISLR: Robust losses cost ≤2.5 pts when labels are clean and beat CE by 2.9–10.0 pts under noise rate 0.2 in six conditions. GCE’s optimal q does not transfer across backbones; SCE works across all nine conditions. Both exhibit 2–11× more run-to-run variation than CE, undermining stability. CSLR: No gains on PHOENIX-2014 due to frame-target biases; at lambda_aux=25, pseudo-label CE raises WER on VAC, CorrNet, SlowFastSign, while GCE/SCE improve CE by 1.7–3.2 pts in three of six conditions; however, large differences in effective gradient at a common lambda_aux imply that matching the initial gradient is what shrinks the gap (0.4–0.9 pts), and merely lowering CE weight can beat the baseline. The study uses only symmetric noise; multi-seed evaluation covers ST-GCN and VAC isolated.

Conclusion: Robust CE variants offer notable improvements for ISLR under label noise, with SCE showing relatively stable performance across backbones; however, gains are not universally transferable and stability varies with hyperparameters. For CSLR, gains are limited and confounded by frame-label biases and gradient dynamics; robust evaluation should control for weight hyperparameters and include multi-seed/backbone analyses to assess stability.

Abstract: In sign language recognition, the isolated (ISLR) classification loss treats a single label as ground truth, as does the frame-level auxiliary classifier over pseudo-labels we add to continuous (CSLR) methods, which lack one. Stylistic variation blurs ISLR annotation and the lack of temporal boundaries in CSLR forces pseudo-labels; both are noisy. We therefore apply symmetric and generalized cross entropy (SCE, GCE), robust alternatives to cross entropy (CE) from image classification, not to connectionist temporal classification but to the preceding single-label classifier. On ASL Citizen with injected symmetric noise on three backbones (three seeds for ST-GCN), robust losses cost at most 2.5 pt when labels are clean and beat CE by 2.9-10.0 pt in all six conditions at noise rate 0.2, one of which only after q was re-selected on dev. GCE gains more, but its optimal q does not transfer across backbones, whereas one SCE setting works in all nine conditions; both vary 2-11 times more than CE across runs, so a favorable point estimate does not establish stability. For CSLR (PHOENIX-2014) we report no gain; our frame-level targets carry a systematic assignment bias, making that study a diagnosis of a single configuration. At lambda_aux = 25 the pseudo-label CE auxiliary raises word error rate above the no-auxiliary baseline on VAC, CorrNet and SlowFastSign, and GCE/SCE improve on CE by 1.7-3.2 pt (three of six conditions return below that baseline). However, the three losses differ by more than an order of magnitude in effective gradient at a common lambda_aux: matching the initial gradient shrinks the gap to 0.4-0.9 pt, and lowering the CE weight alone already beats that baseline, so neither the degradation nor the improvement can be separated from the effect of the weight. We use only symmetric noise; multi-seed evaluation covers only ST-GCN and VAC isolated.

</details>


### [61] [Beyond Accuracy: Uncertainty-Guided Boundary Refinement for Reliable Biomedical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12892)
*Anima Kujur*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Two-stage RABR-Net refines boundary pixels in biomedical segmentation using a boundary-aware reliability representation to guide a gated residual refiner, improving boundary metrics with interpretable uncertainty cues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High Dice alone can mask boundary uncertainty; boundary reliability is crucial for clinically meaningful delineation in blood-smear images.

Method: Base UNet++-EfficientNet-B4 produces initial predictions; construct boundary-aware reliability from predictive entropy, TTA variance, margin uncertainty, probability gradients, and soft boundary cues; a gated residual refiner selectively corrects uncertain boundaries while preserving confident regions; evaluate with multiple metrics including calibration and risk-coverage.

Result: Held-out test shows small but significant gains: Dice 0.9602→0.9614, Boundary Dice 0.3448→0.3611, HD95 3.0354→2.8274; calibration unchanged; statistical tests confirm improvements; qualitative maps show gate focusing on uncertain boundaries.

Conclusion: RABR-Net offers an interpretable, reliability-focused boundary refinement strategy for boundary-sensitive biomedical segmentation, improving boundary-specific metrics without necessarily improving calibration.

Abstract: Accurate biomedical image segmentation requires not only high global overlap but also reliable delineation of clinically meaningful boundaries. In blood-smear microscopy, cytoplasm and nucleus contours provide the structural basis for downstream morphology analysis; however, deep segmentation models may remain uncertain or overconfident near ambiguous boundary regions even when achieving strong Dice scores. This work proposes a Reliability-Aware Boundary Refinement Network (RABR-Net), a two-stage framework for trustworthy image segmentation. A strong UNet++ EfficientNet-B4 base segmenter first produces initial class probabilities and logits. Predictive entropy, test-time augmentation variance, margin uncertainty, probability gradients, and soft boundary cues are then combined into a boundary-aware reliability representation. This representation guides a gated residual refiner that selectively corrects uncertain boundary pixels while preserving confident regions of the base prediction. The framework is evaluated using overlap accuracy, class-wise Dice, Boundary Dice, HD95/ASSD, calibration, risk--coverage analysis, robustness under image perturbations, qualitative correction maps, and paired statistical testing. On the held-out test set, the proposed method improves Dice from 0.9602 to 0.9614, Boundary Dice from 0.3448 to 0.3611, and HD95 from 3.0354 to 2.8274 compared with the cached base prediction. Statistical analysis confirms significant improvements in Dice, Boundary Dice, and HD95. Qualitative results show that the learned gate concentrates around uncertain cytoplasm and nucleus boundaries, and correction maps confirm localized boundary refinement. Although calibration does not automatically improve after refinement, the proposed framework provides an interpretable and reliability-focused strategy for boundary-sensitive biomedical image segmentation.

</details>


### [62] [Parallel Training Using a CNN-DNN Architecture for Accelerated Development of Diagnostic Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12902)
*Janine Weber-Hamacher,Astha Jaiswal,Philipp Fervers,Dorotya Móré,Athanasios Giannakis,Ricarda Fischbach,Andreas Michael Bucher,Rahil Shahzad,Jonathan Kottlors,Thorsten Persigehl,Axel Klawonn*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Hybrid CNN-DNN with image decomposition enables parallel training for CT-based COVID-19 pneumonia classification; 300 scans, 156 models, up to 31x training time reduction; test accuracy around 75%, indicating efficiency gains but modest diagnostic performance warrant further validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable efficient training of large DL models on expanding imaging datasets (e.g., pandemic scenarios) and to demonstrate parallelizable training that can accelerate model development across centers.

Method: Retrospective study using 300 CT scans from three German centers. A hybrid CNN-DNN network with image decomposition into subdomains (e.g., 4x4x1, 4x4x2) and localization was trained in 156 models across three architectures, yielding 12 patient-level COVID-19 diagnosis models. Parallel training was employed to measure efficiency gains. Performance metrics reported include training, validation, and test accuracies; time savings were quantified.

Result: DenseNet121 with 4x4x1 subdomains: train 88.78%, val 76.67%, test 76.03%. 3D CNN with 4x4x1: train 87.72%, val 76.82%, test 74.86%. Strongest training-time reduction observed for 3D CNN with 4x4x2 subdomains: 31x. Overall validation/test accuracies around 76% and ~75% respectively.

Conclusion: Parallel CNN-DNN with image decomposition improves training efficiency and supports rapid model development for pandemic preparedness, though external validation and more robust performance metrics are needed to confirm clinical utility.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence has shown promise in assisting radiologists in imaging-based diagnosis across a wide range of diseases. Efficient training of large deep learning models is essential to cope with extremely large data sets or dynamically growing disease data, like in a pandemic like situation. In this retrospective study, we collected 300 CT scans from COVID-19 and non-COVID-19 pneumonia patients from three different centers in Germany. We investigated a hybrid CNN-DNN network model based on image decomposition and localization that naturally supports parallel and efficient training of deep learning models. In total, 156 models with three different architectures were trained to capture features at different levels resulting in 12 patient-level COVID-19 diagnosis models. Diagnostic performance as well as time saving were measured.
  The highest accuracy was obtained from DenseNet121 and 3D CNN models with a parallel CNN-DNN approach, resulting in $88.78\%$ training, $76.67\%$ validation and $76.03\%$ test accuracy for the DenseNet121 with $4\times4\times1$ subdomains and $87.72\%$ training, $76.82\%$ validation and $74.86\%$ test accuracy, respectively, for the 3D CNN with $4\times4\times1$ subdomains. The strongest reduction in parallel training time by a factor of $31$ was observed for the 3D CNN model and $4\times4\times2$ subdomains.
  Our parallel training approach improves efficiency as well as performance enabling rapid model development, among others crucial for pandemic preparedness.

</details>


### [63] [PA-CDM: Position-Aware Character Detection Matching for Evaluating Handwritten Mathematical Expression Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12917)
*Shiliang Luo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes PA-CDM, a position-aware metric for handwritten math recognition, plus StructPerturb v2.0 benchmark and a cross-metric protocol; PA-CDM correlates best with human judgments among automatic metrics (rho=0.9535) and rivals a frontier LLM judge while remaining deterministic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation metrics for HMER either ignore positional errors (string-exactness, simple token counts) or are blind to where errors occur (render-based CDM, tree-edit distances). This leads to misalignment with human judgments and inconsistent error salience.

Method: Introduce PA-CDM, combining character-detection matching with a position-forest encoding and divergence-level weighting. Release StructPerturb v2.0 (1,340 controlled perturbation pairs across 15 type–intensity cells) and a cross-metric consistency protocol (sensitivity matrix, human study, LLM calibration). Conduct six-annotator study comparing seven automatic metrics against human judgments; benchmark frontier LLM judge.

Result: PA-CDM yielded the highest Spearman correlation with human judgments among seven automatic metrics (rho=0.9535, n=990). A frontier LLM judge achieved rho=0.9613 but has drawbacks: cost, nondeterminism, API dependence; PA-CDM approaches this performance deterministically and at zero marginal cost with diagnosable behavior.

Conclusion: Position-aware, deterministic metric PA-CDM, together with StructPerturb v2.0 and the cross-metric protocol, provides a reliable and interpretable framework for evaluating HMER and aligning automatic metrics with human judgments.

Abstract: Handwritten mathematical expression recognition (HMER) is conventionally scored by exact-match rates and string-similarity metrics that are blind to where an error occurs: two predictions with identical token-error counts receive identical scores whether they misplace a subscript or swap the operands of a fraction. Render-based character detection matching (CDM) aligns glyphs robustly but remains position-blind---on controlled fraction-operand swaps it scores 0.8595 where position-aware scoring yields 0.6253. Tree-edit metrics exhibit a complementary blind spot: rewrites outside the parser's normalization coverage are penalized as structural errors (0.8552 where render-based metrics score 1.0). We propose PA-CDM, a position-aware metric that couples character detection matching with position-forest encoding and divergence-level weighting; StructPerturb v2.0, a frozen benchmark of 1,340 controlled perturbation pairs across 15 type--intensity cells; and a cross-metric consistency protocol combining a sensitivity matrix, a human study, and LLM-judge calibration. In a six-annotator study, PA-CDM attains the highest correlation with human judgments among seven automatic metrics (Spearman rho=0.9535, n=990). A frontier LLM judge correlates slightly higher (rho=0.9613) but is costly, nondeterministic, and API-dependent; PA-CDM approaches it at zero marginal cost with deterministic, diagnosable behavior.

</details>


### [64] [Input Resolution Matters: Real-Time Object Detection Latency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12920)
*Qingyang Zhang,Fumio Machida,Laura Carnevali*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A resolution-aware convolution-based model of end-to-end latency in an object-detection pipeline, parameterizing stage distributions by image resolution and validating against fixed-parameter baselines; Normal and Gamma often provide the best distributional fits in the measured setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Characterize and predict end-to-end latency distributions under varying image resolutions in resource-constrained detectors, moving beyond fixed-parameter latency models to capture distributional behavior.

Method: Decompose latency into preprocessing, inference, and postprocessing; model each stage with parametric distributions (Exponential, Erlang, Normal, Gamma) whose parameters depend on source-image resolution; obtain the total latency distribution by convolving the stage densities; evaluate using the CDF; compare to fixed-parameter baselines using Kolmogorov–Smirnov, Anderson–Darling, and Cramér–von Mises statistics; experiments on YOLOv11n with NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX and COCO2017 across multiple resolutions.

Result: Resolution-aware parameterization improves distributional approximation relative to fixed-parameter baselines, particularly for Normal and Gamma models; fit quality is distribution-dependent.

Conclusion: Provides a lightweight, theoretically grounded framework to study resolution-dependent latency distributions in measured object-detection pipelines, enabling better understanding and design choices.

Abstract: We model total latency as the convolution of preprocessing, inference, and postprocessing distributions under a simplifying independence approximation, with selected stage parameters expressed as functions of source-image resolution. Under this assumption, the probability density of the total latency is the convolution of the stage-wise densities, and its cumulative distribution function (CDF) provides the distribution of end-to-end detection time. Each stage is modeled by a parametric distribution (e.g., Exponential, Erlang, Normal, Gamma), with parameters expressed as functions of the source-image resolution. Experiments with YOLOv11n on NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX using COCO2017 images across multiple resolutions assess the proposed models against fixed-parameter baselines using Kolmogorov Smirnov, Anderson Darling, and Cramér von Mises statistics. The results indicate that resolution-aware parameterization can improve distributional approximation in the measured setting, particularly for the more flexible Normal and Gamma models, while the quality of fit remains distribution dependent. Our contribution is a theoretically grounded and lightweight formulation for studying resolution-dependent latency distributions in a measured object detection pipeline.

</details>


### [65] [DementiaCare-Bench: A Modality-Validated Video Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12929)
*Afrouz Sheikholeslami,Yuankai Qi,Xuyun Zhang,Luping Zhou,Amin Beheshti,Quan Z. Sheng,Ming-Hsuan Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A benchmark (DementiaCare-Bench) evaluates video-language models on caregiving for dementia, using 56 training videos (94 clips, 9 BPSD categories) and 2023 transcript-grounded questions across four visual conditions to measure the required visual input. The study tests 12 VLMs and a LoRA-finetuned model (DemCare-VLM), revealing that many questions require ordered frames and that language models can rely on clinical knowledge alone; visual dependence can be improved via targeted fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the lack of benchmarks for VLMs that support caregiving decisions in dementia, where responses depend on triggering events and context.

Method: Construct DementiaCare-Bench with professionally produced caregiver videos, segment into clips, generate 2023 grounded questions using a multi-agent pipeline tied to verbatim transcripts, assess VLM performance under four visual conditions, and label questions by their minimum visual requirement. Evaluate 12 current VLMs, quantify performance, and apply a lightweight LoRA fine-tune (DemCare-VLM) to improve video reliance.

Result: Best overall accuracy about 85% across models, but much of this is from questions answerable via clinical knowledge alone. Accuracy drops by 17 points for questions requiring ordered clips; a leading open model performs at chance on judging caregiver appropriateness. DemCare-VLM, a LoRA-finetuned model, shifts video dependence from -3.3 to +4.5 points, indicating the benchmark can identify and repair reliance on visuals.

Conclusion: DementiaCare-Bench exposes how VLMs handle temporally and contextually grounded caregiving questions and demonstrates that targeted fine-tuning can improve video dependence, offering a pathway to more reliable VLM-assisted caregiving support.

Abstract: Dementia affects an estimated 57 million people worldwide, and for most families the hardest part of care is not memory loss but the behavioral and psychological symptoms of dementia (BPSD): agitation, wandering, resistance to care, sundowning. Understanding these symptoms requires more than recognizing the behavior itself; it also requires knowing what happened beforehand. The same behavior may call for a different response depending on its trigger. Video-language models (VLMs) could potentially support caregivers, yet no existing benchmark evaluates this capability. To fill this gap, we present DementiaCare-Bench: 56 professionally produced caregiver training videos segmented into 94 clips across nine BPSD categories, with 2023 questions generated by a multi-agent pipeline that grounds every clinical claim in a verbatim transcript span. Each question is then probed under four visual conditions and labelled by the least it requires, so its visual demand is measured rather than assumed. Measurement contradicts intent: we wrote 77.7% of the questions to require ordered frames, and 34.8% do. Across 12 current VLMs the pattern is uniform. The best reach 85% overall, but that average is carried by questions a language model can answer from clinical knowledge alone; accuracy falls by 17 points on average on questions that require the ordered clip, and a leading open model scores at chance on judging whether a caregiver's response was appropriate. A lightweight LoRA fine-tune, DemCare-VLM, moves video dependence from -3.3 to +4.5 points, so what the benchmark exposes can be repaired and not only measured.

</details>


### [66] [Fast and Faithful: Principled Conditional Flow Matching for Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12953)
*Shirin Shoushtari,Edward P. Chandler,Xiao Shi,Ulugbek S. Kamilov*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A principled measurement-conditional velocity field for flow-based imaging inverse problems, linking the velocity to the posterior mean and enabling explicit data-consistency updates; trained end-to-end with flow-matching, achieving state-of-the-art PSNR with far fewer function evaluations and enabling test-time distortion-perception control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing flow-based methods often condition on measurements or add separate data-consistency steps without enforcing forward-model consistency within the learned velocity field, leading to inefficiencies and potential fidelity gaps.

Method: Under linear interpolation, parameterize v(x_t,t,y) via the posterior mean E[x_1|x_t,y], characterize it as the unique minimizer of a variational objective with an explicit data-consistency term; show the velocity induces a probability flow from source to the measurement-conditioned posterior; split the objective to obtain operator-dependent data-consistency updates; train end-to-end with the flow-matching objective, without extra inference guidance.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art PSNR with ~50x fewer function evaluations than the strongest flow baseline; test-time control over distortion-perception trade-off achievable by varying sampling steps without retraining.

Conclusion: Principled measurement-conditioned velocity parameterization yields accurate, efficient flow-based solvers for imaging inverse problems with explicit data fidelity and flexible at test time.

Abstract: Flow matching approaches to imaging inverse problems commonly incorporate measurements in two ways. Conditioning-based approaches supply measurement-derived information as a network input, often through concatenation, while inference-guided approaches combine an unconditional velocity field with a separate data-consistency update. In these common formulations, the forward model is not explicitly enforced within the learned conditional velocity field. We propose a principled parametrization of the measurement-conditional velocity field to solve inverse problems. Under linear interpolation, we express the conditional velocity $v(x_t,t,y)$ in terms of the posterior mean $E[x_1 | x_t,y]$, and characterize that mean as the unique minimizer of a variational objective whose data-consistency term is explicit. We further prove that the velocity field defines a probability flow from the source distribution to the measurement-conditioned posterior. Splitting the variational objective yields a conditional velocity parameterization with operator-dependent data-consistency updates, which we train end-to-end under the flow-matching objective, with no additional guidance at inference. Our method achieves state-of-the-art PSNR with $50\times$ fewer function evaluations than the strongest flow baseline. Varying the sampling steps provides test-time control over the distortion-perception trade-off without retraining.

</details>


### [67] [Generative Retrieval for Unsupervised Text-Based Person Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12965)
*Mang Ye,Yucheng Ji,Yang Bai,Min Cao,Siyuan Chai,Bo Du,Min Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes GTR+ unsupervised TBPS with two-stage generation-then-retrieval, tiered description generation, adaptive GMM-based retrieval weighting, and LargeFine-Person dataset; shows improved performance and generalization; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tackle text-based person search (TBPS) without labeled image-text pairs, addressing reliance on supervised data; mitigate noisy pseudo-text during retrieval; provide scalable unsupervised pre-training resources and evaluation.

Method: Two-stage framework: generation stage with tiered description generation (base tier via Q&A for basic attributes; intermediate tier via inter-sample contrastive refinement; advanced tier via stylized expansion for diversity). Retrieval stage uses adaptive confidence-weighted learning with a Gaussian Mixture Model to separate clean/noisy image-text pairs using real-time image-text similarity and prior text-generation probability, enabling adaptive sample weights. A large-scale TBPS dataset, LargeFine-Person, provides high-quality fine-grained annotations for unsupervised pre-training.

Result: Experiments on multiple TBPS benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and generalization of GTR+ and the new LargeFine-Person dataset.

Conclusion: The work advances unsupervised TBPS by integrating structured, tiered text generation with robust, confidence-aware retrieval and by supplying a scalable dataset, enabling stronger pre-training and transfer in label-scarce settings; code is released.

Abstract: Text-based person search (TBPS) aims to retrieve images of a target person from a large image gallery based on a given natural language description. Most existing methods rely on supervised learning with manually annotated image-text pairs. In this paper, we explore unsupervised TBPS, with only unlabeled images. We propose GTR+, a two-stage generation-then-retrieval framework. In the generation stage, we introduce a tiered description generation framework designed to produce fine-grained and stylistically diverse textual descriptions through a three-tier sequential process. The base tier leverages an automated question-and-answer mechanism to generate basic visual attribute descriptions; the intermediate tier enhances fine-grained detail using an inter-sample contrastive mechanism; the advanced tier further enriches textual diversity via a stylized expansion mechanism. In the retrieval stage, to mitigate the impact of noisy pseudo texts, we develop an adaptive confidence-weighted retrieval learning framework. We model image-text pairs as clean or noisy using a Gaussian Mixture Model, calibrated by real-time image-text similarity and static text generation probability from the prior stage, yielding adaptive sample weights during training. Beyond that, we also contribute LargeFine-Person, a large-scale TBPS dataset with high-quality, fine-grained, and diverse textual annotations, enabling a practical and generalizable TBPS pre-training benchmark under unsupervised setting. Experiments on multiple TBPS benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and generalization of both GTR+ and LargeFine-Person. Code is available at: https://github.com/Flame-Chasers/GTR.

</details>


### [68] [Investigating Temporal Motion Features for Pose-to-Text Indian Sign Language Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12993)
*Manav Dhamecha,Praveen Kumar Chandaliya,Pruthwik Mishra*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Pretrained T5 scale and explicit motion features boost pose-to-text Indian Sign Language Translation (SLT); motion-augmented T5-small yields the largest BLEU gain, while T5-large attains the best chrF; overall 5th on the WSLP 2026 leaderboard; code/model weights publicly available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess how model size (T5-small/base/large) and explicit motion cues (frame-to-frame pose differences) affect SLT performance, to determine effective configurations for pose-to-text translation in the WSLP 2026 setting.

Method: Project pose sequences into T5 embeddings via a lightweight pose encoder; fine-tune the entire model to generate English text. Compare T5-small, T5-base, T5-large; introduce motion-augmented variant (T5-small + Motion) by adding explicit frame-to-frame pose differences to inputs. Evaluation on WSLP 2026 data with 5,334 test and 5,257 validation examples using BLEU, ROUGE, and chrF.

Result: T5-small achieves best BLEU and ROUGE among spatial-only models; T5-large achieves highest chrF; adding motion features (T5-small + Motion) provides the largest single improvement in BLEU over the spatial baseline and makes it the strongest model on this metric. The submitted system ranked 5th on the official WSLP 2026 SLT testing leaderboard. Source code and trained models are publicly available on GitHub and HuggingFace.

Conclusion: Explicit motion features substantially boost SLT performance, with motion-augmented T5-small delivering the strongest BLEU gains and overall competitive performance. T5-scale effects vary by metric (small for BLEU/ROUGE, large for chrF). Public release of code and models facilitates replication and further exploration in sign-language translation tasks.

Abstract: We investigate the effect of pretrained T5 model scale and explicit motion features on pose-to-text Indian Sign Language Translation (SLT) for the WSLP 2026 Shared Task. Pose sequences are projected into the embedding space of T5 through a lightweight pose encoder, with the complete model fine-tuned to generate English text. The shared task data used for this work consists of a test set with 5,334 examples and a validation set with 5,257 examples. We compare T5-small, T5-base, and T5-large, and additionally introduce a motion-augmented variant, T5-small + Motion, that adds explicit frame-to-frame pose differences to the input representation. T5-small achieves the best BLEU and ROUGE scores among the spatial-only models, while T5-large obtains the highest chrF score. Augmenting T5-small with motion features yields the largest single improvement observed in our study, substantially improving BLEU over the spatial-only baseline and making it the strongest model overall on this metric. Our submitted system ranked 5th on the official WSLP 2026 SLT testing leaderboard. The source code and trained models are publicly available on GitHub and HuggingFace.

</details>


### [69] [SV-Cine: Diagnosis-Conditioned Segmentation of Single Ventricle Physiology via Generative Data Augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12997)
*Lila Cunge,Yuehong Liu,Hang Xu,Thomas Coudert,Pierangelo Renella,J Paul Finn,William Hsu,Kim-Lien Nguyen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes SVP-specific cardiac MRI segmentation using synthetic 3D meshes and diagnosis-conditioned adaptation of a foundation model (SV-Cine) to improve LV/RV and myocardium segmentation; shows strong LV/RV Dice gains on internal data but limited external generalization, highlighting the value and limits of diagnosis priors and synthetic augmentation in rare, morphologically diverse conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the data scarcity and morphological heterogeneity of single ventricle physiology (SVP), which impede robust segmentation with conventional methods.

Method: 1) Data augmentation via synthetic 3D cardiac meshes generated with SDF4CHD and corresponding synthetic MRI via generative modeling. 2) SV-Cine: a diagnosis-conditioned adaptation of CineMA using FiLM (Feature-wise Linear Modulation) layers to inject patient-level diagnostic information into segmentation. 3) Evaluation on an internal SVP cohort; comparison to nnU-Net.

Result: SV-Cine yielded median Dice: LV 0.89 (IQR 0.80–0.91); RV 0.72 (IQR 0.54–0.84); RV improvement over nnU-Net by 0.39 Dice points. Median EF error for dominant ventricle = 5.55 percentage points (IQR 3.41–7.69). External cohort showed lower LV and myocardium Dice and comparable RV Dice, indicating limited generalization beyond the internal cohort.

Conclusion: Pretraining on large MRI datasets plus diagnosis-aware feature adaptation can tailor foundation models to specialized tasks like SVP segmentation, improving performance on internal data; however, external generalization is limited, underscoring the need for diverse training data and robust domain adaptation for rare, morphologically varied conditions.

Abstract: Single Ventricle Physiology (SVP) is a rare subtype of congenital heart disease characterized by the presence of a single functional cardiac ventricle with atypical anatomic configurations that challenge conventional image segmentation approaches. The scarcity of clinical data and the morphological diversity across SVP subtypes make the development of robust segmentation methods particularly difficult. To address these limitations, we propose a cardiac MRI segmentation framework focused on ventricular chambers and myocardium segmentation tailored for SVP. First, we introduce a data augmentation pipeline that generates synthetic 3D cardiac meshes using SDF4CHD and corresponding synthetic cardiac MRI through generative modeling. Second, we introduce SV-Cine, a diagnosis-conditioned adaptation of the foundation model CineMA that incorporates patient-level diagnostic information through Feature-wise Linear Modulation layers, enabling diagnosis-aware feature adaptation during segmentation. We evaluated the framework on an internal cohort with varying SVP subtypes. SV-Cine achieved median Dice scores of 0.89 (IQR: 0.80--0.91) for the left ventricle and 0.72 (IQR: 0.54--0.84) for the right ventricle, outperforming the strongest baseline, nnU-Net, by 0.39 Dice points on right ventricle segmentation. It also yields a median ejection fraction error of 5.55 percentage points (IQR: 3.41--7.69) for the dominant ventricle. Compared with the internal cohort, LV and myocardium segmentation performance was lower for the external cohort; whereas RV Dice scores were comparable for both cohorts. Our findings suggest that a pretrained foundation model can be adapted for highly specialized downstream tasks through usage of diagnosis priors while leveraging anatomic knowledge learned from large-scale MRI datasets during pretraining.

</details>


### [70] [Physics-Aware Video Generation via Agentic Planning and Graph-Guided Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13006)
*Minh-Loi Nguyen,Xuan-Vu Le,Thanh-Toan Do,Tam V. Nguyen,Minh-Triet Tran,Trung-Nghia Le*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A training-free framework (PhysPlan) improves video diffusion models' physical realism by shifting from stochastic visual interpolation to agentic physics simulation using an iterative VLM-based planner and object-centric gradient routing with dynamic hyperparameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VDMs often produce visually plausible but physically implausible sequences due to lack of physical reasoning; existing training-free methods rely on global gradient updates that can degrade backgrounds and poorly model dynamic state changes.

Method: PhysPlan uses a Visual-Language Model as an iterative cognitive simulator to generate a Chain-of-Visual-Thought representing kinematics and depth; performs object-centric test-time optimization guided by Object-Centric Gradient Routing that isolates kinematic edits and keeps passive background fixed; employs Kinetic Intensity Profiling to adapt hyperparameters to deformation severity.

Result: On PhyGenBench and Physics-IQ benchmarks, PhysPlan significantly outperforms both foundational and controllable VDM baselines in producing physically coherent video content.

Conclusion: PhysPlan demonstrates that training-free, physics-aware guidance with object-centric optimization and adaptive hyperparameters is a promising direction to enhance the physical understanding of video generation and reduce structural/physical inconsistencies.

Abstract: Video diffusion models (VDMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in synthesizing high-fidelity, photorealistic video content. However, they fundamentally lack an intrinsic understanding of physical laws and frequently produce visually appealing but causally illogical sequences characterized by structural hallucinations and physically implausible dynamics. Injecting physical awareness via training-free test-time optimization is a promising alternative, yet existing methods rely on global gradient updates and rigid scheduling heuristics that inadvertently corrupt passive backgrounds and fail to model complex dynamic state changes. To address this, we propose PhysPlan, a novel training-free guidance framework that shifts the paradigm from stochastic visual interpolation to agentic physics simulation. First, a VLM operates as an iterative cognitive simulator, decomposing multimodal inputs into a Chain-of-Visual-Thought to create a multimodal representation of kinematic trajectories and 3D depth geometries. Second, these signals drives an object-centric test-time optimization. Unlike prior training-free methods that rely on global gradients and rigid scheduling heuristics, PhysPlan introduces Object-Centric Gradient Routing to isolate kinematic modifications and completely lock the passive environment. Furthermore, our Kinetic Intensity Profiling dynamically parameterizes framework hyperparameters to accommodate the varying severity of physical deformations. Extensive evaluations on the PhyGenBench and Physics-IQ benchmarks demonstrate that PhysPlan significantly outperforms both foundational and controllable VDM baselines, offering a promising approach for improving the physical understanding of video generation.

</details>


### [71] [Pixel Decodability Is Not a Compression Signal: Causally Evaluating Importance Proxies for Visual KV-Cache Eviction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13012)
*Chenyu Zhou,Qiliang Jiang,Shuning Wu,Xu Zhou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Pixel-decodable visual content in vision-language model KV caches is largely task-inert; retention does not predict causal reliance; attention tracks utilization; encoder-free caches retain more; pixel-reconstructability is not a strong KV compression signal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Disentangle static pixel content retention from true causal use of KV caches in VLMs; assess whether pixel-level data in cache supports task performance.

Method: Quantify retention via a learned pixel-inversion decoder; assess causal use via single-super-patch KV ablation and teacher-forced drop in gold-answer log-probability; preregistered held-out design; compare encoder-free vs encoder-based architectures; analyze at super-patch vs token granularity; relate metrics across attention.

Result: Retention is task-inert and decoupled from attention and causal utilization; attention modestly tracks utilization; encoder-free retains ~2.7x more pixel-decodable content; at super-patch granularity KV eviction ~ random; at token granularity weak inverse-importance signal; pixel-decodable reconstructability not a competitive KV compression signal.

Conclusion: Pixel-decodable retention defines an informational axis orthogonal to functional KV content; architectural differences influence retention; practical implication: negative result for using pixel reconstruction as KV compression; attention remains a better proxy for downstream causal reliance.

Abstract: Vision-language models retain a substantial amount of pixel-decodable visual content in their visual key-value cache. We show, in our setting, that this retention is task-inert: across our preregistered tests, how much a unit retains never positively tracks whether the computation that answers the question causally relies on it. We measure retention with a learned pixel-inversion decoder and causal use with single-super-patch KV ablation, the teacher-forced drop in gold-answer log-probability, and relate the two within images under a preregistered, sign-calibrated, held-out design. Retention is decoupled from attention and, in a well-powered null, from causal utilization.
