Calibration increased Dice at $τ$ = 0.2 to 0.6178 $\pm$ 0.0025, increased false-positive (FP) support (11,836$\rightarrow$18,663), and reduced false-negative (FN) support (22,107$\rightarrow$17,536). This reflects near-threshold support calibration rather than improved biological predictive capability. Code is publicly available at https://github.com/jsudg436/longitudinal-proxy-forecasting.

</details>


### [123] [Beyond the Query: Do Retrieval Signals Improve Adaptive Multimodal RAG Routing?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12437)
*Qiaomu Li,Qiuyuan Zhang,Nong Ming*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Retrieval-state signals do not reliably improve routing decisions beyond a query-only baseline in document, audio, and video RAG; evaluation should credit routing value only relative to matched query-only controls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether retrieval-time signals add routing value when the query is already known, across modalities, by comparing query-only versus query+retrieval routers with fixed actions, router family, training, and evaluation.

Method: Systematically compare matched query-only and query+retrieval routing architectures across document, audio, and video RAG, holding actions, router family, training procedure, and evaluation fixed; evaluate on held-out final results and analyze whether retrieval signals predict later-step usefulness and improve RUN/SKIP decisions.

Result: Retrieval signals do not yield reliable routing improvements over the query-only baseline; some signals correlate with whether a later step helps, but this does not consistently translate into better RUN/SKIP decisions.

Conclusion: The value of retrieval-state features for routing is not guaranteed; they should be credited only if they outperform a matched query-only control. The findings emphasize methodology: demonstrate incremental value rather than assume it.

Abstract: Adaptive RAG often uses retrieval-time signals to decide whether another retrieval, reranking, or multimodal step should run. We ask whether these signals add routing value once the query itself is already known. Across document, audio, and video RAG, we compare matched query-only and query+retrieval routers while holding the optional actions, router family, training procedure, and evaluation fixed. On the held-out final evaluation, adding the tested retrieval signals does not produce a reliable routing improvement over the query-only baseline. Some retrieval signals are associated with whether a later step will help, but that predictability does not consistently lead to bet- ter RUN/SKIP decisions. The main lesson is therefore methodological: retrieval-state features should not be credited with routing value unless they improve over a matched query-only control. Our results do not show that routing or retrieval state is generally useless; they show that the incremental value of retrieval signals must be demonstrated rather than assumed.

</details>


### [124] [3D Digital Twin Visualization of Multiclass GRF-Based Gait Disorder Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12442)
*Nayoung Son,Minwoo Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An integrated GRF/COP-based gait classifier with ε-LRP explanations and Blender-based 3D visualization achieves high validation accuracy and solid test performance across healthy vs musculoskeletal impairment groups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To deliver accurate and interpretable gait classification, with local explanations and interactive visualization to support clinical decision-making and model transparency.

Method: Preprocess GRF/COP signals by normalizing over stance phase and standardizing using training-set statistics. Train a classifier to distinguish healthy gait from multiple musculoskeletal impairment groups using bilateral GRF and COP. Evaluate with a session-level split. Apply class-specific ε-LRP to identify positive/negative contributions across sides, signal components, and stance phases. Synchronize processed signals with model predictions in a Blender-based 3D visualization for sample-level inspection. Source code available in the referenced GitHub repository.

Result: Validation accuracy 99.00% and test accuracy 90.07% under a session-level split. Class-specific ε-LRP highlights positive/negative contributions across both sides, multiple signal components, and different stance phases. Blender-based visualization enables sample-level inspection of gait trials and classification results, enhancing model transparency.

Conclusion: The framework successfully integrates classification, explainability, and 3D visualization to improve transparency and interpretability of gait analysis, with accessible source code for replication.

Abstract: Automated gait analysis requires accurate classification and interpretable outputs. We propose an integrated framework for classifying healthy gait and multiple musculoskeletal impairment groups using bilateral ground reaction force (GRF) and center-of-pressure (COP) signals. The signals were normalized over the stance phase and standardized using training-set statistics. The model achieved a validation accuracy of 99.00\% and a test accuracy of 90.07\% under a session-level split. Class-specific $ε$-LRP identified positive and negative contributions across both sides, multiple signal components, and different stance phases. Separately, the processed GRF signals and model predictions were synchronized within a Blender-based 3D visualization, enabling sample-level inspection of gait trials and classification results. The proposed framework integrates classification, explainability, and 3D visualization to improve model transparency. The source code is available in the following repository: https://github.com/nyoico/grf-gait-3d-visualization.git

</details>


### [125] [SAGE-Loop: Reliable Closed-Loop LLM-Driven AutoML with Trial-and-Correction and Adaptive Ensembling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12455)
*Junquan Gu,Shibo Cui,Xiangfeng Luo,Hang Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAGE-Loop is a closed-loop, self-adaptive AutoML framework driven by LLMs that enables trial correction and evidence-based ensemble use, improving reliability and performance across supervised and unsupervised tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AutoML currently lacks feedback-driven correction during execution, leading to suboptimal or failed runs. A one-way pipeline with static ensemble decisions undermines reliability and effective use of model diversity.

Method: The framework performs multi-round generation and validation for trial-and-repair and adaptively selects ensemble strategies for both supervised and unsupervised tasks, integrating generation with model usage to create a closed-loop system.

Result: Empirical evaluation on 20 public datasets shows consistent improvements in accuracy/stability for classification, regression, and clustering, and demonstrates recovery from execution failures with robust pipeline behavior.

Conclusion: SAGE-Loop provides a reliable, closed-loop AutoML paradigm with LLMs, enabling adaptive optimization, resilience to failures, and unified handling of generation and model diversity across tasks.

Abstract: Automated machine learning (AutoML) is reshaping data-driven science and industrial practice, and as large language models are introduced into AutoML, pipeline reliability becomes as important as automation efficiency. However, existing AutoML still struggles to realize instant feedback and adaptive optimization during execution, so once a run drifts into a suboptimal or failed state, it lacks a process-level correction mechanism. The fundamental pathology lies in its one-way pipeline: intermediate failures are typically terminated or bypassed, while fixed paradigms often strengthen model generation but leave ensemble decisions static, weakening both execution reliability and the controlled use of structural diversity. This indicates that LLM-driven AutoML needs a closed-loop ability for trial-correction-improvement together with evidence-based use of model diversity. To this end, we propose SAGE-Loop, a reliable closed-loop, self-adaptive, LLM-driven AutoML framework that performs multi-round generation and validation for trial-and-repair, and adaptively selects ensemble strategies in both supervised and unsupervised tasks, thereby unifying how to generate with how to use models. Across 20 public datasets, SAGE-Loop consistently improves performance and stability on classification, regression, and clustering tasks. Additional results further show its ability to recover from execution failures and maintain robust pipeline behavior.

</details>


### [126] [A Differentially Private Federated Proximal Optimization Framework for Customer Churn Prediction in Heterogeneous Federated Telecom Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12470)
*Joydeb Kumar Sana,Subrata Chakraborty,M M Manjurul Islam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DP-FedProx provides a practical privacy-preserving federated learning solution for telecom churn prediction that mitigates non-IID data effects and achieves competitive accuracy with centralized models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Telecom churn prediction faces data privacy concerns and non-IID data across operators. Centralized ML risks data protection violations; FedAvg performance degrades on heterogeneous data; need for privacy-preserving, robust federated approaches.

Method: Empirical evaluation on two public churn datasets comparing FedAvg, DP-FedAvg, FedProx, and DP-FedProx against centralized and local baselines. SHAP analysis used for interpretability. Seven evaluation metrics employed.

Result: FedProx-based models outperform FedAvg under non-IID conditions. DP-FedProx achieves competitive accuracy relative to the best centralized model, with a small accuracy drop but with privacy guarantees. SHAP indicates DP-FedProx emphasizes revenue-related features, suggesting sensible feature prioritization for churn risk.

Conclusion: DP-FedProx offers a favorable trade-off between predictive performance and data privacy, addressing non-IID challenges in federated telecom churn prediction and providing practical deployment potential.

Abstract: Customer churn is one of the major issues in the telecommunication industry. To predict customer churn, conventional centralized machine learning approaches have been widely used. This centralized approach requires customer data to be stored in a central repository, which raises privacy concerns and may violate data protection regulations. Federated learning addresses this problem by allowing multiple telecom operators to collaboratively train a global model without transferring their raw customer data. However, real-world customer data are often heterogeneous (non-IID), which may negatively affect the performance of standard federated learning. Trained models can also suffer from privacy attacks. To address those issues, we propose a Differentially Private (DP) based Federated Proximal optimization (FedProx) framework. All experiments were performed on two publicly available telecom churn datasets. We trained Federated Averaging (FedAvg), DP-FedAvg, FedProx, and the proposed DP-FedProx framework. For baseline comparison, we also used several centralized and local models. To evaluate the models, we employed seven widely used evaluation metrics. The experimental results show that the FedProx based models consistently outperform the FedAvg based models. Compared with the best centralized model, the proposed DP-FedProx framework achieves competitive prediction performance with only a small reduction in accuracy while providing privacy guarantees. To explain our model, we conducted SHAP analysis which shows that DP-FedProx method priorities revenue group features. These results indicate that the proposed DP-FedProx framework provides a practical balance between prediction performance and data privacy protection.

</details>


### [127] [Temporal Recurrence Favors Fewer Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12531)
*Ivan Anokhin,Johan Obando-Ceron,Irina Rish,Sebastian Risi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Temporal recurrence reduces required per-step depth, shifting compute toward shallower within-step stacks while maintaining or improving performance under similar compute budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand how compute-allocation between within-step depth, expert width, and temporal recurrence affects performance, to determine whether recurrence changes the optimal depth within each step.

Method: Systematically vary within-step depth, expert width, and the number of parallel experts per layer across several compute budgets; compare best recurrent vs non-recurrent allocations under approximately matched per-step computation on Sokoban and autoregressive FineWeb language modeling.

Result: Temporal recurrence shifts the optimal compute allocation toward substantially fewer layers per step, with performance comparable to or better than non-recurrent configurations under matched per-step compute.

Conclusion: Temporal recurrence enables efficient use of temporal information, allowing shallower per-step depth while achieving similar or better performance; design guidance favors allocating compute to temporal recurrence rather than deepening per-step stacks.

Abstract: In streaming tasks, recurrent models can carry latent computation across time, allowing each update to build on representations produced earlier. This raises a basic question: once temporal recurrence provides sequential computation across steps, how much depth is still needed within each step? Prior work has shown that recurrence can make shallow models competitive. We instead study this question as a compute-allocation problem, varying within-step depth, expert width, and the number of parallel experts per layer across several compute budgets. For each budget, we compare the best observed recurrent and non-recurrent allocations and the performance they achieve under approximately matched per-step computation. Across Sokoban and autoregressive FineWeb language modeling, we find that temporal recurrence shifts the best observed compute allocation toward substantially fewer layers, with comparable or better performance.

</details>


### [128] [$\text{GSF-}χ$: Global Stereochemical Fields for Chiral Graph Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12532)
*Jiaqing Xie,Yuxin Wang,Xipeng Qiu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GSF-χ is a chiral-aware graph transformer that modulates all pairwise atom interactions via stereogenic units, yielding a Chiral-RoPE operator with a reflection-even phase and a handedness pseudoscalar χ that governs relative rotations; a C2 projection enforces enantiomer-consistency by separating mirror-even/odd signals. The authors prove even–odd decomposition and invariances, validate with coordinate-reflection audits, and report state-of-the-art gains on central-ECD tasks, Rotation, and Symbol under various supervision regimes, with robustness against mirror supervision scarcity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enantiomers share atoms, bonds, and pairwise distances yet can behave differently in chiral environments. Graph encoders must respect atom relabelings and proper rotations while remaining sensitive to reflections; conventional methods risk mixing or ignoring mirror information. The work aims to develop a chiral-equivariant encoder that treats stereogenic units as global modulators of all pairwise interactions.

Method: Introduce GSF-χ, a graph-transformer where central/axial stereogenic units create a reflection-even phase field over all atoms; a handedness pseudoscalar χ sets the direction of a relative rotation on latent query–key blocks, forming a Chiral-RoPE that inverts under reflection rather than staying fixed. A C2 projection separates mirror-even ECD peak counts/positions from mirror-odd peak signs. The authors provide an even–odd decomposition, and annotation-inversion, permutation, and unit-order identities under explicit canonical-role conditions; they perform property tests and a coordinate-reflection audit. They compare GSF-χ to baselines under equal budgets and conduct ablations, with primary results on central-ECD outputs, Rotation, and Symbol.

Result: GSF-χ improves Rotation by 12.6% and Symbol by 7.9% over the strongest baseline. Equal-budget controls attribute the Rotation advantage to global signed support rather than parameter count or edge count. The C2 projection yields exact enantiomer-pair consistency at small raw-accuracy cost under complete supervision and becomes predictive when mirror supervision is scarce.

Conclusion: The operator’s even–odd decomposition and the annotation-inversion/permutation/unit-order identities hold under the stated canonical-role conditions; property tests and coordinate-reflection audits validate the laws end-to-end. GSF-χ leads every central-ECD output and yields robust enantiomer-consistent predictions, with a measurable performance edge and resilience across supervision regimes.

Abstract: Enantiomers share atoms, bonds, and pairwise distances yet can behave differently in chiral environments, so molecular encoders must respect atom relabelings and proper rotations without becoming blind to reflection. We introduce GSF-$χ$, a graph transformer in which stereogenic units modulate all pairwise interactions rather than single out one atom as special. Each central or axial stereogenic unit creates a reflection-even phase field over all atoms, a handedness pseudoscalar $χ$ sets the direction of a relative rotation on latent query--key blocks, giving a \textbf{Chiral-RoPE} that reflection inverts rather than leaves fixed. A $C_2$ projection separates mirror-even ECD peak counts and positions from mirror-odd peak signs. We prove the operator's even--odd decomposition and its annotation-inversion, permutation, and unit-order identities under explicit canonical-role conditions; property tests and a coordinate-reflection audit verify the laws end to end. GSF-$χ$ leads every central-ECD output and improves axial Rotation and Symbol by $12.6\%$ and $7.9\%$ over the strongest baseline. Equal-budget controls attribute the Rotation advantage to global signed support rather than parameter or edge count; the $C_2$ projection yields exact enantiomer-pair consistency at a small raw-accuracy cost under complete supervision and becomes predictive when mirror supervision is scarce.

</details>


### [129] [Quality-Constrained Routing over a Fixed Pool of Quantized Mixture-of-Experts Instances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12550)
*Zhenghong Huang,Hongfan Wu,Jiheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Quantized MoE routing in a fixed resident pool uses Fragility-Weighted Perplexity (FWP) to predict risk and guide allocation, achieving higher offline throughput than baselines across 88 prompts and multiple W stories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantization damage varies across requests and bitwidths; pre-materialization and replication are memory- and reconfiguration-costly. The problem is routing within a fixed pool to maximize throughput while honoring a class-level quality budget.

Method: Introduce FWP computed from reference-instance prompt tokens, calibrated to candidate-instance degradation. Leverage an exact two-expert affinity–fragility decomposition and a conditional multi-layer top-k expansion with explicit bias, interaction, route-change, separability, and higher-order terms. Use window-level linear programming to produce a signed reduced-reward score that aligns with LP optimum under optimal prices and tie allocations (KKT-consistent).

Result: Empirical evaluation on 88 extended Qwen prompts across W2, W3, W4 with 6,144 expert blocks shows mean ΔNLL of 0.9437, 0.1832, 0.0513. With τ=0.1513, FWP-based allocation yields a 1.284× offline model-based multiplier versus 1.253× for request-agnostic mixing and 1.000× for static W4, amounting to ~2.5% relative gain for FWP.

Conclusion: FWP-guided routing within a fixed-pool, quantized MoE framework effectively predicts risk and improves throughput under a quality budget, offering a principled decomposition and optimization approach with tangible gains over baselines.

Abstract: Quantized Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) services can hold several pre-materialized instances of one base model, but quantization damage varies sharply across requests and bitwidths. Because instance materialization and replica counts consume memory and require slow reconfiguration, we treat them as upstream provisioning decisions and study routing within a fixed resident pool. Within this fixed-pool boundary, we route each request to maximize modeled throughput under a class-level expected quality-degradation budget and measured instance capacities. To predict this request-specific risk, we introduce FWP (Fragility-Weighted Perplexity), computed from prompt tokens on a reference-instance prefill and calibrated to candidate-instance degradation. Underlying FWP is an exact two-expert affinity--fragility decomposition and a conditional multi-layer top-$k$ expansion whose bias, interaction, route-change, separability, and higher-order terms remain explicit. Using these calibrated risks, a window-level linear program yields a signed reduced-reward score that is KKT-consistent with the LP optimum under optimal prices and primal-feasible tie allocation. On 88 extended Qwen prompts, complete W2, W3, and W4 instances quantizing all 6,144 expert blocks incur mean $Δ$NLL of $0.9437$, $0.1832$, and $0.0513$. Under the same population and $τ=0.1513$, FWP allocation reaches a $1.284\times$ offline model-based multiplier versus $1.253\times$ for request-agnostic mixing and $1.000\times$ for static W4, an incremental $2.5\%$ relative FWP gain.

</details>


### [130] [TokenMapper: A Step Toward Interoperable Speech Token Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12563)
*Tal Kozakov,Tal Rosenwein,Eliya Nachmani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TokenMapper enables direct token-to-token translation across heterogeneous discrete speech tokenizers, enabling cross-model interoperability without waveform decoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous token vocabularies and codebooks impede cross-model communication in neural audio codecs, forcing waveform decode/re-encode and causing latency and potential information loss.

Method: A direction-aware framework that maps between dissimilar token spaces (including single and multi-codebook representations) under a shared effective token rate, enabling discrete-domain translation without waveform reconstruction.

Result: Cross-model translation quality approaches native reconstructions (2.5-6.8 percentage points WER gap); MOS for TokenMapper outputs is 2.29–4.39; end-to-end latency reduced by 4.8–94.5% versus waveform bridging, up to 972 ms per utterance.

Conclusion: TokenMapper enables practical cross-model speech token interoperability in the discrete domain, avoiding intermediate waveform reconstruction while preserving quality and reducing latency.

Abstract: Neural audio codecs discretize speech into token sequences, but the resulting token spaces differ in vocabulary and codebook structure, preventing direct communication across models. This limitation affects applications such as conversational voice agents and speech to speech translation systems where multiple speech models must interact. As a result, transferring information between speech systems typically requires decoding to waveform audio and re-encoding with a second tokenizer, increasing latency and introducing potential information loss. To address these limitations, we present TokenMapper, a direction aware framework for direct token to token translation between heterogeneous speech tokenizers in the discrete domain. TokenMapper supports structurally mismatched token spaces, including mappings between single codebook and multi codebook representations, under a shared effective token rate. Experiments on GLM-4-Voice, MiMi and DualCodec show consistent cross model performance. Specifically, translation WER approaches native reconstructions within 2.5-6.8% absolute WER, human MOS for TokenMapper outputs ranges from 2.29 to 4.39, following the same direction level trends as UTMOS and end to end latency is reduced by 4.8-94.5% relative to waveform bridging, reaching up to 972 ms per utterance. These results provide a practical step toward cross model speech token interoperability without intermediate waveform reconstruction.

</details>


### [131] [SCOPE-OPSD: Fisher-Conditioned Privileged Subspaces for On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12579)
*Yunmeng Chen,Kunyu Wang,Peihan Li,Yi Wang,Shuyin Xia,Yi Liu,Xinyong Cheng,Dehui Wang,Xiangyong Zhai,Yanxing Liu,Song Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Structured (SCOPE-OPSD) improves on on-policy self-distillation by using a Fisher-conditioned privileged subspace to capture the final-layer discrepancy, without extra inference-time modules, outperforming pure OPSD and a matched random control across 25/50/75/100-step budgets on Qwen model scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address whether the aligned final-layer discrepancy provides a second supervision channel in OPSD and design a test that isolates channel quality from auxiliary strength by controlling geometry and distortion.

Method: Project the teacher-student residual onto a frozen rank-64 factor derived from residual covariance and language-model-head Fisher sensitivity (Fisher-conditioned subspace). Reuse existing OPSD forwards; no rollouts or extra inference modules. Include a matched Random control with preserved factor rank/nonzero spectrum and per-arm gradient-RMS calibration to isolate orientation effects.

Result: Across 25/50/75/100-step trajectories for Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B, Structured never falls below Pure OPSD, with 11 of 12 model-checkpoint gains and a tie at 4B step 25. Structured also beats matched Random in 10 of 12 cases. At step 75 on 1.7B, Structured exceeds matched Random by 1.39 Macro Avg@12 in two runs. Cross-fitting shows 4.40x greater captured privileged-gap than matched orientation.

Conclusion: A compact Fisher-conditioned privileged subspace provides a reliable second supervision channel for short-budget OPSD, improving over baseline OPSD and resisting random-oriented baselines.

Abstract: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) scores student-generated prefixes with a solution-conditioned self-teacher, yet transfers supervision only through next-token probabilities. We ask whether the aligned final-layer discrepancy offers a useful second channel, and how to test that channel without confusing its geometry with auxiliary strength. SCOPE-OPSD projects the privileged teacher-student residual onto a frozen rank-64 factor estimated from residual covariance and language-model-head Fisher sensitivity. It reuses the forwards already required by OPSD and adds neither rollouts nor inference-time modules. A matched Random control preserves the structured factor's rank and nonzero spectrum and uses per-arm gradient-RMS calibration, isolating the effect of the data-dependent orientation. Across the complete 25/50/75/100-step trajectories for Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B, Structured is never below Pure OPSD, with strict gains in 11 of the 12 model-checkpoint combinations and an exact tie at 4B step 25. Structured also exceeds matched Random in 10 of the 12 combinations. At step 75 on Qwen3-1.7B, Structured exceeds matched Random by 1.39 Macro Avg@12 points in each of two independent training reruns. A cross-fitted diagnostic also shows 4.40 times greater held-out privileged-gap capture than the matched random orientation. The results support a compact, Fisher-conditioned privileged subspace for short-budget OPSD.

</details>


### [132] [Clustering-Based Balanced Sampling and Allocation with Data Parallelism for High-Performance Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12584)
*Hyunjin Kim,Youngeun Nam,Jaemin Han,Wonhyeok Choi,Jae-Gil Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CluSTER introduces a cluster-aware, DP-aware sampling framework to create a representative, reduced instruction-tuning dataset that preserves data distribution and coverage, achieving significant speedups with minimal accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Instruction-tuning datasets for LLMs are often large, redundant, and imbalanced. Under data parallelism (DP) across multiple GPUs, naive large-batch fine-tuning overemphasizes overrepresented groups and undercovers informative but underrepresented samples. A data-efficient, balanced reduction is needed.

Method: Gradient-space clustering to curate a representative reduced dataset and DP-aware balanced allocation to ensure dual-level coverage across clusters and workers. Weighted updates preserve the original data distribution during training.

Result: By reducing redundant computation while maintaining model quality, CluSTER achieves up to 69.6% training time reduction with almost no accuracy loss across multiple instruction-tuning datasets.

Conclusion: CluSTER reduces redundant computation and improves training stability without compromising model quality; code is publicly available (GitHub).

Abstract: Instruction-tuning datasets for large language models (LLMs) are often large, redundant, and imbalanced, limiting efficient adaptation. Naive large-batch fine-tuning repeatedly includes overrepresented sample groups while weakly covering underrepresented but informative ones, especially under data parallelism (DP) across multiple GPUs. We propose CluSTER, a Cluster-aware balanced Sampling framework for Training Efficient data Reduction in DP instruction tuning. CluSTER curates a representative reduced dataset through gradient-space clustering and DP-aware balanced allocation, ensuring dual-level coverage across clusters and workers, while preserving the original data distribution by weighted update. As a result, CluSTER reduces redundant computation and improves training stability without compromising model quality. Across multiple instruction-tuning datasets, CluSTER reduces training time by up to 69.6% with almost no accuracy loss compared to prior sampling and data reduction methods. Code is available at https://github.com/kaist-dmlab/CluSTER.

</details>


### [133] [Where Decoder Cosine Similarity Fails for SAE Feature Flow Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12591)
*Hendrik Droste,Christian Medeiros Adriano,Kathrin Korte,Holger Giese*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A transition atlas of feature interactions in MLP updates using sparse autoencoders reveals many strong ablation-effect triples, but low state/update decoder cosine similarities, suggesting complex feature flow and potential diagnostic value for steering model updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand how representation-update interactions drive downstream features during fine-tuning, model editing, and alignment; provide interpretable diagnostics via SAEs.

Method: Construct a transition atlas of triples s_k + u_j -> t_l, where residual-state feature s_k and MLP-update feature u_j jointly predict target residual t_l; validate via ablation of decoded update feature; experiments on Pythia-160M (L7->L8) and Gemma-3-4B (L21->L22).

Result: Pythia run yields 38,125 strong ablation-effect transitions; 88.0% of them have both state-target and update-target decoder cosine similarity < 0.7. Gemma cross-model check confirms directional consistency; 53.6% of strong-effect triples have low cosine similarity. Update cosine recovers many strong Gemma effects but not a full atlas.

Conclusion: Feature-flow atlases can diagnose representation-update mechanisms and guide tooling for steering updates; future work to extend across layers/models/SAE families and validate more complex patterns.

Abstract: Foundation models are increasingly adapted through fine-tuning, model editing, and alignment procedures while retaining previously acquired capabilities. Understanding the internal computations that support these adaptations is therefore becoming increasingly important for continual model evolution. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) provide interpretable feature dictionaries for residual-stream activations and sublayer outputs, but it remains unclear how state features and update features interact to produce downstream residual features. In this work, we focus on MLP updates as a first test case. We construct a transition atlas of triples $s_k + u_j \rightarrow t_\ell$, where a residual-state feature and an MLP-update feature jointly predict a target residual feature, and validate candidate triples by ablating the decoded update feature. In a 20M-token Pythia-160M $L_7 \rightarrow L_8$ run, we find 38,125 strong ablation-effect transitions, but 88.0% have both state-target and update-target decoder cosine similarity below 0.7. As a preliminary cross-model check, a run of 20M-token Gemma-3-4B $L_{21} \rightarrow L_{22}$ causally validates only the top 30,000 ranked candidate triples by ablating the decoded update feature, and 53.6% of strong-effect triples have both state-target and update-target decoder cosine similarity below 0.7. The Gemma result is directionally consistent with Pythia, but weaker, since update-target cosine recovers many of the strongest Gemma effects and the run is not a full-atlas causal validation. Ultimately, our results suggest that feature flow atlases can serve as diagnostics of representation-update mechanisms and thereby inform tools for steering model updates. Future work will validate more complex patterns across layers, models, and SAE families.

</details>


### [134] [Poisson-Corrector Complexity Bounds for Moreau--Yosida Unadjusted Langevin Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12594)
*Yuchen Xin,Zhihua Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Analyzes the classical MYULA for π ∝ e^{-f-g} with f m-strongly convex and g convex Lipschitz. Establishes a bound on W2 distance between the Moreau-smoothed target π_λ and the MYULA invariant law, yielding O(h)+(log factors) with mild λ dependence; derives total iteration complexity ~ε^{-4/3} to reach ε accuracy in W2, by combining discretization error with Moreau bias. Uses discrete Poisson corrector, active-trace estimates, and a shared-noise coupling approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide provable convergence and explicit iteration bounds for the Moreau-regularized unadjusted Langevin algorithm on composite targets with a smooth strongly convex part and a nonsmooth convex part, including the impact of smoothing and discretization on accuracy.

Method: Apply Moreau smoothing to g with parameter λ, study the invariant law of the discrete MYULA with step size h, prove a W2 bound between π_λ and π_{λ,h} that scales as O(h) + Ō(h^{3/4}) under 0 < h(L_f + λ^{-1}) ≤ c; combine with Moreau bias to control full-accuracy target π; employ a discrete Poisson corrector, active-trace estimates, and shared-noise bounds for two-point curvature to obtain results.

Result: A precise bound: sqrt(m) W2(π_λ, π_{λ,h}) = O(h) + Ō(h^{3/4}) with logarithmic dependence on λ^{-1} in constants; total iterations to reach sqrt(m) W2(μ_N, π) ≤ ε are Ō~(ε^{-4/3}) for fixed parameters; demonstrates finite-time convergence guarantees for MYULA on composite targets.

Conclusion: The work delivers explicit, parameter-dependent convergence guarantees for MYULA under a composite-objective framework, quantifying the trade-off between smoothing (λ), discretization (h), and bias (Moreau approximation). The approach—discrete Poisson corrector and shared-noise coupling—offers a blueprint for analyzing similar Langevin schemes; potential extensions include relaxing strong convexity or Lipschitz assumptions and optimizing the λ–h balance.

Abstract: We study the classical Moreau--Yosida unadjusted Langevin algorithm (MYULA) for $π(\,\mathrm{d} x)\propto e^{-f(x)-g(x)}\,\mathrm{d} x$, where $f\in C^2(\mathbb{R}^d)$ is $m$-strongly convex with $L_f$-Lipschitz gradient and $g:\mathbb{R}^d\to\mathbb{R}$ is convex and globally $G$-Lipschitz. For the Moreau-smoothed target $π_λ$ and the MYULA invariant law $\widehatπ_{λ,h}$, we prove \[
  \sqrt m\,W_2(π_λ,\widehatπ_{λ,h})
  =O(h)+\widetilde O(h^{3/4}) \] under $0<h(L_f+λ^{-1})\le c$, with only logarithmic dependence on $λ^{-1}$ in the error coefficients. Combining this estimate with the Moreau approximation bias yields $\widetilde O(\varepsilon^{-4/3})$ iterations to achieve $\sqrt m\,W_2(μ_N,π)\le\varepsilon$, for fixed model parameters and initialization. The proof combines a discrete Poisson corrector with active-trace estimates and a shared-noise bound for the exact--Euler two-point curvature.

</details>


### [135] [SIMS: Scale-Invariant Merit-Function-Based Scalarization for Multi-Task Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12599)
*Zebin Chen,Fei Xing,Yang Chen,Hua Liu,Andy HF Chow,Yuhua Qian,Yu Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Scale-Invariant Merit-function-based Scalarization (SIMS) for multi-task learning uses a logarithmic transformation to achieve scale invariance in MOO, preserving weak Pareto optimality and enabling a smooth surrogate; empirically achieves state-of-the-art results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-task learning, different task losses often differ by orders of magnitude. Traditional merit-function scalarizations depend on loss scales, causing domination by larger-scale tasks. A scale-invariant scalarization is needed to faithfully approximate Pareto optimal solutions.

Method: Propose SIMS with a transformation-induced merit function that converts the multi-objective problem into a single objective. Prove that the scale-invariance requirement uniquely determines the transformation to be logarithmic. Show that the transform preserves weak Pareto optimality and admits a smooth surrogate with controllable approximation error.

Result: Empirical evaluation on representative multi-task benchmarks shows SIMS consistently outperforms existing scalarization methods and achieves state-of-the-art performance.

Conclusion: The scale-invariant, logarithmic transformation is theoretically unique for MOO in MTL and practically effective, offering both Pareto-preserving properties and improved empirical performance.

Abstract: Multi-task learning (MTL) requires navigating unavoidable trade-offs among competing objectives. This paradigm is frequently formulated as multi-objective optimization (MOO), where the scalarization is favored to reduce an MOO problem to a single objective. We empirically find that existing merit-function-based scalarization approaches are sensitive to the relative scales of different objectives in practical MTL, where task losses commonly differ by orders of magnitude. The optimization process often favors objectives with larger scales even though the underlying Pareto optimal solutions remains invariant to rescaling (i.e., multiplying an objective by a positive constant). To address this issue, we propose Scale-Invariant Merit-function-based Scalarization (SIMS) for MTL. Specifically, SIMS adopts a transformation-induced merit function to convert the MOO problem of MTL to a single objective that renders optimization invariant to the magnitudes of losses. Theoretically, we prove that the requirement for scale invariance uniquely determines this transformation to be logarithmic. We further show that this general transformation-induced merit function preserves weak Pareto optimality and admits a smooth surrogate with controllable approximation error. Extensive experiments on representative multi-task benchmarks demonstrate that SIMS consistently outperforms existing scalarization methods and achieves state-of-the-art performance.

</details>


### [136] [Correlation-Guided Fast Machine Unlearning via Hessian Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12620)
*Ayushi Thakur,Ruchir Gupta,Amit Kumar Jaiswal,Prayag Tiwari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Efficient unlearning framework for machine learning in security contexts that identifies correlated training data and applies a closed-form update with Hessian damping to remove their influence. It achieves an 82× wall-clock speedup over standard influence-function unlearning and a 0.01 accuracy improvement over baselines, with theoretical guarantees and numerical stability. Evaluated on seven dataset-architecture pairs, including CIFAR-100 with ResNet-50, demonstrating strong forgetting (e.g., MEM attack S = 0.660; tug-of-war score = 0.950).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need to support privacy (GDPR right to be forgotten) and security applications by removing the influence of compromised or adversarial training data. Existing unlearning methods are computationally prohibitive due to repeated Hessian-inverse-vector computations, hindering deployment in real-world security systems (e.g., IDS, spam filters, threat intel).

Method: Identify correlated data points in the training set and apply a closed-form parameter update rule derived theoretically to remove their influence. Use Hessian damping to ensure numerical stability. Provide theoretical guarantees of forgetting. No full retraining required, enabling fast unlearning suitable for production environments.

Result: 82× wall-clock speedup over standard influence-function unlearning; 0.01 absolute improvement in accuracy over state-of-the-art baselines; strong forgetting evidenced by evaluation on seven diverse dataset-architecture pairs, including CIFAR-100 with ResNet-50; membership inference attack success rate of 0.660; tug-of-war score of 0.950.

Conclusion: The proposed framework delivers practical, provable data unlearning for security-sensitive ML deployments, balancing fast response, theoretical guarantees, and maintained utility, scalable to large models and datasets.

Abstract: The increasing adoption of machine learning in network and distributed security systems has created an urgent need for mechanisms that can selectively and efficiently remove the influence of specific training data to eliminate compromised or adversarial data points from production models. Privacy regulations such as GDPR's \emph{right to be forgotten} also pose similar requirements. However, existing approximate unlearning techniques remain computationally prohibitive for deployment in real-world security systems, as they require repeated expensive Hessian-inverse-vector computations for each data point removal, creating a bottleneck when processing multiple related requests in scenarios such as intrusion detection systems, spam filters, and threat intelligence platforms. Thus, we introduce a computationally efficient unlearning framework that identifies correlated data points in the training set and applies a theoretically derived closed-form parameter update rule, achieving an $82\times$ wall-clock speedup over standard influence function unlearning while preserving model utility with a $10^{-2}$ improvement in accuracy over state-of-the-art baselines. Our method establishes theoretical guarantees and ensures numerical stability through Hessian damping. Our evaluation across seven diverse dataset architecture combinations, including large-scale CIFAR-100 with ResNet-50, demonstrates superior forgetting effectiveness, with membership inference attack success rates of 0.660 and tug-of-war scores of 0.950.

</details>


### [137] [Geometric-to-Semantic Spherical Transfer Learning for Cortical Sulci Labeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12627)
*Saeb Tounsi,Joël Chavas,Pietro Gori,Vincent Frouin,Denis Rivière,Jean-François Mangin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A self-supervised geometric pretraining on cortical surface curvature/depth with a soft topological prior injector enables a semantic sulcal labeling task in a data-scarce regime, outperforming fully supervised baselines (mean Dice 0.77) and yielding largest gains for variable/tertiary sulci.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Capture the complex topology of many sulci on the cortical surface while facing extreme label scarcity (N=62 subjects) and high topological ambiguity for small sulci.

Method: Pretrain a spherical encoder on large unlabeled data (UK Biobank, ~30k subjects) using a curvature/depth-based self-supervised task. Introduce downstream semantic sulcal lines as inputs via a soft-initialized Topological Prior Injector to bridge the geometric-to-semantic gap without catastrophic forgetting.

Result: Outperforms fully supervised models trained from scratch with a mean Dice of 0.77. Local analysis shows geometric priors help most on variable/tertiary sulci (up to 14.8% gain).

Conclusion: Geometric priors learned from vast unlabeled cortical surface data are beneficial for identifying rare and topologically ambiguous sulcal structures when labeled data are scarce, and a soft semantic injector effectively transfers these priors to a semantic segmentation/labeling task.

Abstract: Deep learning on cortical surfaces faces a dilemma: capturing the complex topology of over 60 nomenclature-dependent sulci per hemisphere requires high-capacity models, yet the extreme scarcity of expert annotations ($N=62$ subjects) inevitably causes overfitting. Standard supervised approaches fail to generalize in this data-scarce regime, particularly for variable and small sulci where topological ambiguity is high. To overcome this limitation, we introduce a Geometric-to-Semantic Spherical Transfer Learning framework.
  First, we leverage massive unlabeled data (UK Biobank, $\approx$30,000 subjects) to pre-train a spherical encoder using a locally-optimized strategy. By relying solely on continuous surface features (curvature and depth), the relevance of this pre-training is confirmed by the model's ability to detect localized and rare topological traits, such as sulcal interruptions. The downstream labeling task, however, introduces extracted sulcal fundi (lines) as an explicit semantic input. To bridge this dimensional domain gap (from purely geometric to semantic) without causing catastrophic forgetting, these anatomical lines are integrated into the pre-trained backbone via a soft-initialized Topological Prior Injector.
  Our experiments demonstrate that this approach outperforms fully supervised baselines trained from scratch, achieving a mean Dice of 0.77. Crucially, a local analysis reveals that the self-supervised geometric priors yield the largest performance gains on variable and tertiary sulci (up to 14.8%), confirming that learning the cortex shape is highly beneficial for identifying its rarest parts.

</details>


### [138] [Explaining Time Series Forecasting with Horizon-Resolved Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12639)
*Seunghan Lee,Jun Seo,Jaehoon Lee,Junhyeok Kang,Sangjun Han,Sungdong Yoo,Minjae Kim,Tae Yoon Lim,Dongwan Kang,Hwanil Choi,Soonyoung Lee,Wonbin Ahn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HRX introduces horizon-resolved explanations for time-series forecasting, providing a per-step importance map rather than a single vector. It includes an estimator, horizon-aware evaluation protocol, and a rank criterion to decide when to resolve the horizon axis. The approach reveals low-dimensional, shared explanations across steps, and improves explanations across backbones/datasets. Code available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing TS explanation methods output a single importance vector for all forecast steps, assuming uniform dependence on past values. This misses step-specific dependencies, reducing interpretability and fidelity.

Method: HRX adds a horizon axis to explanations. It offers: (1) an estimator that extracts step-wise maps from any differentiable forecaster without modifying the backbone; (2) an evaluation protocol that tests horizon relevance by removing inputs ranked highly for a specific step and measuring impact; (3) a rank criterion to predict whether resolving the horizon axis is worthwhile for a given TS. The step-wise dependence is shown to be low-dimensional, with explanations composed from a small set of shared maps.

Result: Empirical results show that the horizon axis improves explanations across different backbones and datasets, and that the proposed estimators remain compatible with prior explanation methods. The shared maps imply reduced complexity as forecast length grows. Code is publicly available.

Conclusion: HRX provides a simple, effective plug-in to obtain horizon-resolved explanations for time-series forecasts; step-wise dependencies are low-dimensional, enabling compact explanations and improved interpretability across models and datasets.

Abstract: Recent advances in explaining time series (TS) models have produced methods that identify which past values a prediction depends on. However, most existing methods return a single importance vector, assuming that every predicted step depends on the same past values. In this paper, we show that this assumption does not hold, as different forecast steps depend on different past values. Motivated by this observation, we propose Horizon-Resolved eXplanation (HRX), which adds a horizon axis to the explanation, so that every forecast step receives its own importance map. HRX is a simple yet effective plug-in framework with three components: 1) an estimator that reads these maps out of any differentiable forecaster without modifying the TS backbone, 2) an evaluation protocol that validates the horizon axis by measuring how much a single forecast step changes when the inputs an importance map ranks highest are removed, and 3) a rank criterion that predicts in advance whether the axis is worth resolving on a given TS. We further show that this step-wise dependence is low-dimensional, as the explanations of all steps are built from a few shared maps whose number does not grow with the forecast length. Extensive experiments across various backbones and datasets show that the improvement comes from the horizon axis and holds for estimators of previous explanation methods. Code is available at https://github.com/seunghan96/HRX.

</details>


### [139] [Distortion of AI Alignment Revisited: RLHF is a Decent Utilitarian Aligner](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12651)
*Kazusato Oko,Annie Ulichney,Nika Haghtalab,Han Bao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A refined theoretical study of RLHF distortion under reward clipping in pluralistic user preferences. It shows that exponential distortion is not inherent to RLHF but arises from mismatch between data-generating preferences μ and the KL reference policy π_ref; with tight bounds in different KL-regularization regimes, the distortion scales as ~Θ(βB+β) where B bounds log density ratio between μ and π_ref; no-mismatch yields O(β). Practically, using on-policy data or fine-tuning on data close to μ is recommended to maximize average utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Question the claim that RLHF distortion can grow exponentially with the Bradley–Terry temperature β in heterogeneous settings; determine whether such degradation is fundamental or due to distribution mismatch, and provide principled bounds and guidance.

Method: Theoretical analysis under the Bradley–Terry model for user preferences with reward clipping, comparing the data-generating distribution μ to the KL reference policy π_ref across multiple KL regularization regimes; derive tight upper and lower bounds on distortion, introducing B as an upper bound on log density ratio log(dμ/dπ_ref).

Result: In a representative regime, distortion is ~Θ(βB + β). When μ = π_ref (no distribution mismatch), RLHF achieves the optimal distortion O(β) up to constants. Exponential degradation is not fundamental; distortion is governed by distribution mismatch and KL regularization strength.

Conclusion: To maximize average utility with RLHF, prefer on-policy sampled preference data or fine-tuning on data from a source close to μ, reducing distribution mismatch and yielding tighter distortion bounds; the findings reconcile prior claims of exponential growth with a mismatch-based explanation.

Abstract: While Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is the standard paradigm for aligning large language models with human preferences, its effectiveness in pluralistic settings has been called into question. Notably, recent work by Gölz et al. (2025) demonstrated that the \textit{distortion} -- defined as the multiplicative gap between the average user utility of the RLHF policy and the optimal average utility -- can scale exponentially with the Bradley-Terry temperature parameter $β$ when users have heterogeneous preferences. In this work, we present a fine-grained analysis of the distortion of RLHF with reward clipping and demonstrate that such exponential degradation is not a fundamental property of the algorithm but rather a consequence of distribution mismatch between the distribution generating preference data ($μ$) and the KL reference policy ($π_{\mathrm{ref}}$). To this end, we establish tight upper and lower bounds on the distortion of RLHF across multiple regimes of the KL regularization strength. We show that in a representative regime, under the Bradley-Terry model, the distortion is $\tildeΘ(βB + β)$, where $B$ is an upper bound on the log density ratio between $μ$ and $π_{\mathrm{ref}}$. In particular, when there is no distribution mismatch (i.e., $μ= π_{\mathrm{ref}}$), RLHF achieves the optimal distortion of $O(β)$ up to a constant. Our results suggest that, to reasonably maximize average utility with RLHF, it is preferable to use on-policy sampled preference data or to fine-tune before RLHF on data from a source close to $μ$.

</details>


### [140] [ProactiveBench: Can Streaming Video Models Really Interact Like Humans?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12658)
*Kaixuan Du,Xin Wan,YuKun Wang,Hang Zhang,Meng Cao,Dai Guan,Ming Chen,Ni Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProactiveBench evaluates proactive, interval-based responses in streaming video understanding with six subtasks to measure timing, response, and silence decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the gap in current reactive benchmarks by evaluating not just what the model chooses to answer, but when it should respond after a target event, enabling human-like interactive timing.

Method: Propose ProactiveBench, a benchmark that interrogates models at 1-second stream intervals without an explicit response cue. Six subtasks vary trigger ambiguity and timing tolerance: Event Sensitivity (combines response and silence rates on the same recording); four window-based subtasks that differentiate early, in-window, and missed responses; and Duplicate Counting that penalizes omissions and repetitions.

Result: Across four of six evaluated systems, premature responses exceed missed responses, revealing a notable gap in temporal decision-making for proactive interaction; the benchmark exposes timing-related weaknesses not captured by reactive evaluations.

Conclusion: ProactiveBench fills a methodological gap by quantifying when a model should respond in a streaming context, highlighting the need to improve temporal decision-making for human-like proactive interaction and offering a structured suite of tasks to diagnose where responses go awry.

Abstract: Streaming video understanding requires models to process continuous multimodal input while maintaining temporal context. Existing evaluations are predominantly reactive: they query a model at a selected timestamp and therefore do not assess when it should respond. Proactive interaction instead requires monitoring a standing request, responding within an appropriate interval after the target event, and otherwise remaining silent. We introduce ProactiveBench, which evaluates models at one-second stream intervals without an explicit response cue. Its six subtasks vary trigger ambiguity and timing tolerance. Event Sensitivity geometrically combines response and silence rates on the same recording; four window-based subtasks distinguish early, in-window, and missed responses; and Duplicate Counting penalizes omissions and repetitions. Premature responses outnumber missed responses for four of the six evaluated systems, revealing a substantial gap in the temporal decision-making required for human-like interaction.

</details>


### [141] [SIFPBPNet: A Dual-Path Network for Wearable and Cuffless Blood Pressure Estimation via Individualized Steady-state Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12690)
*Shuailong Tang,Xiaoyu Li,Donglin Xie,Wei Chen,Guangpu Zhu,Yelei Li,Yali Zheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dual-path SIFPBPNet for cuffless BP estimation from PPG, combining a steady-state path via GAT on multi-day history with an instantaneous path for short-term dynamics, fused by cross-attention; achieves state-of-the-art MAE on large wearable dataset and demonstrates strong transferability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome population heterogeneity and one-to-many mapping between PPG and BP by leveraging long-term individual-specific steady-state patterns and short-term instantaneous dynamics.

Method: A dual-path architecture: Steady-state Feature Path (SFP) uses Graph Attention Network (GAT) to extract long-term, individual-specific features from multi-day PPG history. Instantaneous Feature Path (IFP) captures short-term dynamics from current PPG segments and integrates steady-state priors via cross-attention. The outputs are fused to predict BP.

Result: On a large-scale wearable dataset, SIFPBPNet achieves MAE of 8.57 mmHg for systolic BP and 5.97 mmHg for diastolic BP, outperforming state-of-the-art models. Incorporating the SFP into various backbone architectures yields 2.8–13.1% relative MAE reductions for systolic BP.

Conclusion: The SFP module demonstrates strong generalizability and plug-and-play transferability, highlighting its potential for accurate cuffless BP monitoring.

Abstract: Continuous and cuffless blood pressure (BP) monitoring using photoplethysmography (PPG) is of great interest for low-cost and personalized cardiovascular health management. However, significant population heterogeneity and the "one-to-many mapping" problem, where similar waveforms across individuals correspond to different BP levels, limit the accuracy of conventional population-based models. To address this challenge, we propose a dual-path architecture termed SIFPBPNet, which separately represents steady-state and instantaneous features, through a Steady-state Feature Path (SFP) and an Instantaneous Feature Path (IFP). The SFP employs a Graph Attention Network (GAT) to extract individual-specific and long-term characteristics from multi-day historical PPG trajectories. In parallel, the IFP captures short-term dynamics from current PPG segments and incorporates the steady-state prior via a cross-attention mechanism. Experiments on a large-scale wearable dataset demonstrate that SIFPBPNet achieves a Mean Absolute Error (MAE) of 8.57 and 5.97 mmHg for systolic and diastolic BP, respectively, outperforming state-of-the-art models. Furthermore, the SFP module consistently improves performance when integrated into various backbone architectures, yielding 2.8-13.1% relative MAE reductions for systolic BP. These results highlight the strong generalizability and plug-and-play transferability of the SFP module, underscoring its great potential for accurate cuffless BP monitoring.

</details>


### [142] [Write on Paper and Get the Online Digital Trace:\newline A New Era for Handwriting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12702)
*Florent Imbert,Yann Soullard,Eric Anquetil,Tanja Harbaum,Alexey Serdyuk,Fabian Kress,Tim Hamann,Peter Kampf*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A low-cost, paper-based handwriting digitization method using a sensor-enabled pen and embedded AI to reconstruct handwriting traces in real time without an external reference system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digitize handwriting on regular paper with rich haptics and no latency, without relying on specialized substrates or external tracking, addressing the lack of affordable, effective solutions.

Method: Develop a sensor-equipped pen and embed AI-powered software to reconstruct trajectories in real time. The approach combines hardware development with adaptive AI techniques for trajectory reconstruction and processing.

Result: No empirical results are reported; the paper outlines a framework and proposed pipeline for real-time trace reconstruction, aiming to demonstrate feasibility.

Conclusion: The work seeks to advance automated handwriting trace reconstruction and enable seamless bridging between traditional handwriting on paper and digital capture through an integrated pen–AI system.

Abstract: Capturing the digital trace of handwriting usually requires a specific stylus and a compatible substrate, be it a capacitive touchscreen, an ElectroMagnetic Resonance (EMR) tablet as used in Wacom systems or special paper. While writing on regular paper offers rich haptics, no latency and is well known for improving information retention, no low-cost and widely accepted, effective solution exists to digitize such a pen trace. The challenge is to accurately track the pen's trajectory without an external reference system while allowing unrestricted freedom of pen movement across a surface. We propose an innovative solution that combines a digital pen, advanced artificial intelligence algorithms, and adaptive AI techniques to reconstruct the digital trace of handwriting. Our approach integrates hardware development, focusing on a sensor-equipped pen, with software innovations to optimize trajectory reconstruction and processing in real time using an embedded AI. This work aims to advance the state-of-the-art in automated trace reconstruction of handwriting, enabling a seamless connection between traditional handwriting on paper and capturing the trace digitally.

</details>


### [143] [InRTL: Effective Intra-Inter Interaction Learning for Relational Tables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12712)
*Weichen Li,Ken Zhong,Zheng Wang,Li Pan,Jianhua Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: InRTL is a unified, scalable framework for relational table learning that captures both intra-table row interactions and inter-table dependencies across PK-FK links using a column-aware encoder, Transformer-based self- and cross-attention, and efficient linearized attention with heterogeneous GNNs; demonstrated on 10 datasets and 24 tasks with open-source code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Relational table learning involves complex dependencies within a table and across related tables. Prior work lacks a principled, unified framework that explicitly models both intra- and inter-table interactions, particularly across PK-FK connections, at scale.

Method: Introduce a column-aware table encoder to initialize row representations, followed by Transformer-based self-attention for intra-table learning and cross-attention for inter-table learning. To improve scalability, employ linearized attention and heterogeneous graph neural networks to simplify computations while handling schema heterogeneity and PK-FK links.

Result: Extensive experiments on ten datasets spanning 24 real-world tasks show the effectiveness of InRTL, outperforming baselines or achieving competitive results. Code is released at the provided GitHub link.

Conclusion: InRTL provides a principled, scalable framework that effectively models both intra-table and inter-table interactions in relational tables, enabling improved predictive performance on diverse tasks and real-world datasets.

Abstract: Relational table learning has recently emerged as an important research direction for modeling multiple tables connected through primary key-foreign key (PK-FK) relationships. Despite recent advances, a principled modeling framework tailored to this task remains underexplored. In this paper, we propose Intra-Inter Relational Table Learning (InRTL), a unified framework that explicitly models dependencies both within and across relational tables. Specifically, InRTL formalizes two complementary interaction patterns: intra-table interactions, describing associations among rows within the same table, and inter-table interactions, describing dependencies between rows across PK-FK-linked tables. To model these dependencies, we develop a column-aware table encoder to generate initial row representations, followed by Transformer-based self-attention and cross-attention modules for intra-table and inter-table learning, respectively. To further improve scalability, InRTL incorporates linearized attention and heterogeneous graph neural networks to simplify the self-attention and cross-attention operations. Extensive experiments on ten datasets covering 24 real-world tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach. Code is available at https://github.com/W1nterFloW/InRTL.

</details>


### [144] [Physics-Guided Synthetic High-Frequency Ultrasound Generation for Skin Layer Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12735)
*Junkyung ju,Kyungho Yoon,Minwoo Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Physics-guided synthetic HFUS data for skin-layer segmentation enabling transfer learning; pretraining on synthetic data with real-domain fine-tuning achieves performance close to real-only training and improves several architectures; demonstrates transferability while highlighting remaining domain gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address scarcity of densely annotated high-frequency ultrasound data for deeper skin structures; enable training of segmentation models across multiple skin layers by providing dense labels in synthetic data.

Method: Create multilayer acoustic skin phantoms with layer-specific properties; simulate HFUS images using k-Wave to generate paired images, dense layer masks, and metadata; use synthetic data for pretraining four CNN architectures, then fine-tune on real Mendeley HFUS data.

Result: Synthetic pretraining plus real fine-tuning yields real-domain performance comparable to real-only training; mean Dice/IoU improved in 3 of 4 architectures; code and data released.

Conclusion: Physics-guided synthetic HFUS can provide transferable anatomical cues for real skin layer segmentation; gap between synthetic and real appearance persists and should be reduced to unlock greater gains.

Abstract: High-frequency ultrasound (HFUS) enables noninvasive visualization of superficial skin structures, but automated skin-layer analysis is limited by the scarcity of densely annotated data. Existing real HFUS datasets commonly provide annotations for superficial targets such as the epidermis and subepidermal low-echogenic band (SLEB), while dense labels for deeper structures such as dermis, subcutaneous tissue, fascia, and muscle are rarely available. We propose a physics-guided synthetic HFUS generation framework for skin layer segmentation. The framework constructs multilayer acoustic skin phantoms, assigns layer dependent acoustic properties, and uses k-Wave simulation to generate paired synthetic HFUS images, dense layer masks, and simulation metadata. To evaluate whether the generated data provide transferable supervision, we use it for downstream segmentation pretraining and fine-tune the models on real Mendeley HFUS data. Synthetic pretraining followed by real fine-tuning achieved real-domain performance comparable to real-only training and improved mean Dice/IoU in three of four evaluated trainable architectures. These results suggest that physics-guided synthetic HFUS images contain transferable anatomical and textural cues for real-domain skin layer segmentation, although further reduction of the synthetic-real appearance gap is needed to enable greater gains. The code and data are available at: https://github.com/Finn-02/synthetic-hfus-skin-layer-segmentation.

</details>


### [145] [Optimizing for the decision not the prediction: an exploration of Smooth Net Benefit as a training objective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12752)
*Koen M. F. Gorgels,Lasai Barreñada,Maarten van Smeden,Ben Van Calster,Ewout W. Steyerberg,Wouter A. C. van Amsterdam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: σNB is a differentiable training objective for Net Benefit that aims to align model optimization with threshold-specific clinical utility; evaluated on multiple models and datasets with mixed results; modest gains in logistic regression and limited benefit for flexible models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between likelihood-based training (e.g., NLL) and decision-relevant utility that depends on risk thresholds, by optimizing a differentiable approximation of Net Benefit.

Method: Introduce Smooth Net Benefit (σNB) as a differentiable proxy for Net Benefit and train logistic regression, generalized additive models (GAMs), and XGBoost using three Hessian implementations. Evaluations conducted on the Framingham cardiovascular risk dataset and 44 TabZilla datasets (72 dataset–threshold combinations).

Result: σNB training did not consistently improve Net Benefit in Framingham. On TabZilla, LR: Net Benefit rose from 0.5669 (NLL) to 0.5765 (σNB) — mean diff 0.0096 (95% CI -0.0001 to 0.0193). GAMs: 0.5921 (NLL) to 0.5625 (σNB) — diff -0.0296 (95% CI -0.0721 to 0.0129). XGBoost: NLL = 0.6745, σNB ranged 0.6723–0.6735. Additionally, σNB gains correlated with XGBoost’s advantage over NLL-trained LR. 

Conclusion: σNB is not a universal replacement for NLL training; its benefits are context-dependent and appear modest in limited-model settings (e.g., LR) but limited for flexible models like GAMs and XGBoost. Further exploration of decision-focused objectives is warranted where standard likelihood training may miss decision-relevant structure.

Abstract: Objective Prediction models are commonly trained using objectives such as Bernoulli negative log-likelihood (NLL), although downstream clinical decisions may depend on specific risk thresholds. We introduce Smooth Net Benefit ($σ$NB), a differentiable approximation of Net Benefit designed to align model training with threshold-specific clinical utility.
  Materials and Methods We evaluated $σ$NB as a training objective for logistic regression, generalized additive models (GAMs), and XGBoost with three Hessian implementations. Experiments used the Framingham cardiovascular risk dataset and 44 TabZilla datasets comprising 72 dataset-threshold combinations.
  Results $σ$NB training did not consistently improve Net Benefit in Framingham. Across the TabZilla benchmark, mean standardized Net Benefit for logistic regression increased from 0.5669 with NLL to 0.5765 with $σ$NB (mean difference 0.0096, 95% CI -0.0001 to 0.0193). For GAMs, mean standardized Net Benefit decreased from 0.5921 to 0.5625 (mean difference -0.0296, 95% CI -0.0721 to 0.0129). For XGBoost, NLL achieved 0.6745 compared with 0.6723--0.6735 across $σ$NB implementations. In logistic regression, $σ$NB gains were positively associated with the performance advantage of XGBoost over NLL-trained logistic regression.
  Discussion The effect of $σ$NB was context dependent, with modest gains concentrated in logistic regression and little benefit for more flexible model classes. This suggests that decision-focused optimization may be most useful when limited model flexibility leaves greater scope for improvement.
  Conclusion Our results do not support $σ$NB as a general replacement for NLL training, but support further investigation of decision-focused objectives in settings where conventional likelihood-based training may not adequately capture decision-relevant structure.

</details>


### [146] [Curriculum-Based Adversarial Heterogeneous Agent Reinforcement Learning for Autonomous Quad-Copter Landing in Maritime Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12758)
*Allan Minh-Tam Nguyen,Sree Showrya Kotala,Stefan Banioi-Crijman,Kurt Driessens,Rico Möckel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HARL-AC with curriculum and adversarial wind learns robust multi-agent policy for UAV capture by a ship arm, achieving strong in-distribution performance and superior generalization to unseen sea states compared to domain randomization, with safer recovery but slightly longer timeouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Maritime UAV recovery is challenging due to wind turbulence and ship motion; evaluating RL-based cooperative control to improve reliability and safety across varying sea states.

Method: Train heterogeneous-agent PPO (HAPPO) with curriculum and an adversarial wind agent (HARL-AC) in NVIDIA Isaac Lab; compare with curriculum-based domain randomization and a single sea-state baseline; evaluate on sea states 0/4/5 (in-distribution) and 7/8/10 (out-of-distribution); measure success rates, crash rates, and timeouts.

Result: In-distribution, HARL-AC comparable to domain randomization with up to 97.5% success. Out-of-distribution, HARL-AC generalizes better, up to 16% higher median success at sea state 10, and up to 14% lower crash rates vs domain randomization. Adversarial policy also yields more cautious behavior, increasing timeouts by <3%.

Conclusion: Adversarially trained HARL-AC improves generalization to unseen sea states and yields safer recovery under severe conditions, with a modest increase in timeout latency.

Abstract: Recovering unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) in maritime environments is challenging due to wind turbulence and ship-deck motion, making it a valuable test case for alternative control and learning approaches as conventional landing approaches often become unreliable. We study simulated mid-air capture of quadrotor UAVs by a ship-mounted robotic arm, learning robust cooperative control policies with Heterogeneous-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization (HAPPO) Reinforcement Learning. We train with HAPPO using a curriculum and an adversarial wind agent (HARL-AC) in NVIDIA Isaac Lab, and compare the obtained control policies against those generated through curriculum-based domain randomization and a benchmark trained on a single sea state. In-distribution evaluation on sea states $0/4/5$ shows comparable success for HARL-AC and domain randomization of up to $97.5\%$. On out-of-distribution sea states $7/8/10$, HARL-AC generalizes better, achieving up to $16\%$ higher median success rate at sea state 10, and substantially lower crash rates of up to $14\%$ compared to the domain randomization policy. Furthermore, we show that the adversarially trained policy shows more cautious behavior, slightly increasing timeouts by $<3\%$, but yields safer recovery behavior in severe, unseen conditions.

</details>


### [147] [Convergence of Stochastic Gradient Methods under Heavy-Tailed Noise and Hölder Smoothness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12785)
*Misbah Uz Zaman,Anirbit Mukherjee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Extends nonconvex SGD theory to Hölder-smooth objectives (gradient Hölder with exponent s in (0,1]) under heavy-tailed gradient noise with bounded α-moment (α in (1,2]). Provides three convergence guarantees: (i) SGD with rate O(T^{-s/(1+s)}) for α≥1+s; (ii) δ-regularized clipping with rate O(T^{-2s(α-1)/((1+s)(2α-1))}); (iii) standard clipping recovering (i) for α≥1+s and giving a new rate O(T^{-2s(α-1)/((α-1)+s(2α-1))}) for α<1+s (first such guarantee for SGD-based methods in this regime).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical convergence analyses assume Lipschitz-smooth objectives and finite-variance gradient noise, often violated in practice. The work investigates nonconvex stochastic optimization under weaker, more realistic conditions: Hölder smooth gradients and heavy-tailed noise, and studies how gradient clipping can restore convergence guarantees.

Method: The authors analyze stochastic gradient methods under (L,s)-Hölder gradient continuity (s in (0,1]) and gradient noise with bounded α-th moment (α in (1,2]). They derive explicit convergence rates for standard SGD, δ-regularized clipping (δ-GClip), and standard gradient clipping (G-Clip) under varying regimes of α (relative to 1+s).

Result: Three main results: (1) SGD achieves rate O(T^{-s/(1+s)}) when α≥1+s; (2) δ-GClip attains stationary rate O(T^{-2s(α-1)/((1+s)(2α-1))}) under the same α condition; (3) G-Clip matches the SGD rate for α≥1+s and, in the very heavy-tailed regime α<1+s, yields rate O(T^{-2s(α-1)/((α-1)+s(2α-1))})—the first guarantee of this kind for any SGD-based method.

Conclusion: Relaxed smoothness and noise assumptions are compatible with convergence guarantees for SGD and clipping-based methods. δ-GClip and G-Clip extend robustness to heavy-tailed noise, with precise rates depending on α and s. The results bridge theory and practice for training nonconvex models under realistic stochastic disturbances.

Abstract: Classical convergence guarantees for stochastic gradient methods typically assume Lipschitz-smooth objectives and finite-variance gradient noise, both frequently violated in practice. In contrast, we study nonconvex stochastic optimization under the joint relaxation of these assumptions: objectives with $(L,s)$-Hölder continuous gradients, $s\in(0,1]$, and gradient noise satisfying only a bounded $α$-th moment condition for $α\in(1,2]$. We establish three convergence results. Firstly, that standard SGD converges at rate $O(T^{-s/(1+s)})$ whenever $α\ge1+s$, extending the classical nonconvex SGD rate to heavy-tailed noise and Hölder smoothness simultaneously. Secondly, we analyze $δ$-regularized gradient clipping ($δ$-GClip), a provable trainer of wide and deep nets, and establish a stationarity rate of $O(T^{-2s(α-1)/[(1+s)(2α-1)]})$ under the same condition. Thirdly, we analyze standard gradient clipping (G-Clip) and show that it recovers the above rate for $α\ge1+s$ while in the very heavy-tailed regime $α<1+s$, it has a convergence rate $O(T^{-2s(α-1)/[(α-1)+s(2α-1)]})$ --- the first convergence guarantee in this regime for any stochastic gradient based method.

</details>


### [148] [VertiFuseX: Generalizable Financial Forecasting via Multi-Stream Temporal Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12793)
*Aashish Bohra,Vivek Vijay*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Vertical- fused penultimate-layer multi-scale temporal representations (VertiFuseX) for stock-price forecasting; end-to-end training with fixed hyperparameters; demonstrates strong accuracy and efficiency gains across 15 years of global indices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial time series are non-stationary and noisy; existing models rely on rigid decision-level fusion, ad hoc hyperparameters, and compressed final-layer outputs, causing information loss, overfitting, and limited cross-market generalization.

Method: Hybrid LSTM architecture that stacks and reweights penultimate features from LSTM, Bi-LSTM, and St-LSTM branches, includes a parallel DNN stream, and trains end-to-end under a fixed hyperparameter configuration; evaluated with strict chronological out-of-sample testing (last 365 trading days held out) on 15 years (2010–2024) of closing prices from 10 global equity indices; model has 675k parameters, 2.6 MB memory, and 1.5 ms/sample inference.
