Result: MAPE reduced by 30–54%; MAE and RMSE improved by over 40%; outperforms seven state-of-the-art models across 33 metric-dataset comparisons; ablation studies show penultimate-layer fusion is superior to final-layer fusion and decision-level ensembling; gradient-based saliency highlights mid-range dependencies at lags 9–15 days; economic validation via algorithmic trading simulations indicates reduced maximum drawdowns and superior risk-adjusted returns.

Conclusion: VertiFuseX provides a lightweight, interpretable, deployment-ready framework for robust financial forecasting with enhanced accuracy, generalization, and efficiency.

Abstract: Stock price prediction remains challenging due to the non-stationary and noisy nature of financial time series. Existing deep learning models often rely on rigid decision-level fusion, ad hoc hyperparameter tuning, and compressed final-layer outputs, causing information loss, overfitting, and limited cross-market generalization. We propose VertiFuseX, a hybrid LSTM architecture using penultimate-layer vertical fusion of multi-scale temporal representations. VertiFuseX stacks and reweights penultimate features from LSTM, Bi-LSTM, and St-LSTM branches, integrates a parallel DNN stream, and jointly optimizes all components via backpropagation under a fixed hyperparameter configuration. This preserves richer intermediate temporal information across scales. Evaluated on 15 years (2010-2024) of closing prices from 10 global equity indices using strict chronological out-of-sample testing with the final 365 trading days held out, VertiFuseX achieves 30-54% MAPE reductions and over 40% improvements in MAE and RMSE versus LSTM-based baselines, and outperforms seven state-of-the-art models across 33 metric-dataset comparisons. Ablation studies confirm penultimate-layer fusion drives these gains over final-layer fusion and decision-level ensembling. Gradient-based saliency analysis shows consistent emphasis on mid-range dependencies at lags 9-15 days. Economic validation via algorithmic trading simulation under extreme market regimes shows reduced maximum drawdowns and superior risk-adjusted returns. With 675k parameters, a 2.6 MB memory footprint, and 1.5 ms/sample inference latency, VertiFuseX offers a lightweight, interpretable, deployment-ready framework for robust financial forecasting.

</details>


### [149] [RunningTensor: Generalizing Linear Attention to Higher-Order Recurrent States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12814)
*Luca Herranz-Celotti,Vincent Guigue*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RunningTensor extends memory from a matrix to an order-o tensor, enabling linear-time sequence processing with higher-order interactions. Order-2 equals linear-attention; order-3 is demonstrated as a proof of concept. This increases memory capacity from O(W^2) to O(W^o) while keeping linear complexity in sequence length T. It outperforms baselines on synthetic tasks and improves downstream language and retrieval tasks after pretraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Linear attention and state-space models are limited by a second-order (matrix) memory, restricting the range of interactions they can model. A higher-order recurrent memory could raise expressivity and memory capacity without sacrificing linear-time computation.

Method: Introduce RunningTensor: a memory tensor of order o updated by a rank-1 outer product and read by contracting against o−1 vector queries. Order-2 recovers linear attention; the paper presents order-3 as a proof‑of‑concept, preserving both recurrent and parallel forms and maintaining linearity in sequence length T, with memory capacity scaling as O(W^o).

Result: On synthetic multi-query associative recall, RunningTensor outperforms linear-attention and SSM baselines. Pretraining further improves performance on language-understanding and non-synthetic retrieval tasks, suggesting the higher-order state provides useful additional memory capacity beyond matrix-valued state.

Conclusion: Higher-order recurrent states (beyond matrices) can offer meaningful memory capacity gains and improved performance, while maintaining linear-time sequence processing; order-3 serves as a viable starting point to explore richer, higher-order memory in sequence models.

Abstract: Linear attention and state-space models provide linear-time sequence modeling, but their recurrent memory remains a second-order tensor (a matrix), limiting the order of interactions that can be represented in the state. We introduce the RunningTensor, which generalizes this memory to an order-$o$ tensor, updated by a rank-1 outer product and read by contracting against $o-1$ vector queries. Order $2$ recovers linear attention; we study order $3$ as a proof of concept, retaining both recurrent and parallel forms while remaining linear in sequence length $T$ and improving working memory capacity from $\mathcal{O}(W^2)$ to $\mathcal{O}(W^o)$. On synthetic multi-query associative recall, RunningTensor outperforms linear-attention and SSM baselines. After pretraining, it also improves performance on language-understanding and non-synthetic retrieval tasks, suggesting that higher-order recurrent state can provide useful additional memory capacity beyond matrix-valued state.

</details>


### [150] [GenOR-Twin: A Semantic Middleware for Integrating Operational Discourse with Mathematical Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12863)
*Rahimeh Neamatian Monemi,Shahin Gelareh,Lubin Cui,Nelson Maculan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GenOR-Twin uses LLMs as semantic translators to bridge unstructured operational logs with exact combinatorial optimization via a dynamic constraint-injection mechanism, enabling real-time reconfiguration of the optimization model while maintaining feasibility guarantees. It features bidirectional Digital Twin coupling and an adaptive policy to switch between low-cost repairs and full re-optimization, demonstrated across six optimization domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the translation gap between qualitative, unstructured operational data and rigorous optimization, while preserving feasibility guarantees and enabling real-time adaptation within a Digital Twin framework.

Method: A neuro-symbolic architecture where LLMs act as semantic translators rather than solvers; a dynamic constraint injection mechanism translates qualitative disruption events into formal mathematical constraints, allowing real-time modification of the optimization problem's feasibility region. bidirectional coupling updates the virtual model state from observations and propagates optimized decisions back into a Knowledge Graph, enabling Digital Twin synchronization. An adaptive decision policy selects between low-complexity schedule repairs and full re-optimization based on available system slack. Generalization is demonstrated across six optimization domains.

Result: The approach is demonstrated to generalize across six distinct optimization domains, converting static models into resilient systems capable of adapting to operational uncertainty and variability, while retaining the feasibility guarantees of exact combinatorial methods and maintaining Digital Twin synchronization.

Conclusion: GenOR-Twin provides a principled framework for turning static optimization models into adaptive, resilient systems by leveraging LLM-based semantic translation, dynamic constraint injection, and bidirectional digital twin coupling, with broad applicability across multiple domains.

Abstract: We introduce GenOR-Twin, a neuro-symbolic framework that bridges the translation gap between unstructured operational logs and rigorous mathematical optimization. Our architecture uniquely positions Large Language Models as semantic translators rather than direct solvers, ensuring that the system retains the feasibility guarantees of exact combinatorial methods. { \color{red}We design a dynamic constraint injection mechanism (the runtime translation of qualitative disruption events into formal mathematical constraints) that allows the system to structurally modify the optimization problem's feasibility region in real-time based on qualitative human inputs. The resulting bidirectional coupling---where operational observations update the virtual model state and optimized decisions are reflected back into the Knowledge Graph---satisfies the synchronization requirement of a proper Digital Twin. The framework features an adaptive decision policy} that automatically selects between low-complexity schedule repair and full re-optimization by analyzing the available system slack. Finally, we demonstrate the generalization of this approach across six distinct optimization domains, {\color{red}turning static models into resilient systems that adapt to the operational uncertainty and variability of real-world environments.}

</details>


### [151] [What an odour descriptor corpus can and cannot measure: valence, attenuation, and the ceiling of the public record](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12875)
*Stylianos Kampakis,Fabio Rovai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Descriptor words across four Pyrfume corpora disagree on when to apply a label; pooling descriptors without calibration is limited by large cross-corpus variation, especially for valence; direct measurement of valence is necessary to recover true pleasantness, and only a subset of raters suffices to saturate beyond structure-plus-descriptor data. A crosswalk and twenty theorems are released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether a shared descriptor vocabulary measures the same construct across different corpora and determine the ceiling of what pooling descriptors can achieve in machine olfaction. Identify sources of variance and the role of valence in explaining pleasantness.

Method: Audit four Pyrfume corpora, condition on molecule to apply McNemar's test for corpus effects; quantify heterogeneity via I^2 and labeling breadth via z-statistic; compute median tetrachoric agreement and Cohen's kappa across descriptors; analyze ETS-scale differential functioning; compare machine representations (Morgan fingerprints + RDKit descriptors) against human panel reliability; test whether adding more descriptors, models, or data reduces gaps; assess valence variance via perceptual ratings and descriptor-derived estimates; provide a descriptor crosswalk and machine-checked theorems.

Result: High heterogeneity across corpora (I^2 ≈ 80%) and non-uniform labeling breadth prevent a single offset from bridging pools. Median tetrachoric agreement (0.795) exceeds median κ (0.413), indicating agreement on molecule-descriptor applicability but discordant application. Of 109 estimable descriptors, 36 show substantial differential functioning on the ETS scale. On human-panel reliability, full descriptor RDKit block accounts for 32.9% of potential performance; merging two corpora adds only to 33.9%. Increasing model capacity or data does not close the gap. Valence accounts for most unexplained variance: a single pleasantness rating reaches 54.6% (57.1% on older instrument); descriptor-based valence (ρ=0.457) explains only 16.6% of variance.

Conclusion: Cross-corpus descriptor pooling is fundamentally limited by valence variance and descriptor-function heterogeneity. Direct valence measurement is necessary for accurate pleasantness mapping. A descriptor crosswalk and twenty machine-checked theorems are released to enable reproducibility and further study.

Abstract: Machine olfaction trains on pooled public descriptor corpora, but whether a shared descriptor word measures the same thing across corpora has not been tested, nor has the ceiling of what any of them can measure.
  We audit four corpora from Pyrfume. Conditioning on the molecule makes McNemar's test the exact conditional test of the corpus effect. Corpora disagree heterogeneously across descriptors ($I^2 = 80\%$) and non-uniformly with labelling breadth ($z = 17.2$), so no single offset repairs pooling. Median tetrachoric agreement is 0.795 against median $κ$ of 0.413: sources largely concur on which molecules deserve a word and differ on how readily they apply it. Of 109 descriptors with an estimable effect, 36 show large differential functioning on the ETS scale.
  Against a human panel's reliability, Morgan fingerprints with the full RDKit descriptor block reach 32.9\% of achievable; adding every label from two merged corpora reaches 33.9\%. The gap does not close with model capacity, encoding choice, more molecules, or more words.
  The missing variance is valence. One pleasantness rating per molecule reaches 54.6\% of achievable (57.1\% on an independent older instrument). Valence recovered from descriptors ($ρ= 0.457$) yields only 16.6\%, so it must be measured. Five raters exceed structure plus the full descriptor record; fifteen to twenty saturate. We release a descriptor crosswalk and twenty machine-checked theorems.

</details>


### [152] [Large Distant Gradients Need Not Be Reliable: reliability-weighted credit assignment for long-horizon autoregressive forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12890)
*Junhao Zhao,David Michael Simberg,Jacob Kang,Colin Connor Kurniawan,Nan Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A backward-only intervention (Internal-DW) weights gradient routes in autoregressive models to preserve long-horizon learning while suppressing unreliable distant gradients, improving forecasting compared to full BPTT and TBPTT under certain conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Distant gradients in BPTT can dominate updates and mix reliable signal with noise; need a method to leverage long-horizon supervision without trusting every backward contribution.

Method: Derives bounded Wiener gains for identity and nonlinear routes at each residual block, estimating gains from route-level gradient statistics and a noise model; preserves full forward rollout and horizon losses.

Result: In controlled SNR settings, Internal-DW reduces held-out error and improves forecasting; 5.2–13.8% improvement over full BPTT on four history-dominated testbeds; outperforms gradient clipping and Jacobian regularization on all four; outperforms TBPTT on three; extends training-horizon range; benefit boundary depends on history availability and drive-dependent variation representation.

Conclusion: Long-horizon supervision can be retained without trusting every backward contribution equally; Internal-DW offers a principled balance between preserving signal and suppressing unpredictable variation, with applicability limited by history and sampler adequacy.

Abstract: In autoregressive forecasting, long prediction rollouts provide distant supervision, but backpropagation through time (BPTT) carries gradients from those losses through many autoregressive steps. Repeated Jacobian products can make distant gradients dominate the update while amplifying predictable signal and unpredictable noise together; a large distant gradient therefore need not carry reliable learning signal. Motivated by this observation, we introduce Internal Dual-Wiener routing (Internal-DW), a principled backward-only intervention that preserves the full forward rollout and all horizon losses while reliability-weighting internal gradient routes. At each residual block, we derive bounded Wiener gains for the identity and nonlinear routes that balance preserving predictable learning signal against suppressing unpredictable variation, and estimate them from route-level gradient statistics and an explicit noise model. In a controlled system with known gradient signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), we show that distant gradients can grow even as their SNR falls, and that Internal-DW reduces held-out error in recovering predictable gradient signals and improves forecasting. On four history-dominated, weak-drive testbeds, Internal-DW reduces forecast error by 5.2%-13.8% relative to full BPTT, outperforms gradient clipping and Jacobian regularization on all four, and outperforms validation-selected truncated BPTT (TBPTT) on three. It also extends or preserves the fitted optimal training-horizon range across these four testbeds. Across the full benchmark suite, the current Internal-DW estimator has a clear applicability boundary: its benefit diminishes or reverses when usable history is limited or when the selected sampler fails to represent dominant drive-dependent variation. The results show that retaining long-horizon supervision does not require trusting every backward contribution equally.

</details>


### [153] [Quantifying the Value of Privileged Information Using a PAC-Bayesian Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12891)
*Vasily Bokov,Sebastian Schmitt,Vedran Dunjko,Hao Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Algorithm-agnostic PAC-Bayes framework for LUPI; defines a training-time metric to bound potential test-time gains from privileged information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General theory is lacking: existing results are per-algorithm and setting-specific; need a universal measure of how valuable privileged information can be, without access to test data.

Method: An information-theoretic PAC-Bayes approach comparing the best possible risk bounds with and without PI; derives a training-risk-based metric that estimates the upper bound on potential gain and is algorithm-agnostic.

Result: The training-time metric robustly correlates with actual test-time performance gains in both supervised and unsupervised settings, providing an upper bound on extractable gain from PI.

Conclusion: Marks progress toward an information-theoretic understanding of LUPI, enabling assessment of PI value prior to model training and guiding future theoretical and empirical work.

Abstract: In practice, various learning scenarios provide access to auxiliary features exclusively during training. Incorporating such data to enhance model performance gave rise to a paradigm known as Learning Using Privileged Information (LUPI). While this extra information is intended to improve the resulting model, establishing a generalized, cohesive understanding of how privileged information (PI) transfers useful knowledge remains a challenge. Vapnik's original theory and subsequent works offer performance guarantees in certain cases, but these results are inherently per-algorithm and rely on setting-specific proof approaches. Consequently, a more general framework explaining how and when PI transfers useful knowledge is still missing. To bridge this gap, we introduce an algorithm-agnostic, information-theoretic approach based on the PAC-Bayes framework. Rather than asking whether a particular algorithm exploits PI, we ask how much value it could offer: comparing the tightest achievable risk bound with and without PI yields its potential - an upper limit on the extractable gain. We introduce a metric that quantifies this potential directly from empirical training risk, bypassing the need for test-time data access, and validate our findings in both supervised and unsupervised settings. The results demonstrate a robust correspondence between our training-time metric and true test-time performance gains. Ultimately, this work takes a necessary step toward an information-theoretic understanding of LUPI, and quantifying the potential of privileged features before committing to a model.

</details>


### [154] [Behavior Quotient Learning for Low-Rank Adaptation of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12896)
*Pengyang Zhou,Xiaobin Tu,Zhengxi Liu,Rongkun Xue,Haochen Li,Miancan Liu,Ziyuan Chen,Yinggui Wang,Jinkui Ren,Xiantao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BQ-LoRA is a single LoRA framework that uses a trajectory-aware, quotient-manifold approach to learn from heterogeneous agent traces under a fixed rank, balancing updates and preserving decision distributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To avoid the routing overhead and increased storage of multiple adapters while learning from diverse trajectories constrained by a fixed low-rank budget, and to prevent redundant updates and decision distortion.

Method: Introduce Behavior Quotient Balancing (BQB) and Decision Preserving Compression (DPC). Build a quotient manifold from decision distributions, reweight trajectory update directions by local density in the quotient tangent space (BQB). Project the balanced gradient onto the intrinsic fixed-rank tangent space and refactorize to control effective weight error and decision distortion (DPC).

Result: Experiments on AppWorld and BrowseComp-Plus show that BQ-LoRA outperforms standard LoRA and recent low-rank adaptation methods; ablation studies confirm the individual contributions of BQB and DPC.

Conclusion: BQ-LoRA provides an effective, storage-efficient single-LoRA adaptation that preserves decision behavior while respecting a fixed rank budget, validated by empirical results and ablations.

Abstract: LLM-based agents rely on heterogeneous interaction capabilities to accomplish complex tasks. Existing approaches often distribute these capabilities across multiple LoRA adapters, which increases adapter storage requirements and introduces routing overhead during inference. A single LoRA avoids this overhead, but learning from diverse agent trajectories under a fixed rank budget presents two challenges. First, trajectories with different interaction traces and parameter gradients can induce equivalent changes in decision distributions, causing repeated updates to overemphasize redundant behavioral changes. Second, an aggregated update may exceed the rank budget of the adapter, and approximating it in weight space can distort the decision changes that it is intended to produce. We propose BQ-LoRA, a low-rank adaptation framework that organizes trajectory updates through a local behavior quotient manifold. It contains two modules, i.e., behavior quotient balancing (BQB) and decision preserving compression (DPC). BQB constructs the quotient manifold from decision distributions and reweights trajectory update directions according to their local density in the quotient tangent space. DPC projects the balanced gradient onto the intrinsic fixed rank tangent space and refactorizes the resulting target by jointly controlling effective weight error and distortion of decision distributions. Experiments on AppWorld and BrowseComp-Plus compare BQ-LoRA with standard LoRA and recent low-rank adaptation methods, while separate ablations evaluate the complementary contributions of both components.

</details>


### [155] [Physical-State-Guided Diffusion Sampling for Full-Waveform Inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12899)
*Chen Min,Haowen Jiang,Zheng Ma,Xiongbin Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PSG couples a persistent physical velocity to a diffusion prior via a Gaussian bridge in FWI, separating wave-equation and denoiser gradients to improve inversion accuracy and robustness across OpenFWI benchmarks, including large models, without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FWI is ill-posed and highly nonlinear; naive diffusion priors risk unphysical guidance when coupled directly to the wave solver; need a method that preserves data fidelity and optimization history.

Method: Introduce Physical-State-Guided Diffusion Sampling (PSG): use a Gaussian bridge to connect a persistent physical velocity with the diffusion prior; refine physical state via waveform fitting regularized by the denoised velocity; reverse diffusion guided by this physical state; decouples gradients; maintain traditional initialization/history; evaluate on four OpenFWI families; ensemble runs; use frozen OpenFWI-prior for larger models.

Result: PSG's terminal denoised estimates outperform classical and diffusion-based baselines under clean and missing-trace acquisitions and with measurement noise; repeated stochastic runs preserve major geological structures; ensemble variability concentrates near interfaces and correlates with inversion error; frozen priors enable inversion of Marmousi, Overthrust, BP2004 Salt without retraining; recovering complex structures.

Conclusion: PSG provides a robust framework for physics-informed diffusion priors in FWI, achieving improved accuracy and stability across diverse models and scales, and enabling transfer with frozen priors.

Abstract: Full waveform inversion (FWI) estimates subsurface velocity from seismic recordings, but its ill-posedness and nonlinearity make accurate reconstruction strongly dependent on initialization and prior information. Diffusion posterior sampling provides a learned geological prior, yet directly coupling its denoiser to the nonlinear wave solver can yield unreliable physical guidance. We propose Physical-State-Guided Diffusion Sampling (PSG), which couples a persistent physical velocity to the diffusion prior through a Gaussian bridge. The physical state is refined by waveform fitting regularized by the denoised velocity, and in turn guides the reverse diffusion process. This formulation separates the wave-equation and denoiser gradients while preserving conventional FWI initialization and accumulated optimization history. On four OpenFWI families, PSG's terminal denoised estimates outperform classical and diffusion-based baselines under clean and missing-trace acquisitions and maintain strong structural recovery under measurement noise. Repeated stochastic runs preserve the dominant geological structures, with ensemble variability concentrated near geological interfaces and positively associated with local inversion error. A frozen OpenFWI-trained prior further supports inversion of the larger Marmousi, Overthrust, and BP2004 Salt models, recovering complex geological structures without retraining.

</details>


### [156] [Hidden in Rounds: Predicting the Time Cost of 802.11 Contention in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12903)
*Satwat Bashir,Tasos Dagiuklas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedAvg over 802.11 is studied via ns-3 network measurements to predict communication-time effects on learning, using a simple estimator for update admission. All runs achieve target accuracy within the round budget, but communication time grows with client density; estimator error is modest but limited by using a derived time from the round equation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand how wireless channel sharing in federated learning affects update admission and timing, and provide a lightweight estimator for communication time under realistic network conditions.

Method: ns-3 measurements of frame-delivery ratio and saturation throughput across varying client densities and offered loads; a separate FedAvg trainer uses the observed FDR as a proxy for update-admission probability and a round-duration equation to estimate communication time; 720 runs across two datasets, two partitions, six densities, six loads, five seeds; compare uniform vs persistent heterogeneous participation; no simulation of full model delivery or end-to-end training time.

Result: All 720 runs reached target accuracy within the round budget. Rounds-to-target largely insensitive to offered load; communication time-to-target increases by ~100x across the density range. A Bianchi-anchored estimator yields MAE from 2.3% to 10.2% on held-out configs, evaluated against the time derived from the round equation rather than true completion time.

Conclusion: The lightweight estimator can reasonably predict communication-time impact in density-varying federated learning over 802.11, but results are configuration-specific with no general convergence or fairness guarantees; higher density dramatically inflates communication time, while learning performance remains robust within tested configurations.

Abstract: Federated learning over IEEE~802.11 shares the wireless channel among clients that send model updates. We use ns-3 to measure the frame-delivery ratio and saturation throughput for different client densities and offered loads. A separate FedAvg trainer uses the frame-delivery ratio as a first-order proxy for the update-admission probability and uses an equation to estimate communication time. The method does not simulate the delivery of a complete model update or measure end-to-end training time. Across 720 evaluated runs with two datasets, two data partitions, six client densities, six offered loads, and five seeds, all runs reached their predefined target accuracy within the round budget. Rounds-to-target changed little with offered load, while communication time-to-target increased by about two orders of magnitude across the client-density range. A Bianchi-anchored estimator produced a mean absolute percentage error from $2.3\%$ to $10.2\%$ on held-out configurations. This error is measured against communication time constructed from the same round-duration equation, not against independently measured completion time. We also compare uniform participation with persistent heterogeneous participation. The study does not detect a statistically distinguishable excluded-class accuracy gap over five seeds, but the confidence intervals are wide. The results apply only to the evaluated configurations and do not provide a general convergence or fairness guarantee.

</details>


### [157] [Offline Reinforcement Learning for Wind Farm Control: A Wind Tunnel Study under Dynamic Wind Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12905)
*Yuhan Su,Hongyang Dong,Simone Tamaro,Filippo Campagnolo,Carlo L. Bottasso,Xiaowei Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes MTD3-BC offline RL for wind farm yaw control under changing wind directions; shows ~10% farm power gains in wind tunnel, matching model-based wake-steering without using a wake model and with reduced training cost; first experimental validation of offline RL in wind farms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wind direction changes create nonstationary wake interactions making wake steering challenging. Online RL is data/time-intensive; offline RL can leverage precollected data to learn effective policies with lower computational cost.

Method: Model-free Modified Twin Delayed DDPG with Behavior Cloning (MTD3-BC), an offline RL algorithm. Introduces an action consistency term to promote smooth yaw changes. Trains on precollected offline data for yaw control; validates via wind tunnel experiments across varying wind directions; does not rely on wake models during training.

Result: Farm-level power gains of ~10% over baseline greedy strategy; performance comparable to a data-calibrated model-based wake-steering benchmark; no wake model used during training; significantly reduced training cost compared with online RL; first experimental validation of offline RL wind-farm control policy.

Conclusion: MTD3-BC demonstrates that offline RL can yield competitive wake-steering performance with reduced data/computational requirements and without wake models, and that it can be validated experimentally, highlighting practical potential for wind-farm control under wind-direction variability.

Abstract: This paper addresses the wind farm power maximization problem in the presence of wind direction changes. Specifically, a model-free Modified Twin Delayed Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient with Behavior Cloning (MTD3-BC) algorithm is proposed to tackle this task through yaw control under varying wind direction conditions. MTD3-BC is an offline reinforcement learning (RL) algorithm that aims to infer good behavior from only a precollected offline dataset. Additionally, to ensure smooth and moderate yaw adjustments, a new action consistency term is introduced into the policy optimization objective. Unlike online RL methods, MTD3-BC does not require extensive interactions with a wind farm simulator during training, significantly reducing computational costs and training time. A wind tunnel experiment is conducted to validate the effectiveness of the algorithm under varying wind directions. The results demonstrate that MTD3-BC successfully mitigates wake effects, delivering farm-level power gains of approximately 10\% over the baseline greedy strategy and performance on par with a data-calibrated model-based wake-steering benchmark, while requiring no wake model and only a small fraction of the training cost of online RL. To our knowledge, this is the first time an offline RL wind farm control policy has been validated and demonstrated experimentally.

</details>


### [158] [A Large-Scale AIS Dataset from Finnish Water](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12938)
*Debayan Bhattacharya,Ikram Ul Haq,Carlos Pichardo Vicencio,Sebastien Lafond*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: New large AIS dataset for Baltic Sea and Finnish lakes (229M points) with curated datasets, ship-type taxonomy, features, and visualisations to support maritime research and collaboration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address fragmentation in AIS data and enable comprehensive studies of maritime activity in a high-traffic, geopolitically and ecologically significant region (Baltic Sea and Finnish lakes).

Method: Dataset collection and integration from multiple AIS sources; curation and categorisation of datasets; extraction of ship types and features; generation of visualisations; documentation of dataset characteristics.

Result: A collected Baltic Sea AIS dataset (229M points) plus a catalogue of existing AIS datasets; detailed ship-type list; feature set; traffic visualisations; ready for research use.

Conclusion: Publishing the dataset will catalyse innovation and collaboration, enabling deeper insights into maritime activities in the Baltic Sea and Finnish lakes.

Abstract: This research paper contributes to the maritime research community by introducing a comprehensive AIS dataset from Finnish waters, specifically the Baltic Sea region. AIS data, initially designed for collision prevention, have evolved into a versatile tool with applications across diverse maritime domains. Our paper not only curates and categorises existing AIS datasets but also introduces a collected AIS dataset from the Baltic Sea area, renowned for its intercontinental cargo routes, military activities, and frozen water expanses. This dataset includes 229 millions data points and provides researchers with a resource for studying maritime activities and vessel behaviour in this dynamic region, notably distinguished by the inclusion of data from Finnish lakes. Our analysis includes a detailed listing of ship types and relevant features, empowering researchers to explore various maritime domains. To enhance comprehension and analysis, we provide visualisations of maritime traffic patterns. By publishing this AIS dataset, we aim to catalyse innovation and collaboration in maritime research, offering a gateway to deeper insights into maritime activities in the Baltic Sea and Finnish lakes.

</details>


### [159] [Information-Induced Training Geometry: Exact Reduction, Canonical Completion, and Structured Expressivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12991)
*Zavier Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Partial information channels constrain optimizer geometry; a unique, affine-invariant full PD compression admits explicit completion; completions form a gauge-invariant rank stratification; enabling exact reduction and interpretable geometry under visible data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterize how restricted (visible) covectors defined by a channel determine the underlying Riemannian metric on the positive-definite cone, and to derive explicit completions and invariants for optimization geometry.

Method: Model: affine-invariant Riemannian geometry on PD matrices; define a compression map as a split-Hadamard metric submetry; derive closed-form completion to the full geometry; prove gauge-invariant rank stratification; derive pullback metric, Gram matrix; prove metric theorems (ball submetry, fiber distance, lossless reduction); establish split-Hadamard theorem; discuss priors and bounds.

Result: There exists a unique full PD completion for a given visible constraint; the compression yields a visible-to-full reduction with exact variational equivalence; completions form a gauge-invariant rank stratification; explicit pullback metric and Gram matrix; closed-form prior-data shrinkage; diagonal/block optimizers reduce to conic tests with certificates; finite-sample bounds quantify recovery of visible geometry; exact reduction and completion characterized.

Conclusion: Provides a complete geometric/analytic framework for how partial information channels determine and constrain optimizer geometry under affine invariance, including explicit constructions, invariants, and bounds; clarifies the degree of freedom remaining and the nature of the recoverable structure.

Abstract: Training data constrains optimizer geometry through the covectors visible to a declared information channel. We study how such partial information determines a full positive cometric relative to a reference and which degrees of freedom remain unidentified. Our central result resolves full-column-rank positive-definite compression under affine-invariant Riemannian geometry. The compression map is a split-Hadamard metric submetry and admits an explicit unique completion that is the affine-invariant nearest full geometry realizing a visible target and yields exact full-to-visible variational reduction. When the channel moves, the completions form a gauge-invariant rank stratification of the positive-definite cone. Its closed-form pullback pair metric separates visible-metric motion from subspace rotation through a reference-mismatch weight, yields an explicit positive-semidefinite multi-direction Gram matrix, and exposes the precise singularity of reference-valued modes. The mechanism is explained by a metric theorem equating ball submetry, attained fiber distance, and lossless reduction of every monotone radial visible decision problem. A smooth split-Hadamard theorem supplies coherent information sheets, proximal commutation, and solution-wise gradient-flow lifting. The positive-definite realization also gives closed-form prior-data shrinkage. Diagonal and block optimizer families reduce to relative-interior conic image tests with valid facial certificates, while deterministic and finite-sample bounds quantify recovery of the visible geometry and its subspace. Together these results characterize exact reduction, reference-dependent completion, and structured expressivity for the stated finite-dimensional affine-invariant model.

</details>


### [160] [Dimension-Corrected Hitting Times for Heavy-Tailed Spectral Emergence in Neural Optimizer Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12994)
*Zongmin Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A dimension-corrected spectral-hitting-time law explains heavy-tail emergence in neural-network weight matrices, using a right-censored survival framework. The onset time scales as τ_HT ≈ C Δ1^{-γ} d^ρ with γ≈0.626 and ρ≈0.772 (R^2=0.683, n=330). Right-censored lognormal accelerated-failure-time models favor this model over a gap-only description (AIC improvement from 706.62 to 628.70).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand when heavy-tailed empirical spectral densities arise in neural-network weight matrices, quantify onset time under censoring, and disentangle mechanisms (gap-based vs. spreading) across training dynamics and optimizers.

Method: Formulate as a right-censored hitting-time problem in controlled full-batch teacher–student dynamics; fit lognormal accelerated failure-time models; derive dimension-corrected spectral-gap law; provide theoretical proofs for linear networks, Adam recurrences, and projected-singular-basis spreading; perform empirical analyses using kernel profiles and real pretrained models (Qwen2.5-0.5B, Pythia-70M) and Gaussian-null comparisons across optimizers (Adam, AdamW, GD, signGD).

Result: Found a dimension-corrected hitting-time relation τ_HT ≈ C Δ1^{-γ} d^ρ with γ=0.626, ρ=0.772, R^2=0.683 across 330 runs; lognormal-AFT models improve AIC from 706.62 to 628.70; evidence supports the spreading mechanism over gap-only explanations; supported by empirical spectral-tail observations in real models and non-Gaussian tails relative to Gaussian nulls; theoretical results separate early loss dynamics, Adam recurrences, and spreading effects.

Conclusion: Proposes a reproducible spectral hitting-time law with conditional theory, not a blanket claim that Adam alone generates heavy tails; emphasizes dimension-corrected spreading as the mechanism driving onset under censoring, with validation across simulations and real pretrained weights.

Abstract: Heavy-tailed empirical spectral densities of neural-network weight matrices are widely used as diagnostics of implicit self-regularization, but the step complexity of heavy-tail emergence remains poorly understood. We formulate spectral heavy-tail formation as a right-censored hitting-time problem: a run that does not reach a heavy-tail diagnostic within the observation horizon is treated as censored rather than discarded. In controlled full-batch teacher--student dynamics, we find that the first-step spike--bulk gap alone does not explain onset time. Instead, finite-onset regression supports a dimension-corrected spectral-gap law, (τ_{\mathrm{HT}}\approx CΔ_1^{-γ}d^ρ), with (R^2=0.683), (γ=0.626), and (ρ=0.772) across 330 completed runs. Right-censored lognormal accelerated-failure-time models further favor the dimension-corrected model over a gap-only model, improving AIC from 706.62 to 628.70. Theoretically, we prove that exact early loss dynamics in linear networks do not determine factor spectral tails, that Adam recurrences alone do not imply spectral redistribution, and that projected singular-basis spreading implies contraction of a spectral-tail potential and hence a dimension-corrected hitting-time bound. Empirically, projected-kernel profiles support the sufficient spreading mechanism, Adam and AdamW agree under tested grids, GD and signGD do not reach onset in the same regimes, and real pretrained Qwen2.5-0.5B and Pythia-70M transformer weights show non-Gaussian spectral-tail structure relative to matched Gaussian nulls. The result is a reproducible spectral hitting-time law with rigorous conditional theory, not a claim that Adam necessarily generates heavy tails from first principles.

</details>


### [161] [A Full Adam Theorem for Spectral Heavy-Tail Onset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12996)
*Zongmin Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Presents a comprehensive Adam-theorem in a closed Gaussian Stein-Hermite teacher-student state-evolution, deriving explicit spectral-hit time law and showing limits of arbitrary-gradient Adam and two-step linear networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand Adam dynamics under spectral heavy-tailed onset within a structured Gaussian Stein-Hermite framework, linking optimizer steps to spectral properties via Stein-Hermite calculus.

Method: Derive full-batch Adam recurrences; compute population gradient by Stein-Hermite calculus; prove finite-width covariance concentration; map multi-step momentum to an exact non-centered Gaussian sign kernel; control diagonal Adam denominator via a basis-homogenization theorem; obtain a regularly varying projected update from Hermite edge-transfer; propagate through exact Gram update; prove approximate-target KL contraction with upper/lower hitting bounds, yielding a closed-form hitting law.

Result: Final hitting time law τ_ε = Θ(Δ_1^{-γ} d^ρ log(Ψ_0/ε)); the result is fully formalized within the closed state-evolution model; also shows that a stronger arbitrary-gradient Adam theorem is impossible and that exact two-step linear-network loss dynamics do not identify factor spectra or heavy-tail hitting times.

Conclusion: The work delivers a rigorously framed Adam-theorem in a Gaussian Stein-Hermite setting, clarifying the limits of optimizer-generalizations and the insufficiency of simple linear-network dynamics to capture heavy-tail spectral features.

Abstract: We prove a full Adam theorem for spectral heavy-tail onset in a closed Gaussian Stein-Hermite teacher-student state-evolution model. The theorem begins with the actual full-batch Adam recurrences, derives the population gradient by Stein-Hermite calculus, proves finite-width covariance concentration, converts multi-step Adam momentum into an exact non-centered Gaussian sign kernel, controls the diagonal Adam denominator by a basis-homogenization theorem, derives a regularly varying projected update response from a Hermite edge-transfer theorem, pushes the response through the exact Gram update, and proves approximate-target KL contraction with matching upper and lower hitting bounds. The final law is (τ_\varepsilon=Θ(Δ_1^{-γ}d^ρ\log(Ψ_0/\varepsilon))), where (Δ_1) is the first spike-bulk spectral gap. The result is full in the following precise sense: every step from Adam's momentum and denominator to the spectral hitting law is formalized inside the closed state-evolution model. We also prove that a stronger arbitrary-gradient Adam theorem is impossible, and that exact two-step linear-network loss dynamics do not identify factor spectra or heavy-tail hitting times.

</details>


### [162] [Dual-guided Hierarchical Edge Localization for Large-scale Optimal Transport Across Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13010)
*Wenzhou Xia,Qiaoqiao Ding,Jingwei Liang,Xiaoqun Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HELLO is a hierarchical solver for large-scale unregularized discrete OT that localizes edges via dual potentials, using coarse-to-fine initialization and refinement to achieve global optimality with linear memory and massive scalability; it supports general costs and acts as an OT oracle for several variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the prohibitive memory and computation cost of solving unregularized discrete OT as a full linear program, enabling exact or near-exact solutions at million-point scales and high dimensionality.

Method: Introduce HELLO: a hierarchical edge-localization framework guided by dual potentials. Initialize edges by propagating coarse duals through a recursive subsampling hierarchy; refine by iteratively inserting the largest dual violators per row/column until the relative KKT residual meets tolerance, with budgeted pruning for linear memory. Prove finite termination to global optimum under a symbolic lexicographic rule for exact-arithmetic refinement. Supports general pairwise costs and serves as a scalable OT oracle for semi-discrete OT, Gromov–Wasserstein, unbalanced OT, and OT-based Flow Matching.

Result: Demonstrates an order-of-magnitude speedup over strong baselines at million-point scales across diverse feature dimensions, achieving lower transport objectives. Scales to 1.28 million samples per marginal in 8192 dimensions on a single H100 with 41.6 GiB peak memory, while attaining a full relative KKT residual below 1e-6.

Conclusion: HELLO delivers scalable, exact- or high-precision OT solutions for very large problems, with linear memory and broad applicability beyond standard discrete OT to several OT variants; it provides a practical, theoretically grounded framework for edge localization in OT.

Abstract: Optimal transport (OT) compares distributions and aligns datasets in machine learning, yet unregularized discrete OT requires a linear program with quadratically many transport variables. We propose HELLO, a hierarchical solver that casts large-scale discrete OT as edge localization and uses dual potentials to guide both coarse-to-fine initialization and within-level refinement. Initialization propagates coarse dual potentials across a recursive subsampling hierarchy to assign candidate edges. Refinement then iteratively inserts the largest dual violators in each row and column until the relative KKT residual meets a prescribed tolerance, while budgeted pruning ensures linear memory complexity. For exact-arithmetic refinement, we prove finite termination at a global optimum under a symbolic lexicographic rule. At the million-point scale, HELLO attains lower transport objectives with order-of-magnitude runtime improvements over strong baselines across feature dimensions from single digits to thousands. It further scales to 1.28 million samples per marginal in 8192 dimensions on a single H100, using 41.6 GiB peak GPU memory while satisfying a full relative KKT residual below $10^{-6}$. Beyond standard discrete OT, the framework supports general pairwise costs and serves as a scalable balanced-OT oracle for semi-discrete OT, Gromov--Wasserstein, unbalanced OT, and OT-based Flow Matching.

</details>


### [163] [Attention Quantization for Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13031)
*Jonas M. Kübler,Benjamin Jäger,Klemens Flöge,Noah Hollmann,Frank Hutter*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Attention-centric quantization for tabular foundation models yields substantial inference speedups with FP8 and aligned training/test quantization, minimizing accuracy loss on TabPFN-v3 and TabICLv2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tabular foundation models are increasingly deployed; unlike LLMs, their inference bottleneck lies in attention computation rather than weight or KV cache quantization, necessitating new efficiency strategies.

Method: Develop FP8 quantization for queries, keys, and values and utilize explicit FP8 matrix multiplication instructions via a Triton kernel. Emphasize aligning quantization error between training and test data to preserve accuracy.

Result: Achieved up to 1.7x speedup over conventional 16-bit kernels. On TabPFN-v3 and TabICLv2, accuracy remains robust across TabArena and BeyondArena when quantization errors are aligned between training and test rows.

Conclusion: Attention-focused FP8 quantization with aligned training/test error, implemented in a Triton kernel, is effective for accelerating tabular foundation models without compromising accuracy on standard benchmarks.

Abstract: With the recent rise and adoption of tabular foundation models, optimizing their inference performance becomes an emerging field for efficiency research. While the models are architecturally similar to transformer-based large language models (LLMs), the size and serving patterns differ significantly. We show that the focus should be on the attention calculation and less on weight or KV cache quantization, which are more popular in LLMs. We develop a quantization strategy for queries, keys, and values to FP8 and use explicit FP8 matrix multiplication instructions to speed up the attention calculation. We find that it is crucial to align the quantization error in the test rows with the quantization error in the training rows, as otherwise the accuracy drops drastically. Our Triton kernel achieves a speedup up to 1.7x over regular 16-bit kernels, and we show that on TabPFN-v3 and TabICLv2 there is no relevant accuracy loss across TabArena and BeyondArena.

</details>


### [164] [Groupoid-Based Internal State Representations for Reinforcement Learning with Local Symmetries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13035)
*Ben Opperman,Eduardo Alonso,Esther Mondragón*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A groupoid-based reinforcement learning framework exploits local, state-dependent symmetries and dynamically discovered equivalences to perform learning in a symmetry-reduced space, improving sample efficiency in environments with partial symmetries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical RL assumes globally structured MDPs with fixed actions and symmetries, which limits exploitation of modular, local regularities. A framework that captures local symmetries and learns their structure online could improve efficiency and generalization.

Method: Maintain orbit representatives and transporters that map raw states to canonical forms; perform learning in a symmetry-reduced space while preserving local distinctions; dynamically discover equivalence structures during interaction; apply to dense and large-scale environments to enable more efficient Q-learning.

Result: Empirical results show improved sample efficiency and convergence in environments with strong partial symmetries, yielding substantial gains over standard Q-learning.

Conclusion: Dynamically exploiting local symmetry via groupoid structures provides a principled, scalable path to more generalisable RL by reducing redundancy while preserving essential local distinctions.

Abstract: Symmetries play a central role in reducing the complexity of reinforcement learning problems, yet most existing approaches rely on fixed group actions or predefined state abstractions. Classical reinforcement learning algorithms typically assume a globally structured Markov decision process with uniformly applicable actions and transitions, an assumption that limits their ability to exploit modularity and local, context-dependent regularities present in many realistic environments. We propose a reinforcement learning framework using groupoids to capture local, state-dependent symmetries and support the dy- namic discovery of equivalence structures during interaction. The agent maintains orbit representatives together with transporters that map raw states to canonical forms, enabling learning and decision-making to be performed in a symmetry-reduced space while preserving local distinctions. Empirical results demonstrate that the proposed groupoid-based approach improves sample efficiency and convergence in dense and large-scale environments exhibiting strong partial symmetries, yielding substantial performance gains over standard Q-learning. These findings show that dynamically exploiting local symmetry provides a practical and mathematically principled route to scalable and generalisable reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [165] [Transfer Learning for Evolving Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13039)
*Ricardo Ribeiro Pereira,Jacopo Bono,Hugo Ferreira,Pedro Ribeiro,Pedro Saleiro,Pedro Bizarro,Carlos Soares*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Unifies transfer learning under the Transfer Learning for Evolving Domains (TrED) framework, formalizing data availability, learning protocol, and trajectory-based evaluation; shows classical settings are regimes within TrED and current methods do not optimize the full trajectory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridges the gap between isolated transfer-learning settings and real-world scenarios where data accumulates over time, requiring trajectory-aware evaluation and a unifying framework.

Method: Formalizes TrED by defining: (i) a fixed data availability process, (ii) a flexible learning protocol, and (iii) an evaluation criterion that scores the entire trajectory of models; reinterprets classical transfer-learning settings as regimes within TrED; conducts a literature survey to assess applicability of existing mechanisms.

Result: Finds TrED to be well-posed yet unsolved; classical settings are recoverable as regimes within TrED; most existing methods are tailored to a single regime and do not optimize the full trajectory.

Conclusion: Positions TrED as a meaningful, open research direction and calls for developing methods that optimize performance over the evolving trajectory of data and models.

Abstract: Transfer learning explores how to leverage knowledge from various tasks or domains (sources) to enhance predictive performance in related tasks or domains (targets). Typically, transfer learning research is segmented into several isolated sub-areas (such as domain generalisation, domain adaptation, or multi-domain learning), each making distinct assumptions about target data availability, namely how much data and how many labels are available at training time. However, in many real-world applications, data availability is not fixed but evolves over time, as instances and labels are progressively collected from a new domain. Each of the classical settings then describes only a snapshot of a trajectory that a deployed system must traverse in full. We formalise this trajectory as a transfer learning problem in its own right, Transfer Learning for Evolving Domains (TrED), specified by a data availability process fixed by the environment, a learning protocol that the method is free to choose, and an evaluation criterion that scores the whole trajectory of models rather than a single one. Within this formalism, the classical settings are recovered as regimes that a learner may pass through, rather than as separate problems that TrED concatenates. We then examine the transfer learning literature to identify mechanisms that are promising building blocks for a solution, and find that most methods are tailored to a single regime and that even the strongest existing candidates do not yet optimise the whole trajectory. We argue that TrED is a well-posed and unsolved problem, and an important direction for future research.

</details>


### [166] [Quantile-based Loss Filtering for Outlier-Robust Stochastic Gradient Descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13040)
*Jamie Haddock,Anna Ma,Elizaveta Rebrova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Robust finite-sum optimization via Quantile-k-Loss SGD (QkL-SGD): sample k losses per iteration and update from a uniformly chosen index in the lower empirical q-quantile; provides linear convergence under convexity with scalable sample size and a complementary small-sample probabilistic analysis; empirically, intermediate quantiles outperform SGD and min-k-loss SGD and are robust to corrupted gradients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finite-sum optimization with corrupted component gradients can degrade SGD performance. Leveraging loss-based filtering and quantile-based selection offers robustness against a subset of unreliable components, inspired by min-k-loss and quantile methods in linear systems.

Method: Propose QkL-SGD: at each iteration sample k component losses; select an index uniformly from the lower empirical q-quantile of these losses; update using the corresponding gradient. Provide a theoretical analysis showing linear convergence under standard convexity when the sample size scales with the number of corruptions and a subset strong-convexity threshold. Also offer a complementary small-sample probabilistic analysis valid for any k, with convergence depending on the probability of picking an outlier and the curvature of the selected good step.

Result: Theoretical guarantee of linear convergence under convexity with appropriate sample-size scaling and a subset-strong-convexity assumption. A small-sample probabilistic analysis extends results to any k, highlighting the role of outlier probability and step curvature. Empirically, intermediate quantiles outperform both standard SGD and min-k-loss SGD on polynomial regression, regularized logistic regression, and regularized hinge loss; min-k-loss can stall by repeatedly selecting near-solved components.

Conclusion: Intermediate quantiles offer robust and informative updates, achieving better performance than SGD and min-k-loss SGD. The framework combines theoretical convergence guarantees with practical robustness, validating the approach across multiple regression and classification tasks.

Abstract: We study loss-based filtering for finite-sum optimization with a subset of corrupted component functions whose gradients may be highly unreliable. Motivated by minimum-loss-based SGD (min-$k$-loss) and quantile-based methods for corrupted linear systems, we propose and analyze a general loss-filtering framework -- Quantile-\(k\)-Loss SGD (Q\(k\)L-SGD) -- that samples \(k\) component losses at each iteration and updates using an index chosen uniformly from the lower empirical \(q\)-quantile. We prove linear convergence of this family of methods under standard convexity assumptions, requiring the sample size to scale with the number of corruptions and a subset strong-convexity threshold. For the cases when large enough sampling is impossible or undesirable, we give a complementary small-sample probabilistic analysis that covers any sample size $k$ and the convergence behavior depends on the probability of selecting an outlier and on the curvature of the selected good step. Experiments on polynomial regression, regularized logistic regression, and regularized hinge loss show that intermediate quantiles often outperform both standard SGD and min-\(k\)-loss SGD. In particular, min-\(k\) often stalls by repeatedly selecting nearly solved components, while intermediate quantiles retain robustness and produce more informative updates.

</details>


### [167] [DynSHAP: Towards Explainable Dynamic Survival Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13042)
*Nastasya Anokhina,Jonas Jürß,Pietro Liò*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces DynSHAP, a SHAP-based framework for dynamic survival analysis that accounts for time–feature interactions; Temporal DynSHAP handles temporal dependencies and conditional sampling. Evaluated on synthetic data and real clinical datasets with two DSA models, it achieves faithful, temporally aware attributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DSA models leverage longitudinal data but are opaque; existing explainability methods fail to handle the combination of longitudinal, irregular inputs and functional survival outputs, hindering clinical trust and adoption.

Method: Extend marginal SHAP estimators by treating time–feature pairs as Shapley players (DynSHAP); introduce Temporal DynSHAP to learn linear temporal dependencies and apply conditional sampling to address them in explanations.

Result: Temporal DynSHAP recovers temporally dependent feature attributions more accurately than marginal estimators on synthetic data with known ground-truth; DynSHAP yields attributions faithful to model learning on two real-world clinical datasets and two DSA architectures.

Conclusion: DynSHAP provides faithful, temporally aware explanations for dynamic survival analysis, enabling clinicians to understand what information influenced predictions and when, potentially improving trust and adoption.

Abstract: Deep learning models for dynamic survival analysis (DSA) achieve strong predictive performance by incorporating longitudinal patient data, but their black box nature limits clinical trust and adoption. Existing explainability methods cannot handle longitudinal, irregular inputs and functional survival outputs simultaneously, which limits their usability in DSA. We propose DynSHAP, a SHAP framework suited specifically for dynamic survival analysis. It extends common marginal SHAP estimators to this setting by treating time--feature pairs as players in the Shapley game. We further introduce Temporal DynSHAP, which learns linear dependencies in features over time and uses conditional sampling to address them in explanations. When applied to synthetic data with known ground-truth attributions, Temporal DynSHAP recovers temporally dependent features more accurately than marginal estimators for a given state-of-the-art model. Applied to two real-world clinical datasets and two DSA architectures, DynSHAP produces attributions faithful to model learning, allowing medical experts to see which patient information drove the prediction and when.

</details>


### [168] [Robust Policy Optimization via Adversarial Importance Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13044)
*Amine Andam,Jamal Bentahar,Mustapha Hedabou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Advis (Adversarial Importance Sampling) for robust DRL without extra environment interactions or networks, introduces advrl for reproducible robustness research, and shows robustness gains using a large, diverse set of attackers on continuous control tasks; code available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identify and address limitations at three stages of robust DRL: algorithm design, implementation, and evaluation. Specifically, reduce reliance on extra environment interactions, eliminate auxiliary networks, and capture long-term robustness, while ensuring evaluation against diverse learned adversaries.

Method: Introduce Advis, an importance-sampling over trajectories from standard training to estimate and optimize verifiable worst-case returns. Develop advrl, a modular PyTorch library with single-file implementations of robustness methods and adversarial attacks. Evaluate policies against a large, diverse set of attackers (6-14x more configurations) to avoid overestimating robustness.

Result: Advis meets the three stated criteria and enables long-horizon robustness estimation without extra interactions or networks. advrl facilitates rapid prototyping and reproducible evaluations. Empirical evaluation on continuous-control tasks shows robustness improvements over baselines when tested against a broad attacker set.

Conclusion: The work advances robust DRL by addressing design, implementation, and evaluation gaps, providing a practical toolkit (advrl) and empirical evidence that robust policies require evaluation against diverse adversaries. Code is publicly available.

Abstract: Significant progress has been made in safeguarding deep reinforcement learning (DRL) policies against input perturbations. Developing robust DRL involves three main stages: algorithm design, implementation, and evaluation. In this work, we identify and address a key limitation at each stage. First, we introduce Adversarial Importance Sampling (Advis), a method that uses importance sampling over trajectories from standard training to estimate and optimize verifiable worst-case returns. Advis satisfies three desirable criteria not jointly achieved by prior work: it requires no additional environment interactions, no auxiliary networks, and captures long-term robustness. Second, we introduce advrl, a modular PyTorch library that provides clean, single-file implementations of existing robustness methods and adversarial attacks, facilitating rapid prototyping and enabling reproducible and traceable evaluations. Third, we revisit evaluation under learned adversaries and show that optimal adversarial hyperparameters do not transfer across agents, which can lead to an overestimation of robustness when using a limited set of attacker configurations. Accordingly, we evaluate policies against a large and diverse set of attackers, using 6-14x more configurations than prior work. Finally, we evaluate our approach on continuous control environments, demonstrating its effectiveness relative to existing baselines. The code is available at: https://github.com/AmineAndam04/advrl

</details>


### [169] [MCRL2: Multi-resource Cross-attention-based Representation Learning-augmented Reinforcement Learning for Cloud Microservice Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13048)
*Tiangang Li,Shi Ying,Xiangbo Tian,Chuan Shi,Ding Xiao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes MCRL2, a reinforcement learning framework with multi-resource cross-attention representation learning to optimize microservice scheduling, achieving superior load balance, success rate, and average completion time on real traces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic resource imbalance, nonlinear cross-resource coupling, and heterogeneous microservice demands hinder existing RL-based scheduling; need expressive state representations to capture interdependencies.

Method: Introduce MCRL, a multi-resource cross-attention module to model interactions among nodes, resources, and microservices; integrate with an actor-critic RL framework using a maximum entropy objective.

Result: Empirical evaluation on real production cluster traces shows significant improvements in load balancing, scheduling success rate, and average completion time across diverse workloads.

Conclusion: Rich cross-resource representations enable more stable and effective scheduling decisions, addressing major limitations of prior RL-based approaches.

Abstract: Efficient microservice scheduling is crucial for maintaining load balance across nodes in data centers and ensuring high quality of service. However, achieving this in practice remains challenging due to dynamic resource imbalance under fluctuating workloads, nonlinear coupling across multiple resource dimensions, and the heterogeneity of microservice resource demands. While reinforcement learning-based approaches have shown promise, they struggle to capture the complex interdependencies among heterogeneous resources and neglect the importance of learning informative system representations. To address these limitations, we propose MCRL2, a novel reinforcement learning approach augmented with multi-resource cross-attention-based representation learning for microservice scheduling. Specifically, we first propose MCRL, a novel representation learning approach that captures structured and informative interactions among nodes, resources, and microservices via a multi-resource cross-attention mechanism. Then, MCRL2 augments reinforcement learning through MCRL-enhanced actor-critic architecture combined with a maximum entropy objective, improving system state expressiveness and leading to more stable and effective scheduling decisions. Extensive experiments on real production cluster traces demonstrate that MCRL2 significantly outperforms existing baselines in load balancing, scheduling success rate and average completion time across diverse workload patterns.

</details>


### [170] [A Unified and Constrained View of Regularization-Based Robust Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13050)
*Amine Andam,Jamal Bentahar,Mustapha Hedabou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Unifies regularization-based DRL robustness by upper-bounding the performance gap with a term combining existing regularizers and a KL penalty, and proposes jointly adapting the Lagrange multiplier during training for automatic weight tuning; validates theoretically and empirically on continuous control tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide a common, theoretically grounded framework for robustness in DRL via regularization, clarifying the role of KL divergence and addressing regularization weight tuning.

Method: Derives upper bounds on the nominal-vs-worst-case performance gap expressed as an existing regularization objective plus a KL-divergence penalty; frames robust training as a constrained optimization; shows existing methods correspond to a fixed Lagrange multiplier; proposes updating the multiplier jointly with the policy.

Result: Theoretical upper bounds that decompose robustness into regularization plus KL penalty; interpretation of KL penalty’s role; algorithmic approach with jointly updated multiplier; extensive adversarial evaluations across continuous control tasks demonstrating robustness gains.

Conclusion: A unified theory connecting regularization objectives and KL penalties explains current robustness methods and enables automatic tuning of regularization strength, with empirical validation on multiple continuous control benchmarks.

Abstract: Regularization-based methods have become a standard approach for training Deep Reinforcement Learning policies against adversarial input perturbations. In this paper, we unify these methods by deriving new upper bounds on the performance gap between the nominal and worst-case policies. Each upper bound is expressed as an existing regularization objective plus a KL-divergence penalty between the nominal and worst-case policies, which further explains why adding a KL penalty improves robustness in practice. Building on these bounds, we formulate robust training as a constrained optimization problem, showing that existing methods correspond to the special case of a fixed Lagrange multiplier. We instead update the multiplier jointly with the policy to automatically tune the regularization weight. Finally, we conduct extensive adversarial evaluations across several continuous control tasks to validate our theoretical analysis.

</details>


### [171] [Benign Loss Landscapes Can Coexist with Worst-Case Hardness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13057)
*Zach Furman,Stephan Wäldchen,Yangda Bei,Liam Hodgkinson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TTNs can encode hard-to-learn targets while maintaining benign minima for realizable targets; learning difficulty stems from high-order saddle points due to rank-deficiency, with the parity function used to illustrate the geometry-hardness link.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why real-world targets are learnable by neural nets even though worst-case targets exist, by analyzing a model class that sits between deep linear networks and Tucker decompositions (tree tensor networks, TTNs).

Method: Show TTNs can embed arbitrary read-once Boolean formulas, implying polynomial-size targets that gradient descent cannot learn efficiently under the same mechanisms as neural nets; prove that all minimum-norm local minima are global for realizable targets; analyze high-order degenerate saddle points caused by rank-deficiency; illustrate with a parity-function case study.

Result: Demonstrates the existence of hard targets for gradient-based learning within TTNs, while establishing a conditionally benign landscape for realizable targets; identifies high-order saddle points due to rank deficiency as the source of practical learning difficulty; parity serves as a concrete demonstration of the relationship between landscape geometry and computational hardness.

Conclusion: In TTNs, bad local minima do not distinguish typical from worst-case learnability. Instead, learning difficulty arises from high-order degenerate saddle points tied to rank deficiency. TTNs thus offer a framework connecting geometry of the loss landscape to computational hardness, with parity illustrating the mechanism.

Abstract: Deep neural networks are expressive enough to contain worst-case targets that can be evaluated in polynomial time but cannot be learned in polynomial time by gradient descent. For practical tasks they nonetheless learn well, raising the question of what non-generic structure of real-world targets enables this. Existing surrogate models cannot pose this question because they either lack hard-to-learn targets entirely (deep linear networks) or cannot evaluate such targets efficiently (kernel methods, infinite-width limits). We study tree tensor networks (TTNs), a model class that generalizes deep linear networks and Tucker decompositions. We show they embed arbitrary read-once Boolean formulas, and thus contain polynomial-size targets that cannot be learned by gradient descent in polynomial time under the same mechanism as neural networks. Despite this, we prove that their loss landscapes are conditionally benign for every realizable target: every local minimum that is minimum-norm is global. Thus, surprisingly, bad local minima are not what distinguishes between typical and worst-case problems in TTNs. Instead, learning difficulty in TTNs can arise from high-order degenerate saddle points, which we show are caused by rank-deficiency. This is explored through a case study of the parity function, illustrating the potential for TTNs to relate landscape geometry to computational hardness.

</details>


### [172] [CanvasAnneal: Curriculum Reinforcement Learning for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13060)
*Blake Olson,Yuhang Song,Emmett McQuinn,Yuan Shangguan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CanvasAnneal is a curriculum-guided diffusion RL method that injects teacher-provided reasoning traces into the initial diffusion canvas to guide exploration, then gradually removes guidance, accelerating learning on math reasoning and tool-use tasks compared to standard diffusion RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion language models lag autoregressive models in complex reasoning and tool-use tasks, and diffusion RL suffers from exploration bottlenecks. Providing structured reasoning priors from a stronger teacher may steer exploration toward useful trajectories and speed up convergence.

Method: A curriculum-based RL framework (CanvasAnneal) that warm-starts exploration by injecting teacher-generated reasoning traces into the initial diffusion canvas during the early RL phase, then progressively erases guidance so the model must generate more of the reasoning path itself. Evaluated against diffu-GRPO on math reasoning and tool-use benchmarks (MATH500, Countdown, Tau2).

Result: CanvasAnneal yields improved performance over standard diffu-GRPO on MATH500, Countdown, and Tau2 and substantially accelerates reward improvement on several tasks; however gains are task-dependent.

Conclusion: Structured training-time guidance via reasoning priors can alleviate exploration bottlenecks in diffusion RL and speed up convergence on harder tasks.

Abstract: Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) offer promising parallel generation capabilities but lag behind autoregressive models in complex reasoning and tool-use tasks. While Reinforcement Learning (RL) has recently been applied to enhance DLMs, standard RL approaches suffer from an exploration bottleneck. To address this, we inject reasoning priors from a stronger teacher model to guide RL exploration. In this paper, we introduce CanvasAnneal, a curriculum-guided diffusion RL framework. During the initial RL phase, we warm-start exploration by injecting teacher-generated reasoning traces into the initial diffusion canvas. As training progresses, we gradually remove this guidance and require the model to generate more of the reasoning trajectory independently. Across mathematical reasoning and tool-use benchmarks, CanvasAnneal improves over standard diffu-GRPO on MATH500, Countdown, and Tau2 and substantially accelerates reward improvement on several tasks, while gains are task-dependent. Our results suggest that structured training-time guidance can alleviate exploration bottlenecks in diffusion RL and speed up convergence on harder tasks.

</details>


### [173] [MAxBench: A Multinomial Concept Recovery Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13072)
*Divya Appapogu,Freya Behrens,Yonatan Belinkov,Aaron Mueller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MAxBench provides a geometry-agnostic framework to evaluate multinomial concept representations in LMs and finds affine subspaces generally preferable for steering across non-binary concepts, driven largely by offsets; manifold steering is competitive, and prompting remains strong; highlights need for meta-evaluation on non-binary concepts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the challenge of steering non-binary, multinomial concepts (e.g., Animals, Countries) in language models and the lack of robust geometry-agnostic evaluation methods for recovering concept representations.

Method: Introduce MAxBench, a geometry-agnostic evaluation framework that samples from recovered concept representations to evaluate 10 localization methods across 5 geometry types, 6 concepts, and 4 models.

Result: (i) affine subspaces achieve more reliable steering and higher recall than rank-one or linear subspaces; (ii) the advantage largely comes from better non-zero offsets rather than the specific basis; (iii) manifold steering is competitive with the best methods when applicable; (iv) no method consistently outperforms prompting, aligning with prior findings on binary concepts.

Conclusion: Expands interpretability research and meta-evaluation to concepts with richer structure, showing geometry choices and offsets matter and that prompting remains a strong baseline.
