Abstract: Fine-grained control of language model behaviors (e.g., steering) is among the more actionable outcomes of interpretability research. For binary concepts such as refusal, a single direction in activation space often suffices for steering. However, many concepts are not binary: Animals and Countries contain many subcategories, each with multiple instances. For these concepts, the search space over possible representation geometries is far larger than for binary concepts; it is thus not clear what geometries are most appropriate, nor what methods are most effective at recovering them. In this work, we introduce MAxBench, a geometry-agnostic evaluation framework for multinomial concept representations based on sampling from the recovered concept representation. We use MAxBench to compare 10 localization methods (covering 5 geometry types) across 6 concepts and 4 models. Using this framework, we find that (i) affine subspaces steer more reliably and have greater recall than rank-one or linear subspaces; (ii) much of this advantage is due to better non-zero offsets rather than the choice of bases; (iii) manifold steering is competitive with the best methods when applicable; and (iv) no method consistently outperforms prompting, in alignment with prior findings on binary concepts. These findings underscore the importance of expanding the scope of interpretability research and meta-evaluation to concepts with more varied structure.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [174] [Occamy-1.0: Open Pareto-frontier 35B Intelligence for Co-work](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11977)
*Wenhui Chen,Shiwen Cheng,Hao Dong,Chenda Duan,Ruixiang Feng,Zhong Guan,Boqiang Guo,Xueyuan Han,Haojie Hao,Liangmeng Huang,Zhelong Huang,Xinke Kong,Hongyu Li,Jiazheng Li,Junbo Li,Qingchuan Li,Yukun Lian,Chang Liu,Tianyu Liu,Zicheng Liu,Shuyi Ouyang,Yijun Pan,Kunyu Shi,Xiaojun Tang,Bingquan Wang,Kesu Wang,Yuchen Wang,Sibo Wei,Sicong Xie,Xiaoying Xing,Yi Xu,Zhijun Xu,Hongwei Xue,Qingcheng Zeng,Di Zhang,Guannan Zhang,Haochen Zhang,Tianlong Zhang,Tianyu Zhao,Tianyu Zhao,Yanjun Zheng,Jialong Zhu,Zijian Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Occamy-1.0 is a cost-efficient co-work model built via staged post-training on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, achieving strong long-horizon, tool-enabled work at low cost and competitive with larger frontier systems; weights/data released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitigate high cost and latency of co-work agents performing long-horizon workflows requiring state tracking, coordination, and recovery.

Method: Create execution-grounded data/environments; collect replayable long-horizon trajectories across multiple harnesses; apply staged post-training to consolidate complementary execution capabilities; evaluate under a defined cost–performance protocol across four benchmarks.

Result: Occamy-1.0 is consistently among the strongest comparably sized models and competitive with larger systems on several tasks; it lies at the low-cost knee of the cost–performance Pareto frontier; tool-calling, coding, and instruction-following capabilities are preserved.

Conclusion: The approach yields a practical, cost-efficient co-work agent with broad agentic capabilities; model weights and part of training data are released to support research.

Abstract: Co-work agents execute complex workflows that combine information gathering, tool use, coding, and file manipulation across many model invocations. Because cost and latency accumulate over the full episode, their practical value depends not only on peak capability but also on how efficiently that capability is delivered. Yet many steps in everyday work emphasize state tracking, coordination, recovery, and follow-through rather than frontier-scale reasoning. We present Occamy-1.0, a cost-efficient co-work model obtained by further training the post-trained Qwen3.6-35B-A3B checkpoint. We construct execution-grounded data and environments, capture replayable long-horizon trajectories across multiple harnesses, and use staged post-training to develop and consolidate complementary execution capabilities. Across a broad suite of co-work benchmarks, Occamy-1.0 is consistently among the strongest comparably sized models and remains competitive with substantially larger frontier systems on several tasks. Under our stated evaluation and pricing protocol, its aggregate performance across four representative benchmarks places it at the low-cost knee of the observed cost--performance Pareto frontier. Supporting evaluations in tool calling, coding, and instruction following further show that this specialization preserves broad agentic capability. We release the model weights and a subset of the training data to support research on practical co-work agents and agentic post-training.

</details>


### [175] [Harness or Model? Isolating the Harness Effect in Agentic Coding with a Contamination-Controlled Private Suite](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11987)
*Mohsen Arjmandi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: No consistent advantage for vendor-native harnesses over alternatives across models and tasks; effects are small, inconsistent, and sometimes opposite across task types; a replication is needed due to post-hoc partitioning; results include cost differences and data limitations; private task set and revised cost figures; artifacts released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test the common assumption that vendor-specific harnesses (integrations of language models with tools/prompts/control flow) confer superior autonomous coding performance. Compare harness-vendor pairings against non-native harnesses within the same model to quantify any systematic advantage.

Method: Paired same-model contrasts across multiple harness-model configurations. Private, contamination-controlled suite of 256 repository/contest tasks. 80 tasks per model/harness pairing tested across several combinations (Claude-agent-sdk with deepagents on Claude Opus 4.8; OpenAI Codex SDK with deepagents on GPT-5.5; side cells like gemini-3.5-flash, deepseek-v3.2). 792 of 800 runs graded by an isolated oracle. Correctness and completion assessed; wall-clock ceilings caused some cancellations. Costs estimated from per-turn usage at frozen list prices; sensitivity analyses discussed; partition chosen after seeing data (needs replication). The work includes release of orchestrator, grading oracle, reanalysis code, and derived aggregates; tasks kept private.

Result: No average harness advantage for either native or non-native configurations. Opus 4.8 shows -1.25 percentage points (48.8% vs 50.0%), GPT-5.5 shows +1.25 pp (55.6% vs 54.4%). Opus shows a negative effect on 61 repository tasks but a large positive effect on 19 contest tasks (label-permutation p = 0.003), indicating task-type heterogeneity. 22 of 81 wall-clock-ceiling runs produced a passing patch. Neutral harness cost: 1.3–1.6x more expensive per solved task on Opus 4.8 and 1.2x on GPT-5.5. Missing usage records (Anthropic account) prevent stable cost ordering; reallocating those runs would move the Opus ratio to 0.7–2.3. A revision corrected August 2026 manuscript due to a prior usage-semantics defect in telemetry (Section 5.1). Reanalysis code and aggregates released; tasks remain private.

Conclusion: Vendor-native harnesses do not yield a robust, model-agnostic performance advantage in agentic coding within the tested setup. Effects are small and vary by task type; at least one partition effect appears significant but relies on post-hoc stratification and requires replication. Cost signals are uncertain due to incomplete usage data. The study emphasizes replication, transparency, and careful interpretation of heterogeneity across task types and datasets.

Abstract: An agentic coding system couples a language model to a harness: the tools, prompts and control flow that turn a chat model into an autonomous software engineer. Vendors ship harnesses tuned to their own models, and practitioners assume the vendor-native pairing solves more tasks. We measure that assumption with paired same-model contrasts on a private, contamination-controlled suite of 256 repository and post-cutoff contest tasks. The same 80 tasks ran under claude-agent-sdk and under deepagents on claude-opus-4-8, and under the openai-codex SDK and deepagents on gpt-5.5, with gemini-3.5-flash and deepseek-v3.2 as side cells. 792 of 800 planned runs were graded by an isolated oracle. Neither contrast resolves an average advantage for either harness: -1.25 pp for Opus 4.8 (48.8% vs 50.0%, task-bootstrap 95% CI [-10.0, +7.5]) and +1.25 pp for GPT-5.5 (55.6% vs 54.4%, CI [-4.4, +6.9]). The Opus average combines opposite strata: the native harness trails by 9.0 pp on the 61 repository tasks and leads by 23.7 pp on the 19 contest tasks (label-permutation p = 0.003). The partition was chosen after seeing the data and needs a designed replication. Correctness and completion also separate: 22 of 81 runs cancelled at the wall-clock ceiling had produced a passing patch. Re-priced from raw per-turn usage at frozen list prices, the neutral harness cost 1.3 to 1.6 times as much per solved task on Opus 4.8 and 1.2 times on GPT-5.5. These are observed-usage estimates. On the Anthropic account 58 runs left no usage record, and allocating that spend to either cell would move the Opus ratio between 0.7 and 2.3, so the billed ordering is unresolved. This revision corrects an August 2026 manuscript whose cost figures rested on a usage-semantics defect in our own telemetry (Section 5.1). We release the orchestrator, grading oracle, reanalysis code and derived aggregates. The tasks stay private.

</details>


### [176] [Reading the Whole Heart: Latent-Attention Masked Autoencoders for Multimodal Cardiac Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12035)
*Andrea Agostini,Simon Böhi,Moritz Vandenhirtz,Samuel Ruiperez-Campillo,Max Krähenmann,Silke Mühlstedt,Irene Cannistraci,Ece Özkan Elsen,Julia E. Vogt,Thomas M. Sutter*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A structure-aware multimodal self-supervised model (LAMAE) learns cross-modal latent representations for hospital-stay data, improving performance on multiple clinical tasks and remaining robust when modalities are missing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical foundation models typically treat modalities separately or fuse only after training, missing cross-modal evidence and intra-modality structure. A unified latent-space approach could exploit cross-modal interactions and varying observation structures.

Method: LAMAE is a latent-attention masked autoencoder with a study-view-entity hierarchy that exchanges information directly in latent space. It pretrains self-supervised on 1.2M MIMIC-IV hospital stays, enabling variable observation aggregation and graceful handling of missing modalities.

Result: LAMAE outperforms modality-specific pretraining and strong baselines (contrastive and vision-language) on multimodal hospital-stay tasks (in-hospital mortality, ICD-10/DRG coding, length of stay). It remains competitive on unimodal tasks and retains gains when only a single modality is available at test time.

Conclusion: Jointly modeling intra- and inter-modal structure in a latent space yields more robust, transferable representations for clinical prediction, validating the value of structure-aware multimodal pretraining and latent cross-modal exchange.

Abstract: Cardiovascular diagnosis rests on integrating complementary modalities, like ECG, echocardiography, chest radiographs, and clinical variables, each capturing distinct but correlated aspects of cardiac physiology. Yet most medical foundation models remain modality-specific, combining modalities only for finetuning or post-training. This discards the cross-modal evidence clinicians naturally integrate and ignores the structure within each modality. We introduce Latent-Attention Masked Autoencoders (LAMAE), a multimodal, structure-aware masked autoencoder that jointly learns patient-level representations during self-supervised pretraining. Rather than fusing modalities post hoc, LAMAE exchanges information directly in the latent space through a shared latent-attention module operating over a study-view-entity hierarchy, enabling aggregation of variable observations and graceful handling of missing modalities. Pretrained on over 1.2 million MIMIC-IV hospital stays, LAMAE outperforms modality-specific pretraining and strong contrastive and vision-language baselines across multimodal hospital-stay tasks, such as in-hospital mortality, ICD-10 and DRG coding, and length of stay, while remaining competitive on unimodal tasks. These gains persist even when only a single modality is available at test time, showing that modeling both intra- and inter-modal structure yields more robust, transferable representations.

</details>


### [177] [Competence-Gated Pooling of Language Models and Priors for Event Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12101)
*Aditi Tiwari,Aashrith Bandaru,Heng Ji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces a competence gate for hybrid forecasting that estimates domain-level weights to decide when a language model adds value; improves Brier score vs external baseline and global pooling; performance varies by dataset, robust to leakage; outcome-estimated competence aids abstention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In hybrid forecasting, external signals exist; need to quantify the marginal value of a model beyond existing forecasts; improve decision to include model via selective gating.

Method: Define relative competence under Brier loss; derive conditions when model disagreement helps; compare domain-specific vs global pooling weights; propose competence gate that learns domain-level weights from resolved outcomes, shrinks uncertain estimates toward global weight, recalibrates pooled forecast; test on 2,357 binary questions with five LMs; apply leakage controls and time-series prior; analyze FRED; compare Qwen models with confidence vs competence.

Result: Gate reduces Brier score from 0.0771 to 0.0732 relative to external baseline; outperforms global forecast combinations; retains significance under leakage controls and leakage-safe time-series prior; observed on FRED; no significant improvement on ForecastBench market subset (market dominates); among four Qwen models, verbal confidence unreliable, but outcome-estimated competence improves abstention decisions.

Conclusion: Demonstrates practical selective-model-use approach via marginal-value-based gating; domain-aware weighting improves external forecast quality; gating particularly beneficial when external forecast exists; limitations include dependence on dataset, leakage, and market-dominated contexts; suggests using domain-level weights and abstention strategy in practice.

Abstract: In hybrid forecasting, a language model is often one of several available signals. A system may already have a market, crowd, or statistical forecast and must decide whether the model adds useful information or should be ignored. The relevant target is therefore not standalone model accuracy, but relative competence, defined as the model's marginal value beyond the available external forecast. Under Brier loss, we characterize when model disagreement can improve an external forecast and derive the gain from using domain-specific rather than global pooling weights. We then introduce a competence gate that estimates domain-level source weights from resolved outcomes, shrinks uncertain estimates toward a global weight, and recalibrates the pooled forecast. Across 2,357 resolved binary questions and five language models, the gate improves the main external baseline from 0.0771 to 0.0732 Brier and significantly outperforms global forecast combinations. The gain remains significant under leakage controls and against a leakage-safe time-series prior on the pooled structured set, with separate evidence on FRED. In contrast, the gate gives no significant improvement on the official ForecastBench market subset, where it largely defers to the market. Across four Qwen models, verbal confidence does not reliably identify when the model outperforms the external forecast, while outcome-estimated competence supports better abstention decisions. These results provide a practical approach for selective model use based on measured marginal value.

</details>


### [178] [Language Is an Insufficient Substrate for Quantitative Reasoning, and Consequential Domains Need Large Quantitative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12105)
*Reuben Vandeventer,David Imrem,David J. Wild*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Argues that LLMs trained on lossy human descriptions cannot recover the original quantitative records, making reproducibility, traceability, and calibrated uncertainty unattainable; proposes Large Quantitative Models (LQMs) as a distinct model class tailored for high-stakes quantitative tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap between current progress in language models and the reliability requirements of consequential quantitative decision-making, emphasizing irrecoverable description loss and lack of traceability.

Method: Conceptual formalization: treat training data as a lossy description of reality; identify three requirements (reproducibility, lineage to source records, calibrated uncertainty); argue that LLMs cannot supply these by construction; define and motivate Large Quantitative Models (LQMs) as a separate class.

Result: Formalizes the representation-loss property of language-model training, identifies three core properties needed for consequential settings, and posits LQMs as a distinct model class that can satisfy these properties better than LLMs.

Conclusion: Concludes that for high-stakes quantitative tasks, LLMs are insufficient; a new model class, Large Quantitative Models, is required to achieve reproducibility, traceability, and calibrated uncertainty in outputs.

Abstract: The prevailing assumption in applied machine learning is that progress on consequential quantitative decisions such as pricing risk, allocating capital, triaging patients, or containing a network intrusion will follow from progress in large language models (LLMs). A language model is trained on a representation of the world that was produced by human description; description is a lossy encoding of the quantitative record, and the loss is irreversible: no downstream model, at any scale, can recover from a description what the description did not encode. We formalize this as a property of the representation on which a model is trained rather than of the model capacity, and we identify three further properties that consequential settings demand of a model and that a language substrate cannot supply by construction: reproducibility, lineage from every output back to the source records that produced. it, and calibrated uncertainty. We argue that these properties define a distinct model class, which we call the Large Quantitative Model (LQM).

</details>


### [179] [DU-NO: A Parameter-Efficient Double U-Shaped Neural Operator for Phase-Resolving Wave Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12115)
*Enrique Hernandez Noguera,Md Meftahul Ferdaus,Nathan Cooper,Elias Ioup,Mahdi Abdelguerfi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DU-NO is a light-weight multiscale U-shaped spectral operator for nearshore wave modeling that reduces parameters to 3.64M and outperforms U-FNO on FUNWAVE-TVD benchmarks, achieving a 14.9% better autoregressive rollout with 10.8x fewer parameters, and showing cross-domain gains; code is released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the computational cost of phase-resolving wave models to enable ensembles, uncertainty quantification, and real-time forecasting while preserving solver-level accuracy.

Method: Introduce DU-NO (Double U-shaped Neural Operator): a multiscale U-shaped spectral operator that attaches lightweight convolutional U-Net branches only at the two shallow encoder/decoder levels; high-wavenumber content exists on fine grids, so local full-band pathways are placed there while coarse levels remain spectral; employs a depth-decaying mode schedule to reach 3.64M parameters.

Result: On FUNWAVE-TVD benchmark, DU-NO achieves the best autoregressive rollout among six identically trained architectures, improving on U-FNO by 14.9% with 10.8x fewer parameters; the gain persists across all frequency bands, including high-wavenumber where truncated-spectral operators fail. Parameter-matched controls show a 28.6% advantage for DU-NO. Generalizes to 2D Navier–Stokes and PDEBench shallow-water rollouts.

Conclusion: DU-NO delivers high-fidelity forecasts with a substantially smaller parameter budget, with robust performance across bands and domains; code, models, and evaluation artifacts are publicly available.

Abstract: Phase-resolving wave models such as FUNWAVE-TVD are the accuracy standard for nearshore dynamics, resolving the shoaling, refraction, and breaking of individual waves, but their cost rules them out for the ensembles, uncertainty quantification, and real-time warning that operational forecasting demands. Neural operators promise solver-level accuracy at a fraction of that cost, yet on wave-dominated fields the accurate ones are large: hybrid spectral-convolutional operators such as U-FNO (the strongest baseline in our study after DU-NO) buy their fidelity with tens of millions of parameters. We introduce DU-NO (Double U-shaped Neural Operator), a multiscale U-shaped spectral operator that attaches lightweight convolutional U-Net branches only at its two shallowest encoder and decoder levels. The placement follows a sampling argument: high-wavenumber content exists only on fine grids, so the local, full-band pathways go where that content lives, while the coarse, band-limited levels stay purely spectral. A depth-decaying mode schedule holds the model to 3.64M parameters, an order of magnitude below U-FNO. On our publicly released FUNWAVE-TVD benchmark, DU-NO attains the best autoregressive rollout error of six identically trained architectures, improving on U-FNO by 14.9% with 10.8x fewer parameters, and a frequency-band analysis shows the gain holds across all bands, including the high-wavenumber band where truncated-spectral operators collapse. Parameter-matched controls confirm the gain is architectural: rescaled to the same 3.6M budget, the best baseline still trails DU-NO by 28.6%. The advantage carries beyond nearshore waves: DU-NO matches the strongest baselines on 2D Navier-Stokes and wins clearly on PDEBench shallow-water rollouts. Code, trained models, and evaluation artifacts are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/duno-code-5A7B/.

</details>


### [180] [When Successful Knowledge Graph Edits Displace Correct Answers: Rank-Level Locality beyond Parameter Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12116)
*Yi-Cheng Lai,Jerry Wang,Hsin-Ling Hsu,Li-Chu Chi,Ya-Wen Teng,Hen-Hsen Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Editing a knowledge graph embedding (KGE) model to boost a specific answer can cause rank displacement of other correct answers; a three-scope audit plus geometric conditions are proposed to assess and guide updates, with mixed success across methods on FB15k-237 (DistMult, ComplEx).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the risk that targeted edits to KGE models promote a desired answer at the cost of displacing other correct answers, even when locality-based tests rely on the edited parameter.

Method: Introduce a three-scope rank-displacement audit: (1) facts supported by the edited parameter, (2) other correct answers to the target query, (3) correct answers across queries sharing the same relation. Derive dimensional and geometric conditions for updates that improve the target while exactly preserving selected scores. Evaluate editing strategies on FB15k-237 using DistMult and ComplEx, including direct promotion, strict preservation, support-regularized editing, rank-truncated preservation, across dimensions, scorers, ranking conventions, and a learned editor.

Result: Direct promotion consistently places the target in the top ten but damages 76.8–77.0% of edits; strict preservation causes no measured damage but succeeds only 1.3–1.4%. Support-regularized entity editing achieves the highest joint success: 36.3–37.7%, while rank-truncated preservation reaches 32.8–34.7% and reduces mean displaced answers from ~14 to 1.2. Cross-dimension and cross-scorer results show locality depends on protected scope and editing mechanism; learning-based editors vary in locality. The paper argues that KGE editing should report both correction success and the incidence/severity of rank displacement.

Conclusion: Editing KGE models requires reporting both corrective success and the extent of rank displacement; locality depends on the editing mechanism and the protected scope, necessitating broader auditing beyond locality tests that reuse edited parameters.

Abstract: Editing a knowledge graph embedding (KGE) model to promote a desired answer can displace correct answers from the returned list. Locality tests based only on facts that reuse the edited parameter can miss this ranking effect. We introduce a common rank-displacement audit at three scopes: facts supported by the edited parameter, other correct answers to the target query, and correct answers across queries with the same relation. We also derive dimensional and geometric conditions for an update to improve the target while exactly preserving selected scores. On FB15k-237 with DistMult and ComplEx, direct promotion always moves the target into the top ten, but does so without damage in only 23.0--23.2\% of edits. Strict preservation causes no measured damage, yet succeeds in only 1.3--1.4\%. Support-regularized entity editing gives the highest joint success, 36.3--37.7\%, while rank-truncated preservation reaches 32.8--34.7\% and reduces the mean number of displaced answers from about 14 to 1.2. Experiments across dimensions, scorers, ranking conventions, and a learned editor show that locality depends on both the protected scope and the editing mechanism. KGE editing should therefore report correction success together with the incidence and severity of rank displacement.

</details>


### [181] [Mined from Scientific Literature: Process Schemas for Atomic Layer Deposition and Etching in Materials Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12139)
*Sameer Sadruddin,Eleni Poupaki,Alex Watkins,Bora Karasulu,Adriaan J. M. Mackus,Erwin Kessels,Sören Auer,Jennifer D'Souza*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces four domain-expert-reviewed JSON Schemas for ALD and ALE experimental and simulation processes, grounded in QUDT and curated with schema-miner, enabling structured, interoperable representation of materials, conditions, configurations, and results; demonstrated for schema-guided literature extraction and ORKG-based publication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous reporting in ALD/ALE literature hinders comparison, data reuse, and machine-actionable mining. Standardized, semantically grounded schemas are needed to unify experimental and simulation data and support automated curation and dissemination.

Method: Develop four domain-expert-reviewed JSON Schemas for ALD/ALE experimental and simulation processes; ground schemas in QUDT; curate using schema-miner (schema-miner pro); structure entities such as materials, process conditions, configurations, and results; perform comparative analysis of scope, structure, and semantic grounding; demonstrate use through schema-guided literature extraction and publication of structured records via ORKG templates.

Result: Four schemas cover ALD/ALE experimental and simulation workflows, enabling structured representation of materials, conditions, configurations, and results. Demonstrations include schema-guided extraction from literature and publication of structured records using ORKG templates, improving interoperability and machine-actionable reuse.

Conclusion: The work advances standardization and semantic grounding for ALD/ALE data, enabling more efficient literature curation and data reuse. Adoption by the community and integration with broader ontologies and datasets will be key for realizing full benefits.

Abstract: Atomic layer deposition (ALD) and atomic layer etching (ALE) are reported heterogeneously across experimental and simulation literature in materials science, hindering comparison and machine-actionable reuse. We present four domain-expert-reviewed JSON Schemas for ALD and ALE experimental and simulation processes. Curated with schema-miner and grounded in QUDT using schema-miner pro, the schemas structure materials, process conditions, configurations , and measured or predicted results. We compare their scope, structure, and semantic grounding, and demonstrate their use for schema-guided literature extraction and publication of structured records through ORKG templates.

</details>


### [182] [Can LLMs in Draft-Verify-Revise Pipelines Resolve Deictic Ambiguity?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12162)
*Obinna I. Ekekezie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Deictic shifts across stages in draft-verify-revise LLM pipelines cause misinterpretation of context-dependent terms; performance varies by model and reasoning effort; explicit referents recommended.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate how cascading context and deictic resolution affect accuracy in multi-stage LLM orchestration; quantify across models, configurations, and reasoning burdens; derive design implications.

Method: Synthetic dataset of 10 base examples rendered in 3 conditions; manipulated which stage resolves deictic expressions and required reasoning; six models from three providers; 21 configurations; primary and ablation experiments; sequential e-values; separate analysis of meta-evaluator rationale; balanced accuracy; cost considerations.

Result: Accuracy varied from 0.156 (below chance) to near-perfect; GPT-5.2 improved with higher reasoning (0.156 to 0.942); Gemini 3 Pro stayed >0.94 at all levels; low-reasoning Gemini 3 Pro outperformed GPT-5.2 high-reasoning at roughly 5% of trial cost; meta-evaluator relied on surface cues; deictic shifts pose a practical risk.

Conclusion: Design of draft-verify-revise pipelines should ensure explicit referents at each stage to mitigate deictic shifts; model choice and reasoning allocation significantly impact reliability; consider cost-reasoning tradeoffs and targeted mitigations for context cascades.

Abstract: Draft-verify-revise is a common LLM orchestration pattern for scaling inference-time compute. One LLM drafts, a second critiques the draft and provides feedback, and a third uses that feedback to revise the draft into the final output. As context cascades between stages, LLMs at different stages can resolve a context-dependent expression such as "previous" differently. When that happens, the expression undergoes a deictic shift, a change in what it refers to. This phenomenon was studied with a synthetic dataset of 10 base examples, each rendered in three conditions. Holding the shared components constant, the conditions varied whether the draft stage LLM (the assistant) or the verify stage LLM (the grader) resolved the expression correctly, and how much independent reasoning the revise stage LLM (the meta-evaluator) needed to determine which reading was correct. Six models from three providers were tested across 21 reasoning effort configurations using e-values for sequential testing, in a primary experiment and an ablation experiment that removed error classification labels from the grader's feedback. A separate LLM analyzed the meta-evaluator's stated rationale for each wrong verdict. Balanced accuracy (the unweighted mean of sensitivity and specificity) ranged from 0.156, below chance, to near-perfect. GPT-5.2 rose from 0.156 without reasoning to 0.942 at its highest reasoning effort level, while Gemini 3 Pro stayed above 0.94 at every level. Gemini 3 Pro at low reasoning effort outscored GPT-5.2 at xhigh reasoning effort for roughly 5% of the cost per trial. When the meta-evaluator erred, it tended to rely on surface cues rather than operational reasoning. Context engineers implementing draft-verify-revise pipelines should be wary of deictic shifts and make the intended referent explicit at each stage.

</details>


### [183] [GLARE: Generative Learning via Adversarial Reward Estimation For Social Dynamics Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12165)
*Tenghao Huang,Zhaoxuan Tan,Muhao Chen,Jonathan May,Mengting Wan,Longqi Yang,Pei Zhou,Sihao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Benchmark for forecasting multi-party meeting continuations. MDFB uses real meetings to test progress toward a question; GLARE applies adversarial imitation learning to conditional generation, improving over SFT and SPIN on utility and human-likeness. MDFB also serves as a social-reasoning arena for model comparison.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need to track and predict dynamics in long, multi-party meetings (agenda, roles, intentions, disagreements) and to evaluate generated continuations without requiring exact future reproduction.

Method: MDFB presents a transcript prefix and an active question; models generate a plausible multi-turn continuation in one call; evaluation measures utility (progress toward the question) and human-likeness (plausible flow and role consistency). GLARE adapts adversarial imitation learning: a discriminator ranks observed continuations over current-actor samples, using its score as a KL-regularized policy reward; retraining on current-policy negatives evolves the reward landscape.

Result: GLARE achieves average human-evaluated win rates of 0.66 for utility and 0.70 for human-likeness, outperforming SFT and SPIN but still below observed human continuation. MDFB provides a benchmark for task-specific learning and output-based evaluation, and enables cross-model comparisons including closed-source systems.

Conclusion: MDFB is a practical social-reasoning benchmark for evaluating and training meeting-behavior models, while GLARE demonstrates how adversarial imitation-learning signals can guide conditional language generation to improve meeting-like continuations.

Abstract: Meeting continuation requires tracking the agenda, speaker roles, participant intentions, and disagreement across long multi-party discussions. We introduce the Meeting Dynamic Forecasting Benchmark (MDFB), constructed from 2,207 real-world meetings and 24,794 future-facing queries. Given a transcript prefix and an active question, a model generates a plausible multi-turn continuation in one call. We evaluate utility---progress toward the question---and human-likeness---plausible conversational flow and role consistency---without requiring exact reproduction of the observed future. We further present GLARE, an adaptation of adversarial imitation learning to conditional language generation. A discriminator ranks the observed continuation above samples from the current actor, and its score supplies a KL-regularized policy reward; retraining on current-policy negatives allows the reward landscape to evolve with the actor. GLARE attains average human-evaluated win rates of 0.66 on utility and 0.70 on human-likeness, outperforming SFT and SPIN while remaining below the observed human continuation. We also demonstrate MDFB as a social reasoning arena for comparing general-purpose models, including closed-source systems, through reference-assisted judgments. Together, these studies illustrate the benchmark's use for both task-specific learning and output-based evaluation of meeting behavior.

</details>


### [184] [WinSyn: An Automated Pipeline for Realistic Enterprise Question-Answering Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12171)
*Amey Varhade,Ananya Sutradhar,Ravishankar Krishnaswamy,Navin Goyal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes synthetic, enterprise-focused QA dataset pipeline with long-running email-based projects, evaluating frontier models and showing performance below 80% on aggregated queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise QA is hindered by scattered, evolving data and realistic, high-complexity queries; existing benchmarks underrepresent these challenges.

Method: Automated data-generation pipeline simulating months-long projects, up to 25 employees across roles; generates emails, long/short-form questions, and gold answers grounded in data; evaluates standard agent baselines on frontier models.

Result: Aggregate scores across queries stay below 80% across datasets, signaling substantial room for improvement; current models struggle with distributed information and ambiguity in enterprise data.

Conclusion: Realistic high-complexity evaluation data is essential for developing robust enterprise DR systems; further work needed for enterprise deployment.

Abstract: Enterprise settings provide a challenging environment for question-answering agents, which often rely on Retrieval-Augmented Generation, Deep Research (DR), and related techniques. Much of this challenge comes from the complexity of enterprise data: information is often spread across evolving and potentially conflict- ing emails, chat messages, documents, and other artifacts. Existing benchmarks typically have limited real-world complexity, short-form responses, and unnatural queries, so they often fail to capture the challenges of enterprise settings. In this work, we introduce an automated pipeline for generating synthetic datasets of emails reflecting realistic workplace scenarios, along with long- and short-form questions and gold answers grounded in the data. Our method simulates long-running enterprise projects spanning several months and involving up to 25 interacting employees across multiple roles. The data emphasizes ambiguity, distributed information, and naturally occurring queries. To validate the pipeline, we evaluate few standard agentic baselines on our datasets using the latest frontier models. We find that aggregate scores averaged over all queries remain below 80% for each dataset, indicating significant room for improvement. These findings suggest that more work remains to be done for enterprise deployment and underscore the importance of realistic, high-complexity evaluation data for developing stronger real-world enterprise DR systems.

</details>


### [185] [Soft Symbol Grounding for Prototypical Concepts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12247)
*Marcos Galván-López,Nijesh Upreti,Hiram Calvo,Carlos Aguilar-Ibáñez,Vaishak Belle*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Soft-PNet eliminates task-specific loss engineering in neuro-symbolic grounding by reframing concept grounding as a Metropolis walk over a precomputed feasible-symbolic cache. It builds a prototype distribution from a single labeled anchor per concept and trains using a KL objective between prototype-weighted cache and the network's concept predictions, enabling task-agnostic supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current neuro-symbolic models suffer from reliance on final labels that permit reasoning shortcuts (correct labels with wrong concepts). Prototypical ideas anchor concepts with few labeled examples but require hand-crafted, task-specific losses. A loss-free, generalizable grounding method is desirable.

Method: Introduce Soft-PNet, which uses a Metropolis-style walk over a precomputed cache of feasible symbolic solutions. A prototype distribution, built from a single labeled anchor per concept, guides the walk. Training minimizes a single KL divergence between the prototype-weighted cache and the network's concept predictions, with no task-specific loss engineering.

Result: Empirical evaluation on MNIST-EvenOdd, Visual Sudoku, and Kand-Logic under scarce supervision shows Soft-PNet matching loss-engineered prototypical networks at both concept and label levels, while recovering concepts that soft-grounding baselines miss, and achieving lower training time.

Conclusion: Soft-PNet provides a loss-free, task-agnostic grounding framework that reduces reasoning shortcuts and improves concept recovery, achieving competitive performance across multiple tasks with scarce supervision.

Abstract: Neuro-symbolic models are usually trained with supervision only on final labels, leaving the intermediate concepts unobserved. Since many concept assignments are consistent with a given label, training can predict labels correctly while recovering the wrong concepts, a failure known as a reasoning shortcut. Prototypical networks reduce shortcuts by anchoring each concept to a few labeled examples, but existing methods still couple perception and reasoning through a hand-crafted, task-specific differentiable loss that must be redesigned for every task. We introduce \textbf{Soft-PNet}, which removes this loss: it reframes concept grounding as a Metropolis walk over a precomputed cache of feasible symbolic solutions, guided by a prototype distribution built from a single labeled anchor per concept, and trains against one KL objective between the prototype-weighted cache and the network's concept predictions. The objective is identical across tasks and remains applicable when the solution space cannot be enumerated. On \texttt{MNIST-EvenOdd}, Visual Sudoku, and \texttt{Kand-Logic} under scarce supervision, Soft-PNet matches loss-engineered prototypical networks at the concept and label levels and recovers concepts that soft-grounding baselines miss, with no loss engineering and lower training time.

</details>


### [186] [GTA: Graph Theory Agent and Benchmark for Algorithmic Graph Reasoning with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12265)
*Zixiang Xu,Yanbo Wang,Chenxi Wang,Lang Gao,Zirui Song,Yue Huang,Zhaorun Chen,Xiangliang Zhang,Xiuying Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GT Bench introduces a comprehensive graph reasoning benchmark across four input representations (natural language, structured language, adjacency list, adjacency matrix) covering 24 classical problems in 44 task-structure settings with 100k+ examples. It reveals representation-sensitive LLM performance, varying by graph density/size/topology and model. Graph Theory Agent (GTA) combines a representation selector with plan-and-decompose scaffolding around a frozen executor LLM, achieving strong gains and transferring to GraCoRe and NLGraph without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle with reliable multi-step graph reasoning; existing evaluations use narrow tasks, focus on code generation, or fix input formats, leaving representation effects and transferability underexplored.

Method: Assemble GT Bench: 24 graph problems, 44 task-structure settings, 100k+ examples, four representations (natural language, structured language, adjacency list, adjacency matrix). Evaluate eight LLMs to analyze representation effects. Develop GTA: a preference-trained representation selector paired with plan-and-decompose around a frozen executor LLM. Assess on GT Bench easy/hard splits and compare to prompting and agent baselines; test transfer to GraCoRe and NLGraph; provide open-source code.

Result: Performance is strongly tied to input representation; the best representation depends on graph density, size, topology, and model. GTA improves accuracy substantially (Phi-4: 53.5%→69.1% easy; 33.0%→41.5% hard) and beats baselines; transfers to GraCoRe and NLGraph without retraining.

Conclusion: Input representation is a critical factor in LLM-based graph reasoning. A representation selector with plan-and-decompose scaffolding around a frozen executor yields substantial gains and broad transferability, suggesting a viable path for robust, adaptable graph reasoning with LLMs.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly asked to reason over structured data such as graphs, yet how reliably they can carry out multi-step graph algorithms in language remains unclear. Existing evaluations tend to use simple tasks on small graphs, to score code generation rather than reasoning over the graph itself, or to fix a single input format. We introduce Graph Theory Bench (GT Bench), a benchmark covering 24 classical graph problems in 44 task-structure settings, with over 100,000 examples across four representations: natural language, structured language, adjacency list, and adjacency matrix. Evaluating eight LLMs on GT Bench shows that accuracy is strongly tied to the input representation, that the best representation shifts with graph density, size, and topology as well as with the model, and that this sensitivity persists, attenuated, in the strongest reasoning models. Building on these observations, we propose the Graph Theory Agent (GTA), which pairs a preference-trained representation selector with plan-and-decompose scaffolding around a frozen executor LLM. GTA lifts Phi-4 from 53.5% to 69.1% on the benchmark's easy split and from 33.0% to 41.5% on its hard split, outperforming eight prompting and agent baselines, and transfers without retraining to GraCoRe and NLGraph. Code for benchmark generation and evaluation: https://github.com/xzx34/GTA. The project homepage is available at https://xzx34.github.io/gta/.

</details>


### [187] [Learning Symbolic Constraint Representations from Examples: A Neuro-Symbolic Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12267)
*Nassim Belmecheri,Arnaud Gotlieb,Nadjib Lazaar,Helge Spieker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Neuro-symbolic automatic constraint acquisition using Neural Oracle Transformer and FastCA to learn interpretable constraint networks with substantially reduced human input.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the cost and latency of interactive constraint acquisition by replacing or augmenting human queries with learned emulation, enabling automatic recovery of structured symbolic models from data.

Method: Train Neural Oracle Transformer to emulate user responses based on existing examples; integrate with the FastCA constraint engine to refine the oracle's outputs into a sound, consistent, and interpretable constraint network.

Result: Shows that data-driven pattern recognition can align with symbolic reasoning through neuro-symbolic interaction, enabling automated construction of constraint networks in combinatorial domains without relying on domain-specific prior knowledge.

Conclusion: Proposes a robust, automated CA framework that combines neural emulation of user feedback with symbolic constraint learning, advancing automatic model construction in combinatorial domains.

Abstract: Learning user-defined concepts as constraint networks has been extensively studied in the constraint acquisition (CA) literature. However, existing approaches typically rely on intensive interactions with a human oracle, making the learning process costly in terms of time and number of queries. In this paper, we propose a neuro-symbolic framework for automatic CA that significantly reduces user involvement by introducing neural Oracle Transformer models which learn to emulate user responses and to generalize conceptual knowledge. Trained on previously available examples, the learned oracle interacts with a dedicated CA engine, FastCA, which systematically refines the oracle's responses into a sound, consistent, and interpretable constraint network. This neuro-symbolic interaction enables the recovery of structured symbolic models from data without prior domain knowledge. Our results demonstrate that this neuro-symbolic interplay effectively aligns data-driven pattern recognition with symbolic reasoning, offering a robust approach to automating model construction in combinatorial domains.

</details>


### [188] [T-GADE: Thermodynamical Generative-AI-Driven Evolution of LLM Artifacts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12286)
*Kyoko Ogawa,Naoki Mori*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: T-GADE combines thermodynamic genetic algorithms with LLM-based operators to evolve artifact-level outputs (descriptions+code) and uses energy-based diversity control; it reduces training excess on online bin-packing and matches EoH transfer excess via validation-based artifact retention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need to control both population diversity and generative capability when integrating evolutionary search with LLMs, especially for structured artifacts.

Method: Extend thermodynamic GA framework with LLM-based operators, artifact-level diversity evaluation; use a free-energy objective for generational/steady-state updates; employ Fermi-type occupancy excluding repeated genotypes, Bose-type occupancy permitting duplicates; exact one-member removal; conditions for zero-temperature EoH survival; evaluate on online bin-packing with training and transfer excess measures; generational Bose-type at T=0.003.

Result: Generational Bose-type T-GADE at T=0.003 reduced median training excess from 1.152% to 0.815% (20 runs per config); p=0.042 (Mann-Whitney), Cliff's δ=0.378; validation selection among two top candidates achieved transfer excess of 0.496%; results demonstrate utility of thermodynamical selection and validated artifact retention.

Conclusion: Thermodynamic selection coupled with artifact-level diversity and validation-based retention improves LLM-augmented evolution, reducing excess in bin-packing tasks and enabling transferability.

Abstract: Integrating evolutionary computation and large language models (LLMs) requires control of population diversity as well as generative capability. Among LLM outputs, those with explicit structure, such as a description paired with code, are structured artifacts; we use artifact for short. We propose T-GADE, which evolves these artifacts by extending thermodynamical genetic algorithms through LLM-based genetic operators and artifact-level diversity evaluation. A common free-energy objective supports generational and steady-state updates, with Fermi-type occupancy excluding repeated genotypes and Bose-type occupancy permitting them. We establish exact one-member removal and conditions for recovering the zero-temperature survival rule of Evolution of Heuristics (EoH). On the online bin-packing task studied in the EoH paper, excess measures relative bin-count overhead above a volume lower bound. Training excess uses search instances; transfer excess uses instances with another bin capacity. Generational Bose-type T-GADE at $T=0.003$ reduced median training excess by approximately 29%, from 1.152% to 0.815%, over 20 runs per configuration (two-sided Mann-Whitney $p=0.042$, Cliff's $δ=0.378$). Validation selection among its two highest-ranked final candidates reached the same median transfer excess as EoH, 0.496%. These results demonstrate the utility of thermodynamical selection and validation-based use of retained artifacts.

</details>


### [189] [Robust Prototypical Networks for Few-Shot Sensor Fault Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12287)
*Mohammed Ayalew Belay,Amirshayan Haghipour,Pierluigi Salvo Rossi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MEPN improves few-shot fault diagnosis by aggregating prototypes across multiple disjoint support episodes, reducing class-prototype variance without changing the encoder. On the DeFACTO sensor dataset (five-way classification with synthetic faults), MEPN substantially outperforms single-episode baselines in the one-shot setting; with equal 10-shot budgets, MEPN and ProtoNet are statistically indistinguishable, indicating the gain comes from prototype accumulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In industrial fault diagnosis, labeled fault examples are scarce. Prototypical networks suffer from unstable prototypes in very-low-shot regimes due to small support sets; aggregating information across episodes can stabilize representations.

Method: Introduce Multi-Episode Prototypical Networks (MEPN) that aggregate class prototypes from multiple disjoint support episodes by taking their mean as the class representative, keeping the encoder unchanged. Evaluate on DeFACTO with five-way fault classification and synthetic bias, drift, spike, and noise faults injected into real measurements. Use K=1 per-episode shot aggregated over N_agg=10 support episodes; compare to single-episode baselines; also compare at fixed budget (K=10) against ProtoNet.

Result: MEPN achieves a higher per-episode one-shot accuracy than single-episode baselines across 100 runs (exact value reported as a placeholder in the abstract). Under an equal 10-sample budget, MEPN and ProtoNet with K=10 perform equivalently, suggesting the improvement stems from prototype accumulation rather than fixed-budget advantages.

Conclusion: Aggregating prototypes across multiple episodes reduces prototype variance and improves few-shot fault diagnosis performance without changing the encoder architecture, offering a simple, effective enhancement to prototypical networks in industrial settings.

Abstract: Industrial fault diagnosis often operates with only a handful of labeled fault examples, making few-shot learning attractive for sensor monitoring. Standard prototypical networks are simple and effective; however, their class prototypes may become unstable in the very-low-shot regime because each decision relies on a small support set. We propose \emph{Multi-Episode Prototypical Networks} (MEPN), which aggregate prototypes from multiple disjoint support episodes and use their mean as the final class representative, reducing prototype variance without changing the encoder architecture. We evaluate MEPN on the DeFACTO sensor dataset using five-way fault classification with synthetic bias, drift, spike, and noise faults injected into real industrial measurements. Over 100 independent runs, MEPN reaches \textbf{\SensorOneShotGcpn\%} in the per-episode one-shot setting ($K\!=\!1$ shot, aggregated over $N_{\text{agg}}\!=\!10$ support episodes), substantially above single-episode baselines. Under an equal 10-sample support budget, MEPN and ProtoNet at $K\!=\!10$ are statistically indistinguishable, confirming prototype accumulation as the mechanism rather than superior fixed-budget learning.

</details>


### [190] [Hybrid Physics-AI Framework of Body Center of Mass Dynamics from Wrist-Worn Sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12304)
*Shuhao Que,Valentina Breschi,Ying Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Wrist IMU-based mapping to COM acceleration is feasible using a simplified kinematic model (KM) and enhanced via hybrid HKM-NN models; HKM-NN improves gait/SS error and shows robustness differences under noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wrist-worn IMUs do not directly reflect whole-body dynamics; COM is a physiological reference for accurate gait analysis. A mapping from wrist IMU to COM acceleration enables better ambulatory assessment without full-body sensing.

Method: Develop a simplified kinematic model (KM) to map wrist IMU measurements to COM acceleration. Introduce HKM-NN models (hybrid grey-box/black-box) with serial learning (ser-) and two simultaneous-learning variants (sim1, sim2). Train and test on a dataset of 10 healthy volunteers performing six gait activities and sit-to-stand, using wrist IMU data with ground-truth COM measurements. Evaluate estimation error and robustness to noise.

Result: KM: 6.7–12.5% error for gait; 5.6% for sit-to-stand (SS). HKM-NN: 5.3–9.3% for gait; best 3.9% for SS. Robustness: sim1/sim2 display greater robustness under Gaussian noise; KM shows stronger robustness under salt-and-pepper noise.

Conclusion: Hybridizing biomechanical structure with data-driven learning (HKM-NN) yields more accurate COM acceleration estimation from wrist IMU than a purely reduced KM, with notable robustness advantages under common measurement perturbations.

Abstract: Wrist-worn IMU has been widely used for daily-life health monitoring. Yet, it does not fully represent whole-body dynamics, for which the body center of mass (COM) is considered the physiological reference standard. Therefore, this work proposes a simplified kinematic model (KM), which is designed to map the wrist IMU to the COM acceleration. It is built upon several reductive assumptions that enable the solvability of the dynamic equations based on wrist IMU measurements alone. This work further proposes three types of hybrid AI modeling methods, namely human kinematic model-based neural network (HKM-NN) models, to leverage the power of both grey-box and black-box modeling. The HKM-NN methods include serial learning (ser-) and two approaches of simultaneous learning (sim1- and sim2-). The proposed models are trained and tested using our dataset, which includes wrist IMU measurements and ground-truth COM measurements from 10 healthy volunteers during six gait activities and sit-to-stand (SS) transitional movement. The results demonstrate the feasibility of estimating COM acceleration from wrist IMU measurements. Our KM model yields satisfactory results, with an error ranging from 6.7% to 12.5% for gait activities and 5.6% for the SS. In comparison with the KM model, our HKM-NN models significantly enhance the performance, achieving 5.3% to 9.3% errors for gait activities, and the best error of 3.9% for the SS. In addition, the HKM-NN models demonstrate distinct robustness characteristics under noisy test conditions, with sim1-/sim2- generally maintaining greater robustness under Gaussian perturbations, while the KM model exhibits comparatively strong robustness under salt-and-pepper noise. These findings highlight the importance of combining biomechanical structure with data-driven learning for wearable sensing applications operating under imperfect and noisy measurement conditions.

</details>


### [191] [Do Influence-Derived Data Perturbations Enable Machine Unlearning? A Controlled Study of Three Plausible Roles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12313)
*Chenkai Wu,Chrispine Kambimbi,Qinyang Zeng,Jun Yan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DPL's three roles (deletion signal, regularizer, warm start) are tested under matched exact-seed retraining; after auditing and correcting implementation issues, DPL fails to demonstrate deletion, shows inconsistent utility, and underperforms warm-start baselines; Tiny ImageNet results inconclusive; authors provide a role-matched protocol and audit checklist.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To independently verify whether DPL provides a genuine deletion signal or useful regularization/warm-start benefits, and to expose any implementation issues that could misrepresent performance.

Method: Evaluate DPL across three roles with exact-seed retraining baselines, audit public code, correct two correctness issues (image direction handling; label perturbation precision), test on CIFAR-10/ResNet-18 and Tiny ImageNet, compare with warm-start and baseline deletion baselines, report under a matched protocol.

Result: Correcting the image/label issues, DPL fails direct deletion for CIFAR-10/ResNet-18 across seeds; utility effects vary by seed; total computation time for direction calculation makes DPL worse than simple warm-start baselines; Tiny ImageNet check inconclusive due to preprocessing; findings limited to random instance deletion.

Conclusion: The evidence does not support DPL as a robust direct-deletion signal or as a reliable regularizer/warm start under the tested regime; role-matched evaluation protocol and audit checklist are valuable to assess influence-based deletion claims; caution against relying on weaker evidence to justify stronger claims without rigorous, seed-controlled protocols.

Abstract: We evaluate Deep Perturbation Learning (DPL), which perturbs training images and labels along influence-derived directions, in three roles in which prior work has positioned it for machine unlearning: a direct deletion signal (the strongest claim), a utility-preserving regularizer, and a warm start for adversarial unlearning. Evidence for the weaker roles has been used to support the stronger one, so we test each role separately under a matched protocol with exact-seed retraining baselines. An audit of the public implementation identifies two correctness issues: image directions are computed on augmented, normalized tensors but applied to raw images, and the label perturbation falls below float32 resolution, leaving labels unchanged. After correcting the image-perturbation pipeline, DPL fails the direct-deletion criterion on CIFAR-10/ResNet-18 in all three paired seeds. Its utility effects are inconsistent in sign across seeds, and once direction-computation time is counted it underperforms simple warm-start baselines. A one-seed Tiny ImageNet check likewise does not favor DPL as a regularizer or warm start; preprocessing inconsistencies in the released code make the direct comparison there inconclusive. These results cover random instance deletion only and do not rule out influence-based methods in other deletion regimes. We release a role-matched evaluation protocol and an audit checklist for perturbation-based deletion claims.

</details>


### [192] [AIM: A Privacy-Aware Interoperable Memory Framework for Multi-Agent Multi-User LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12320)
*Zachary Johnson,Nigel Boachie Kumankumah,Somya Chatterjee,Tejas Sathyamurthi,Min Chen,Xinyi Alice Li,Xiao Wang,Emily Morgan Gelchie,Jessica Lin,Sadid A. Hasan,Sulaiman Vesal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AIM (Agentic Interoperable Memory) enables privacy-aware, multi-agent, multi-user memory with private/public classification and access controls; accompanied by MUMBench dataset to evaluate multi-user memory operations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional LLMs are limited to single sessions and do not learn across users. Existing memory systems are per-user and miss cross-user knowledge sharing. AIM aims to enable interoperable memories that balance privacy with shared knowledge to improve coordination.

Method: Introduce AIM with memory that classifies data as private or public and enforces index-level access controls. Develop MUMBench, a multi-user memory benchmark across four domains to evaluate retrieval, creation, update, and deletion of memories. Evaluate AIM using three independent runs.

Result: On MUMBench, AIM achieves 96.0% visibility classification accuracy, 58.8% strict operation accuracy, and 70.5% state-aware operation accuracy.

Conclusion: AIM provides a privacy-aware, interoperable memory framework for multi-agent, multi-user LLMs, enabling private data protection and shared knowledge to improve coordination and consistency. MUMBench is the first public dataset to evaluate multi-user memory operations.

Abstract: Traditional large language models (LLMs) are scoped to individual user sessions, limiting their knowledge to a single conversation and preventing them from learning user preferences that evolve over time. Existing agentic memory systems address this limitation but generally operate at the individual-user level, restricting the public knowledge that could be shared across users to improve downstream responses. We introduce AIM (Agentic Interoperable Memory), a unified, privacy-aware memory framework that enables multi-agent, multi-user LLM systems to persistently manage private and shared memory. AIM dynamically classifies information as private, scoped to one user and inaccessible to others, or public, accessible to all users. It enforces index-level access controls so that private memories are retrievable only by their owner, protecting sensitive data while allowing beneficial shared knowledge to improve coordination and consistency. We also introduce MUMBench (Multi-User Memory Benchmark), a dataset of multi-user interactions containing private and shareable information across four domains. To our knowledge, MUMBench is the first public dataset designed to evaluate multiple memory operations, including retrieval, creation, update, and deletion, in a multi-user environment. Across three independent runs on MUMBench, AIM achieves 96.0% visibility classification accuracy, 58.8% strict operation accuracy, and 70.5% state-aware operation accuracy.

</details>


### [193] [Affective Agent: On-Device Personalized Intervention Reasoning for Wearable Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12322)
*Reina Mun,Zishen Wan,Vijay Janapa Reddi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Affective Agent proposes a three-layer on-device architecture that uses a compact language model plus physiological evidence, context, and a self-evolving memory to decide when/how to intervene, without cloud or per-user retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the challenge of on-device, personalized intervention reasoning under uncertainty in wearable contexts, preserving privacy and low latency while avoiding per-user model updates.

Method: Three-layer architecture: perception (sensing), personalization (memory-evolution), and reasoning (two-pass structured reasoning). Uses a sub-billion-parameter language model augmented with physiological data, context, and user history; memory-driven structured memory that evolves on-device managed by the host; evaluated in indoor environmental quality control with simulator-generated longitudinal scenarios.

Result: Memory-driven personalization and two-pass structured reasoning improve intervention decision quality in synthetic evaluations; on-device reasoning enables closed-loop, personalized interventions without cloud dependence or per-user retraining.

Conclusion: Demonstrates a viable path for on-device, personalized intervention in wearables by combining compact LLMs with memory-evolved personalization and structured reasoning, mitigating cloud reliance and per-user retraining; future work should address real-world deployment, energy constraints, safety, and generalization beyond simulator scenarios.

Abstract: Affective computing has advanced wearable state inference, but on-device reasoning about whether, when, and how to intervene remains challenging. We present Affective Agent, a three-layer reference architecture for personalized intervention reasoning under uncertainty on wearable-class hardware. It combines a compact sub-billion-parameter language model with physiological evidence, context, and user history to decide whether, when, and how to intervene, without cloud dependency or per-user retraining. The architecture is organized into three interacting layers (perception, personalization, and reasoning), adapting to individual users through host-managed structured memory evolution rather than per-user weight updates. We instantiate Affective Agent in indoor environmental quality control and evaluate it on held-out, simulator-generated longitudinal scenarios spanning physiological variation, context, signal quality, and intervention history. Results show that memory-driven personalization and two-pass structured reasoning improve intervention decisions within this synthetic evaluation. By moving the decision layer on-device, this work demonstrates a path from wearable state inference toward closed-loop, personalized intervention on wearable-class hardware.

</details>


### [194] [LoRA-RC: Reservoir Computing with Low-Rank Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12327)
*Wenbin Wan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LoRA-RC: online low-rank adaptation of the recurrent matrix in reservoir computing with a spectral-norm projection to guarantee contraction and ISS, improving drift robustness on chaotic systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stable online adaptation of recurrent reservoirs under system drift is challenging: fixed reservoirs degrade; unconstrained adaptation can destroy echo-state/incremental stability.

Method: Apply a low-rank correction to the recurrent matrix via a small core online; fix base reservoir and adaptation bases offline; project the correction onto a spectral-norm ball and apply a low-pass filter each step; this preserves a certified contraction set and enables an incremental input-to-state stability (ISS) bound with path-independent rate/gain.

Result: On a Lorenz system with abrupt parameter drift, LoRA-RC reduces post-drift prediction error by 56% vs a fixed RC and 51% vs readout-only adaptation. Ablations over 20 seeds show removing the projection inflates error by >40x, underscoring the projection’s importance.

Conclusion: LoRA-RC offers a stable, data-efficient online adaptation mechanism for RC that preserves contraction and ISS, with notable performance gains under drift.

Abstract: Reservoir computing (RC) trains only a linear readout over a fixed recurrent layer, making it fast and data-efficient for online prediction. However, a static reservoir degrades under system drift, readout-only adaptation is then insufficient, and unconstrained reservoir adaptation can destroy the echo-state and incremental stability properties that make RC reliable. This paper proposes LoRA-RC, which adapts the recurrent matrix through a low-rank correction driven by streaming prediction errors. The base reservoir and adaptation bases are fixed offline; a small core matrix is adapted online, projected onto a spectral-norm ball, and low-pass filtered at each step. The projection guarantees that every applied recurrent matrix remains within a certified contraction set, and an incremental input-to-state stability bound is established for the reservoir along each online adaptation path, with path-independent rate and gain. On a Lorenz system with an abrupt parameter drift, LoRA-RC cuts post-drift prediction error by 56% versus a fixed RC and 51% versus readout-only adaptation; ablations over 20 seeds show that removing the projection inflates this error by more than a factor of 40.

</details>


### [195] [Toward Robust Personalized Alignment for LLMs: Mitigating Persona Drift in Multi-Turn Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12373)
*Youyuan Zhang,Siyuan Li,Fangming Liu,Jing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CORE addresses persona drift by separating turn-local evidence from persistent persona updates and applying uncertainty-aware belief revision to only revise grounded user preferences. PERSIST is a held-out benchmark to test persona-state robustness under sequential interaction stress. Across ALOE, PersonaChat, and PERSIST, CORE improves personalized alignment and state fidelity, with human evaluation and mechanistic controls supporting explicit update control beyond memory or generation improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: User profiles in personalized LMs drift over long-term interactions, necessitating updates to persistent persona states only when genuine changes occur, while ignoring transient or ambiguous cues.

Method: Introduce CORE to separate turn-local evidence from persistent persona-state revision and use uncertainty-aware belief revision for updating grounded preferences. Develop PERSIST as a held-out benchmark to evaluate robustness under ambiguity, conflict, and social influence in sequential interactions.

Result: CORE improves personalized alignment and robustness across datasets (ALOE, PersonaChat, PERSIST) and yields complementary gains in normalized closed-slot state fidelity. Human evaluation and mechanistic controls corroborate explicit update control beyond stronger generation or persistent memory.

Conclusion: Explicit, uncertainty-aware update control can effectively manage persona drift, enhancing alignment and robustness without simply strengthening generation or relying on memory alone.

Abstract: Persona drift remains a central challenge for personalized language models, as user profiles evolve over long interactions rather than remain permanently fixed. Models must therefore revise persistent persona states when preferences genuinely change, while avoiding updates driven by transient, ambiguous, or unresolved observations. We propose CORE, which separates turn-local evidence from persistent persona-state revision and selectively updates grounded user preferences through uncertainty-aware belief revision. We also introduce PERSIST, a held-out post-anchor benchmark for persona-state robustness under sequential interaction stress, covering ambiguity, conflict, and controlled social influence. Across ALOE, PersonaChat, and PERSIST, CORE improves personalized alignment and robustness, with complementary gains in normalized closed-slot state fidelity. Human evaluation and mechanistic controls further support explicit update control beyond stronger generation or persistent memory alone.

</details>


### [196] [BlueLM-GUI Technical Report: A Real-Device-Centric Flywheel for Self-Improving Mobile GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12394)
*Tong Ye,Kunyang Han,Guozhi Wang,Longqiang Luo,Zhifeng Ding,Yongxiang Zhang,Xiaolei Shen,Yuxuan Zhang,Zhuping Zhang,Tao Xu,Yue Pan,Yucheng Zhao,Yupei Hu,Yuanjiang Ouyang,Danfeng Shen,Runqi Lin,Hongda Cai,Zhaoxiong Wang,Mengjia Yan,Yingjie Zhong,Chen Zhou,Zeyu Zhang,Xuwen Zhu,Penggang Shi,Mingcheng Luo,Ziyang Wu,Min Jin,Mingfu Shen,Zairong Xu,Fan Zhang,Hao Wang,Liang Liu,Zhulin Xie,Lijun Yao,Xiao Liang,Liangmin Wen,Liqiang Feng,Feilong Wu,Min Hu,Min Chen,Guanjing Xiong,Xiaohu Ruan,Xiaoxin Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BlueLM-GUI introduces a real-device-centric training flywheel for mobile GUI agents, addressing sandbox mismatch, real-device utilization, and benchmark stagnation via three principles: Every Sample Matters, Every Rollout Is Real, and Every Query Evolves. It achieves strong open/closed-model competitive results on MobileGUI-VBench (87.4) and AndroidWorld (84.9).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrially deployed mobile GUI agents suffer (1) distribution mismatch between sandbox training and production, (2) underutilization of costly real-device failures, and (3) benchmarks that saturate and fail to guide iteration.

Method: Three principles form the core method: (1) Every Sample Matters with dual-track Heterogeneous Triple-System Consensus evaluation and Error Correction & Derivation Module to convert trajectories into usable supervision; (2) Every Rollout Is Real with a three-stage pipeline—continual pre-training, supervised fine-tuning, and agentic reinforcement learning on hundreds of real phones; (3) Every Query Evolves via a quota-driven benchmark with three orthogonal axes for precise attribution and upgradeability.

Result: Empirical results show 87.4 on MobileGUI-VBench (best among similar models, 5.1 points above best closed-source) and 84.9 on AndroidWorld (best among open-source; competitive with closed-source).

Conclusion: Grounding model training and iterative improvement in real devices and the three guiding principles yields strong, robust, and transferable mobile GUI capability.

Abstract: Mobile GUI agents are shifting from multi-module frameworks to native models trained end-to-end, yet industrial deployment faces three persistent gaps. Sandbox training produces a distribution mismatch with production environments; expensive real-device failures remain underutilized; and fixed benchmarks saturate, losing the power to guide iteration. We present BlueLM-GUI, a 35B-A3B mobile GUI agent built as a real-device-centric flywheel that closes these gaps through three principles. Every Sample Matters: a dual-track pipeline with Heterogeneous Triple-System Consensus evaluation and an Error Correction \& Derivation Module salvages every trajectory into usable supervision. Every Rollout Is Real: a three-stage recipe---continual pre-training, supervised fine-tuning, and agentic reinforcement learning on hundreds of real phones---grounds every rollout in real production environments, so the capability the model learns transfers directly to deployment. Every Query Evolves: a quota-driven benchmark methodology with three orthogonal axes enables precise attribution and allows the benchmark to be systematically upgraded as the model improves. BlueLM-GUI achieves 87.4 on MobileGUI-VBench, surpassing the best closed-source model by 5.1 points, and 84.9 on AndroidWorld, the best result among open-source models and competitive with closed-source models. These results demonstrate that grounding model training and iterative improvement in both real devices and the three Every principles yields strong, robust, and transferable mobile GUI capability.

</details>


### [197] [Is Gaussian Splatting Becoming Neural Again? A Taxonomy and Controlled Study of Learned Parameterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12395)
*YuanHang Wang,Xin Cao,Yi Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A taxonomy and empirical study of neural parameterizations for 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS), showing that neuralization is not binary; selective neuralization—sharing appearance/opacity while preserving local geometry—yields the best reconstructions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how neural components are used to parameterize 3D Gaussian splats, and whether neuralization helps across different design choices or merely adds complexity.

Method: Propose five axes of neural parameterization (attribute decoding, spatial sharing, view-conditioned decoding, topology generation, amortized inference) and analyze 19 representative methods along these axes; complemented by a controlled mip-NeRF 360 study isolating three neural-parameter forms.

Result: The 19-method analysis shows these choices address distinct limitations and cannot be reduced to a binary neural label. In a controlled mip-NeRF 360 study, sharing appearance and opacity improves reconstruction quality, while decoding geometric structure offers no further gain.

Conclusion: Selective neuralization is favored: shared functions aid when they capture reusable correlations without sacrificing the local geometric freedom of explicit splats; design choices should balance shared representations with local control.

Abstract: Three-dimensional Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) combines explicit primitives with efficient rasterization, yet recent systems increasingly use neural networks to generate or share Gaussian parameters. We characterize this trend along five axes: attribute decoding, spatial sharing, view-conditioned decoding, topology generation, and amortized inference. An analysis of 19 representative methods shows that these choices address different limitations and cannot be reduced to a binary neural label. We also isolate three forms of neural parameterization in a controlled mip-NeRF 360 study. Sharing appearance and opacity improves reconstruction quality, while decoding geometric structure offers no further gain. The evidence favors selective neuralization: shared functions help when they capture reusable correlations without sacrificing the local geometric freedom of explicit splats.

</details>


### [198] [Niching Agents in The Core](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12398)
*Gary B. Parker,Jim O'Connor,John Asaro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The Core uses competitive co-evolution to evolve autonomous controllers without a traditional fitness function, via local interactions, tournament selection, crossover, and mutation. This work niches agents to sub-environments and finds niching improves success relative to full-system or different sub-environment niches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate whether niching within sub-environments can improve performance and robustness of agents evolved by The Core, and how localized selection pressures affect competitive co-evolution.
