Method: Apply The Core's local, fitness-free evolution (tournament selection, crossover, mutation) to agents constrained to sub-environments (niching). Compare performances of agents niched to specific sub-environments, to agents niched to the entire system and to those niched to other sub-environments, in the Xpilot domain.

Result: Agents niching to specific sub-environments show superior performance compared to agents niched to the entire system or to other sub-environments, indicating that sub-environment niching provides a beneficial specialization.

Conclusion: Sub-environment niching enhances The Core's effectiveness and suggests potential for targeted specialization and transfer strategies in competitive co-evolution frameworks; future work could explore dynamic niching and cross-niche transfer.

Abstract: The Core is a unique competitive co-evolution algorithm that allows agents to evolve autonomous control without utilizing a traditional fitness function. The agents evolve via local interactions through tournament selection, crossover, and mutation, producing offspring by evolving better controllers. Previous works have shown The Core's ability to evolve agents capable of combat and navigation in the Xpilot video game. This research expands upon that premise by niching agents to specific subsets of the original environment The Core was tested in. Our results demonstrate the niched agents capacity for success over agents niched to the entire system and agents niched to different sub-environments.

</details>


### [199] [OneLA: Scaling Linear-Attention Decoding to Large Beams in Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12399)
*Xiangrui Yang,Cheng Peng,Yunfeng Zhao,Liang Zeng,Ao Hu,Jiawei Yang,Shengzhe Wang,Jingshan Lv,Xiao Liang,Chen Yang,Jiaqiang Liu,Yiming Qiu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OneLA introduces a shared-state linear-attention decoding framework for generative recommendations to avoid per-beam recurrent state. It uses a single shared prompt-derived state plus compact append-only divergent records, an ancestry index to track beam histories, and a fused GPU kernel, yielding 1.54–2.46x end-to-end decode speedups and reduced memory/data movement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce memory footprint and data movement in large-beam linear-attention decoding for generative recommendation by avoiding per-beam recurrent state reconstruction.

Method: Represent all beam histories with a single shared prompt-derived state and compact append-only divergence records. Use an ancestry index to identify the transition records composing each beam's history. Update beams without moving/copying existing records. Employ a fused GPU kernel to reuse the shared state across beams.

Result: End-to-end decode speedups of about 1.54–2.46x with substantial reductions in recurrent-state memory usage and data movement.

Conclusion: OneLA enables scalable linear-attention decoding for generative recommendation workloads by sharing state across beams and using compact, append-only history representations, combined with lightweight tracking and a fused kernel.

Abstract: Generative recommendation (GR) relies on large-beam decoding to generate hundreds of candidate items, creating a new scaling challenge for recurrent linear attention. Existing linear attention serving systems either materialize a full recurrent state for every beam or repeatedly replay shared history, incurring substantial memory and traffic overhead. To address this, we present OneLA, a linear-attention decoding framework that exploits the shared prompt and short divergent suffixes of GR workloads. Specifically, OneLA represents all beam states using a single shared prompt-derived state and compact, append-only records of their divergent transitions. Using this representation, OneLA computes only the state information required at each decoding step, without reconstructing a full recurrent state for every beam. Furthermore, OneLA uses a lightweight ancestry index to track the transition records that make up each beam's history, allowing beams to be updated without moving or copying existing records. A fused GPU kernel further reuses the shared state across beams. Our analysis shows that OneLA achieves 1.54-2.46x end-to-end decode speedups while substantially reducing recurrent-state memory use and data movement.

</details>


### [200] [Decentralized Evolution of Hexapod Gaits with Independent Leg Controllers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12400)
*Gary B. Parker,John Asaro,Jim O'Connor*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Decentralized evolution of per-leg controllers enables emergent, stable hexapod gaits without centralized coordination, outperforming cooperative coevolution in Webots/Mantis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the complexity of gait optimization and scalability by evolving legs independently to harness emergent coordination for robust locomotion.

Method: Independently evolve each leg's controller using a decentralized evolutionary algorithm within the Webots Webots simulator (Mantis hexapod). No central coordination; compare performance against cooperative coevolution.

Result: Decentralized approach yields improved stability and adaptability of gaits, with notable emergent coordination, and reduces optimization complexity relative to cooperative coevolution.

Conclusion: Decentralized leg-controller evolution is effective for hexapod locomotion, highlighting decentralized strategies as scalable and adaptable for robotic locomotion without centralized gait planning.

Abstract: This paper presents a novel approach to hexapod locomotion by evolving each leg's gait independently through a decentralized evolutionary algorithm. Using the Webots simulator and the Mantis hexapod robot, we optimize individual leg controllers without centralized coordination, allowing emergent behaviors to drive the development of efficient, coordinated locomotion. Our decentralized method is benchmarked against cooperative coevolution, demonstrating improved efficacy in generating stable and adaptive gaits while showing interesting emergent coordination. By enabling independent evolution of leg controllers, this method reduces the complexity of gait optimization and highlights the potential of decentralized strategies for scalable and adaptive robotic systems.

</details>


### [201] [Beyond ID Embeddings: Process-Grounded Language Modeling for Cognitive Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12403)
*Minghang Liu,Yuanzhuo Wang,Qiang Qiu,Huawei Shen,Xueqi Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PLCD uses language-derived priors and response histories to calibrate student cognition in cognitive diagnosis models. It leverages LLMs to build concept schemas and cognitive process graphs, uses target-conditioned semantic memory to fetch relevant past responses, and applies a DA-MoE-based Language-to-Cognition mapper with process-level contrastive learning to unify textual evidence in a cognitive space. It achieves better student-performance prediction and demonstrates cognitive transfer and improved cold-start robustness, bridging LLM capabilities with psychometrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discretized, ID-based embeddings in traditional CDMs fail to capture the fluid, symbolically-linked structure of knowledge and struggle with cold-starts when new items or concepts appear. A linguistically grounded prior, updated by actual response histories, can more accurately reflect latent knowledge states and improve generalization.

Method: 1) Use large language models (LLMs) to construct concept schemas and cognitive process graphs from text. 2) Employ target-conditioned semantic memory to retrieve historical responses related to each target exercise. 3) Develop a process-grounded Language-to-Cognition Mapper with DA-MoE (data-aware Mixture of Experts) and process-level contrastive learning to map textual evidence into a unified cognitive space.

Result: Empirical results show PLCD outperforms traditional baselines in predicting student performance and exhibits strong cognitive transfer. It also improves cold-start robustness and cognitive grounding by leveraging structured language priors calibrated with response records.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that combining the computational power of LLMs with psychometric aims is feasible: structured language priors, when calibrated with response histories, can enhance the measurement of latent knowledge states and improve both predictive accuracy and transfer across tasks.

Abstract: Cognitive Diagnosis Models (CDMs) play a pivotal role in personalized online learning. Traditional CDMs rely on discrete, ID-based embeddings to represent students, exercises, and concepts. This paradigm diverges from the nature of learner cognition, where knowledge is not stored and retrieved as isolated symbols. As a result, CDMs suffer from semantic limitations when new exercises or concepts appear. In this paper, we propose a Process-aware Language Cognitive Diagnosis (PLCD) framework that uses language-derived structures as cognitive priors and response records to calibrate student posterior states. PLCD leverages large language models (LLMs) to construct concept schemas and cognitive process graphs, and uses target-conditioned semantic memory to retrieve historical responses that are relevant to each target exercise. A process-grounded Language-to-Cognition Mapper with DA-MoE experts and process-level contrastive learning then maps the textual evidence into a unified cognitive space. Experimental results show that PLCD not only outperforms traditional baselines in predicting student performance but also exhibits strong cognitive transfer capabilities. These results connect the computational power of LLMs with the psychometric goal of measuring latent knowledge states, suggesting that structured language priors calibrated by response records can improve cold-start robustness and cognitive grounding.

</details>


### [202] [VRL-Bench: Benchmarking agents on computer control tasks under finite trial budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12404)
*Yu Bai,Yukai Miao,Dawei Wang,Li Chen,Yanyu Ren,Yuqian Shi,Dan Li,Ying Xiong,Chengqiu Tan,Run Zhou,Li Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VRL-Bench provides a fair benchmark for trial-and-error verbal reinforcement learning; across tasks, verbal-memory updates show mixed benefits; a language-model-driven VEX^2 scheduler achieves robust improvements by balancing exploration and exploitation, outperforming memory-free retries and other updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To fairly evaluate how verbal-memory updates in trial-and-error learning affect performance under finite budgets, and to uncover trade-offs between exploiting past experience and exploring new attempts.

Method: Introduce VRL-Bench harness; evaluate three models on MiniWoB and WebShop; test multiple verbal-memory methods from Reflexion and subsequent work; run replay experiments to study exploitation vs exploration; propose VEX^2 scheduler that jointly selects policies and allocates remaining trial budget using a language model.

Result: Verbal-memory updates improve success in some settings but hurt in others; reflection can decrease success rates in replay scenarios; VEX^2 uniquely yields positive observed success-rate gains over retry across all six settings.

Conclusion: Memory-based verbal-reflection methods are not universally beneficial; a LM-driven exploration-exploitation scheduler (VEX^2) provides robust gains and fair benchmarking is essential to assess such methods.

Abstract: Learning from trial and error is a promising way to improve language agents on complex tasks such as computer control. Reflexion introduced verbal reinforcement learning, which turns failed trials into text that guides later attempts without updating model parameters. We introduce VRL-Bench, a harness for fair evaluation of trial-and-error learning under finite trial budgets. Across three models on MiniWoB and WebShop, we evaluate updates from several prominent verbal-memory methods spanning Reflexion and later work: each improves observed success over memory-free retry in some settings but reduces it in others. Replay experiments show that using reflection can reduce success rates, revealing a trade-off between exploiting experience and continued exploration. We propose VEX$^2$, a verbal exploration--exploitation scheduler that uses a language model to jointly select policies and allocate the remaining trial budget. VEX$^2$ is the only evaluated update to achieve positive observed success-rate gains over retry in all six settings.

</details>


### [203] [SoK: Rethinking Jailbreaking in the Era of Agentic AI: Attacks, Defenses, and Practical Consideration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12413)
*Md Jueal Mia,Yanzhao Wu,Selcuk Uluagac,M. Hadi Amini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A Systematization of Knowledge (SoK) on jailbreak-security for modern LLMs and agentic AI, introducing cross-layer taxonomies and an evaluation framework; finds that native alignment does not ensure jailbreak robustness, defenses are costly and highly dependent on model/attack/component, and intermediate risks can persist even when final responses are safe; advocates cross-layer, execution-aware security.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reassess the validity of established jailbreak-security findings in the era of more capable, agentic LLMs and stronger native safety, and to develop a unified, cross-layer defense framework.

Method: Develop unified taxonomies of attacks and defenses across the full agentic pipeline (user interaction, planning/reasoning, memory, tool use, inter-agent communication), introduce a security–utility–efficiency evaluation framework, and conduct a controlled empirical study within a common agentic framework.

Result: Three gaps identified: (1) strong native alignment does not imply robustness to adversarial jailbreaks; (2) defense effectiveness is highly model/attack/component-dependent and can incur costs in over-refusal, utility, and latency; (3) low final-response attack success can mask severe intermediate compromise in planning/memory/tool interactions. Findings motivate cross-layer, execution-aware security that protects agent state, component transitions, and external actions while preserving utility and efficiency.

Conclusion: Shift defense focus from solely response-centric jailbreak protection to cross-layer, execution-aware security that safeguards agent state and actions across the pipeline without sacrificing practicality.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are rapidly evolving from conversational assistants into agentic AI systems that reason, plan, invoke tools, maintain persistent memory, communicate with other agents, and execute multi-step tasks. At the same time, modern models exhibit substantially stronger native safety alignment than earlier generations on which many jailbreak attacks and defenses were originally studied. This shift raises a fundamental question: \textit{which established jailbreak-security findings remain valid in the era of modern LLMs and agentic AI?} We address this question through a Systematization of Knowledge (SoK) that reframes jailbreak security around the full agentic execution pipeline. We develop unified taxonomies of attacks and defenses spanning user interaction, planning and reasoning, memory, tool use, and inter-agent communication, and introduce a security--utility--efficiency evaluation framework that separates native harmful-prompt safety, adversarial jailbreak robustness, and agent-level security outcomes. We further conduct a controlled empirical study of representative attacks and defenses within a common agentic framework. Our results reveal three important gaps. First, strong native alignment does not imply robustness to adversarial jailbreaks. Second, defense effectiveness is highly model-, attack-, and component-dependent and can come at substantial cost in over-refusal, utility, and latency. Third, low final-response attack success can mask severe intermediate compromise: planning, memory, and tool interactions may remain unsafe even when the final response is successfully filtered. These findings motivate a shift from response-centric jailbreak defense toward cross-layer, execution-aware security that protects agent state, component transitions, and external actions while preserving practical utility and efficiency.

</details>


### [204] [Hierarchical Belief Modeling for Zero-Shot Opponent Adaptation in Partially Observable Multi-Agent Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12422)
*Kowei Shih,Lu Cheng,Zeyu Wang,Yeyun Xu,Kejian Tong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A hierarchical, memory-enabled agent (HORIZON) for Lux AI Season 3 that uses symmetry-aware perception, dual memory belief tracking, graph attention, information-seeking exploration, and opponent-conditioned policies to separate short-horizon control from meta-reasoning, achieving strong adaptive performance in partial observability and randomized dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address partial observability, dynamic episode-level dynamics, and a best-of-five match structure that require both tactical execution and rapid adaptation; enable explicit inference of hidden game parameters and opponent style.

Method: HORIZON integrates symmetry-aware spatial perception, dual memory belief tracking, relic-centric graph attention, information gain-driven exploration, and opponent-conditioned policy mixtures. It separates short-horizon control from cross-match meta-reasoning, and uses auxiliary belief and world-model objectives for stable learning. Trained with PPO in a large-scale JAX simulator to infer hidden game parameters and opponent styles.

Result: Experiments show consistent gains in match win rate, episode win rate, adaptation gain, and league rating over strong recurrent and feed-forward baselines.

Conclusion: HORIZON demonstrates that combining hierarchical control with belief/state inference, graph-based attention, information gain exploration, and opponent-conditioned policies yields robust, adaptable performance under partial observability and dynamic match settings; the approach benefits from explicit meta-reasoning and auxiliary learning signals.

Abstract: Lux AI Season 3 requires agents to act under partial observability, randomized episode level dynamics, and a best of five match structure that rewards both tactical execution and fast adaptation. We present HORIZON, a hierarchical agent that combines symmetry aware spatial perception, dual memory belief tracking, relic centric graph attention, information gain driven exploration, and an opponent conditioned policy mixture. HORIZON separates short horizon control from cross match meta reasoning, while auxiliary belief and world model objectives stabilize learning. Trained with PPO in a large scale JAX simulator, the resulting agent explicitly infers hidden game parameters and opponent style. Experiments show consistent gains in match win rate, episode win rate, adaptation gain, and league rating over strong recurrent and feed forward baselines.

</details>


### [205] [LifeFuse-Mem: Lifecycle-Aware State Fusion Against Temporary Overwriting for Long-Term Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12436)
*Hanyu Zhao,Yuqian Feng,Zhenyu Song,Yuanchao Cheng,Yance Jiao,Tengfei Pan,Li Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Lifecycle-aware memory framework LifeFuse-Mem that separates durable vs transient information to prevent overwrite and behavioral drift in long-running LLM agents; achieves improved retention on a controlled anti-overwrite benchmark and remains competitive on two public long-memory benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-running LLM agents require memory that maintains coherent internal states across interactions. Mismatches between information that should persist vs. be context-limited can cause temporary information to overwrite durable knowledge, leading to drift.

Method: A lifecycle-labeled memory setting with dedicated memory components and lifecycle-aware updates, plus phase-aware (context vs. persistent) readout. The approach separates information by temporal commitment and evolves transient knowledge locally without converting it to durable state.

Result: On the anti-overwrite benchmark, LifeFuse-Mem improves acquisition-controlled retention and reduces temporary overwrite; on two public long-memory benchmarks, it remains broadly competitive.

Conclusion: Explicit lifecycle signals help diagnose and mitigate overwrite in compact online memory for long-running agents.

Abstract: Long-running LLM agents require memory mechanisms that maintain coherent internal states across interactions. We study a lifecycle-labeled memory setting in which write episodes provide lifecycle metadata during training, and phase-aware readout is used during evaluation. This setting reflects the need to distinguish information that should remain influential across future interactions from information that should affect only the current context. A mismatch between these lifecycles can cause temporary information to overwrite durable knowledge, leading to behavioral drift in persistent agents. Within this setting, we introduce \textbf{LifeFuse-Mem}, a lifecycle-aware neural memory framework that separates information according to its temporal commitment. LifeFuse-Mem uses dedicated memory components and lifecycle-aware updates to allow stable and transient knowledge to evolve locally without converting temporary context into durable state. On the controlled anti-overwrite benchmark, LifeFuse-Mem improves acquisition-controlled retention and reduces temporary overwrite; on two public long-memory benchmarks, it remains broadly competitive. These results suggest that explicit lifecycle signals can help diagnose and mitigate overwrite in compact online memory.

</details>


### [206] [Do LLMs Trust the Accuser or the Accusation? Measuring Belief Shifts in Werewolf](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12446)
*Yu-Yu Yang,Ti-Rong Wu,Hung Guei,Hsing-Yu Chen,I-Chen Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A belief-shift benchmark in Werewolf to study how LLMs update beliefs about players after messages that accuse or defend, revealing belief dynamics beyond final outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate communication and belief-updating skills in LLMs within social-deduction games, addressing the limitation of relying on final outcomes for evaluation.

Method: LLM-played Werewolf games with 1,224 annotated messages (suspicion and accusation). A village-side observer model tracks belief changes after each message. Evaluated 40 open-weight LLM configurations (up to 120B params).

Result: Larger models better distinguish wolves from villagers using game history, but accusations strongly affect beliefs. Accusers who are trusted reduce suspicion of the accuser and increase suspicion of the target; accuser trust matters even if the accuser is wolf-aligned. Larger models resist accusations from previously distrusted accusers. Open-weight models up to 120B still struggle to integrate accusation content with source trust.

Conclusion: The benchmark reveals persistent challenges for open-weight LLMs to fuse content and source credibility in strategic communication; the authors provide a benchmark and code for future research.

Abstract: Social-deduction games such as Werewolf are increasingly used to evaluate LLM agents, but existing evaluations often rely on final game outcomes. We propose a belief-shift evaluation benchmark in Werewolf for analyzing communication skills through belief updating. Using LLM-played games, we annotate suspicion and accusation messages and measure how an observing village-side model's beliefs change after each message. We evaluate 40 open-weight LLM configurations on 1,224 annotated messages. Our results show that larger models better distinguish true wolves from villagers based on game history, but accusations still strongly influence their beliefs. Models become more suspicious of the accused target and less suspicious of the accuser, especially when the accuser is trusted, even if the accuser is wolf-aligned. Larger models better resist accusations from accusers they already distrust. Overall, our findings suggest that current open-weight LLMs up to 120B parameters still struggle to integrate accusation content with source trust in strategic communication. Our benchmark and code are available at https://rlg.iis.sinica.edu.tw/papers/werewolf-accusation-benchmark.

</details>


### [207] [EvoRS: On-Policy Self-Evolution of Reward Systems for Open-Ended Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12459)
*Weiyuan Li,Aili Chen,Xintao Wang,Yikai Zhang,Qingqing Dong,Jinghan Xu,Hongru Hou,Wenxuan Zhao,Chengkun Lang,Jun Gao,Yuanli Guo,Hongcheng Guo,Yanghua Xiao,Deqing Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoRS evolves the reward system during training using an executable Reward-DAG to maintain reliability in open-ended RL, reducing reward hacking and preserving informativeness, outperforming the policy across tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-ended RL with rubric-based rewards suffers from reward hacking and drifting discriminability; a fixed reward signal can become unreliable as the policy improves.

Method: Introduce EvoRS: a self-evolving reward framework where an agentic designer updates an executable Reward-DAG from on-policy rollouts and reward traces to keep the reward signal reliable during training.

Result: EvoRS achieves best quality under all three judges, outperforming the policy by 2.107 and 4.767 points, while reducing reward hacking and coverage failures and preserving reward informativeness.

Conclusion: Fixed reward systems cannot remain reliable in open-ended tasks; reward systems must evolve during training. EvoRS demonstrates reliability and performance gains in writing and roleplay tasks, with ablations showing the necessity of evolution.

Abstract: Open-ended reinforcement learning often relies on rubric-based rewards for tasks without directly verifiable answers. Yet the policy and reward system form a dynamic feedback loop: as the policy optimizes the current reward, an initially useful reward system may become unreliable due to reward hacking or reduced response discriminability. The reward system should therefore evolve rather than remain fixed during training. Existing dynamic-rubric methods adapt evaluation criteria, but reward failures can also arise from scoring mechanisms or signal composition. We introduce EvoRS, a self-evolving RL framework that evolves the reward system from on-policy experience, representing it as an executable Reward-DAG. Specifically, an agentic designer updates this system from on-policy rollouts and reward traces to maintain train-time reliability. Across writing and roleplay, EvoRS achieves the best quality under all three judges, outperforming the policy by \(2.107\) and \(4.767\) points, respectively, while reducing reward hacking and coverage failures and preserving reward informativeness. Ablations confirm that a comprehensive fixed reward system cannot remain reliable in open-ended tasks and must evolve throughout training.

</details>


### [208] [Beyond Vector Similarity: Hierarchical Context-Aware Graph RAG vs Standard RAG in Enterprise Code Migration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12464)
*Nilesh Jaiswal,Aniket Agrawal,Arjit Shukla,Divya Malhotra,Saurabh Garg,Suchit Puri,Suddhasatwa Bhaumik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Graph RAG with Hierarchical Context-Resident Graph (HCRG) substantially improves topology-aware code translation over Standard RAG by preserving inheritance and architectural relationships, at the cost of some readability metrics; demonstrated with significant reductions in API hallucinations and improved dependency handling, but lower cyclomatic complexity consistency and docstring preservation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RAG retrieves isolated code chunks, losing topology and inheritance chains, leading to high compilation failures during automated code translation. A topology-aware approach (HCRG) aims to preserve architectural relationships to improve translation quality for enterprise modernization.

Method: 1) Extract ASTs using tree-sitter. 2) Map architectural edges into a Google Cloud Spanner Property Graph. 3) Serialize the graph into a Gemini Context Cache. 4) Perform topological, parent-first code translation. 5) Evaluate using a custom 7-metric Software Engineering framework instead of traditional text overlap metrics like CodeBLEU. 6) Compare Graph RAG against Standard RAG across metrics including API hallucination, Dependency Resolution Quality, Parent-Child Consistency, Cyclomatic Complexity Consistency, and Docstring Preservation.

Result: Graph RAG substantially mitigates dependency loss: API hallucination rate 56.4% → 16.2%, Dependency Resolution Quality 34.8% → 65.9%, Parent-Child Consistency 26.7% → 45.5%. Traditional CodeBLEU (91% for both) masked these structural failures. However, dense global context leads to defensive over-engineering by the LLM, reducing Cyclomatic Complexity Consistency 71.6% → 46.7% and modestly degrading Docstring Preservation 67.0% → 61.0%. Overall, Graph RAG offers a more architecturally sound path for enterprise codebase modernization with some trade-offs.

Conclusion: Graph RAG provides a viable, architecture-preserving approach for automated code modernization by embedding topology into a graph-based cache and prioritizing parent-first translation. While it increases certain code-complexity management challenges and reduces some natural-language documentation retention, it substantially reduces hallucinations and improves dependency correctness, making it a preferable path for enterprise migrations.

Abstract: As enterprises modernize legacy monolithic systems to microservices, Large Language Models (LLMs) are heavily utilized for automated code translation. However, traditional vector-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (Standard RAG) struggles to capture topological relationships. It fetches isolated chunks that sever inheritance chains, leading to high compilation failure rates. This paper introduces a Hierarchical Context-Resident Graph (HCRG) methodology to resolve these limitations. Our pipeline uses tree-sitter for Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) extraction, maps architectural edges into a Google Cloud Spanner Property Graph, and serializes this structure into a Gemini Context Cache for topological, parent-first code translation. We shift evaluation from naive text-overlap to a custom 7-metric Software Engineering framework. Traditional metrics like CodeBLEU (which scored 91% for both methods) effectively masked Standard RAG's structural failures behind syntactically plausible but broken code. Empirically, Graph RAG decisively mitigates dependency loss: API hallucination rates dropped from 56.4% to 16.2%, Dependency Resolution Quality improved from 34.8% to 65.9%, and Parent-Child Consistency rose from 26.7% to 45.5%. However, Graph RAG introduces specific trade-offs. The dense global context causes defensive over-engineering by the LLM, reducing Cyclomatic Complexity Consistency from 71.6% to 46.7%, and slightly degrades Docstring Preservation (67.0% to 61.0%). Ultimately, while trading code complexity for reduced hallucinations, Graph RAG provides a substantially more viable, architecturally sound path for automated enterprise codebase modernization.

</details>


### [209] [TripPattern: A Pattern-based Text Watermarking Method for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12472)
*Sangjun Moon,Dasom Choi,Jingun Kwon,Hidetaka Kamigaito,Taro Watanabe,Manabu Okumura*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TripPattern proposes a three-way partition watermarking scheme for LLMs: neutral, pattern A, pattern B; alternates between A and B to embed detectable patterns; neutral tokens preserve naturalness; detection via pattern-based tests with interpretable p-values; shows maintained text quality and robust detectability across four multilingual datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcomes quality/naturalness loss in traditional two-token green/red watermarking by avoiding forced token selection; provides interpretable, robust detection.

Method: Partition vocabulary into neutral, pattern-A, pattern-B. During generation, alternate token choice between A and B to embed pattern, with neutral tokens chosen independently. Detection via pattern-based statistical tests assessing adjacent alternation between pattern groups, yielding p-values. Theoretical analysis and experiments on four multilingual datasets.

Result: Maintains LLM generation quality while achieving robust watermark detectability across multilingual data.

Conclusion: TripPattern offers a flexible, interpretable watermarking framework that preserves naturalness and provides effective detection; generalizable across languages.

Abstract: Text watermarking techniques have gained significant attention for identifying machine-generated text and mitigating risks from large language models (LLMs). Existing methods typically divide an LLM's vocabulary into green and red tokens, but encouraging generation toward green tokens can reduce text quality and naturalness. To address this, we propose TripPattern, a watermarking framework that formulates text watermarking as a pattern-based matching task using three vocabulary partitions. TripPattern divides the vocabulary into one neutral group and two pattern groups. During generation, the model alternates token selection between the two pattern groups to embed detectable patterns, while neutral tokens are selected independently to improve flexibility and preserve naturalness. For detection, TripPattern uses pattern-based statistical tests that provide interpretable p-values by measuring how often adjacent tokens alternate between the pattern groups. Theoretical analysis and empirical evaluations on four multilingual datasets show that TripPattern maintains LLM generation quality while achieving robust watermark detectability.

</details>


### [210] [When Does AI Augment Work? A Workflow-Level Framework for Human-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12482)
*CIVIC-AI Collaboration,:,Jiaying Wu,Caleb Ziems,Raymond Chan,Nancy F. Chen,Corlyss Chua,Gerard Chung,Jungpil Hahn,Wee Sun Lee,Zhengyuan Liu,Jamie Ng,Desmond C. Ong,Jeryl Ong,Da Ren Soon,Tianqi Song,Zhi-Xuan Tan,Sixing Tao,Emily Yang,Yajing Yang,Stella Xin Yin,Min-Yen Kan,Diyi Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces a six-condition AI augmentation framework to evaluate durable value, human control, accountability, and long-term development, demonstrated via AI-mediated social surveys.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current metrics emphasize automation and public adoption, neglecting how AI augments entire workflows and human–agent collaboration.

Method: Define six conditions for AI augmentation, elaborate on each, and apply the framework in a case study of AI-mediated social surveys; discuss implications for organizations, researchers, and policymakers.

Result: A coherent framework for evaluating AI’s workplace value; a concrete case study; guidance for implementation and governance.

Conclusion: Framework provides a basis for organizations, researchers, and government leaders to assess and plan for the future of work using AI augmentation.

Abstract: We aim to characterise the value of artificial intelligence in the workplace. Current studies largely measure this value in terms of the current automation capabilities and public adoption of AI. However, such metrics ignore the greater impacts of human--agent collaboration in transforming the nature of work. To account for this, we must expand the scope of our analysis beyond atomised tasks of today, and instead focus on how AI can augment entire workflows of the future. To ground this analysis, we establish a precise definition of AI augmentation comprising six conditions, spanning durable net value, meaningful human control, accountability and recovery, and long-term human development through learning, career pathways, and job purpose. We elaborate on these conditions and apply the framework in a case study of AI-mediated social surveys. We conclude by outlining how organisations, researchers, and government leaders can use this framework to make sense of the future of work.

</details>


### [211] [Confidence-Gated Transductive Test Generation for Code Reranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12489)
*Sungjae Lee,Youngsik Yoon,Seockbean Song,Siwei Wang,Wei Chen,Jungseul Ok*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoTT uses a confidence-gated, adaptive test generation strategy: fast inductive generation with selective transductive generation when confidence is low, improving reliability while reducing cost for code reranking tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-quality test cases with reliable expected outputs are hard to obtain; efficient test-time computation allocation is desirable to evaluate LLM-generated programs.

Method: An adaptive two-stage approach: first run an efficient inductive test-generation; assess confidence for the output; if confidence is low, invoke transductive generation for that input. Operates with a single efficient LLM to balance speed and accuracy.

Result: On code reranking benchmarks, CoTT outperforms existing baselines across reported metrics and reduces cost versus applying transductive generation to every input.

Conclusion: Confidence-based allocation of test-time computation yields a favorable efficiency–effectiveness trade-off using a single efficient LLM for test-case generation.

Abstract: Test case synthesis is crucial for evaluating and ranking programs generated by large language models (LLMs). However, constructing high-quality test cases remains challenging because reliable expected outputs are often difficult to obtain. We propose Confidence-Gated Transductive Test Generation (CoTT), which first uses an efficient inductive procedure and invokes transductive generation only when inductive confidence is low. This adaptive design improves output reliability while allocating extra computation only when needed. On code reranking benchmarks, CoTT outperforms prior baselines across the reported metrics while reducing cost relative to applying transductive generation to every input. These results show that confidence-based allocation of test-time computation provides a favorable efficiency-effectiveness trade-off with a single efficient LLM.

</details>


### [212] [Information Specialization and Constrained Synthesis in Multi-Agent LLM Forecasting: A Prospective Live-Study of the 2026 FIFA World Cup](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12495)
*Julian Varghese,Lucas Bickmann,Sarah Sandmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: News-focused specialist outperformed the quantitative specialist; meta/critic stages didn't enhance performance; unstructured signals are valuable alongside structured statistics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether specialization and multi-agent synthesis yield distinct forecasts and utility improvements in live sports forecasting.

Method: Prospective live evaluation across 56 World Cup matches; fixed frontier model; two primary agents (quantitative stats vs news/injuries/tactics); independent critic; meta-agent synthesis; external benchmark: betting market.

Result: News specialist achieved highest mean probability-weighted Top-3 utility; matched betting market for Top-3 exact-score hits; two specialists agreed on at least two of the three scorelines in 50/56 matches; meta-agent never generated more than one scoreline outside the specialists' forecast set; critic/ meta- stages did not improve beyond strongest specialist.

Conclusion: Unstructured, rapidly changing information provides valuable forecasting signal; specialization matters; additional synthesis stages may not yield improvements; caution about diminishing returns from adding intermediate stages.

Abstract: Large language models are being organized into multi-agent systems with specialized roles, but whether such specialization produces distinct forecasts and whether subsequent synthesis improves utility remains unclear. In this study, we carried out a live, prospective evaluation over the final 56 matches of the information-dense 2026 FIFA World Cup, keeping a frontier foundation model constant while assigning two primary forecasting agents contrasting specialist roles: a quantitative specialist focusing on structured performance statistics and a news specialist focusing on current injuries, tactics and information from press conferences. Their forecasts were then reviewed by a separate critic before being combined by a meta-agent, resulting in a sequential four-agent model. Forecasts from the betting market served as an external benchmark. The news specialist obtained the highest mean probability-weighted Top-3 utility and matched the betting market in Top-3 exact-score hits. Nevertheless, the two specialist forecasters agreed on at least two of the three scorelines in 50 out of 56 matches, and the meta-agent never generated more than one scoreline outside the specialists' forecast set. These findings show that rapidly changing, unstructured information can provide a valuable forecasting signal alongside structured statistics, whereas adding critic and meta-agent stages does not necessarily create complementary information or improve on the strongest specialist.

</details>


### [213] [From Collaboration to Capability: Internalizing Routed LLM Experts into Compact Reasoners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12578)
*Frank Nie,Shuyao Wang,Ethan B. Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RIVET uses expert-augmented RL to internalize collaboration with verified trajectories, boosting performance and generalization, even after expert removal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: to improve controller generation by leveraging both its own decisions and experts' reasoning/code, and to maintain capability when experts are removed, via collaboration internalization.

Method: Introduce Rivet: expert-augmented reinforcement learning that applies a shared outcome signal to controller decisions and returned expert spans; verified trajectory internalization via format-aware supervised training; deployment with local Python execution; seven competition-mathematics benchmarks; Stage II tuning; ablations; no external LLM required.

Result: RIVET-1.7B achieves 28.25% and RIVET-4B 44.16% average accuracies; Stage II improves RIVET-4B accuracy after expert removal by 6.49 points; GPQA-Diamond indicates generalization to scientific reasoning; ablations show gains from trajectory supervision and format weighting.

Conclusion: Training on both content and structure of verified collaborations and ordinary trajectory supervision yields robust improvements and generalization, supporting collaboration internalization as an effective paradigm.

Abstract: A compact controller can coordinate stronger experts by selecting whom to consult, formulating requests, and integrating their responses. We study whether learning from both the controller's decisions and the experts' reasoning and code improves its generation after expert removal. We introduce \textsc{Rivet} for \emph{collaboration internalization}: expert-augmented reinforcement learning applies a shared outcome signal to controller decisions and returned expert spans, and verified trajectory internalization consolidates complete successful interactions through format-aware supervised training. The deployed controller generates reasoning, code, and interaction structure with local Python execution and no external LLM. Across seven competition-mathematics benchmarks, RIVET-1.7B and RIVET-4B achieve average accuracies of $28.25\%$ and $44.16\%$; Stage~II improves RIVET-4B's accuracy after expert removal by $6.49$ points, and GPQA-Diamond results provide evidence of generalization to scientific reasoning. Ablations show gains from ordinary trajectory supervision and additional format weighting, supporting the effectiveness of training on the content and structure of verified collaborations.

</details>


### [214] [Reproducing and Evaluating the Generalizability of Subliminal Learning in Open-Weight Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12586)
*Daan van der Weijden,Nathan Brack,Selene Baez Santamaria*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Reproduces subliminal learning via distillation and tests its generality, extending to new traits (actors, politicians), a new task (chess move generation), and an additional open-weight model; includes ablation on answer-space size. Findings suggest the phenomenon is not universally robust across traits and tasks, and model-dependent in strength.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate the reproducibility and generalizability of subliminal learning across broader trait categories, tasks, and model families, given the original study's reliance on GPT-4.x fine-tuned models.

Method: Replicate the original experiments using open-weight HuggingFace checkpoints, extend along three axes (new preferences: actors and politicians; new task: chess move generation; open-weight model Ministral8B), and perform a controlled ablation on the numbers task’s answer-space (1-, 2-, 3-digit sequences). Emphasize open models due to GPT-4.x fine-tuning unavailability.

Result: The reproduction corroborates the original claims on average but shows that extensions are not universal: transmission strength varies across traits and tasks, and one model exhibits almost no effect, indicating substantial model- and task-dependent variability.

Conclusion: Subliminal learning via distillation appears to be contingent on specific trait-task-model combinations rather than a universal phenomenon; generalization claims should be tempered, and further work is needed to identify governing factors and boundary conditions.

Abstract: In this reproduction paper we investigate subliminal learning, a consequence of distillation where teacher models transmit behavioral preference traits through semantically unrelated data. The original paper explores two types of traits (animal preferences and misalignment), three data modalities (number sequences, code, and chain of thought), and several model families. We reproduce their experiments and extend the setup along three axes: new preference categories (actors and politicians), a new task (chess move generation), and an additional open-weight model (Ministral8B). We also run a controlled ablation on the numbers task's answer-space size (1-, 2-, and 3-digit sequences). We focus on open-weight models with accessible checkpoints on HuggingFace, since the original paper's GPT-4.x fine-tuning is no longer available. Our reproduction supports the original paper's claims, but our extensions show they are not universal as transmission strength varies across traits and tasks, and one model shows almost no effect at all.

</details>


### [215] [Beyond Generation and Accuracy: Diagnosing and Enhancing Visual Chain-of-Thought for Geometry Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12606)
*Zhitong Dong,Jicai Pan,Yingguo Gao,Jingting Ding,Hao Chen,Jinjie Gu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Presents GeoVAD-Bench, a five-dimension trajectory diagnostic for visual reasoning in geometry, with No-Aux/Auto-Aux/GT-Aux controls; reveals autonomous VCoT struggles due to compounding errors; introduces GeoWeave-8B achieving +25.3% geometry accuracy and +30.4% process average.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current visual chain-of-thought (VCoT) evaluations focus on final answers or visual generation quality, ignoring whether intermediate visuals are geometrically valid, effectively used, or causally responsible for success. A fine-grained diagnostic is needed to isolate perception, auxiliary generation, utilization, deductive reasoning, and final correctness, and to quantify the autonomy gap.

Method: Define a five-dimensional trajectory diagnosis (perception, auxiliary quality, utilization, deductive reasoning, final correctness) and controlled intervention settings (No-Aux, Auto-Aux, GT-Aux) to isolate error modes and causal effects of visual aids. Build GeoVAD-Bench data pipeline with geometric perception, diagram editing, and interleaved visual-textual reasoning. Develop a progressive supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and multimodal reinforcement learning (RL) framework. Train a large model, GeoWeave-8B, and compare to a base model.

Result: Autonomous visual-aid generation is hampered by compounding errors across perception, faithful diagram manipulation, visual-state grounding, and deductive reasoning, despite high-quality aids offering theoretical gains. GeoWeave-8B achieves +25.3% final geometric accuracy and +30.4% gains in process-average across four diagnostic dimensions compared to the base model.

Conclusion: A diagnostic, intervention-based framework clarifies how and when visual aids aid geometric reasoning, highlighting the autonomy gap and key bottlenecks. The proposed data construction pipeline and training regimen yield substantial performance gains, suggesting future work on improving perception, state grounding, and reasoning fidelity for robust VCoT in geometry.

Abstract: While multimodal reasoning has advanced rapidly, solving complex geometry problems critically hinges on active visual assistance, such as constructing auxiliary lines, spurring the rise of Visual Chain-of-Thought (VCoT). However, existing evaluations typically assess visual generation quality and final answer accuracy in isolation, failing to examine whether intermediate visual aids are geometrically valid, effectively utilized in subsequent reasoning, or causally responsible for task success. To bridge this gap, we introduce GeoVAD-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark that pairs a fine-grained five-dimensional trajectory diagnosis covering perception, auxiliary quality, utilization, deductive reasoning, and final correctness with controlled No-Aux, Auto-Aux, and GT-Aux intervention settings to systematically isolate intermediate error modes, the causal gains of visual aids, and the resulting autonomy gap. Our findings reveal that while high-quality auxiliary aids offer substantial theoretical gains for geometric problem solving, autonomous generation is frequently hampered by compounding errors across geometric perception, faithful visual manipulation, visual-state grounding, and deductive reasoning. Guided by these diagnostic insights, we establish a specialized data construction pipeline encompassing geometric perception, diagram editing, and interleaved visual-textual reasoning trajectories, and develop a progressive SFT and multimodal RL training framework. The resulting model, GeoWeave-8B, outperforms the base model by +25.3% in final geometric accuracy and achieves a +30.4% gain in process average across the four intermediate diagnostic dimensions.

</details>


### [216] [SteerDuplex: Steerable Duplex Speech Dialogue Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12623)
*Utkarsh Tyagi,Ramaneswaran Selvakumar,Advait Gosai,Sonal Kumar,Nikhil Barhate,Isabell Sagar,Steven Li,Miheer Bavare,Daniel Quigley,Fabiola Tapia Carrillo,Jose M Patron E,Diego Macías Gutiérrez,Paul Song,Ramani Duraiswami,Dinesh Manocha,Yunzhong He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes SteerDuplex, a Moshi-based full-duplex speech model with steerability via two-stage RL; introduces SteerBench as a spoken steerability benchmark; shows substantial gains in audio steering and interruption handling, but also reveals reward hacking phenomena.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full-duplex dialogue models currently lack reliable steerability along attributes like tone, persona, speed, and voice style; addressing this gap enables user-guided conversational behavior in real time.

Method: Fine-tune a Moshi-based full-duplex model on natural conversations and synthetic dialogues; apply two-stage reinforcement learning with hybrid rewards (verifiable interaction checks + judge-based semantic feedback) to improve timing and continuity; develop SteerBench (390 prompts, 1,067 rubrics) for evaluation; test on Audio MultiChallenge.

Result: Supervised training improves audio-steering average pass rate by 44.5 percentage points; Audio MultiChallenge pass rate up by 7 points; RL raises source-clean interruption response from 72.5% to 82.5% and reduces synthetic pause barge-in from 26.5% to 9%; steering and aggregate task scores remain comparable or higher; reward analysis shows reward hacking via incomplete responses.

Conclusion: SteerDuplex and SteerBench enable systematic study of spoken steerability; reward analysis underscores that timing gains must be evaluated alongside response completeness to avoid reward gaming.

Abstract: Full-duplex spoken dialogue models support low-latency turn taking, interruption handling, and backchanneling, yet a key capability remains underexplored: steerability, the ability to reliably shift conversational behavior along attributes such as tone, persona, speaking rate, and voice style in response to user instructions. We introduce a taxonomy of text- and audio-based steerability that identifies substantial gaps in current full-duplex models. To address this gap, we introduce SteerDuplex, a Moshi-based full-duplex speech model fine-tuned on natural conversations and synthetic dialogues targeting instruction following, vocal delivery, reasoning, and duplex interaction. We further apply two-stage reinforcement learning (RL) with hybrid rewards, combining verifiable interaction checks and judge-based semantic feedback to improve timing and response continuity. To evaluate full-duplex spoken steerability, we introduce SteerBench, a benchmark with 390 spoken prompts and 1,067 human-authored binary audio and text rubrics spanning tone, persona, style/accent, and speed/length. On SteerBench, supervised training improves audio-steering average pass rate by 44.5 percentage points over the strongest evaluated open baseline. On Audio MultiChallenge, task average pass rate improves by 7 points over its strongest evaluated open baseline. RL further raises source-clean interruption response from 72.5% to 82.5% and reduces synthetic pause barge-in from 26.5% to 9%. Steering and aggregate task scores remain comparable or higher, while reward probes reveal reward hacking through incomplete responses. Our model and benchmark support systematic research on spoken steerability, with reward analysis showing why timing gains must be evaluated alongside response completeness.

</details>


### [217] [Generative AI Use Cases In Real Estate Marketing: Adoption and Constraints in Germany](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12684)
*Victor Kolominsky-Rabas,Leopold Müller,Felicia Perpina,Niklas Kühl*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GenAI is being adopted in German real estate marketing, predominantly for drafting exposé texts; human agents remain in the loop, auditing and deciding. Adoption is limited by data/integration and compliance rather than model capability. The paper provides a grounded map of use cases and practical implications for adoption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the empirical gap on how GenAI is actually used in daily real estate practice and identify practical barriers and opportunities for adoption.

Method: Qualitative study based on eleven semi-structured interviews with real estate agents in Germany; thematic analysis of how GenAI is used and integrated into workflows.

Result: GenAI usage spans multiple marketing activities, with marketing communication being the most prominent. Writing exposé texts is the most established use. GenAI drafts/structures/retrieves content, while agents curate, verify, and decide. Constraints arise mainly from integration with listings/documents, data availability, and compliance in sensitive tasks. The study offers a grounded map of existing and potential use cases and tentative adoption implications.

Conclusion: GenAI has tangible potential in real estate marketing, but practical adoption hinges on data integration, governance, and compliance. Human-in-the-loop remains essential, and the work provides a pragmatic roadmap of current and prospective use cases with implications for stakeholders.

Abstract: Generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) is changing how work is organized and performed. Real estate marketing is a prime example of this, yet evidence of GenAI in real estate agents' day-to-day practice remains scarce. In this work, we report on our insights from a German-based empirical study with eleven semi-structured interviews. GenAI is already utilized across different activities, with marketing communication being the most prominent. Concrete use cases are emergent and unevenly adopted, with writing exposé texts being the only widely established one. Interaction is predominantly human-in-the-loop: GenAI drafts, structures, and retrieves, while real estate agents curate, verify, and decide. Constraints stem less from model capability than from integration with listings and documents, data availability, and compliance in sensitive tasks. The study contributes a grounded map of existing and potential use cases and identifies tentative practical implications for adoption.

</details>


### [218] [Residual Vector-based Reconstruction as Long-Context Recall Regardless of Context Window Size](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12686)
*MyungHoon Ryu,XinYu Piao,Jong-Kook Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: No-training long-context recall method using FFN residual activations to reconstruct facts, enabling near-constant memory usage and single-fact QA on multi-million-token contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context recall is limited by memory that scales with input length; existing optimization and lossy prompt compression fail beyond fixed context windows; need memory-efficient, non-training solution.

Method: Store residual vectors in the LLM's feed-forward layers that capture facts from the source document; reconstruct query-relevant facts deterministically from these activations without referencing the original document; no weight updates; memory remains near-constant as context grows.

Result: Demonstrated ability to answer single-fact questions in contexts up to 2,000,000 tokens where prior methods fail; preserves high fidelity and reduces GPU memory usage.

Conclusion: Feasible, training-free approach to long-context recall via FFN residuals; enables robust long-context QA with constant memory growth, suggesting broader potential for scalable long-context reasoning.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) process long contexts, including long documents and lengthy conversations, but face token-level memory usage that increases proportionally to input length. Although model optimization and lossy prompt compression are widely used, these methods still fail to solve the long-context recall problem beyond pretrained and size-constrained context windows. This paper proposes a long-context recall method that maintains near-constant GPU memory usage as context length increases, without additional training. The main idea is to reconstruct facts using parameter activations in the LLM's feed-forward layers, which store residual vectors representing facts from the source document. Utilizing residual vectors allows the LLM to deterministically reconstruct query relevant facts without referencing the original document, preserving high fidelity and reducing memory usage without fine-tuning weights. Experimental results show that the proposed method enables answering single-fact questions in two-million-token story contexts where previous methods fail.

</details>


### [219] [I Am AdMan: A Pipeline for Automatic Generation of Personalized Advertising Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12694)
*Victor Kolominsky-Rabas,Leopold Müller,Claudius Budcke,Niklas Kühl*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AdMan is a multi-agent pipeline that converts customer data into personas, generates personalized ad images conditioned on product references, and uses an LLM-based judge for automated quality control. It was evaluated on four products with 100 real customer profiles, producing 1745 ads and tested under two model configurations. It yields photorealistic, personalized ads but results vary with product complexity and configuration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study addresses the gap where visual ad creatives are generic despite personalized targeting, and seeks to scale fully AI-generated personalized imagery by systematically translating detailed customer data into personalized visuals while ensuring quality control.

Method: A multi-agent pipeline (AdMan) that (1) transforms customer data into personas, (2) generates personalized ad images conditioned on product reference images, and (3) applies an LLM-based judge for automated quality control. Two model configurations were implemented; evaluation used four products, six celebrity personas for qualitative inspection, and 100 real customer profiles to produce 1745 advertisements. Evaluations combined qualitative expert focus groups with quantitative artifact-rate metrics.

Result: The pipeline can generate photorealistic and personalized advertisements. Performance varies substantially by product complexity and model configuration.

Conclusion: AdMan demonstrates the feasibility of fully automated AI-generated personalized advertising while highlighting current limitations, thus extending AI-based personalized advertising literature and indicating areas for improvement (e.g., handling product complexity, model choice, and quality control).

Abstract: Personalized marketing can increase customer engagement, satisfaction, and conversion. While existing personalization approaches have become effective at matching the right product to the right customer, the visual representation of advertisements remains generic and only weakly tailored to the individual. Prior research shows that generative artificial intelligence can improve the creation of personalized advertisements, particularly for text, and that image generation models can support scalable advertisement production. However, little research has examined how detailed customer information can be systematically translated into fully AI-generated, personalized advertising imagery at scale on a technical level. To address this gap, we propose AdMan, a multi-agent pipeline that transforms customer data into personas, generates personalized advertisement images conditioned on product reference images, and applies an LLM-based judge agent for automated quality control. We implement the pipeline with two different model configurations and evaluate it across four products, using six celebrity personas for qualitative inspection, and 100 real customer profiles, producing 1745 advertisements. The evaluation combines a qualitative expert focus group and a quantitative artifact-rate assessment. The results show that the pipeline can generate photorealistic and personalized advertisements. At the same time, performance varies substantially by product complexity and model configuration. Our findings extend the literature on AI-based personalized advertising by demonstrating the feasibility and current limitations of fully automated image generation for advertising.

</details>


### [220] [Enabling and Understanding Personalization in AI-Generated Advertising Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12697)
*Victor Kolominsky-Rabas,Leopold Müller,Claudius Budcke,Claas Christian Germelmann,Niklas Kühl*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI-generated personalized ad imagery shows optimal performance at moderate personalization; high personalization increases creepiness that offsets benefits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses a gap where dynamic creative optimization focuses on AI-driven text personalization or basic product image modifications; aims to generate imagery directly from customer data to personalize ads.

Method: Two-stage within-subject experiment (N=100) across four products and three personalization levels; participants rated attitude toward the ad, attitude toward the product, and purchase intention; analysis of effects and creepiness.

Result: Different personalization levels yielded differences; moderate personalization produced the most favorable evaluations; high personalization increased perceived personalization but also creepiness, which negatively affected all outcomes and dominated the total effect.

Conclusion: Moderate personalization in AI-generated imagery is advisable; high personalization risks backfiring due to creepiness; future work should optimize privacy-preserving data use and strategies to mitigate creepiness.

Abstract: Personalized marketing traditionally matches static products to customers, while dynamic creative optimization focuses mainly on AI-driven text personalization or basic product image modifications. We address this gap by developing and implementing an AI-based framework that generates personalized advertising imagery directly from customer data. We evaluate this framework in a two-stage within-subject study with N=100 participants across four products and three levels of personalization, varied by the amount and specificity of customer data used. Participants rated each image on attitude toward the advertisement, attitude toward the product, and purchase intention. Results show that participants perceive differences across personalization levels and evaluate AI-generated advertising imagery most positively at a moderate level of personalization. High personalization increases perceived personalization, which is positively associated with all three outcome measures, but also increases perceived creepiness, which is negatively associated with the outcomes and dominates the total effect.

</details>


### [221] [Implicit Personality Representations in Humans and LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12704)
*Yilin Geng,Omri Abend,Eduard Hovy,Lea Frermann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM internal trait geometry aligns with human trait structure; two key axes emerge (social warmth, intellectual competence); holds across trait-level and dialogue, enabling human-grounded evaluation of AI personality representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether a modern LLM encodes human-like, relational trait structure and whether its internal representations align with consensus human impressions of personality across traits.

Method: Construct a human implicit-personality matrix from crowd-sourced ratings of fictional characters across hundreds of traits; construct a matching matrix from model activations for the same traits; compare the relational structures via Mantel correlation; perform dimensionality reduction to identify dominant axes; project model activations onto these axes and compare with human ratings on held-out dialogue.

Result: Mantel correlation r = 0.77, indicating strong alignment of relational structures both trait-by-trait and in aggregate; two dominant axes correspond to social warmth and intellectual competence; projections of model activations yield personality profiles on held-out dialogue that agree with human ratings.

Conclusion: Provides a framework for human-grounded comparison between internal model trait geometry and the shared structure of human personality impressions, supporting the view that LLMs encode human-like trait structure that can be mapped onto established personality dimensions.

Abstract: A century of psychology has found that the trait words people use to describe one another vary, but the relational structure among those traits, which ones go together and which oppose, is strikingly consistent across raters and cultures. We test whether the LLM (Qwen 2.5-7B-Instruct) reproduces this structure in its internal trait representations. From millions of crowd-sourced personality ratings of fictional characters, we build a human implicit-personality matrix over hundreds of traits; from contrastive model activations, we build a matching matrix over the same traits. The two relational structures align strongly (Mantel r = 0.77), and the agreement holds trait by trait as well as in aggregate. Two dominant axes of the model's trait representations recover the social and intellectual dimensions long known to organize human personality impressions, social warmth and intellectual competence. On held-out dialogue, projecting model activations onto these directions yields personality profiles that agree with human ratings. This work establishes a framework that enables comprehensive, human-grounded comparison between internal model trait geometry and the shared structure of human personality impressions.

</details>


### [222] [When Rubrics Fail: Hallucinations Reveal Blind Spots in Medical AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12718)
*Griffin Farrow,Lily Sijia Li,Jack Johnson,Tingyan Wang,Philip Torr,William Bolton,Fabio J. Fehr*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Rubric-based medical LLM evaluation misses clinically relevant hallucinations; more precise rubrics and retrieval-based checks may be needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Determine whether rubric scores reflect clinically important errors and affect clinician trust in medical LLMs.

Method: Controlled study with MedHallu; develop a taxonomy of medical hallucination types; clinician-validated error-injection pipeline generating matched correct vs error-inflicted responses; evaluate across HealthBench, HealthBench Professional, and LiveMedBench.

Result: Rubrics often miss clinically relevant hallucinations; rubric scores remain unchanged in many cases; explicit fact-checking rubrics are most effective; additional or unexpected errors are poorly captured; a retrieval-based factuality check captures some rubric-blind errors.

Conclusion: Rubric scores alone are insufficient for establishing clinical reliability; combining rubric-based evaluation with retrieval-based factuality checks can improve detection of clinically relevant errors and preserve clinician trust.

Abstract: Hallucinations can undermine clinician trust in LLMs, making it important that evaluation methods capture clinically relevant errors. Rubric-based evaluation has become the leading approach for assessing LLMs in medicine, but it is unclear whether rubric scores reflect such errors. We first study this in a controlled setting using MedHallu, finding that more specific rubrics better distinguish correct from hallucinated responses. To test this systematically, we develop a taxonomy of medical hallucination types and a clinician-validated error-injection pipeline that creates matched correct and error-injected responses. Across HealthBench, HealthBench Professional, and LiveMedBench, our clinically relevant hallucinations are missed by rubrics, often leaving scores unchanged. We find that rubrics are most effective when explicitly checking facts, and are less effective for additional or unexpected errors they do not anticipate. A preliminary retrieval-based factuality check recovers some of the rubric-blind errors, suggesting a complementary approach. These findings reveal systematic blind spots in current medical evaluation of LLMs and suggest that rubric scores alone are insufficient to establish clinical reliability, potentially undermining clinician trust and confidence in clinical deployment.

</details>


### [223] [Skill Issue: Lessons from Optimizing Repository SKILLs for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12742)
*Mykhailo Kozyrev,Andrei Kozyrev,Anton Podkopaev*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GEPA mines harder real-world tasks from merged PRs to generate documentation that improves agent performance; SkillOpt yields negligible improvement on the same setup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: to evaluate the actual utility of automatically generated SKILL documentation for code-reading agents by testing on tasks derived from repository history rather than synthetic benchmarks.

Method: mine merged pull requests fixed at a single base commit; score a candidate document by whether the agent with the document performs better than without it; evaluate on three Kotlin repositories.

Result: GEPA yields an average +4.9 percentage points in the agent performance score; SkillOpt yields +0.1 percentage points; the GEPA gain is comparable to prior reports using the same optimizer; variance and dataset size prevent clear separation from run-to-run variance in a single repository.

Conclusion: With limited data, statistically distinguishing improvements is challenging; GEPA shows qualitative improvements in document readability and potential utility, but more tasks/data are needed for robust significance.

Abstract: Coding agents increasingly read repository knowledge from SKILLs --- plain \texttt{.md} files versioned alongside the code. Recent work synthesizes these files automatically, by optimizing the document against a benchmark. A bare repository comes with no benchmark, and the synthetic tasks prior work builds are small enough that a capable agent saturates them with no document at all. We mine harder tasks --- merged pull requests of the repository, reverted at a single frozen base commit; and score a candidate document by whether the same agent does better with it than without it. On three Kotlin repositories, the documents GEPA finds raise this score by $4.9$pp on average, and the ones SkillOpt finds leave it where it started, $0.1$pp above the seed. The GEPA gain matches what prior work reports with the same optimizer, and at the dataset size a single repository supplies it cannot be separated from the agent's run-to-run variance; settling that would take more tasks than one repository's history yields. The documents themselves read better than the score: a maintainer of one repository found in them knowledge one only gets by working in the project.

</details>


### [224] [What Drives Recovery in Agentic Text-to-Cypher? LAST-CQ: An LLM Agent Self-Refinement Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12746)
*Ioannis Prokopiou,Athanasios Aidinis,Panagiotis-Christos Kyrmpatsos,Pantelis Vikatos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Five-agent, training-free LAST-CQ study shows failure-detection and retry are the main drivers of gains in structured-query generation; feedback sophistication and sample count are less impactful, while parallel sampling can harm quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Disentangle which components of agentic pipelines for structured-query generation contribute to performance gains, by isolating correction, feedback, sampling strategies, and error handling using an instrumented testbed across queries and backbones.

Method: Five-agent LAST-CQ framework (execution-grounded T2Cypher), three counterfactuals over 2,471 live-database queries, six backbones across three vendor scale tiers. Comparisons include: presence vs absence of correction; schema-grounded LLM feedback vs raw database error strings; single-pass vs multi-pass; parallel sampling budgets. Metrics include execution-BLEU, exact-match, end-to-end, set-F1; calibration against blind human labels.

Result: - Removing correction yields improvements: 3.1% aggregate execution-BLEU vs single-pass; 12.3% vs no-refinement; up to 80.7% for the weakest backbone. - Replacing feedback with raw error strings costs little (naive exact match 20.9% vs 19.9%; end-to-end <0.2%; set-F1 within ±0.075). - Parallel sampling with the same call budget degrades quality by 10–11%. - The key gain comes from detecting failure and routing to a retry, not from feedback sophistication or number of samples. - LAST-CQ recovers 91.7% of queries that fail under single-pass; a first-pass success costs exactly one LLM call. - N-gram overlap on serialized results is not a dependable bound (over-scores against set equivalence on 65.9% and under-scores against judged semantics). - The LLM judge is optimistic by 9 points relative to blind human labels.

Conclusion: The principal design implication is to prioritize robust failure detection and retry policies over complex feedback mechanisms or heavy sampling. Allocation of queries to retries yields large gains and stabilizes performance, while increasing sampling or sophisticating feedback yields diminishing returns. LLM-based judgements show optimistic bias, and lexical overlap is an unreliable proxy for semantic equivalence. Future work should explore broader domains, latency-cost trade-offs, alternative error signals, and more rigorous judge calibration.

Abstract: Agentic pipelines for structured-query generation are rapidly expanding, but it is unclear which part of the loop produces the gain. We use LAST-CQ -- a five-agent, training-free, execution-grounded Text-to-Cypher framework -- as an instrumented testbed, running three counterfactuals over 2,471 live-database queries and six backbones spanning three vendor scale tiers. Removing correction is worth between 3.1% aggregate execution-BLEU against the single-pass system and 12.3% against a no-refinement counterfactual (up to 80.7% for the weakest backbone). Replacing schema-grounded, LLM-synthesised feedback with raw database error strings costs almost nothing (20.9% vs. 19.9% naive exact match; <0.2% end-to-end; equivalent within $\pm 0.075$ set-F1 by two one-sided tests). Spending the same call budget on parallel sampling degrades quality by 10-11%. What works is detecting failure and routing it to a retry, not the feedback sophistication or number of samples. LAST-CQ itself recovers 91.7% of queries that fail under single-pass generation, while a query that succeeds first time still costs exactly one LLM call. We also show that n-gram overlap on serialised results is not a bound in either direction: it over-scores against set equivalence on 65.9% of results while under-scoring against judged semantics. Finally, we calibrate our LLM judge against blind human labels and find it optimistic by 9 points.

</details>


### [225] [Assisted Spatial Cognition Through Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12747)
*H. Riaz,J. B. Fernandez,I. Mills,D. Hickey,F. Cleary,M. I. Ali*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes an on-device multimodal navigation framework for visually impaired and neuro-divergent users, integrating LLMs, VLMs, SLAM3R-based 3D reconstruction, and digital twins to deliver spatially cognizant scene understanding and precise object distances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap in existing navigation aids that rely on constrained route-based strategies and lack comprehensive 3D scene understanding, aiming to empower autonomy for over 43 million visually impaired and neuro-divergent individuals through richer spatial perception and reasoning.
