Method: An end-to-end pipeline that captures monocular video on a mobile device, uses SLAM3R to generate dense 3D point clouds in real time, applies a custom post-processing step for multi-view point cloud alignment without reference points, enhances SpatialLM to produce structured 3D representations (architectural elements and oriented bounding boxes), and runs a locally deployed LLM to interpret 3D contexts and output scene descriptions and accurate distance measurements.

Result: Evaluation across diverse video scenarios with variable perspectives and environments shows consistent accuracy in 3D scene interpretation and object localization, indicating strong potential for assistive navigation when fused with LLM/VLM and digital-twin approaches.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates the feasibility and promise of spatially cognitive navigation for visually impaired and neuro-divergent users by combining advanced visual perception, spatial reasoning, and on-device large-language-model processing.

Abstract: Multimodal AI, powered by Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs), is transforming assistive technologies by enabling simultaneous processing of visual and textual data. This advancement holds significant promise for over 43 million visually impaired and neuro-divergent individuals worldwide who face persistent challenges in navigating indoor and outdoor environments due to limited spatial awareness and insufficient environmental cues. Existing navigation aids often lack comprehensive 3D scene understanding, relying on constrained route-based strategies that hinder user autonomy. In this paper, we introduce a novel end-to-end framework that integrates LLMs, VLMs and digital twin technologies to deliver a spatially cognitive navigation support for visually impaired and neuro-divergent users. Our system captures video input via standard mobile phone cameras, and employs SLAM3R to generate dense 3D point clouds from monocular RGB sequences in real-time. Our custom post-processing algorithm ensures accurate point cloud alignment across multiple viewpoints without requiring predefined reference points. This enhances the capabilities of SpatialLM to produce structured 3D representations, including architectural elements and oriented object bounding boxes. The enriched spatial data is then processed by a locally deployed LLM, which interprets 3D contexts to generate detailed scene descriptions and precise distance measurements between users and surrounding objects. We evaluated our approach across diverse video scenarios featuring various perspectives, looped walking views and captured in multiple environments. The evaluation results demonstrate consistent accuracy in 3D scene interpretation and object localisation, underscoring the potential of our system as a transformative assistive navigation solution that combines advanced visual perception with spatial reasoning

</details>


### [226] [SCQ: Stabilizing Conservative Q-Learning with Sigmoid-Bounded Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12749)
*Xiefeng Wu,Shu Zhang,Zhaojie Chu,Mingyu Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCQ introduces a sigmoid-bounded, strictly positive entropy term for offline-to-online RL, replacing the standard log-entropy. It preserves conservative Q regularization and lower-bound calibration, yielding stable policy updates and competitive performance with strong real-robot transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard log-entropy term in entropy-regularized RL can become negative, destabilizing policy updates during offline-to-online learning. A positive, bounded alternative is needed to maintain exploration while stabilizing optimization.

Method: Replace the log-entropy term with a sigmoid-bounded, strictly positive formulation while retaining conservative Q regularization and return-based lower-bound calibration. Conduct ablations including direct clipping of negative log-probability contributions and gradient-matched positive-score controls to isolate the effect of positivity.

Result: SCQ matches or exceeds baseline performance on D4RL Minari benchmarks (single-demonstration and standard datasets) and on simulation and real-world visual tasks. It exhibits stronger training stability across state-based and visual benchmarks and transfers to four real-robot platforms (manipulation, wheeled, quadruped, humanoid). Ablation confirms that positivity, not a specific score shape, drives much of the improvement.

Conclusion: Positivity of the entropy-like term is a key driver of stable offline-to-online RL. The sigmoid-bounded conservative Q approach achieves stable policy optimization without sacrificing exploration and demonstrates robust transfer to diverse real-world robotics tasks.

Abstract: Offline-to-online reinforcement learning reduces interaction cost for real-world robot learning but suffers from persistent value estimation instability. Existing methods address this through pessimistic regularization, lower-bound calibration, and architectural normalization, but an overlooked source of instability lies in the entropy formulation: the standard log-entropy term can become negative, destabilizing policy updates. We introduce SCQ (Sigmoid-Bounded Conservative Q-Learning), which replaces this term with a sigmoid-bounded formulation that stays strictly positive. SCQ retains conservative Q regularization and return-based lower-bound calibration, stabilizing policy optimization without sacrificing exploration. We evaluate SCQ on D4RL (Minari) benchmarks under both single-demonstration and standard dataset settings, as well as on simulation and real-world visual tasks. SCQ matches or exceeds baseline performance while exhibiting more stable training dynamics across state-based and visual benchmarks, and transfers to four real-robot platforms including manipulation, wheeled, quadruped, and humanoid systems. A direct clipping intervention that removes negative log-probability contributions, together with gradient-matched positive-score controls, indicates that positivity rather than a particular score shape alone drives much of the improvement. Project website: https://scq-rl.github.io.

</details>


### [227] [Unified Agentic Video Editing Across Levels of Complexity and Creativity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12769)
*Surabhi S. Nath,Kim Ferres,Milan Petrović,Lion Schulz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Presents agentic tooling for automated video editing across scene previews, video summaries, and cinematic trailers, with evaluation and discussion of automation and agency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore how agentic systems can enable automated yet controllable video editing under varying editorial constraints.

Method: Develops an agentic editing toolkit and workflows for three tasks with differing goals; conducts evaluation of outputs and discusses implications.

Result: Outputs are evaluated; the paper discusses implications for automation and user agency.

Conclusion: Agentic tooling can support automated video editing across diverse editorial objectives while highlighting considerations about control, responsibility, and user agency.

Abstract: Editing is a core component of video production, requiring creative planning and decisions under multiple constraints. Here, we report methods for agentic tooling for automated video editing across three tasks varying in editorial goal, complexity and creativity, namely scene previews, video summaries and cinematic trailers. We evaluate the outputs and discuss implications for automation and agency.

</details>


### [228] [MPT: Missing Prototype Tracking via Barycentric Reconstruction in Vehicular Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12771)
*Hanju Jang,Gyeongmin Han,Sungmin Lee,Kichang Lee,Chunghan Lee,JeongGil Ko*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MPT is a cross-vehicle federated learning framework that preserves rare-class recognition by reconstructing its prototypes at every round from privacy-preserving class-level statistics. It combines barycentric decomposition to track drift with remaining-class prototypes, covariance-based residual drift prediction, and adaptive calibration to weigh rare-class samples by reliability, enabling recovery without raw data or per-sample features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In cross-vehicle FL, transient participation results in departure of vehicles that may hold most samples of a target class. This causes rare-class degradation in the shared model, and privacy constraints prevent centralized access to raw data or per-sample features, making recovery challenging.

Method: MPT reconstructs rare-class prototypes each round from privacy-preserving class-level statistics using a barycentric decomposition to track drift alongside remaining-class prototypes, a covariance-based residual drift predictor to estimate out-of-span drift, and an adaptive calibration mechanism to weight remaining rare-class samples by reliability. This approach maintains rare-class recognition without sharing raw data or per-sample features and without retraining.

Result: Evaluated on three vehicle classification tasks and four backbones, MPT outperforms calibration and drift-compensation baselines in rare-class F1, achieving 0.516 on the nuImages dataset with only 1% of rare-class samples remaining.

Conclusion: MPT enables maintenance of rare-class performance in cross-vehicle FL under privacy constraints by reconstructing class prototypes from class-level statistics and compensating drift via barycentric, residual, and adaptive calibration components; it achieves superior rare-class recognition without raw data or per-sample features and without retraining.

Abstract: Cross-vehicle federated learning enables vehicles to collaboratively improve perception models while keeping locally collected driving data private. However, vehicle participation is transient, and a vehicle may depart before training converges while permanently taking its local data. When this departing vehicle holds most samples of a target class, the class becomes rare in the remaining FL network, and its recognition can silently degrade as the shared backbone continues to evolve. Recovering the class is difficult since the few remaining samples provide a noisy prototype estimate, while FL privacy constraints prevent centralized access to raw data or per-sample features. This paper presents MPT, a cross-vehicle FL framework that maintains rare-class recognition by reconstructing its prototype at every round from privacy-preserving class-level statistics. MPT combines a barycentric decomposition that tracks drift shared with remaining-class prototypes, a covariance-based residual prediction that estimates out-of-span drift, and an adaptive calibration that weighs the remaining rare-class samples according to their reliability. We evaluate MPT on three vehicle classification tasks and four backbones against representative calibration and drift-compensation baselines. MPT outperforms all baselines in rare class F1, reaching 0.516 on the nuImages dataset with only 1\% of rare-class samples remaining, without raw data, per-sample features, or retraining.

</details>


### [229] [Interpreting the predictions of neural network classification based on a Taylor Coefficient Analysis (TCA)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12801)
*Markus Klute,Artur Monsch,Lars Sowa,Roger Wolf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Taylor Coefficient Analysis (TCA), a formal, Taylor-based framework to interpret NN predictions in event classification by analyzing coefficients with respect to input features; demonstrates intuition on simple tasks and a CERN-like LHC dataset, arguing second-order terms capture most relevant structure for typical LHC-scale problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide a rigorous, extensible introspection paradigm to understand how input features shape NN predictions in high-energy physics event classification; address the need for interpretability in complex collider analyses and connect model behavior to physically meaningful input properties.

Method: Define Taylor Coefficient Analysis (TCA) as the Taylor expansion of the NN output in the input feature space X; use two simple benchmark tasks to illustrate interpretable insight from the expansion; apply TCA to a complex CERN LHC-like classification problem to explore levels of introspection and practical considerations; assess whether second-order terms suffice for typical LHC-like tasks.

Result: TCA yields a structured view of which input properties influence predictions, enabling intuitive understanding of NN decisions; in the LHC-like application, the analysis indicates that second-order terms capture the most relevant information for tasks of the complexity considered, supporting the practicality of the method.

Conclusion: TCA offers a rigid taxonomy and practical paradigm for introspection of NN-based event classifiers. The paper discusses practical considerations for applying TCA to collider data and provides evidence that, for typical LHC-scale problems, second-order Taylor terms are often sufficient to capture the key influences on predictions.

Abstract: We introduce a rigid and comprehensive taxonomy and paradigm for characterizing the influence of the input feature space $X$ on the predictions $\hat{y}$ of a neural network (NN) used for event classification, based on a Taylor expansion of $\hat{y}$ in $X$. The complete process of introspection we refer to as Taylor Coefficient Analysis (TCA). Based on two simplistic example tasks, which can be easily understood and bencmarked, we illustrate the power of the TCA when it comes to revealing, what properties of $X$ have led to what value of $\hat{y}$, of a given NN model, building up intuition for the method. A more complex application is meant to represent $X$ of a typical classification task at a CERN LHC experiment. Based on this application, we play through the different levels of introspection that the TCA offers and discuss a number of practical aspects for a TCA application typical for the analysis of CERN LHC data. We conclude with a study to support the assumption that those properties of $X$ most relevant for tasks of the complexity typical for a CERN LHC experiment, are usually caught by a TCA up to the second order.

</details>


### [230] [K-Bench: A Benchmark for LLM Unlearning in Agentic Deployments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12808)
*Guangsheng Yu,Yanna Jiang,Qin Wang,Baihe Ma,Xu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: K-Bench benchmarks unlearning in deployed agentic LLMs by evaluating six information channels (CoT, tool calls, tool observations, elicited summary) in a ReAct-style agent. It shows that model-level forgetting (e.g., TOFU/MUSE certificates) does not transfer to deployed agents, with leaks occurring across channels when secrets reside in weights, prompts, or retrieval stores. Many published methods fail to remove secrets from weights; only input-corruption can achieve selective forgetting under some observers. The top methods vary by base model and a refusal-tuning approach can resist extraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivate evaluation of unlearning effectiveness in real-world, agentic deployments of LLMs, where forgetting must hold across multiple observable channels beyond the final answer.

Method: Introduce K-Bench, which examines six channels exposed by a ReAct agent (final answer, chain-of-thought, tool calls, tool observations, elicited summary, and internal channels). For each experiment, place a secret in exactly one source (weights, prompt, or retrieval store). Compute K-Score for each source to measure forgetting while keeping the agent usable. Compare against TOFU/MUSE baselines and evaluate twenty published methods, plus an input-corruption intervention and a refusal-tuning method.

Result: On structured retrieval, the secret remains verbatim in the tool-observation channel, leaving leakage unchanged. If the secret is in the prompt or retrieval store, TOFU/MUSE report no leakage, yet the deployed agent leaks 22–86% of queries. If the secret is in weights, none of the twenty methods remove it; only input-corruption achieves selective forgetting under the evaluated observer. Top-ranked method varies across base models; a refusal-tuning method resists extraction without verified knowledge removal.

Conclusion: Model-level forgetting certificates do not guarantee agent-level unlearning in deployed systems. K-Bench provides a realistic, channel-aware evaluation framework; current methods are insufficient for secrets in weights or when deployed as agents, highlighting the need for robust, source-targeted forgetting strategies and handling of refusal-based defenses.

Abstract: Unlearning benchmarks such as TOFU and MUSE certify forgetting by reading the model's final answer, where a model that refuses to answer already counts as having forgotten. We show that this model-level certificate does not transfer once the model is deployed as an agent. We introduce K-Bench, a benchmark that scores LLM unlearning under agentic deployment. K-Bench inspects all six channels a ReAct agent exposes, including its chain-of-thought (CoT), tool calls and tool observations, and elicited summary. A query counts as leaked if the secret appears in any of them. Each experiment places the secret in exactly one of the agent's three sources (the weights, the prompt, or the retrieval store). The K-Score is computed separately for each source and credits forgetting only when the agent remains usable. Clearing the answer channel does not make the secret unrecoverable. On structured retrieval, the secret stays verbatim in the tool-observation channel and the aggregate leak rate is unchanged. When the secret lives in the prompt or the retrieval store, TOFU and MUSE report no leakage, while the deployed agent still leaks it on 22--86\% of queries. When the secret is in the weights, none of the twenty evaluated published methods demonstrably removes it, and only an input-corruption intervention reaches selective forgetting under the evaluated observer. The top-ranked method changes across base models. A refusal-tuning method resists the evaluated extraction without verified knowledge removal.

</details>


### [231] [Scaling Clinical Judgment to Evaluate Medical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12822)
*Thomas A. Buckley,Zahir Kanjee,Peter G. Brodeur,Byron Crowe,Anthony M. Pettinato,Aashna P. Shah,Adrian D. Haimovich,Liam G. McCoy,Daniel Restrepo,Ethan Goh,Jonathan H. Chen,Laura Zwaan,Katherine E. Goodman,Daniel J. Morgan,Raja-Elie E. Abdulnour,Adam Rodman,Arjun K. Manrai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces PrecepTron, a physician-level evaluator fine-tuned with LoRA for open-ended medical reasoning; release of GRAND-ROUNDS, a large physician-annotated benchmark; demonstrates frontier LLMs as judges often disagree with physicians and with each other; enables scalable, reproducible medical reasoning evaluation; code/data released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable scalable, rigorous assessment of clinical reasoning in LLMs by moving beyond small, single-institution evaluator panels and toward reproducible, large-scale physician evaluation of open-ended responses.

Method: Fine-tune a 32B model using LoRA on a small set of physician examples to create PrecepTron, a physician-level evaluator. Release GRAND-ROUNDS with 9,217 scores from 11 physicians across seven studies. Compare frontier LLMs as judges to physicians, then fine-tune PrecepTron to achieve physician-level consistency. Use PrecepTron to reproduce five landmark clinical AI studies (JAMA, Science, Nature Medicine) without new human grading, and explore token-by-token diagnostic reasoning.

Result: PrecepTron yields physician-level, consistent scoring across tasks. Frontier LLMs often disagree with physicians and with each other, but PrecepTron aligns more closely with physician judgments. GRAND-ROUNDS comprises 9,217 scores from 11 physicians across seven studies, and the authors successfully reproduce major findings without new grading. The framework enables novel analyses of medical reasoning (e.g., token-by-token diagnostic evaluation).

Conclusion: PrecepTron plus GRAND-ROUNDS provide a scalable, reproducible foundation for studying LLM medical reasoning, with freely available code, data, and labels to advance research.

Abstract: Blinded physician evaluation has been considered by many to be the gold standard for assessing clinical reasoning in large language models (LLMs). This is difficult to scale; thus, prior studies typically rely on small physician panels, often from a single institution or specialty, which both limits the scientific questions investigated and makes it unclear whether findings would be reproduced with a different set of evaluators. To more rigorously and scalably study clinical reasoning in AI models, here we introduce PrecepTron, an LLM fine-tuned for physician-level evaluation of open-ended responses. PrecepTron was trained using low-rank adaptation (LoRA) of a 32-billion-parameter model on a small number of physician examples. We also release GRAND-ROUNDS, a new large-scale physician-annotated benchmark of 9,217 scores by 11 physicians across seven studies. We show that frontier LLMs in typical "LLM-as-a-judge" approaches often disagree with physicians and with each other, but fine-tuning PrecepTron on a small number of cases enables physician-level consistent scoring across tasks. We use PrecepTron to reproduce headline findings from five influential studies assessing LLMs for clinical care in JAMA, Science, and Nature Medicine without new human grading. Using PrecepTron, we then pose new questions about how LLMs reason in medicine that would have been infeasible with human grading alone, including measuring the diagnostic accuracy of frontier LLMs when clinical cases are provided piecemeal, even token by token. Together, PrecepTron and GRAND-ROUNDS provide a foundation for reproducible, large-scale study of how LLMs reason in medicine. All code, data, and labels are made freely available for researchers.

</details>


### [232] [MedRoundsQA: A Persona and Difficulty Aware Evaluation for Multi-Turn Medical Consultations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12851)
*Youssef Mohamed,Ahmed Heakl,Qinrong Cui,Junhong Liang,Rafiq Ali,Bdour Babillie,Nazira Dunbayeva,Lang Gao,Omar Hussein,Ahmed Nada,Ahmed Mohamed Magdy Mohamed,Jinghui Liu,Salman Khan,Imran Razzak,Yuxia Wang,Xiuying Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-turn diagnostic benchmark, MedRoundsQA, converts 1,387 board-exam cases across 17 specialties into 24-slot structured records instantiated as doctor–patient dialogues. It uses model-based uncertainty to grade difficulty and evaluates 15 LLM doctors, revealing large performance drops in multi-turn settings, diminishing returns with more turns, and significant equity effects from patient personas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond single-turn, multiple-choice benchmarks and reflect real clinical consultations where evidence is elicited interactively and patient communication varies, enabling study of turn-taking, difficulty, and equity effects.

Method: Convert board-exam cases into structured 24-slot records; instantiate controlled doctor–patient dual-agent dialogues with varying patient personas; use model-based uncertainty to label case difficulty; evaluate 15 LLM doctor agents on multi-turn dialogues.

Result: Transitioning from single-turn to multi-turn diagnostics causes large performance degradations (~13–39 points). More turns improve question relevance but yield diminishing diagnostic gains, plateauing after 6–12 turns. Persona differences shift accuracy by ~7–8 points (low vs high education).

Conclusion: Single-turn benchmarks underestimate difficulty and equity risks; multi-turn benchmarks with adjustable difficulty reveal behavior of LLM clinicians more faithfully and highlight the need to consider dialogue structure and patient diversity in evaluation.

Abstract: Medical benchmarks are dominated by single-turn, multiple-choice clinical cases that poorly reflect real consultations. Practically, clinicians elicit evidence interactively and patient communication varies widely. We introduce MedRoundsQA, a multi-turn diagnostic benchmark derived from 1,387 board-exam cases across 17 specialties. Each case is converted into a structured 24-slot clinical record, and then instantiated as controlled doctor-patient dual-agent dialogues under varying patient personas, with the underlying clinical content held fixed. We further classify cases by difficulty using model-based uncertainty to enable easy-to-hard analysis. Evaluations of fifteen LLM doctor agents show that (i) moving from a single-turn diagnosis on the standardized records to multi-turn consultations causes large degradations of roughly 13-39 points; (ii) more turns reliably improves question relevance, but diagnostic accuracy exhibits diminishing returns and typically plateaus after 6-12 turns; and (iii) patient persona differences can shift diagnosis accuracy by about 7-8 points (lowest to highest education), highlighting equity risks that single-turn benchmarks miss.

</details>


### [233] [Tracing and Coordinating Cross-Layer Influence for Multimodal Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12897)
*Pengyang Zhou,Xiaobin Tu,Zhengxi Liu,Rongkun Xue,Haochen Li,Miancan Liu,Ziyuan Chen,Yinggui Wang,Jinkui Ren,Xiantao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TAC-Merge traces cross-layer multimodal influence to guide merging of expert updates, using a two-module approach (MIM and CMC) to jointly fuse unimodal and cross-modal updates for better consolidation and generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Updating one expert in a multimodal model propagates changes through depth and across modalities, creating coupled effects that complicate merging. There is a need to characterize cross-layer multimodal influence and coordinate updates accordingly.

Method: TAC-Merge comprises two components: Modal multimodal influence mapping (MIM) builds graphs of update effects across layers and modalities, using Ricci curvature and expert predictions to define a shared fusion objective. Coupled merge control (CMC) models interactions among coefficient adjustments and optimizes regional weights to synthesize a single shared model.

Result: Empirical evaluation across diverse multimodal tasks shows TAC-Merge effectively consolidates complementary expert capabilities and generalizes to unseen tasks.

Conclusion: The framework provides a principled way to trace and coordinate cross-layer, cross-modal influence during merging, improving performance and generalization for multimodal model fusion.

Abstract: Multimodal model merging aims to consolidate task experts into a single model that retains their complementary capabilities. Most unimodal model merging methods combine expert updates within individual layers, and multimodal approaches largely follow this design. However, an expert update changes the representations passed to subsequent layers, allowing its influence to propagate across depth and affect how visual and textual information interact. When visual and language updates are combined, later updates act on inputs already modified by earlier ones, coupling their effects. This poses two challenges: (1) how to characterize the multimodal influence of individual expert updates across depth, and (2) how to jointly combine expert updates based on their multimodal influence. To address these challenges, we propose TAC-Merge for tracing and coordinating cross-layer influence in multimodal model merging. It contains two modules, i.e., multimodal influence mapping (MIM) and coupled merge control (CMC). MIM constructs graphs of update effects and uses Ricci curvature together with expert predictions to define a shared fusion objective. CMC models interactions among coefficient adjustments and jointly optimizes regional weights to synthesize one shared model. Experiments across diverse multimodal tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of TAC-Merge in consolidating complementary expert capabilities and supporting generalization to unseen tasks.

</details>


### [234] [EduFair-Bench: Evaluating Pedagogical Fairness of LLM Tutors Across Student Demographics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12949)
*Jiaxu Zhao,Bahar Radmehr,Fares Fawzi,Tanya Nazaretsky,Tanja Käser*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EduFair-Bench provides a benchmark to audit pedagogical fairness of LLM tutors across demographics using a controlled student-tutor interaction setup; reveals that capability and fairness are largely orthogonal, with larger biases in advanced models and language/immigration cues driving bigger gaps; includes ablations to separate tutor-driven vs student-driven bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address whether LLM tutoring quality varies with student demographics and quantify the relationship between model capability and fairness; fill gap in rigorous fairness auditing of AI tutoring systems.

Method: Constructs a multi-domain (math, physics, chemistry) question bank; simulates a fixed LLM student interacting with each tutor across nine demographic levels along four dimensions (gender, immigration background, first language, SES). Evaluates tutoring quality on five turn-level and four conversation-level metrics using an LLM judge validated against three-annotator consensus on 180 turns. Measures bias with paired Wilcoxon tests and bootstrap CIs. Conducts two ablations: (1) demographic cues conveyed via names; (2) conflicting demographic information between tutor and student.

Result: Across five tutors, capability and demographic fairness are largely orthogonal. The smallest model is most consistent; the four more capable tutors show wide demographic gaps without a clear capability-to-fairness ordering. Pedagogy-specific RL training redistributes rather than eliminates bias. Language- and immigration-related cues yield larger gaps than gender- and SES-related cues.

Conclusion: Fairness in LLM tutoring cannot be inferred from capability alone; targeted auditing and bias mitigation are needed. The benchmark provides a controlled, multi-domain framework to disentangle tutor-driven vs student-driven bias and to inform remediation strategies.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as tutors, but it is unclear whether they support all students equally well. We introduce \textbf{EduFair-Bench}, a benchmark for auditing the pedagogical fairness of LLM tutors---whether tutoring quality varies systematically with student demographics. EduFair-Bench pairs a multi-domain question bank (mathematics, physics, chemistry) with a controlled simulation in which a fixed LLM student interacts with each tutor across nine demographic levels spanning four dimensions: gender, immigration background, first language, and socioeconomic status (SES). Tutoring quality is scored on five turn-level pedagogical metrics and four conversation-level dimensions, using an LLM judge validated against three-annotator consensus on 180 tutor turns. Bias is measured via paired Wilcoxon signed-rank tests and bootstrap effect-size confidence intervals. Two ablations (demographic cues conveyed through names; conflicting demographic information between tutor and student) disentangle tutor-driven from student-driven bias. Across five tutors, we find that model capability and demographic fairness are largely orthogonal: the smallest model is the most consistent while the four more capable tutors all exhibit wide demographic gaps with no clear capability-to-fairness ordering, pedagogy-specific RL training redistributes rather than removes bias, and language- and immigration-related cues produce larger gaps than gender- and SES-related cues.

</details>


### [235] [How Good Are Frontier Models at Physics? Expert Re-Grading Reveals Broken Evaluations and Near-Saturation of Leading Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13009)
*Ali Ansari,Haoran Sun,Andy Zeyi Liu,Mark Jabbour,Yongshan Ding,Steven Girvin,Yu He,Sohrab Ismail-Beigi,Aleksander Kubica,Owen D. Miller,Corey O'Hern,Vidvuds Ozolins,David Poland,A. Douglas Stone,Frank C. van den Bosch,Logan Wright,Navid Akbari,Santanu Antu,Kangle Cai,Andrew Calabrese-Day,Mateo Cárdenes Wuttig,Meng Cheng,Barry T. Chiang,Ali Ghorashi,Shouzhen Gu,Haoyang Huang,Zhibo Kang,Lukas Kienesberger,Hantian Liu,Charles Lomba,Zhongling Lu,Wenchao Ma,Rohin E. McIntosh,Evan McKinney,Ivan Rojkov,Xulei Sun,Yarone Meir Tokayer,Naveen Balaji Umasankar,Mira Varma,Leda Wang,Qimin Wang,Tyler Wang,Haoyu Wei,Jinming Yang,Jinchen Zhao,Sherlock Tingrui Zhao,Qinyuan Zheng,Jay S. Zou,Lucas Baker,Arman Cohan,John Sous*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Expert-audited evaluation of frontier physics benchmarks shows many low scores are due to benchmarking issues; after expert corrections, model performance improves dramatically, implying current benchmarks understate capabilities and prompting more demanding evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether frontier LMs truly underperform on physics tasks or if benchmarks and reference solutions misrepresent their abilities; to introduce expert-validated evaluation to obtain a truer picture of physics reasoning and problem solving.

Method: Evaluate six physics benchmarks using text-only problems. Domain experts (faculty/grad researchers) review problem statements, reference solutions, and model responses to distinguish model errors from grader/reference issues and ambiguities. Experts correct erroneous references and flawed questions. Calculate corrected scores on retained evaluation subsets after expert review and compare to original results.

Result: Measured mean@4 for GPT-5.6-Sol rises from 47.3% to 78.7% on HLE-Physics and from 61.0% to 87.2% on CMT-Benchmark. Corrected pass@4 reaches 94.4% on the 54 retained CritPt challenges. Scores on audited subsets UGPhysics, PRISM-Physics, and PHYBench also rise after correction.

Conclusion: Current benchmarks substantially understate frontier models' ability to solve well-posed physics problems. Near-saturation on these closed-ended tasks indicates the need for more demanding, expert-validated evaluations that capture genuine scientific reasoning and uncertainty handling.

Abstract: Low reported scores on leading physics benchmarks, including those featured in the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index (2026), suggest that frontier language models still struggle with advanced physics, a demanding test of their scientific reasoning and quantitative problem-solving abilities. Yet this impression does not always align with domain experts' experiences using these models in their work. We revisit these reported findings by evaluating frontier models on six widely used physics benchmarks and auditing them with experts, focusing on text-only problems with verifiable final answers. For each subfield of physics, faculty and graduate researchers with relevant expertise carefully review problem statements, reference solutions, and model responses to distinguish genuine model errors from grader errors, incorrect reference solutions, and ambiguous or underspecified questions. Most audited cases initially evaluated as incorrect reflect these benchmarking issues rather than errors in the models' physics reasoning. We then ask experts to address these benchmarking issues by correcting erroneous reference solutions and repairing or excluding flawed questions. We find that GPT-5.6-Sol's measured mean@4 rises from 47.3% to 78.7% on HLE-Physics and from 61.0% to 87.2% on CMT-Benchmark, while its corrected pass@4 reaches 94.4% on the 54 retained CritPt challenges. Corrected scores are computed on the retained evaluation subsets following expert review. Scores on the audited subsets of UGPhysics, PRISM-Physics, and PHYBench also rise substantially after correction. These findings suggest that current benchmarks substantially understate frontier models' ability to solve well-posed physics problems. Near-saturation on these closed-ended tasks highlights the need for more demanding, expert-validated evaluations.

</details>


### [236] [Diffusion Models and Concept Formation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13047)
*Zekun Wang,Karthik Singaravadivelan,Christopher J. MacLellan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Diffusion models implicitly implement Cobweb-like concept hierarchies via their noisy marginals, with a basic level emerging at intermediate noise; empirical mapping to MNIST shows diffusion hierarchy can be recovered, reframing diffusion as a cognitive model and giving Cobweb a continuous instantiation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To unify a classic cognitive theory of concept learning (Cobweb) with modern diffusion-based generative models, arguing that both form hierarchical Bayesian density models with Gaussian prototypes and yield a basic level at mid-level abstraction.

Method: Conceptual analysis establishing a four-way correspondence between Cobweb and diffusion models (hierarchical density models, hierarchical-Bayesian with Gaussian prototypes, score-following categorization, emergence of a basic level). It then recovers the diffusion hierarchy via mode-finding on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST to compare the basic levels.

Result: Four respects of correspondence are demonstrated: both are hierarchical density models and hierarchical-Bayesian with Gaussian prototypes; both treat categorization as score-following to reduce uncertainty; a basic level emerges in both. The diffusion hierarchy can be recovered from data and compared to Cobweb’s basic level, indicating diffusion encodes a continuous, interpolable hierarchy.

Conclusion: Diffusion models can be interpreted as cognitive models of concept formation, offering a continuous, scalable instantiation of Cobweb and reframing diffusion as a cognitive theory of concept learning.

Abstract: Humans organize knowledge into a taxonomy of concepts with nested levels of abstraction and a \emph{basic level} at which people recognize and name objects with the least cognitive effort. Cobweb is a classic cognitive account of this ability, an incremental learner that builds a probabilistic concept hierarchy by maximizing category utility. We argue that diffusion models, although designed for image synthesis, implicitly perform the same computation. The noisy marginals of a diffusion model are Gaussian smoothings of the data distribution, and the modes of these marginals form a hierarchy that corresponds to a Cobweb tree of probabilistic prototypes in four respects. Both are hierarchical density models, both are hierarchical-Bayesian models with Gaussian prototypes, both treat categorization as score-following that reduces uncertainty, and in both a basic level emerges. We locate this basic level for a diffusion model at an intermediate noise level, where recent analyses show that the reverse process commits to the class identity of a sample. The two models differ mainly in how they represent and learn the taxonomy. Cobweb learns a discrete tree incrementally, whereas a diffusion model encodes a continuous, interpolable hierarchy in a single learned score field fit to the data distribution. We test the correspondence on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST by recovering the diffusion hierarchy through mode-finding and comparing the basic levels of the two models. This reframes diffusion as a cognitive model of concept formation and offers Cobweb a continuous, scalable instantiation.

</details>


### [237] [Anchoring Clinical Events in Time: UID-Preserving Multimodal Reconstruction and Source-Grounded Adjudication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13062)
*Sayantan Kumar,Nicolas Grimaldi,Jack Cummins,Jeremy C. Weiss*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UID-preserving multimodal framework with a GAVEL judge improves temporal alignment and event recovery in clinical timelines, approaching clinician-annotator performance; reports a 43% gain in event recovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address chronology obscurity in discharge summaries and partial coverage in structured EHRs by linking narrative events to source spans with UIDs and providing an evaluation judge to support robust timeline reconstruction.

Method: Introduce a UID-preserving pipeline that links narrative events to source spans, retains identity through text-only estimation, structured-evidence retrieval, timestamped grounding, and joint revision; add GAVEL, an LLM judge comparing UID-aligned timelines against narrative and structured records; evaluate across six open-weight models and 40 mixed-critical-care summaries; compare GLM 5.2 multimodal revision vs text-only; perform ablations; conduct blinded human review and adjudication.

Result: GLM 5.2 multimodal revision improves temporal agreement without reducing event recovery and is competitive with clinician annotations; other revisions show smaller gains. Ablations show UIDs mainly preserve event retention, while source-row linkage aids temporal placement. Blinded review supports GAVEL findings; adjudication favored multimodal over text-only GLM 5.2 but not for DeepSeek V3.2. Overall, the UID-and-judge pipeline yields 43% increased event recovery and a provenance-enabled framework competitive with clinician annotations.

Conclusion: A UID-driven, multimodal revision framework supplemented by a judge (GAVEL) enhances reconstruction and evaluation of clinical timelines, providing strong provenance and performance approaching clinician-level annotations; ablations clarify roles of event retention vs temporal placement.

Abstract: Clinical timelines support treatment-window analysis and leakage-free modeling, but discharge summaries often obscure chronology and structured EHR tables describe only part of the patient course. We present a UID-preserving framework that links each narrative event occurrence to its source span and retains that identity through text-only estimation, structured-evidence retrieval, timestamped source-row grounding, and joint revision. We also present GAVEL, an LLM judge that compares two UID-aligned timelines against the narrative and structured record, to augment prior matching and temporal assessments. Across six open-weight models and 40 mixed-critical-care summaries, the GLM 5.2 multimodal revision, as compared to its text-only variant, improved temporal agreement without reducing event recovery and performed competitively with clinician annotations, while other model revisions showed smaller gains and lower overall performance. Ablations showed that UIDs primarily preserve event retention, whereas source-row linkage supports temporal placement. Blinded human review upheld most GAVEL findings, and controlled adjudication favored multimodal over text-only GLM 5.2 but did not for DeepSeek V3.2. In developing the UID and judge pipeline, we are able to demonstrate 43\% increased event recovery, a framework competitive with clinician annotations, and a system with occurrence-level provenance for both reconstruction and evaluation.

</details>


### [238] [Autonomous Research for Open-Ended Problems: A Case Study on Telecom Ticket Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13073)
*Junghyun Min,Huseyin Uzunalioglu,Mohamed Trabelsi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Autonomous ML research can approach state-of-the-art performance on open-ended, industry-grade problems (telecom ticket retrieval) but requires human collaboration due to creativity and operational overhead; achieves about 90% of SOTA (0.34 vs 0.38 Recall@1) in 10 weeks at a modest cost (~$200 per Cursor campaign).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether autonomous research frameworks can tackle open-ended, real-world ML problems beyond narrow search spaces and understand their capabilities, limits, and practical viability.

Method: Case study applying autonomous agents (commercial and open-source) to telecom ticket retrieval, exploring choices in representation, architecture, and training data generation; benchmarking against SOTA using Recall@1; evaluating time-to-result and cost; analyzing the role of human supervision and operational overhead.

Result: Autonomous research excels in narrow hyperparameter optimization but struggles with human-like intuition and creativity; incurs operational overhead. It achieved ~0.34 Recall@1 vs ~0.38 SOTA (≈90% of SOTA); completed in 10 weeks versus 10 months for humans; cost up to $200 per Cursor campaign.

Conclusion: Human researchers and autonomous research frameworks should collaborate for best ML results; AML shows promise for speed and efficiency but cannot yet fully replace human intuition in open-ended problems.

Abstract: Recent breakthroughs in LLM-based systems and their abilities in problem solving and coding have allowed progress in the AI for Science paradigm, potentially replacing human roles in machine learning (ML) research. However, while several frameworks of fully autonomous end-to-end ML research have been proposed, successful implementations of them are often limited to problems with narrow search spaces, like language modeling or biomedical ML benchmarks. In this paper, we explore how autonomous research can be adapted to solve open-ended, industry-grade ML problems, by considering a case study: telecom ticket retrieval, an open-ended task with degrees of freedom in representation, architecture, and training data generation. We discover that autonomous research for open-ended problems with commercial and open-source agents shows both promise and limitations: while autonomous research can excel in narrow hyperparameter optimization, it lacks human-like intuition and creativity and requires operational overhead. Even with minimal human supervision, autonomous research can reach $90\%$ of state-of-the-art performance (0.34 vs. 0.38 Recall@1) in a much shorter time period (10 weeks vs. 10 months of human work) at a modest cost (up to \$200 per Cursor campaign). Our empirical evidence recommends that human researchers and autonomous research frameworks work together for best results in ML research.

</details>


### [239] [Embodied-BenchForge: A Closed-Loop Agentic Workflow for Embodied Benchmark Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13082)
*Baoyang Jiang,Fengchun Zhang,Leyuan Wang,Haotian Li,Yida Wang,Zhe Ji,Jinshan Lai,Xi Ren,Danyang Li,Zheng Yang,Jianwei Hu,Qiang Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: End-to-end embodied benchmark construction via an agentic framework (Embodied-BenchForge) that combines synthesis, verification, and repair to produce complete benchmark artifacts, including six offline benchmarks and one interactive benchmark with 220 tasks, enabling better model differentiation and efficient reuse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agentic benchmarking approaches typically cover isolated stages and fail to verify downstream artifacts, allowing local defects to propagate. There is a need for a closed-loop, artifact-aware construction framework that guarantees artifact integrity and supports reuse across benchmarks.

Method: Closed-Loop Benchmark Synthesis that couples forward artifact synthesis with backward verification and repair; Skill-Orchestrated Artifact Synthesis to assemble typed, reusable skills into executable workflows; an artifact dependency graph to record intermediates and dependencies; Requirement-Guided Verification and Repair using artifact-specific contracts and provenance-triggered re-execution or upstream rollback; construction of six offline benchmarks plus one interactive benchmark with 220 tasks.

Result: Evaluations with representative MLLMs and embodied agents show the benchmarks distinguish capabilities in observation-based understanding and closed-loop execution; quality assessments and ablations validate benchmark quality and the effectiveness of verification and repair; repair and skill reuse analyses demonstrate efficient localized recovery and cross-benchmark reuse.

Conclusion: Embodied-BenchForge provides a complete, verifiable, and reusable end-to-end means to construct embodied benchmarks, with demonstrated applicability across diverse scenarios and measurable improvements in reliability, repairability, and cross-benchmark reuse.

Abstract: Agentic systems offer a promising way to automate embodied benchmark construction, but existing approaches typically cover isolated stages or remain specialized to predefined environments and task families. More importantly, multi-step construction produces dependent intermediate artifacts that are often passed downstream without artifact-specific verification, allowing local defects to propagate into the final benchmark. We present Embodied-BenchForge, an agentic framework that transforms user-specified evaluation intents into complete embodied benchmark artifacts. It formulates construction as Closed-Loop Benchmark Synthesis, integrating forward artifact synthesis with backward verification and repair. Skill-Orchestrated Artifact Synthesis composes typed and reusable skills into executable workflows, while an artifact dependency graph records intermediate outputs and their dependencies. Requirement-Guided Verification and Repair applies artifact-specific contracts throughout construction and uses provenance to trigger local re-execution or upstream rollback when verification fails. Embodied-BenchForge constructs six benchmarks covering diverse embodied scenarios in the Offline EQA Track, together with one interactive benchmark containing 220 executable tasks in the Interactive Embodied Track. Evaluations of representative MLLMs and embodied agents show that the benchmarks distinguish model capabilities in both observation-based understanding and closed-loop execution. Quality assessment and ablations validate benchmark quality and the effectiveness of verification and repair, while repair and skill-reuse analyses demonstrate efficient localized recovery and cross-benchmark reusability.

</details>


### [240] [CMA-OT: Hierarchical Expert Supervision for Dance-to-Music Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13118)
*Jinting Wang,Chenxing Li,Dong Yu,Li Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CMA-OT introduces a curriculum-guided, multi-scale representation alignment framework with scale-aware optimal transport to improve D2M generation by leveraging hierarchical supervision from an external music expert, yielding better rhythm, structure, and musicality than prior weakly-supervised methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a semantic gap between sparse dance cues (rhythm/style) and the dense musical information (structure, instrumentation, dynamics). Prior methods that supervise only final audio struggle to learn rich music representations, limiting musicality and coherence.

Method: Propose CMA-OT with three components: (1) curriculum-guided multi-scale learning to progressively transfer knowledge from an external music expert to the generator's latent features; (2) a scale-aware optimal transport mechanism to align soft correspondences across hierarchical expert representations and generator features under temporal mismatch; (3) leveraging hierarchical supervision to stabilize and enhance representation learning.

Result: Experimental results on two datasets show state-of-the-art performance in rhythmic synchronization, perceptual quality, and overall music generation.

Conclusion: CMA-OT effectively bridges the semantic gap between dance cues and music generation, enabling stable, effective learning of rich music representations and yielding superior D2M synthesis.

Abstract: Dance-to-music (D2M) generation aims to synthesize music that is rhythmically and stylistically aligned with dance videos. A key challenge arises from the semantic mismatch between sparse dance cues, such as rhythm and style, and the dense information required for music composition, including structure, instrumentation, and expressive dynamics. Existing methods typically rely on these sparse cues and supervise only the final audio output, resulting in poorly learned music representations and generated music with limited musicality and structural coherence. To address these issues, we propose Curriculum-guided Multi-scale representation Alignment with scale-aware Optimal Transport (CMA-OT), a novel paradigm that leverages an external music expert to provide hierarchical supervision for the generator's latent features, bridging the semantic gap and enhancing representation learning. To effectively incorporate hierarchical supervision, we introduce a curriculum-guided multi-scale learning strategy that progressively transfers musical knowledge from the expert to the music generator, enabling stable and effective representation learning. Moreover, to accommodate the semantic and structural variations across different expert scales and achieve fine-grained alignment under temporal mismatch, we propose a scale-aware optimal transport alignment mechanism, which models soft correspondences between hierarchical expert representations and the generator's latent features. Extensive experiments on two datasets demonstrate that CMA-OT achieves state-of-the-art performance in rhythmic synchronization, perceptual quality, and overall music generation.

</details>


### [241] [A Hybrid LSTM-XGBoost Framework for Multi-Horizon Stock Return Prediction Across Diversified Equity Portfolios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13125)
*Seif ElDein Mostafa,Yahia Ahmed,Farah Datwish,Marwa Solayman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hybrid two-stage model: LSTM-derived temporal embeddings fed into an XGBoost regressor for multi-horizon equity return prediction, showing strong short-term RMSE gains and high long-horizon directional accuracy, with a composite scoring framework for portfolio ranking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial time series are non-stationary, nonlinear, and noisy. There is a need to improve predictive accuracy across multiple horizons by leveraging sequential dynamics and nonlinear regression while mitigating look-ahead bias.

Method: A two-stage architecture: (1) an LSTM with 2 layers (64 hidden units) processes 60-day windows of 5 market features to produce 64-dimensional embeddings; (2) embeddings are concatenated with 14 handcrafted technical indicators to form a 78-dimensional feature vector. An XGBoost regressor (CV-tuned with three-fold grid search) regresses stock returns. Training uses a multi-stock pooled corpus with strict chronological splits and per-stock MinMax scaling. Evaluations cover horizons of 30, 90, 252, and 365 trading days.

Result: On the 30-day horizon, test RMSE = 0.0949 (about one-third of the standalone LSTM). The hybrid model largely matches or slightly surpasses the XGBoost-only baseline across most stocks. Directional accuracy increases with horizon, reaching 97.6% at 365 days; however, this largely reflects a high base rate of positive returns. A naïve always-positive predictor is used as a baseline for directional performance. A composite investment scoring framework based on multi-horizon predictions is proposed for portfolio ranking.

Conclusion: The hybrid LSTM+XGBoost approach effectively harnesses learned temporal dynamics and nonlinear regression to improve short-horizon predictive accuracy while delivering strong directional signals at longer horizons. The evaluation design is robust (chronological splits, per-stock scaling, cross-validation), and the authors propose a practical portfolio scoring framework, though further ablation, larger datasets, and realism checks (e.g., transaction costs) would strengthen the claim.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of equity returns remains a major challenge in computational finance due to the non-stationary, nonlinear, and low signal-to-noise ratio nature of financial time series. This paper proposes a hybrid two-stage architecture that combines a long short-term memory (LSTM) network with an XGBoost gradient-boosted regressor for multi-horizon stock return prediction across a diversified panel of 14 U.S. equities spanning six industry sectors. The LSTM component, comprising two stacked layers with 64 hidden units, processes 60-day sliding windows of five sequential market features to produce 64-dimensional temporal embeddings that encode learned sequential market dynamics. These embeddings are concatenated with 14 hand-crafted technical indicators to form a 78-dimensional hybrid feature vector, which is subsequently passed to an XGBoost regressor tuned via 3-fold cross-validation grid search. The framework is trained on a multi-stock pooled corpus using strict chronological splits and per-stock MinMaxScaling to prevent look-ahead bias, and evaluated across four prediction horizons of 30, 90, 252, and 365 trading days. Experimental results demonstrate that the hybrid model achieves a test RMSE of 0.0949 on the 30-day horizon, roughly one-third that of the standalone LSTM baseline, while marginally matching or surpassing the XGBoost-Only baseline across the majority of stocks. Directional accuracy rises with horizon length, reaching 97.6% at 365 days; we show, however, that this largely tracks the high base rate of positive long-horizon returns in the sample, and we therefore benchmark directional accuracy against a naive always-positive predictor and treat the above-base-rate gap at short horizons as the more informative signal. A composite investment scoring framework derived from multi-horizon predictions is further proposed to support portfolio ranking and decision support.

</details>


### [242] [Rethinking Heterogeneous System Disaggregation for Subquadratic Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13134)
*Arya Tschand,Yaosheng Fu,Vikram Sharma Mailthody,Nicolai Oswald,Po-An Tsai,Ritchie Zhao,Oreste Villa,Vijay Janapa Reddi,Karu Sankaralingam*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SQD proposes a fine-grained disaggregation of inference around subquadratic attention (not per operator) to boost throughput and energy efficiency on DRAM/ SRAM heterogeneous systems, achieving substantial tokens-per-J and throughput gains on multiple LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Broaden efficiency in LLM inference by exploiting the arithmetic intensity and memory footprint differences of subquadratic attention, addressing limitations of dense attention-centric disaggregation.

Method: Introduce SQD, disaggregate decode by quadratic vs subquadratic attention (and variants: sparse, linear, sliding-window). For sparse LLMs: split into top-k selection (needs full KV) and top-k attention+FFN (static footprints). For linear/sliding-window: split into dense attention vs subquadratic attention + FFN. Evaluate on 8xB200-like proxy and a Rubin+LPX model, compare to attention-FFN disaggregation baselines.

Result: Average tokens/J improvements: 53% (GLM 5.2), 31% (Nemotron 3 Ultra), 56% (Gemma 4 31B) over strongest GPU baselines. In Rubin+LPX, 1.2x–1.5x tighter latency and up to 3.6x higher throughput vs best attention-FFN baseline. Provides architectural insights for chip/interconnect provisioning for future heterogeneous systems.

Conclusion: Fine-grained disaggregation aligned with subquadratic attention characteristics yields meaningful efficiency gains and practical guidance for hardware-software co-design in next-generation heterogeneous LLM serving.

Abstract: Frontier language models are more aggressively using subquadratic attention to reduce the memory footprint and compute requirements during inference while still delivering frontier accuracy. While existing systems make dense attention-centric disaggregated serving decisions, we show that disaggregating inference around the unique arithmetic intensity and memory footprint of subquadratic attention LLMs can achieve significant throughput and energy efficiency gains on emerging DRAM-based and SRAM-only heterogeneous systems.
  We introduce SQD (SubQuadratic Disaggregation), a fine-grained heterogeneous disaggregation scheme that splits decode by quadratic and subquadratic attention rather than by operator type, and that applies across subquadratic attention variants. For sparse attention LLMs, we disaggregate decode into top-k selection, which must index through the full KV, and top-k attention plus FFN, which have static memory footprints. For linear and sliding-window attention LLMs, we disaggregate decode into dense attention layers and subquadratic attention layers plus FFN. In an adjusted 8xB200 heterogeneous system proxy, we observe average tokens/J improvements of 53% on GLM 5.2, 31% on Nemotron 3 Ultra, and 56% on Gemma 4 31B over the strongest GPU-only baselines. In an analytical model of a Rubin plus LPX system with fixed power budgets, we observe 1.2x to 1.5x tighter achievable latencies and up to 3.6x higher throughput over the best baseline of attention-FFN disaggregation. Our experiments also reveal architectural insights on chip and interconnect provisioning for next-generation heterogeneous systems serving subquadratic attention.

</details>


### [243] [Evidence-Aligned Local Composition of Discrete Experts for Sequence Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05801)
*Mohammad Panahazari,Usman A. Khan,Shuchin Aeron*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduce evidence-aligned local composition to infer soft, position-wise weights over domain experts for corrupted documents, estimating evidence from denoising losses and smoothing; yields mixture tracking and improved restoration relative to a single global weight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Documents can span multiple domains (prose, code, configuration). When corrupted, restoration requires deciding missing content and which expert to trust at each position without region labels or a trained router. A soft, local mixture over experts can adapt to the true composition.

Method: Compute marginal evidence of the corrupted observation under a corruption model; estimate evidence from each expert's denoising loss; produce position-wise soft weights; smooth across positions; apply to a mix of categorical simulators, byte-level experts, and fine-tuned from a 1.3B discrete flow-matching model; compare to a single global weight.

Result: Inferred weights track true regions with 0.85 field accuracy on naturally mixed scientific documents and 0.98 on constructed, lexically disjoint mixtures. Restoration improves over a single global weight when experts are distinct and reduces to it when they converge; indicates effective expert separation.

Conclusion: Evidence-aligned local composition provides robust, adaptive mixture weights that reflect expert separation and enable accurate restoration of multi-domain corrupted documents without region labels or routers.

Abstract: A document modeled as a discrete sequence of tokens can be thought of as being generated from a composition of texts from different domains; a README file, for example, moves between prose, code, and configuration. When such a document is corrupted and only frozen domain experts are available, restoring it requires deciding both what is missing and which expert to trust at each position, at test time and without region labels or a trained router. We introduce evidence-aligned local composition, which infers a soft, position-wise weighting over the experts from the marginal evidence of the corrupted observation under a given corruption model, estimating the evidence from the experts' own denoising losses and smoothing the weights across positions. Because the weighting is soft, it recovers a mixture when the true composition is mixed and concentrates on one expert when that suffices. Across a categorical simulator, byte-level experts, and experts fine-tuned from a $1.3$B discrete flow-matching model, the inferred weights track the true regions at $0.85$ field accuracy on naturally mixed scientific documents, and at $0.98$ on constructed mixtures whose regions are lexically disjoint. Restoration improves over a single global weight when the experts are genuinely distinct and reduces to it when they converge, tracking a measure of expert separation.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [244] [Scenario-Independent Criticality Assessment and Prediction for Vulnerable Road Users in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11947)
*Jörg Gamerdinger,Victor Schwarzenberger,Philipp Schmid,Sven Teufel,Oliver Bringmann*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes a VRU-centric criticality metric and a scenario-independent criticality prediction framework for automated driving. Evaluated on the DeepAccident dataset, it improves pedestrian criticality classification and outperforms existing metrics with a high F1-score.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing criticality metrics are often scenario-specific and vehicle-centric, inadequately addressing vulnerable road users (VRUs) whose unpredictable motion requires specialized assessment. A scenario-independent framework aims to provide universal criticality prediction across all traffic participant classes.

Method: Introduce two components: (1) a VRU-centric criticality metric tailored for vulnerable road users, and (2) a scenario-independent criticality prediction framework. Evaluation uses the DeepAccident dataset across diverse safety-critical scenarios.

Result: VRU metric improves pedestrian criticality classification by up to 50%. The criticality prediction framework outperforms state-of-the-art metrics by 275%, achieving an F1-score of 0.96 and enabling cross-class scenario-independent assessment.

Conclusion: The approaches show strong potential to enhance criticality assessment for safety evaluation in automated driving systems and enable robust, universal criticality predictions across traffic participant classes.

Abstract: Increasing safety is the primary objective of automated vehicles. Achieving this goal requires reliable safety metrics that incorporate safety-relevant factors such as object type, velocity, and criticality. A key capability of such metrics is the distinction between critical and non-critical objects, which is addressed through criticality or relevance estimation. Existing criticality metrics are typically designed for specific scenarios and primarily focus on vehicle-to-vehicle interactions. In this paper, we therefore propose a novel criticality metric tailored to vulnerable road users (VRUs), which require special consideration due to their less predictable motion behavior. Furthermore, to avoid the complexity introduced by scenario-specific metrics, we introduce a scenario-independent criticality prediction framework applicable to all traffic participant classes. The effectiveness of both the proposed VRU-centric criticality metric and the criticality prediction framework is evaluated using the DeepAccident dataset, which contains a diverse set of safety-critical traffic scenarios. The proposed VRU-centric criticality metric improves pedestrian criticality classification performance by up to 50 %. In addition, the proposed criticality prediction framework outperforms state-of-the-art metrics by 275 %, achieving an F1-score of 0.96 and enabling scenario-independent criticality assessment across all object classes. These results demonstrate the strong potential of the proposed approaches to enhance criticality assessment for safety evaluation in automated driving systems.

</details>


### [245] [Pelican-Sim 1.0: A General World Model Simulator for Embodied Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12036)
*Shilong Zou,Shilin Zhang,Yingji Zhang,Yuhang Huang,Yi Zhang,Zeyuan Ding,Han Dong,Junwei Liao,Yong Dai,Jian Tang,Xiaozhu Ju*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Pelican-Sim 1.0 is a general world model simulator for embodied AI that unifies actions, connects actions to pixels, uses sparse MoE for heterogeneous dynamics, and enables efficient rollouts. It achieves large improvements in video quality and downstream policy performance across multiple Robo tasks, demonstrating strong generalization across trajectories, scenes, objects, embodiments, and viewpoints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create a single, scalable world model that works across heterogeneous devices and dynamics, enabling reliable decision making by connecting action representations to visual observations and providing efficient rollouts for downstream learning.

Method: Four design features: (1) Unified 28-dimensional action space for broad embodiment compatibility; (2) Action-visual injection using URDF- and camera-rendered action videos to link actions and pixels, improving controllability (PSNR +0.904 vs baselines); (3) Sparse mixture-of-experts (MoE) to accommodate heterogeneous dynamics and absorb action modality while reducing inter-modality conflicts (FVD -6.530 vs dense backbone); (4) Efficient rollout generation via causal adaptation and few-step distillation to produce a four-step autoregressive simulator, achieving 5.67× speedup over a 35-step model. Training on ~1M real and simulated trajectories; four downstream RoboTwin applications demonstrated strong gains in controllability and video quality.

Result: Quantitative gains include PSNR improvements of +0.904 (action-visual injection over baselines), FVD reduction of 6.530 (vs dense backbone); four-step autoregressive simulator speedup of 5.67× vs 35-step model. Dataset-wide PSNR gains across benchmarks: AgiBotWorld Beta +4.636, RoboMIND +2.080, RoboTwin +10.343; adapted EWMBench DYN score +0.426 on RoboTwin. Downstream outcomes on RoboTwin: adding 500 generated trajectories to 50 demonstrations per task raises policy success from 70% to 93%; policy evaluation Pearson correlation across five checkpoints = 0.994; relative gains: action selection +47.7%, policy improvement +20.3%. Qualitative generalization across trajectory, scene, object, embodiment, and viewpoint shifts."

Conclusion: Pelican-Sim 1.0 offers a general-purpose world model capable of cross-trajectory, cross-scene, cross-object, cross-embodiment, and cross-viewpoint generalization, delivering meaningful improvements in action controllability, video quality, rollout efficiency, and downstream policy performance, thereby exhibiting strong potential as a universal world model for embodied AI.

Abstract: In this technical report, we propose Pelican-Sim 1.0, a general world model simulator for embodied intelligence that predicts future observations from visual context and robot actions to support downstream learning and decision making. The model incorporates four key design features: (1) Unified action representation: a 28-dimensional action value space covering most mainstream embodiments, keeping one model valid across heterogeneous devices. (2) Action-visual injection: URDF- and camera-rendered action videos bridge actions and pixels, giving markedly better controllability across embodiments, scenes, and tasks (PSNR +0.904 over alternative fusion baselines). (3) Sparse mixture-of-experts (MoE): sparse MoE layers add capacity for heterogeneous dynamics and absorb the action modality while reducing inter-modality conflict (FVD -6.530 vs. the dense backbone). (4) Efficient rollout generation: causal adaptation and few-step distillation yield a four-step autoregressive simulator, achieving a 5.67-fold speedup over the 35-step model. Benefiting from these designs, we train on approximately one million real-world and simulated trajectories and obtain large gains in action controllability and video quality: PSNR improves over the strongest evaluated baselines by 4.636 on AgiBotWorld Beta, 2.080 on RoboMIND, and 10.343 on RoboTwin, with the adapted EWMBench DYN score up 0.426 on RoboTwin. Relying on this, four downstream applications on RoboTwin succeed: 500 generated trajectories added to 50 demonstrations per task raise policy success from 70% to 93%; policy evaluation reaches a Pearson correlation of 0.994 across five checkpoints; and relative success gains reach 47.7% for action selection and 20.3% for policy improvement. Qualitative generalization across trajectory, scene, object, embodiment, and viewpoint shifts highlights its potential as a general-purpose world model simulator.

</details>


### [246] [MoPA: Coordinated Mobile Manipulation via Subsystem-Specific Perception Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12081)
*Guangyu Chen,Qiwei Liang,Shaolong Zhu,Tianxing Chen,Zikuan Xiao,Yifan Xie,Lingfeng Zhang,Ping Luo,Renjing Xu,Wenbo Ding*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MoPA introduces dual perceptual streams and coupled flow matching to align perception with mobility and manipulation in mobile manipulation; achieves state-of-the-art on ManiSkill-HAB and real-world tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address mismatch between unified perceptual representations and subsystem-specific perception-action mappings in mobile manipulation; enable explicit, coordinated perception-to-action conditioning across base motion and arm control.

Method: Propose MoPA: Dual Perceptual Streams with two masked query banks in a shared vision-language context; Perception2Action Adaptation updating each query bank and its action stream at every layer of a Mixture-of-Transformers decoder with inter-stream information exchange; Coupled Conditional Flow Matching learning a joint vector field for coordinated generation of action chunks.

Result: State-of-the-art on ManiSkill-HAB across all three task suites; mean full-task real-world success 76.3%, 12.5-point improvement over best baseline; ablation studies support effectiveness.

Conclusion: MoPA effectively aligns perceptual conditioning with mobility and manipulation while preserving cross-subsystem coordination, yielding robust, coordinated policies for mobile manipulation; demonstrates strong generalization in both simulation and real-world settings.

Abstract: Mobile manipulation requires perceptual evidence at different spatial scales for base motion and arm control, while the two action modalities remain kinematically coupled. Existing policies often employ specialized action generation for different subsystems but condition heterogeneous action branches on a shared perceptual representation, leaving subsystem-specific perception-action correspondence implicit. We present MoPA, a framework that aligns perceptual conditioning with mobility and manipulation while preserving coordination at the action level. Dual Perceptual Streams employ two mutually masked query banks to extract separate perceptual representations from a shared vision-language context. Perception2Action Adaptation jointly updates each query bank and its corresponding action stream at every layer of a structured Mixture-of-Transformers decoder, while enabling information exchange between the two action streams. Coupled conditional flow matching learns a joint vector field for coordinated generation of both action chunks. On the ManiSkill-HAB benchmark, MoPA achieves state-of-the-art performance across all three task suites. Across four real-world tasks, MoPA achieves a mean full-task success rate of 76.3%, outperforming the best baseline by 12.5 percentage points. Ablation studies and further analyses validate the effectiveness of the proposed design. Website is available at: https://mopa-policy.github.io/.

</details>


### [247] [Uncertainty-Aware Conflict Detection Against Operator-Conditioned Weather Hazards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12095)
*Balram Kandoria,Seulki Kim,Aryaman Singh Samyal*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Uncertainty-conditioned trajectory prediction with polyhedral hazard representation for AAM pre-flight conflict detection; uses NURBS+Kalman Filter, LPA-inspired sigmoid noise model, and grid-based weather hazard polyhedra with buffers; detects conflicts by mesh intersection of uncertainty tube and hazard polyhedra over time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robust planning in Advanced Air Mobility requires reliable prediction of state uncertainty and early detection of conflicts with dynamic weather hazards over a planning horizon.

Method: Combine NURBS-based trajectory generation with Kalman Filter state covariance propagation; adopt an LPA-inspired sigmoid measurement-noise model to capture info improvements as RTA approaches; derive velocity-to-time variance for arrival-time uncertainty; convert weather-grid data into 3D hazard polyhedra with safety buffers; perform conflict detection via mesh intersection between the probabilistic trajectory tube and hazard polyhedra with temporal overlap, enabling continuous pre-flight validation.

Result: Derives closed-form temporal uncertainty bounds and probabilistic arrival-time deviations along the path; provides a framework for continuous, time-evolving pre-flight conflict assessment in AAM environments.

Conclusion: Enables proactive, dynamic strategic validation of flight plans throughout the pre-flight horizon as environmental conditions evolve, potentially improving safety and throughput in AAM operations.

Abstract: Strategic flight plan validation in Advanced Air Mobility (AAM) environments requires robust methods for predicting aircraft state uncertainty and detecting potential conflicts with dynamic airspace hazards. This paper presents a novel framework for uncertainty-conditioned trajectory prediction combined with polyhedra hazard representation for pre-flight conflict detection. We introduce a closed-form uncertainty estimation method that couples non-uniform rational B-spline (NURBS) curve fitting for kinematic trajectory generation with a Kalman Filter for state covariance propagation. Drawing from the Light Propagation Algorithm (LPA) paradigm, we employ a sigmoid-blended measurement noise model that captures the uncertainty reduction behavior of flight management systems approaching the required time of arrival (RTA) for waypoints. The resulting temporal uncertainty bounds are derived through a velocity-to-time variance transformation, enabling probabilistic assessment of arrival time deviations along the flight path. For hazard representation, we develop an operator-conditioned classification scheme that transforms gridded environmental data, specifically weather phenomena, into three-dimensional polyhedra volumes with intensity-based stratification. These hazard polyhedra incorporate aircraft-specific safety buffers computed from vehicle performance characteristics. Conflict detection is performed through mesh intersection algorithms operating on the spatial uncertainty tube surrounding the mean trajectory against the hazard polyhedra and temporal overlap. The framework enables the continuous strategic validation of flight plans throughout the pre-flight planning time horizon as environmental conditions evolve.

</details>


### [248] [RodForesight: A World Model Enhanced Diffusion Policy for Slender and Material Agnostic Rod Insertion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12103)
*Chuanbo Yu,Mingyu Yue,Yan Lyu,Chuhan Song,Peng Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A two-stage RodForesight framework for slender rod insertion that combines coarse visual-servoing-based approaching with predictive insertion using a diffusion policy and an action-conditioned world model to pre-evaluate action chunks, achieving higher success rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
