Motivation: Rigid-peg assumptions fail for high-aspect-ratio slender rods that bend and have tip pose dependent on configuration and contact; a predictive, staged approach is needed to reliably insert with tight clearances.

Method: Stage 1: coarse approaching via visual servoing to map varied initial configurations into a near-hole hand-off region. Stage 2: predictive insertion where a diffusion policy proposes action chunks; an action-conditioned world model predicts their effects on rod-hole alignment; a pre-execution evaluator selects the best chunk based on predicted tilt and radial errors; end-to-end variant possible.

Result: Experiments show that RodForesight improves insertion success from 88.9% to 96.7% relative to baselines such as diffusion-policy-only methods.

Conclusion: A two-stage learning framework with predictive evaluation effectively handles bending and contact dynamics in slender-rod insertion, with potential end-to-end integration.

Abstract: Slender rod insertion arises in precision manufacturing, where millimetre scale diameter and tight clearances demand accurate perception and control. Conventional peg-in-hole methods assume a rigid object whose tip pose is fixed relative to the gripper. This assumption breaks down for a high aspect ratio rod, which can bend during manipulation, making its tip motion dependent on the rod configuration, grasp, material properties, and contact. We present RodForesight, a learning framework that factorises the task into two stages: 1) coarse approaching, which uses visual servoing to map diverse initial configurations into a compact near hole hand-off region; and 2) predictive insertion, which performs fine alignment and completes the insertion. It is worth noting that the two stages can be wrapped into an end-to-end design. During insertion, a diffusion policy generates candidate action chunks, while an action conditioned world model predicts their effects on rod-hole alignment. This pre-execution evaluation enables RodForesight to select the best action chunk based on predicted tilt and radial errors before execution. Experiments investigate the performance of different stages and the end-to-end setting, where RodForesight improves the success rate from 88.9% to 96.7%, compared to baseline methods such as diffusion policy.

</details>


### [249] [Multi-Objective Agent-Based Model Predictive Controller for Plug-and-Play Vehicle Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12108)
*Jiaming Zhong,Ladan Khoshnevisan,Shucheng Huang,Mohammad Pirani,Yash Vardhan Pant,Amir Khajepour*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A multi-objective AMPC using ADMM is proposed to decouple conflicting objectives among distributed controllers, achieving near-global optimality with flexibility and potential computational savings; three convergent formulations are developed and tested on vehicle control, including real-world EV implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The plug-and-play trend in vehicle control increases the need for flexible, reliable distributed control. Traditional integrated MPC struggles with distributed conflicting objectives, coupling between agents, and scalability. AMPC offers a distributed framework but faces challenges in coordinating interdependent objectives; this work aims to make AMPC practical for multi-objective, coupled control scenarios.

Method: Develop three convergent distributed control formulations by incorporating ADMM into agent-based MPC to decouple objectives while enforcing consensus and handling control regularization and inequality constraints. Apply to complex vehicle control systems with a multi-objective topology, compare formulations via simulations, identify the most efficient, and validate on an electric vehicle.

Result: Three formulations are evaluated in simulations under a multi-objective topology; the most computationally efficient formulation is implemented on an electric vehicle. The results show that multi-objective AMPC converges approximately to the global optimum of integrated MPC while offering greater flexibility and potential computational savings.

Conclusion: Multi-objective AMPC provides a practical, flexible distributed control framework that approximates centralized optimal performance. It demonstrates convergence to near-global optima and is suitable for complex vehicle control tasks, with the potential for reduced computation and enhanced plug-and-play adaptability.

Abstract: Functional integration is a growing trend in vehicle control, often involving the coordination of multiple controllers to achieve various objectives simultaneously. The need for flexibility and reliability has led to a "plug-and-play" approach in control system design, which presents challenges for traditional integrated model predictive control (MPC). Agent-based model predictive control (AMPC) has recently emerged as a distributed solution that treats controllers as agents, creating a collaborative framework among them to reach a common goal. However, this approach struggles to manage distributed conflicting objectives when agents are coupled or interdependent. To address this, we propose a novel, practical distributed control scheme called multi-objective AMPC, which adapts the alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM) into a general control strategy that approximates global optimization while decoupling objectives. We systematically develop three formulations that maintain convergence while addressing control regularization and inequality constraints, applying them to complex vehicle control systems for the first time. The proposed method has been tested on two vehicle control scenarios with a multi-objective topology. Different formulations are compared through simulations, and the most computationally efficient one was implemented on an electric vehicle for real-world evaluations. The results demonstrate that the proposed multi-objective AMPC can converge approximately to the same global optimum as integrated MPC with greater flexibility and the potential to reduce computational costs.

</details>


### [250] [A Data-Driven Distributed Control Scheme: Learning Multi-Objective Agent-Based MPC for Path-Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12142)
*Jiaming Zhong,Reza Valiollahi Mehrizi,Yash Vardhan Pant,Amir Khajepour*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A learning-driven multi-objective AMPC based on ADMM decouples distributed vehicle controllers (e.g., braking, torque vectoring) to match integrated MPC performance with lower real-time computation; adds learning-based initialization, data management, and an authentication module; demonstrates 43.5% CPU-time reduction and substantial speedups with learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To alleviate computational burden and maintenance difficulty of integrated MPC when handling multiple conflicting objectives and distributed controllers in vehicle systems; provide a flexible, real-time capable framework.

Method: Extend AMPC with ADMM to decompose the multi-objective problem into decoupled subproblems; iteratively converge to integrated performance under information exchange. Introduce learning-based initialization to speed convergence; implement data management for real-time efficiency; add an authentication module to ensure learning reliability.

Result: Path-tracking simulation with multiple controllers shows equivalent performance to integrated MPC; computation time reduced by 43.5%; learning-based initialization yields an additional 88.6% saving vs non-learning.

Conclusion: Learning multi-objective AMPC provides flexible, scalable, and real-time capable control for distributed vehicle controllers, achieving integrated-MPC parity with significant computational gains; suitable for real-time autonomous-vehicle applications.

Abstract: Agent-based model predictive control (AMPC) has recently been proposed for vehicle systems with various controllers, such as differential braking and torque vectoring, where controllers are regarded as distributed agents contributing to the same objective. However, this scheme is challenging in handling multiple conflicting objectives with coupled agents. A common approach for such tasks is the integrated MPC, where all objectives and agents are stacked together in one optimization. Nevertheless, as more agents and objectives are involved, the integrated MPC will face challenges like computational burdens and maintenance difficulties in practice. To this end, this paper proposes a learning multi-objective AMPC that can improve design flexibility and computing efficiency. First, under the assumption of information exchange, a multi-objective AMPC tailored from the alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM) is proposed to decouple the system and achieve the same performance as the integrated scheme iteratively. Second, a learning-based method for initializing iterations is proposed to accelerate convergence. In addition, a data management method is proposed for real-time efficiency, and an authentication module is designed for learning reliability. We compare the proposed scheme against the integrated scheme via a combined path-tracking simulation for autonomous vehicles with various controllers. The proposed scheme achieves the same control performance as the integrated one while reducing the computational time by 43.5%. Furthermore, the learning-based method saves 88.6% more computational time than without learning, making it suitable for real-time implementation.

</details>


### [251] [A Physics-Based Closed-Loop Robotic Bioprinting Framework Towards Volumetric Muscle Loss Treatment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12159)
*Omid Rezayof,Jerin T. Andrews,Ehsan Zobeidi,Ali Ghasemkhani,Meenakshi Kamaraj,Maryam Tilton,Johnson V. John,Farshid Alambeigi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A physics-based closed-loop DIW system enabling real-time parameter adaptation to control bead thickness in robotic bioprinting; robust and fast convergence across configurations, outperforming open-loop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce reliance on time-consuming DOE methods and large training datasets in DIW bioprinting, enabling autonomous, rapid tuning to achieve desired bead geometry for volumetric muscle loss applications.

Method: A 3D point cloud camera and autonomous vision-based evaluation feed a controller that adjusts printing parameters (e.g., extrusion pressure) to achieve a target bead thickness. Four configurations were tested, each with three repeats; comparisons made against open-loop DIW.

Result: All trials converged; tracking error < 0.5 mm; average convergence time 5.2 s; low converged pressure variance (0.04 bar). Closed-loop outperformed open-loop in achieving the target geometry.

Conclusion: The proposed physics-based closed-loop robotic bioprinting framework enables near real-time, robust control of bead geometry in DIW, reducing dependence on extensive DOE or large datasets and improving reproducibility for VML-relevant constructs.

Abstract: Robotic bioprinting and Direct Ink Writing (DIW) are being explored towards the treatment of Volumetric Muscle Loss (VML). While previous studies have shown the importance of proper parameter selection on the print outcome, existing approaches often rely on time- and material-intensive design of experiments methods, or require large, well-curated datasets for training machine learning models. In this paper, we propose a physics-based closed-loop robotic bioprinting system capable of near real-time parameter adaptation. The system integrates a 3D point cloud camera and fully autonomous vision-based algorithms to provide quantitative evaluation of printed constructs. This evaluation is fed into a controller that adjusts printing parameters to achieve a desired bead thickness. To assess the framework's performance, four experimental configurations were tested, each repeated three times. In these tests, printing began from an arbitrary initial parameter value, and the controller was tasked with adjusting the parameters to reach the desired thickness. The system converged in all trials, achieving a tracking error below 0.5 mm within an average of 5.2 seconds from the start of printing. The low standard deviation of the converged pressure over different tests (0.04 bar on average) demonstrates robustness and repeatability. Additional experiments were conducted with the controller turned off, enabling direct comparison with open-loop DIW bioprinting, further confirming the effectiveness of the proposed closed-loop framework in achieving the desired bead geometry.

</details>


### [252] [Battery-Aware Predictive Trajectory Planning and Control for Multirotors Under Disturbances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12188)
*Krishna Bhavithavya Kidambi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Battery-aware trajectory planning for multirotors that couples vehicle, motor, and battery dynamics to assess energy, actuator capability, and disturbances during planning; shows energy savings and improved tracking with a reduced-order battery model verified against a reference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address energy-constrained UAV operation under localized disturbances by integrating battery dynamics into trajectory planning, enabling energy-efficient and robust paths.

Method: Evaluate candidate trajectories via closed-loop vehicle–motor–battery propagation, incorporating disturbance-induced control demand, electrical energy, battery evolution, and terminal-voltage-dependent actuator capability. Uses a reduced-order battery model benchmarked against a Simscape reference. Tests in a 150 s, 640 m mission with three disturbance regions.

Result: Power-model NRMSE 0.64% against reference; cumulative-energy discrepancy <0.7%. In the mission, selected trajectory reduces electrical energy by 7.46% and position-tracking RMSE by ~72% vs disturbance-aware fixed-reference baseline. Ablations show battery-dependent terms nonbinding at nominal SOC but influential under depleted SOC; controller selection alters tradeoffs among tracking, energy, and actuator use.

Conclusion: Incorporating predicted closed-loop energetic and battery–actuator consequences into trajectory selection yields significant energy savings and tracking improvements under disturbances, with minimal impact when the battery is near nominal state.

Abstract: This paper presents a battery-aware predictive trajectory-planning and control framework for multirotors operating under spatially localized disturbances. Candidate trajectories are evaluated through closed-loop vehicle--motor--battery propagation, allowing disturbance-induced control demand, electrical energy, battery evolution, and terminal-voltage-dependent actuator capability to enter the planning process. % A reduced-order battery model is numerically benchmarked against an independently implemented Simscape equivalent-circuit reference, with a power NRMSE of $0.64\%$ and a cumulative-energy discrepancy below $0.7\%$. % In a $150$-s, $640$-m mission containing three disturbance regions, the selected trajectory reduces electrical energy consumption by $7.46\%$ and position-tracking RMSE by approximately $72\%$ relative to the disturbance-aware fixed-reference baseline. % Planner ablations show that battery-dependent terms are nonbinding at nominal SOC but alter the selected trajectory under a depleted-battery stress condition. % Execution with multiple feedback controllers further demonstrates that controller selection changes the tradeoff among tracking accuracy, energy consumption, and actuator utilization. % The results demonstrate the benefit of accounting for predicted closed-loop energetic and battery--actuator consequences during trajectory selection.

</details>


### [253] [An Automated Thickness Evaluation Procedure Using an Integrated Structured Light 3D Camera in a Robotic Bioprinting Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12206)
*Ehsan Zobeidi,Omid Rezayof,Farshid Alambeigi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Automated vision-based thickness measurement for bioprinted constructs using segmentation and geometry on RGB and structured-light 3D data; validated via simulation and experiments with high accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for robust, automated, quantitative measurement of BioPrinted Construct (BPC) thickness for complex geometries to improve maturation and integration; current literature lacks such automated methods.

Method: Image-based segmentation of BPCs from RGB images using a structured-light 3D camera. Geometry-based thickness measurement is performed on the fused data by combining the segmentation mask with robot forward kinematics and a 3D point cloud to compute thickness for complex-shaped BPCs. Evaluation included simulation (known thickness) and experimental deployment on a robotic bioprinting setup.

Result: Simulation showed mean absolute errors (MAE) between 0.025 mm and 0.057 mm at a 0.1 mm × 0.1 mm per-pixel spatial resolution. Experimental deployment on a robotic bioprinting system demonstrated accurate thickness measurements for complex patterns.

Conclusion: The approach provides a fully automated, accurate method for measuring BPC thickness with complex geometries, integrating vision, kinematics, and point-cloud data; suitable for quantitative assessment in bioprinting workflows and potentially adaptable to other tissue constructs.

Abstract: Bioprinting is emerging as a tissue engineering technique to replace common treatment methods for large scale injuries. While thickness of the BioPrinted Constructs (BPCs) have shown to be of importance in the cell maturation and integration, the literature lacks a robust, automated, and quantitative method for measuring these metrics. In this paper, we propose a fully automated vision-based method for measuring the thickness of the BPCs with complex geometries. Leveraging the point cloud and RGB images of a structured light 3D camera, our proposed method performs an image-based segmentation for delineating the BPCs from the RGB images, accompanied by novel geometry-based thickness measurement algorithms performed on the point cloud scans. These algorithms combine the segmentation mask with the robot's forward kinematics data and a 3D point cloud scan to precisely measure the aforementioned metrics for complex-shaped BPCs. The proposed method was evaluated in simulation and experimental studies. In simulation studies, the algorithms were used to measure the thickness of some virtually created BPCs with known thickness. The comparison between the measured and true thicknesses demonstrates the high accuracy of the proposed method, achieving mean absolute errors between 0.025 mm and 0.057 mm in simulation at a spatial resolution of 0.1 mm x 0.1 mm per pixel. Furthermore, we successfully deployed the algorithms on our robotic bioprinting setup utilizing a structure light 3D camera, where complex patterns were printed and the developed methods utilized to accurately measure the thickness of printed BPCs.

</details>


### [254] [Guardrailed Meta-Agent Loops: Stress-Testing Policy Pinning, Budget Bounds, and Crash Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12216)
*Qinzhen Ma,Jialin Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GuardrailLoop: a simulation-based testbed to audit self-improving agents by separately evaluating policy preservation, per- prefix compute accounting, and state recovery, exposing limitations of outcome-recovery as evidence of exactly-once execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-improving agents face an audit problem when a single controller can modify both behavior and the criteria by which it is judged. The work provides a bounded, testable boundary and evaluation protocol to decouple useful adaptation, state recovery, and repeated execution.

Method: Introduce GuardrailLoop with a hash-pinned policy, restricted machine-directed evolution within a code-owned feature catalog and bounded knobs, and three operational contracts (policy preservation, per-prefix compute accounting, and state recovery after crashes). Validation via a paired 50-seed 2x2 study and 240 crash injections to test recovery and trace preservation.

Result: In the 50-seed 2x2 study, round-stage growth improves target attainment by +1.00 and reduces target compute by -56.97 simulated GPU-hours (95% CI [-58.91,-54.70]); idle growth shows no utility. All 240 crash injections recover the defined outcome, but only 210 preserve the normalized trace; 30 pre-commit crashes repeat a planner call. Resource-drift, kill-switch, integrity, and output-guard checks meet specifications.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that outcome recovery alone does not ensure exactly-once execution. GuardrailLoop provides a calibrated deterministic testbed with an evaluation protocol that separates adaptation, recovery, and repeated execution, establishing conformance within this boundary rather than asserting real-world safety.

Abstract: Self-improving agent workflows create an audit problem when the same controller can change both its behavior and the conditions under which that behavior is judged. We present GuardrailLoop, a simulation-based testbed that makes three operational contracts jointly testable: preservation of human-defined policy, compute accounting at every recorded execution prefix, and recovery of a specified scientific state after crashes. A hash-pinned policy fixes goals, scope, evaluation identity, budget, and release conditions; machine-directed evolution is restricted to a code-owned feature catalog and bounded knobs. The contribution is an executable boundary and an evaluation protocol that separates useful adaptation, state recovery, and repeated execution. In a paired 50-seed 2 x 2 study, round-stage growth changes target attainment by +1.00 and restricted mean compute to target by -56.97 simulated GPU-hours (95% paired-bootstrap interval [-58.91,-54.70]); idle growth has zero measured utility effect. Across 240 enumerated crash injections, all runs recover the defined outcome, but only 210 preserve the normalized trace: 30 pre-commit crashes repeat a planner call. Resource-drift, kill-switch, integrity, and output-guard matrices satisfy their specified checks. These findings show why successful outcome recovery is insufficient evidence of exactly-once execution. They establish conformance within one calibrated deterministic testbed, rather than general safety or real-world self-improvement.

</details>


### [255] [Chain-SLAM: Globally Consistent Backend for Multi-Session LiDAR SLAM via Chained Loop Closure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12221)
*Zhiheng Li,Xinhao Liu,Juexiao Zhang,Yongqing Liang,Chen Feng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A scalable Chain-SLAM backend enabling online multi-session LiDAR map alignment with global consistency via a chained loop-closure mechanism over an adjacency graph, GNSS-assisted initialization, and unified factor-graph optimization; demonstrated improved trajectory accuracy and released code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the challenge of maintaining long-horizon geometric consistency when integrating LiDAR maps across multiple sessions, where drift accumulation and inter-session alignment are difficult to manage.

Method: Introduce a chained loop closure mechanism that propagates geometric constraints across inter-session keyframes via an adjacency graph; initialize inter-session alignment with GNSS-proximity place recognition; perform on-the-fly loop closures and joint optimization of loaded maps and newly acquired trajectories within a unified factor graph; maintain inter- and intra-session geometric consistency without dynamic object removal; ensure cross-platform robustness with minimal hyperparameter tuning.

Result: Experimental results show improved trajectory accuracy and robust multi-session integration on large-scale datasets; source code released with project site for reproducible research.

Conclusion: Chain-SLAM provides online, globally consistent multi-session LiDAR SLAM at large scale through efficient constraint propagation and unified optimization, with practical robustness and reproducibility.

Abstract: Maintaining consistency over long spatial and temporal horizons remains a fundamental challenge in large-scale LiDAR SLAM, particularly when integrating maps collected across multiple sessions. We present Chain-SLAM, a LiDAR SLAM backend enabling online multi-session map alignment and reuse with global consistency at large scale. We implement a chained loop closure mechanism that efficiently propagates geometric constraints across inter-session keyframes through an adjacency graph, enabling robust long-horizon consistency triggered by reliable short-horizon loop closures. The system initializes inter-session alignment with GNSS-proximity place recognition, then performs on-the-fly loop closure detections and joint optimization of loaded maps and newly acquired trajectories within a unified factor graph, maintaining both inter- and intra-session geometric consistency without dynamic object removal, and cross-platform robustness with minimal hyperparameter tuning. Experimental results show improved trajectory accuracy and robust multi-session integration on large-scale datasets. We release our source code to support reproducible research in large-scale multi-session LiDAR SLAM. Project site: https://ai4ce.github.io/Chain-SLAM/

</details>


### [256] [DIA: Denoising Intermediate Advantage for Diffusion Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12245)
*Arjun Sohal,Yuchi Zhao,Miroslav Bogdanovic,Alan Aspuru-Guzik*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DIA introduces a denoising intermediate value function to assign step-wise credit in diffusion-policy fine-tuning, enabling a denoising-level advantage per step and combining it with PPO's environment-level advantage; this improves final performance, sample efficiency, and exploration beyond pretrained behavior across multiple robotic manipulation benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion-based policies optimized via behavior cloning suffer from limited data coverage; standard policy-gradient fine-tuning assigns uniform environment-level credit to all denoising steps, hindering learning about which intermediate denoising decisions matter for return.

Method: Propose Denoising Intermediate Advantage (DIA): learn a value function over partially denoised actions to compute a denoising-level advantage for each step in the denoising chain, and combine this inner credit with the standard environment-level PPO advantage during refinement of diffusion policies.

Result: Across Robomimic, FurnitureBench, Franka Kitchen, and D3IL, DIA consistently improves final performance over existing diffusion-policy fine-tuning methods; it also accelerates reaching successful states and enables larger shifts from the pretrained distribution to discover more effective task-level strategies and subtask sequences that baseline methods miss.

Conclusion: Incorporating step-wise, denoising-level credit via a value function over partially denoised actions enhances diffusion-policy fine-tuning, yielding better performance, faster success, and greater exploration across diverse robotic manipulation benchmarks.

Abstract: Diffusion-based robot policies have become widely used in robotic manipulation, where they are typically trained with behavior cloning. However, policies trained purely from demonstrations are limited by the quality and coverage of the available data. Reinforcement learning can further improve the performance of these pretrained policies through interaction. A common approach is to use policy-gradient methods that formulate diffusion-policy fine-tuning as an outer environment MDP together with an inner denoising MDP. However, existing methods typically assign the same environment-level credit to all denoising steps used to construct an action chunk, without distinguishing which intermediate decisions contributed most to the final return. We introduce Denoising Intermediate Advantage (DIA), a policy-gradient method that learns a value function over partially denoised actions and uses it to construct a denoising- level advantage for each step of the generative process. DIA com- bines this inner credit signal with the standard environment-level PPO advantage, providing state-dependent credit throughout the denoising chain. Across Robomimic, FurnitureBench, Franka Kitchen, and D3IL, DIA consistently improves final performance over existing diffusion-policy fine-tuning methods. Beyond final reward, DIA reaches successful states more efficiently and can shift farther from the pretrained behavior distribution, enabling it to discover more effective and efficient task-level strategies and subtask sequences that baseline methods fail to reach.

</details>


### [257] [Trajectory Bundle Method in SE(3) for Black-Box Fixed-Wing Aircraft Trajectory Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12248)
*Matthew D. Osburn,Cameron K. Peterson,John L. Salmon*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Derivative-free trajectory planning on SE(3) using the Trajectory Bundle Method (TBM) with Lie-algebra bundles and exponential/log maps; provides interpolation error bounds and demonstrates a nontrivial fixed-wing maneuver with black-box dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Planar and rigid-body trajectories on SE(3) under black-box dynamics; existing TBM assumes Euclidean space and differentiable dynamics, which is inadequate for non-Euclidean geometry and non-derivative models.

Method: Construct trajectory bundles in the Lie algebra se(3); propagate through nonlinear rigid-body dynamics using exponential and logarithmic maps; perform derivative-free planning; extend TBM from Euclidean space to SE(3) and derive corresponding error bounds.

Result: In Euclidean space, TBM interpolation error is quadratically bounded by bundle diameter. Extends to SE(3) with a bound that also depends on the local Lipschitz constant of the Log map. Numerical experiments validate the bounds; demonstrate SE(3) TBM by optimizing a complex, collision-free fixed-wing maneuver through a rotated aperture without explicit models or derivatives of dynamics, aerodynamics, or collision model.

Conclusion: SE(3) TBM provides a practical, derivative-free framework for planning non-Euclidean trajectories on SE(3) with black-box dynamics, enabling aggressive maneuvers and complex collision-aware planning.

Abstract: Dynamically feasible trajectory optimization for rigid-body systems is naturally formulated on the special Euclidean group SE(3) but is challenging when dynamics are available only as black-box computations without derivatives. This paper formulates the Trajectory Bundle Method (TBM) for motion planning implicitly on SE(3). Bundles are constructed in the Lie algebra and propagated through nonlinear rigid-body dynamics using exponential and logarithmic maps, enabling derivative-free planning of non-Euclidean trajectories. We show that Euclidean TBM interpolation error is bounded quadratically by bundle diameter and extend this result to SE(3), where the bound additionally depends on a local Lipschitz constant of the Log map. Numerical experiments corroborate these bounds. Finally, we demonstrate SE(3) TBM by optimizing an acrobatic, collision-free fixed-wing maneuver through a rotated aperture without explicit models or derivatives of the vehicle dynamics, aerodynamics, or collision model.

</details>


### [258] [Pneumatic neurons for soft robots enable inflate-and-fire networks for rhythmic motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12258)
*Dongting Li,Michael Tolley,Nick Gravish*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A soft-robot control architecture uses Pneuron modules that couple energy conversion, logic, and actuation in a single unit to create distributed, self-excited oscillatory networks without centralized electronics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable electronics-free, environment-adaptive control in soft robots by embedding computation and actuation in material-level modules inspired by neural circuits.

Method: Introduce Pneuron: a soft actuator module containing a low-boiling-point fluid, a heater, and a mechanical switch. Boiling the LBF inflates the module and triggers excitation/inhibition of neighboring modules (inflate-and-fire). Networks of Pneur-ons with excitatory-inhibitory rings produce stable, sequential oscillations; the oscillation frequency depends on material dynamics and environmental conditions. Dynamical modeling yields a dimensionless bifurcation diagram governing behavior.

Result: Networks sustain oscillations under mechanical load and thermal variations; frequency emerges from material dynamics and environment; these oscillations can drive oscillatory locomotion in soft robots; logic and actuation are encoded in material units, enabling electronics-controller-free adaptation.

Conclusion: Material-level encoding of logic and actuation offers a new path for adaptive, electronics-free soft robots, enabling distributed, environment-responsive control through physical dynamics.

Abstract: Animals coordinate their movements through distributed neural circuits, but soft robots still typically depend on external, centralized electronics for control. Building soft robots that operate without centralized electronic controllers while remaining responsive to their environment remains a frontier challenge in soft robotics. In this work we introduce a soft-robot control architecture inspired by leaky integrate-and-fire models of biological neural circuits. The Pneumatic neuron (Pneu-ron) is a soft actuator that unifies energy conversion, logic, and actuation in one component. Each module combines a low-boiling-point fluid (LBF), a heater, and a mechanical switch into a self-excitable unit. Boiling the LBF inflates the module and triggers excitation and inhibition of adjacent modules in a process we call "inflate-and-fire". When interconnected into excitatory-inhibitory rings, Pneu-rons generate stable, sequential oscillations whose frequency emerges from the material dynamics and environmental conditions. By harnessing the inflation of Pneu-rons for actuation these networks can drive oscillatory locomotion of soft robots. Pneu-ron networks sustain oscillation under mechanical load and thermal variations, adapting through material physics rather than computation. Dynamical modeling of these networks reveals a dimensionless bifurcation diagram that dictates the network's oscillatory behavior. Encoding logic and actuation into material-level modules presents a new avenue for adaptive, electronics controller-free, soft robots.

</details>


### [259] [AnchorVLN: Geometry-Anchored Vision-Language Grounding Reasoning for Open-Vocabulary Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12285)
*Long Giang Vu,Chengkai Yao,Yuxin Liu,FNU Aryan,Rajath Chandrashekar Aralikatti*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AnchorVLN is an open-vocabulary vision-language navigation system that separates semantic grounding from metric geometry by using a VLM for semantics and a geometry module for metrics, implemented as a Model Context Protocol server (EMBODIED-NAV-MCP) driven by a VLM via a compact toolset. It achieves 64.4% instruction-following on CMU V-N Challenge tasks and improves object-reference accuracy by geometric anchoring (median center error 2.48 m vs 3.37 m; 10/45 questions cleared vs 0).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLN in unseen indoor environments requires reliable open-vocabulary grounding plus metric reasoning (range, bearing, spatial relations). Existing pipelines either hardcode geometry or require downstream changes for different robots or vocabularies. The study aims to keep a fixed autonomy stack while leveraging open-vocabulary models for semantics and a geometry component for metric outputs.

Method: An open-vocabulary VLN system built on a simple rule: VLM provides semantics while geometry provides metrics. Realized as EMBODIED-NAV-MCP server driven by a VLM agent through a compact set of callable tools; the schema enforces a semantic–geometry boundary so no tool accepts metres or radians, preserving the downstream autonomy stack. Evaluation on CMU VLN Challenge 2026 with 30 instruction-following questions over 15 scenes and 45 object-reference questions.

Result: Full system achieves 64.4% instruction-following; performance drops by 13.3 percentage points without controller modeling (t = 2.77). On object-reference, geometric anchoring clears overlap threshold on 10/45 questions (vs 0/45 for direct coordinate estimation) and reduces median center error from 3.37 m to 2.48 m.

Conclusion: Separating semantics and geometry via a Model Context Protocol allows robust open-vocabulary VLN without modifying the control stack. Geometric anchoring improves metric reasoning and object-localization accuracy, highlighting the value of a boundaries-driven integration of VLMs with metric grounding.

Abstract: Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) in unseen indoor environments is useful in real-world robotics, where an agent must follow natural-language instructions, locate objects, and answer spatial questions without a pre-built map or fixed object vocabulary. Multimodal vision-language models (VLMs) provide strong open-vocabulary grounding and zero-shot reasoning, but struggle to emit reliable metric quantities such as range, bearing, and comparative spatial relations directly from images. Existing approaches address this by folding geometry into hand-engineered pipelines or asking models to output waypoints, requiring changes to the control stack for different robots, tasks, or vocabularies. We introduce AnchorVLN, an open-vocabulary VLN system built on a simple rule: the VLM proposes semantics; geometry decides metrics. It is realised as EMBODIED-NAV-MCP, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server driven by a VLM agent through a compact set of callable tools. Since no tool accepts distance in metres or bearing in radians, the schema enforces the semantic-geometry boundary without modifying the downstream autonomy stack. We benchmark both tasks of the CMU Vision-Language Navigation Challenge 2026: 30 instruction-following questions over 15 scenes and a frozen 45-question object-reference set. The full system achieves 64.4 percent on instruction following, dropping by 13.3 percentage points without controller modeling (t = 2.77). On object reference, geometric anchoring clears the challenge overlap threshold on 10 of 45 questions, versus 0 of 45 for direct coordinate estimation, reducing median center error from 3.37 m to 2.48 m.

</details>


### [260] [Mission Performance: Automatic and Adaptive Race Pace Progression for Autonomous Racing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12292)
*Giovanni Lambertini,Matteo Pini,Nicola Musiu,Ayoub Raji,Francesco Iacovacci,Marko Bertogna*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A Mission Performance module for autonomous racing that adaptively tunes sector-wise performance (longitudinal, lateral, combined) to speed up lap times while maintaining safety, using predefined warm-up and continuous safety monitoring; demonstrated on EAV-25 in A2RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitigate the risks of real-time grip estimation in critical modules and avoid altering vehicle model parameters; enable faster lap-time progression by adapting target performance instead of model changes.

Method: Pre-defined tire-warmup progressions; sector-based safety and vehicle dynamics monitoring; adaptive scaling of target performance for each sector (longitudinal, lateral, combined); converges to the maximum allowed value without changing the vehicle model.

Result: Empirically demonstrated on the EAV-25, a fully autonomous Dallara Superformula, at Yas Marina Circuit during the Abu Dhabi Autonomous Racing League (A2RL) Season 2, showing effectiveness of the approach.

Conclusion: An adaptive, safety-conscious mechanism that modulates sector-wise performance to accelerate lap times without relying on real-time grip estimation, with demonstrated applicability to autonomous racing stacks.

Abstract: In this paper, we describe the Mission Performance module implemented for a fully autonomous racing car to automatically manage the longitudinal, lateral, and combined performances, aiming to speedup the laptime progression while assuring safety. Motivated by the difficulty and risks of applying the real-time estimation of the grip to critical modules like the motion planner and controller, the Mission Performance guides these modules adapting their target performance instead of changing the vehicle model parameters. The module is formed by pre-defined progressions to warm up the tires at the beginning of a run. Then, the system continuously monitors safety and vehicle dynamics metrics on a per-sector basis to adaptively reduce, maintain, or increase the performance levels for each sector, progressively converging toward the maximum allowed value. The solution's effectiveness is demonstrated on the EAV-25, a fully autonomous Dallara Superformula, at the Yas Marina Circuit during the Abu Dhabi Autonomous Racing League (A2RL) Season 2.

</details>


### [261] [DATAFARM: Distribution-Aligned Task and Motion Planning for Fine-Tuning Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12316)
*Samrat Sahoo,Yixuan Huang,Tom Silver*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DATAFARM aligns Task and Motion Planning (TAMP) trajectories with the VLA pretraining data distribution to fine-tune a robot foundation model, yielding substantial gains over raw TAMP and approaching human teleoperation; preserves pretrained performance on deformable-object tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collecting high-quality robot data is costly, and planner-generated trajectories from TAMP may have a distribution mismatch with the data used to pretrain vision-language-action (VLA) models, limiting their effectiveness for fine-tuning.

Method: DATAFARM inserts the pretraining distribution into TAMP trajectory generation, aligning generated trajectories with the pretraining data in robot joint configurations, motion style, and temporal execution profiles.

Result: DATAFARM achieves 56.7% average success across three tabletop manipulation tasks and a cloth-folding task (vs 8.3% for raw TAMP; near teleoperation at 61.7%). On deformable object manipulation, the fine-tuned model retains 85% success compared with 90% for the pretrained model.

Conclusion: Aligning planner-generated demonstrations with the pretraining distribution enables TAMP to be an effective data source for fine-tuning VLA robot models, broadening the applicability of TAMP-generated data.

Abstract: Collecting high-quality robot data remains a fundamental challenge for training robot foundation models. Task and motion planning (TAMP) offers a scalable way to generate demonstrations, but our experiments show that raw TAMP trajectories provide surprisingly little benefit when used to fine-tune pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) models, despite successfully solving the target tasks. We hypothesize that this failure arises from a behavioral distribution mismatch between planner-generated trajectories and the data used to pretrain the VLA. To address this mismatch, we introduce DATAFARM: Distribution-Aligned Task And motion planning for Fine-tuning A Robot foundation Model, an approach that incorporates the pretraining distribution directly into TAMP trajectory generation. DATAFARM aligns generated trajectories with the pretraining data in robot joint configurations, motion style, and temporal execution profiles. We evaluate DATAFARM on three tabletop manipulation tasks that TAMP can perform and a cloth-folding task beyond the capability of TAMP. DATAFARM achieves an average success rate of 56.7%, substantially outperforming raw TAMP (8.3%) while approaching human teleoperation (61.7%). On Deformable Object Manipulation, which is outside the fine-tuning distribution, the fine-tuned model retains 85% success, compared with 90% for the pretrained model. These results show that aligning planner-generated demonstrations with the pretraining distribution can make TAMP an effective source of data for VLA fine-tuning. Website and code: https://prpl-group.com/datafarm/

</details>


### [262] [DWMP: Leveraging Dual World Models for Humanoid Obstacle Traversal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12347)
*Rongjun Jin,Jianming Ma,Yue Gao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DWMP combines a Koopman-based linearized dynamics model for proprioception with an RSSM-based visual world model to provide a dual, complementary latent representation for humanoid obstacle traversal, improving performance and enabling real-world deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Proprioceptive signals are low-dimensional but governed by nonlinear robot dynamics, while egocentric vision is high-dimensional, noisy, and redundant. Explicitly leveraging their distinct characteristics should improve obstacle-aware control.

Method: Develop two parallel world models: (1) Koopman-based dynamics model that lifts proprioception into a latent space with approximately linear evolution; (2) RSSM-based visual model that compresses egocentric depth into compact stochastic states encoding obstacle geometry. The policy (student) fuses these latent representations to generate actions.

Result: DWMP improves obstacle traversal performance over baselines in both simulation and real hardware (Unitree G1) and demonstrates deployment feasibility under randomized obstacle layouts.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling and fusing complementary latent representations for proprioception and vision yields better control of humanoid obstacle traversal and enhances sim-to-real transferability.

Abstract: Humanoid robots must traverse cluttered obstacle fields using onboard proprioceptive and visual observations, yet existing methods usually process multimodal observations without explicitly considering their different characteristics: proprioceptive observations are low-dimensional but governed by highly nonlinear robot dynamics, while egocentric visual observations are high-dimensional, noisy, and redundant. We propose DWMP (Dual World Model Policy), a framework that provides the actor with separate but complementary world-model representations for humanoid obstacle traversal. A Koopman-based dynamics world model lifts proprioceptive observations into a latent space where their temporal evolution is approximately linear, making the dynamics features easier for the actor to learn from. An RSSM-based visual world model compresses egocentric depth observations into compact stochastic states while preserving obstacle-related geometry. The student policy receives the fused latent representation for action generation, combining linearized proprioceptive dynamics with compressed visual perception. Experiments in simulation and on a Unitree G1 humanoid robot show that DWMP improves obstacle traversal performance over baselines and supports real-world deployment under randomized obstacle layouts.

</details>


### [263] [A Deployable Architecture for Robot-Mediated Tasks (DART): Evaluation in Socially Assistive Robot-Guided Cognitive Behavioral Therapy Exercises](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12349)
*Mina Kian,Lydia Ignatova,Jiong Wang,Ji Min Lee,Jiancheng Li,Qianwei Guo,Emily Weiss,Amy O'Connell,Kaitlin Zareno,Jiani Li,Reyna Patel,Leyaa George,Minyu Huang,Justin Yang,Maja J. Matarić*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DART enables cloud-enabled extensions to low-cost SARs via a web/cloud stack, enabling content, input, remote computation, and persistence; evaluated in a university-student CBT task with a Blossom robot across in-lab and six-week in-home deployments, showing high usability and short-term anxiety/stress reductions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hardware and cost constraints limit interaction complexity and longitudinal real-world deployments of socially assistive robots; a deployable architecture is needed to synergize cloud/web capabilities with robot embodiment.

Method: Design and instantiate DART in a full-stack HRI system; participatory design process; in-lab study (n=103) and six-week home deployment (n=4) using CBT homework exercises; measures include stress, state anxiety, negative affect, and System Usability Scale (SUS); qualitative feedback collected.

Result: Significant within-session reductions in stress, state anxiety, and negative affect in-lab; SUS 78.89 (lab) and 87.5 (home) indicating high usability; positive qualitative feedback; participants prioritized speech input, visual presentation, and web-robot synchronization.

Conclusion: DART is an effective architecture for extending low-cost SAR capabilities to perform both short-term interactions and longitudinal real-world deployments, validated by lab and home studies.

Abstract: Socially assistive robots (SARs) can support structured health and well-being interventions, but hardware and cost constraints limit interaction complexity and longitudinal real-world deployments. We present DART: Deployable Architecture for Robot-Mediated Tasks, an architecture that extends SARs through a web application and cloud infrastructure, enabling visual content, user input, remote computation, and persistent data storage synergistically with the robot's physical embodiment, speech, and movement. We evaluated DART by instantiating it in an interatively-developed full-stack HRI system for helping university students with elevated generalized anxiety to complete cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT) homework exercises. The resulting system, which used the low-cost open-source Blossom robot platform, was refined and evaluated through a participatory design process and multiple user studies, and finally evaluated in an in-lab study with 103 participants, and then a six-week in-home deployment with four participants. In the in-lab evaluation, participants showed significant within-session reductions in stress, state anxiety, and negative affect, and gave the platform a mean System Usability Scale score of 78.89. In the home deployment, the mean System Usability Scale score was 87.5, with positive qualitative feedback on usability. Participants across both groups identified speech input, visual presentation, and web-robot synchronization as priorities for improvement. These findings validate DART as an effective architecture for extending the capabilities of a low-cost SAR in both in-lab single-session and in real-world longitudinal deployments.

</details>


### [264] [READ: Learning Risk-Informed Fields for End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12371)
*Zhiyuan Liu,Yuanxin Tian,Zehong Ke,Jinhao Li,Hao Cheng,Zhenhua Xu,Wenhao Yu,Jianqiang Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: READ learns a differentiable, continuous spatiotemporal risk field that aligns planned trajectories with low-risk regions, providing an explicit, adaptable safety representation for autonomous-vehicle planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current planners rely on implicit scene encodings or fixed safety fields; there is a need for an explicit, adaptable risk map that can be queried along trajectory candidates for interpretability and refinement.

Method: Introduce READ: a framework that constructs a continuous spatiotemporal risk field from geometric and behavioral constraints; enables differentiable queries along trajectories; can be plugged into end-to-end planners and Vision-Language-Action models; trained/evaluated on NAVSIM (and NAVSIM v2).

Result: NAVSIM experiments show consistent gains across matched backbones; strong performance in VLA settings; competitive results on NAVSIM v2, validating learned spatial risk as explicit and adaptable.

Conclusion: Learned spatial risk serves as an explicit, adaptable bridge between scene understanding and action selection, enabling safer planning and better inspectability/refinement, with broad compatibility across planning architectures.

Abstract: Autonomous driving requires more than recognizing what is present in a scene: a planner must determine how road structure, surrounding agents, and their motion states should influence a future maneuver. Existing learning-based planners can capture these influences through latent scene features and trajectory decoders, but the relationship between environmental factors and candidate actions often remains implicit. This limits the ability to inspect, diagnose, or refine how scene context affects the safety of a predicted trajectory. Classical safety fields provide an explicit spatial representation of this relationship, but their risk shapes and relative weights are prescribed in advance and do not adapt to each scene. We introduce READ, a framework that learns an explicit, planning-aligned risk representation from complementary geometric and behavioral constraints. READ instantiates this representation as a continuous spatiotemporal field, enabling differentiable queries along candidate trajectories. The learned field connects scene understanding with action selection by encouraging predicted trajectories to align with low-risk regions, while retaining a differentiable interface for trajectory evaluation and refinement. READ integrates with both end-to-end planners and Vision-Language-Action models. Experiments on NAVSIM show consistent gains across matched end-to-end backbones and strong performance in a VLA setting; READ also achieves competitive results on NAVSIM v2. These results establish learned spatial risk as an explicit, adaptable representation for safe planning.

</details>


### [265] [Understanding Whole-Body Robot Teleoperation Strategies Under Diverse Task Objectives and Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12384)
*Tsung-Chi Lin,Juo-Tung Chen,Chien-Ming Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Hybrid free-form and constrained control for whole-body teleoperation of the TIAGo robot, with a user study showing coordinated control boosts efficiency and lowers the chance of hitting joint limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable safe, efficient whole-body teleoperation by tightly coordinating perception, manipulation, and navigation, and understand user control strategies under task constraints.

Method: Develop a hybrid control framework that merges free-form and constraint-based control, implement on the TIAGo mobile manipulator, and conduct a user study under varying task constraints (limited time, low error tolerance) to examine strategies and performance.

Result: Coordinated whole-body control improves task efficiency and reduces risk of reaching joint limits; the study reveals how users adapt their strategies under different constraints.

Conclusion: Findings inform design principles for future whole-body teleoperation systems, highlighting the importance of coordinated perception, manipulation, and navigation and suggesting directions for interface and control framework improvements under time and accuracy constraints.

Abstract: This work investigates the control strategies of complex whole-body robot teleoperation that coordinate active perception, bimanual manipulation, and navigation. We developed a hybrid control framework, combining the free-form and constrained control, for the whole-body teleoperation of the TIAGo mobile manipulator. We conducted a user study to explore people's control strategies under different task constraints such as limited time and low tolerance of errors. Our results highlight the effective use of coordinated control in improving task efficiency and reducing the risk of reaching individual joint limits. We discuss our results and their implications for designing future whole-body robot teleoperation systems.

</details>


### [266] [FoldNet++: a Large-Scale Synthetic Dataset for Robotic T-Shirt Folding and Unfolding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12433)
*Yuxing Chen,Zhiyuan Wei,Bowen Xiao,Zhizheng Zhang,He Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Large-scale synthetic dataset for robotic T-shirt folding/unfolding enabling cross-embodiment visuomotor policies; demonstrates strong sim-to-real transfer (over 90% success) on unseen garments and initial configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deformable garment manipulation is highly challenging due to strong variability in garment appearance and pose; existing datasets/policies lack generalization across embodiments and real-world variations. A large, diverse, labeled synthetic dataset can facilitate training robust policies that transfer to real robots.

Method: Extend FoldNet to generate physically simulatable T-shirts with diverse appearances. Create 6 robotic embodiments, 1K garments, 1K environmental assets, and 120K episodes with rich annotations. Derive semantic keypoints, and generate demonstration data via a unified rule-based framework. Train visuomotor policies end-to-end from synthetic demonstrations.

Result: Policies trained exclusively on the synthetic data achieve >90% end-to-end task success when deployed to unseen real-world environments and unseen T-shirts from arbitrary initial configurations.

Conclusion: The dataset and pipeline enable robust cross-embodiment garment manipulation with strong sim-to-real transfer; the approach supports broad policy learning for T-shirt folding/unfolding and potentially other deformable-object tasks.

Abstract: Due to the highly deformable nature of garments, training a generalizable policy for robotic T-shirt folding and unfolding remains a significant challenge. In this work, we present a large-scale synthetic dataset for robotic T-shirt folding and unfolding, covering 6 robotic embodiments, 1K T-shirts, 1K environmental assets, and 120K episodes with rich annotations, which can be used to train a wide range of manipulation policies. We first follow the FoldNet pipeline to generate a large-scale dataset of physically simulatable T-shirts with diverse appearances and annotated semantic keypoints. Based on these semantic keypoints, we then generate manipulation demonstrations for different robotic embodiments through a unified rule-based framework. We use these demonstrations to train visuomotor policies, and experimental results demonstrate that models trained solely on our synthetic data can achieve over 90\% end-to-end task success rates when directly deployed to unseen real-world environments and previously unseen T-shirts from arbitrary initial configurations. Project URL: https://pku-epic.github.io/FoldNetXX/.

</details>


### [267] [IMPLY: Physically Anchored Consistency for World-Model Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12441)
*Aman Mehta,Riya Baviskar*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: IMPLY shows that evaluating world-models via self-consistency among futures is insufficient for identifying object-level physics; anchoring rollouts to calibration evidence about the object enables near-oracle model selection and accurate per-object grounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current vetting of world-action models relies on cross-rollout consistency, but this can be satisfied by ignoring the object entirely. The paper argues that anchoring evaluations to observed calibration pushes is necessary to reveal whether a model has internalized object-specific physics.

Method: IMPLY inverts a simulator to extract the implied physics from rollouts and scores how well a single object explains all rollouts. Evaluation anchors rollouts to two calibration pushes observed by the model, then compares self-consistency versus anchored evidence across synthetic and real models (V-JEPA 2-AC adapted to scene).

Result: Self-consistency alone yields partial discrimination: in controlled settings, a model that ignores the object can still score perfectly but anchoring reveals this. On real models, per-object correlation with truth is 0.91 when using the correct object, but 0.05 with a wrong object. Self-consistency remains around chance (52%), while anchored disagreement achieves 73% correct and correlates 0.92–0.99 with rollouts’ error. The method approaches an oracle (within 0.003) for selecting among rollout sets.

Conclusion: Consistency checks must be anchored to empirical evidence to distinguish correct object representations; self-consistency can be fooled by models that neither observe nor model the object. Anchored evaluation provides robust discrimination and aligns with the true physics in the rollouts.

Abstract: A world model asked what happens if an object is pushed at several speeds produces several futures. If the model has the object in mind, those futures agree about it: each implies the same mass and friction. The consistency checks now used to vet world-action models ask whether a model's futures agree with each other, and none of them knows any physics. We show that this is not enough, and what to do instead. IMPLY reads the physics each rollout implies by inverting a simulator and scores a set of rollouts by how well one object explains all of them, anchored to two calibration pushes the model has observed. In a controlled setting, self-consistency gives a perfect score to a model that ignores the object and always predicts a typical push; anchoring exposes it (AUROC 0.70 versus 1.00). On a real model, V-JEPA 2-AC adapted to the scene, the same thing happens. Given its own calibration pushes the model tracks the object (per-object correlation with the truth 0.91); given another object's, it does not (0.05). Self-consistency cannot tell these apart, preferring the right evidence on 52% of objects, chance level, while anchored disagreement prefers it on 73% and correlates 0.92-0.99 with the rollouts' error. Used to choose among candidate rollout sets, it comes within 0.003 of an oracle that sees the truth. A model that has internalised the wrong object is exactly as self-consistent as one that has internalised the right one; consistency has to be anchored to evidence.

</details>


### [268] [PATH: Continuous Target Sensing among Autonomous Cooperative Drones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12456)
*Heegyeong Kim,Alice James,Avishkar Seth,Endrowednes Kuantama,Jane Williamson,Yimeng Feng,Richard Han*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PATH enables geometry-assisted handoff of target tracking between two UAVs by reconstructing a 3D target on the sender, projecting into the receiver via pose estimation, and validating via a cross-view handshake, achieving high accuracy with low bandwidth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuous tracking with limited flight endurance requires cooperative handoffs between UAVs; existing global localization or appearance-based methods struggle with viewpoint, scale, and appearance ambiguities.

Method: Sender builds a metric 3D target model from RGB-D data; receiver estimates its relative pose using fiducial observations and projects the target point into its image as a spatial prior; the receiver proposes a candidate, which is verified by a Mutual Agreement Handshake before transferring tracking responsibility; operation is real-world, sensor-error analysis identifies pose uncertainty as main contributor; communication is compact (<16 kB/s at 60 Hz).

Result: Mean relative-position error: 0.047 m; mean target-position error: 0.030 m; frame-level receiver target acquisition accuracy under ambiguity: 96.0% with 2.0% false positives and 2.0% false negatives; pipeline runs at video rate; relative-pose uncertainty is the dominant source of projection error.

Conclusion: A lightweight, geometry-assisted handoff framework is feasible on resource-constrained UAVs, enabling reliable cross-view target handoff with low communication overhead and identifying pose estimation as the key accuracy limiter.

Abstract: Continuous target sensing by uncrewed aerial vehicles (UAVs) is constrained by limited flight endurance, motivating the transfer of tracking responsibility between cooperating UAVs. Such a handoff requires the receiver to identify the same physical target currently tracked by the sender despite differences in viewpoint, scale, and target appearance. Existing approaches based on global target localization or appearance-based cross-view association are limited by positioning uncertainty or ambiguous visual features. This paper presents Perspective Alignment \& Tracking Handoff (\textbf{PATH}), a platform-agnostic, geometry-assisted sensing and verification framework for target handoff between two moving UAVs. The sender reconstructs the tracked target as a metric 3D point using RGB-D sensing, while the receiver estimates its relative pose from a fiducial observation and projects the transmitted target point into its own image as a spatial prior for target acquisition. The receiver-generated candidate is then returned to the sender and verified through a cross-view Mutual Agreement Handshake before tracking responsibility is transferred. Real-world UAV experiments show mean relative-position and target-position errors of 0.047~m and 0.030~m, respectively. Under visually ambiguous conditions, PATH achieves 96.0\% frame-level receiver-side target acquisition accuracy, with 2.0\% false-positive and 2.0\% false-negative rates. A sensor-error sensitivity analysis shows that relative-pose uncertainty is the dominant contributor to receiver-view projection error. The implementation operates at video rate with compact inter-UAV communication below 16~kB/s at 60~Hz, demonstrating the feasibility of lightweight geometry-assisted target handoff on resource-constrained UAV platforms.

</details>


### [269] [ArtManip: Category-Level Articulated In-Hand Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12498)
*Yang Yang,Tengyu Liu,Puhao Li,Zeyuan Chen,Yuyang Li,Xingwan Wang,Yingying Wu,Zhaopeng Cui,Siyuan Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ArtManip enables category-level articulated in-hand manipulation across object instances and initial grasps, using automated object generation and a two-stage training pipeline with articulation physics randomization, reward curriculum, and latent distillation; it achieves sim-to-real zero-shot transfer on 12 real objects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tackle two core bottlenecks in dexterous in-hand manipulation: (1) coupling internal object DOF control with maintaining stability on a free-floating base, and (2) the need for diverse, scalable object models and functional grasps for generalization.

Method: Propose ArtManip, the first category-level articulated in-hand manipulation method. It includes (a) an automated pipeline to procedurally generate diverse articulated objects and synthesize task-oriented functional grasps for initial configurations, and (b) a robust two-stage policy training strategy incorporating articulation physics randomization, reward curriculum, and latent representation distillation to handle complex contact and joint dynamics during deployment.

Result: Extensive experiments across four object categories show generalization to unseen instances and varied configurations in simulation, with zero-shot transfer to 12 real-world objects featuring diverse shapes and joint mechanics.

Conclusion: ArtManip demonstrates category-level generalization for articulated in-hand manipulation and effective sim-to-real transfer, addressing key bottlenecks and enabling robust policies across varied object instances and initial grasps.

Abstract: Category-level in-hand manipulation of articulated objects is a formidable yet underexplored challenge for dexterous robotic hands. This difficulty stems from two core bottlenecks: first, controlling an object's internal degrees of freedom is tightly coupled with maintaining grasp stability on a free-floating base; second, acquiring diverse object models and functional grasps at scale is highly labor-intensive, yet vital for generalization given the system's sensitivity to initial configurations. In this work, we present ArtManip, the first category-level articulated in-hand manipulation method that generalizes across object instances and diverse initial grasps. For initial configuration construction, we develop an automated pipeline that procedurally generates diverse articulated objects and synthesizes task-oriented functional grasps. For policy learning, we propose a robust two-stage training strategy that incorporates articulation physics randomization, reward curriculum, and latent representation distillation to handle complex contact and joint dynamics during deployment. Extensive experiments across four object categories demonstrate that our policy generalizes to unseen instances and varied configurations in simulation, and achieves zero-shot transfer to 12 real-world objects featuring diverse shapes and joint mechanics.

</details>


### [270] [Communication-Constrained Multi-Robot Exploration With Adaptive Communication Windows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12502)
*Ben Rossano,Jaein Lim,Jonathan P. How*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MACE enables decentralized, communication-aware exploration by treating rendezvous decisions as a Vehicle Orienteering Problem variant, enabling more frequent communication with less detour and up to 23% faster exploration in simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Intermittent communication in multi-robot exploration can cause inefficiencies; a decentralized policy is needed to balance the benefits of sharing information against the travel cost to establish communication.

Method: Robots schedule communication windows, estimate the cost to reach communication locations, and solve a variant of the Vehicle Orienteering Problem to select routes that maximize exploration along the way while establishing communication. The approach is decentralized and avoids fixed rendezvous strategies.

Result: Simulation studies across varied environments show up to 23% reduction in total exploration time compared with existing communication-constrained strategies; the method increases communication frequency relative to opportunistic strategies while reducing unnecessary travel.

Conclusion: Active, communication-aware route planning improves exploration efficiency in multi-robot teams. The VOProblem-based decision framework enables scalable, decentralized coordination and may generalize to other communication-constrained tasks.

Abstract: Exploring unknown environments with multi-robot teams can improve efficiency by allowing robots to explore in parallel. However, realizing these gains requires effective information sharing. When communication is intermittent, robots must balance the benefits of sharing information against the cost of diverting from exploration to establish communication. This paper introduces MACE, a decentralized exploration framework that actively evaluates whether establishing communication is worthwhile. At scheduled communication windows, robots estimate the cost of reaching previously identified communication locations. By formulating this decision as a variant of the Vehicle Orienteering Problem, robots evaluate routes based on the travel required to establish communication and the exploration that can be completed along the way. This approach enables robots to communicate more frequently than under purely opportunistic strategies while reducing the unnecessary travel associated with fixed rendezvous strategies. Across a set of simulated environments with varying size and geometry, we demonstrate that MACE reduces the total exploration time by up to 23% compared to existing communication-constrained exploration strategies.

</details>


### [271] [Autonomous Precision Milling of Biological Structures via Generic Anatomical Priors and Active Boundary Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12530)
*Enduo Zhao,Xiaofeng Lin,Yifan Wang,Yuhan Song,Weihan Li,Saul Alexis Heredia Perez,Kanako Harada*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Uncertainty-aware autonomous milling framework that combines generic anatomical priors with active boundary perception to achieve precise, autonomous milling of biological structures, validated on biological surrogates and in vivo mouse cranial window models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Incomplete knowledge of target geometry, local material thickness, and internal boundaries; static models cannot determine boundary status during execution; repeated target-specific imaging is not scalable; need a scalable, autonomous solution.

Method: Use a generic anatomical prior for global guidance, transformed via semantic-guided registration and hybrid vision-force calibration into robot-executable guidance. As milling approaches uncertain boundaries, the robot probes the structure and uses relative stiffness changes to infer boundary status. A state-adaptive controller switches between active perception and spatially selective incremental refinement, cycling until completion.

Result: Hierarchical experiments on biological surrogates and in vivo mouse cranial window creation show accurate anatomical prior transfer, reliable boundary adaptation, and autonomous precision milling of biological structures.

Conclusion: An uncertainty-aware autonomous milling framework that integrates priors with active perception and adaptive control enables scalable, robust precision milling in biological contexts despite boundary uncertainties.

Abstract: Autonomous precision milling of biological structures is challenged by incomplete knowledge of target geometry, local material thickness, and critical internal boundaries. Subject-specific preoperative models can address geometric and thickness variations, but static models cannot determine boundary status encountered during execution, while repeated target-specific imaging limits scalability. This article presents an uncertainty-aware autonomous milling framework that assigns complementary roles to generic anatomical priors and active boundary perception. A generic anatomical prior provides conservative global guidance and is transformed through semantic-guided registration and hybrid vision-force calibration into robot-executable guidance for individual targets. As milling approaches uncertain boundaries, the robot actively probes the remaining structure and uses relative stiffness changes to estimate boundary status and structural detachability. A state-adaptive controller governs transitions between active perception and spatially selective incremental refinement, repeating this cycle until the termination criterion is satisfied. Hierarchical experiments on biological surrogates and in vivo mouse cranial window creation demonstrate accurate anatomical prior transfer, reliable boundary adaptation, and autonomous precision milling of biological structures.

</details>


### [272] [STAR: Sparse Tactile Representation Learning in Vision-Tactile-Language-Action Models for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12549)
*Xiangcheng Liu,Tianhao Wu,Le Zheng,Yidong Wang,Bowen Jiang,Mingjie Pan,Xinlin Ren,Yi Liu,Jianlan Luo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A large-scale visuo-tacto-language-action dataset for dexterous manipulation and a STAR training recipe that achieves 61% average success on four real-world tasks after task-specific post-training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dexterous manipulation is hampered by scarce real-world data and sparse tactile signals; existing models struggle to extract meaningful representations from tactile data and to combine vision, touch, and language for control.

Method: Collect 200 hours of bimanual dexterous manipulation data (10,576 trajectories across 65 tasks) with synchronized vision, touch, and language annotations; develop STAR, an integrated VTLA (vision-tactile-language-action) training recipe that tackles sparsity via (1) visual-tactile joint pre-training, (2) sparse-global tactile token representation, and (3) sparse future tactile prediction.

Result: STAR trained on the dataset achieves 61% average success across four real-world tasks with 100 post-training trajectories per task.

Conclusion: A scalable, multimodal data-and-method approach enables improved dexterous manipulation; the combination of a large, multi-modal dataset and STAR’s sparsity-aware training yields tangible performance gains in real-world tasks.

Abstract: Dexterous manipulation requires coordinated multi-finger control and effective tactile feedback, yet learning these capabilities remains challenging due to the lack of large-scale real-world data and the difficulty of extracting effective representations from sparse tactile signals. We build a robot platform and teleoperation system to collect a 200-hour bimanual dexterous manipulation dataset with synchronized visual, tactile, and language annotations, comprising 10,576 trajectories across 65 tasks, 69.5% of which involve dexterous multi-finger manipulation. We further propose STAR, an integrated training recipe for vision-tactile-language-action (VTLA) models that addresses the spatial, temporal, and informational sparsity of tactile signals through visual-tactile joint pre-training, sparse-global tactile token representation, and sparse future tactile prediction. Trained on this dataset, STAR achieves a 61% average success rate across four real-world tasks with 100 post-training trajectories per task, demonstrating dexterous performance under task-specific post-training.

</details>


### [273] [A Hierarchical Coverage Path Planning Algorithm for Unknown Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12595)
*Zongyuan Shen,Haodong Liu,Gao Wang,Hongbin Ma,Yaming Ou,Shancheng Zhao,Dehua Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Online coverage path planning in unknown environments using an incrementally built decomposition tree of subareas; global tour updated online; local planners within subareas; improved path length and reduced overlap versus three baselines in high-fidelity simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Covering unknown environments efficiently requires balancing global exploration with local coverage while progressively revealing obstacle information; a scalable, online hierarchical approach can adapt as the map evolves.

Method: As the robot explores, the search area is incrementally decomposed into disconnected subareas. These are organized into a decomposition tree with hierarchical parent–child relations. A global coverage tour is maintained and updated online by prioritizing newly generated child subareas based on their exploration state and distance. A local planner generates coverage motions within the selected subarea, allowing adaptation as the environment is revealed. The approach is evaluated via high-fidelity simulations in complex scenarios andCompared against three baseline algorithms.

Result: The method achieves improved coverage efficiency, evidenced by shorter path lengths and lower overlap ratios compared with the baselines.

Conclusion: An online hierarchical decomposition and planning framework effectively handles unknown environments, providing adaptive global coverage with local refinement and improved efficiency.

Abstract: This paper presents an online coverage path planning algorithm for unknown environments. During navigation, the initially unknown search area is progressively decomposed into disconnected subareas as new obstacle information is acquired and coverage proceeds.
