These subareas are organized in an incrementally constructed decomposition tree that preserves their hierarchical parent-child relationships. Based on this tree, a global coverage tour is maintained and updated online by prioritizing newly generated child subareas according to their exploration states and distances from the robot. A local planner then generates coverage motions within each selected subarea, allowing the robot to adapt its trajectory as the environment is gradually revealed. Its performance is evaluated via high-fidelity simulations in complex scenarios. The results show improved coverage efficiency in terms of path length and overlap ratio in comparison to three baseline algorithms.

</details>


### [274] [Quantifying Spectral Differences in Vehicle Between Production Autonomous and Human-Driven Vehicles Across Driving Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12609)
*Peiyi Fang,Xiangyu Li,Yonglin Weng,Ke Ma*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A real-world study uses a frequency-domain framework to quantify PAV-HV kinematic differences, revealing scenario-dependent spectral differences and highlighting the value of spectrally-based analysis over time-domain metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses limitations of time-domain metrics and simulation-based studies by leveraging real-world data from multiple PAV platforms to compare PAV and HV behaviors across diverse scenarios in the frequency domain.

Method: Transforms kinematic signals into the frequency domain, extracts spectral features, and compares PAV-HV distributions using kernel density estimation and Wasserstein distance, applied to a real-world dataset with four PAV platforms across varying states, lighting, weather, and vehicle densities.

Result: Identified clear, scenario-dependent spectral differences between PAVs and HVs. Speed-related differences are smaller during car-following than cruising; rainy conditions amplify acceleration differences relative to clear conditions.

Conclusion: Frequency-domain analysis on real-world data is valuable for characterizing PAV-HV kinematic differences across multiple scenarios, and multi-scenario evaluations are essential to capture the variability in PAV behavior.

Abstract: Differences in vehicle kinematic characteristics between production autonomous vehicles (PAVs) and human-driven vehicles (HVs) have been limitedly investigated by empirical studies. Most recent studies rely on simulation-based models, while some further investigate low-level adaptive cruise control (ACC) systems in controlled experiments. These methods commonly adapt some time-domain metrics to characterize PAV-HV differences across limited driving conditions. However, current PAVs equipped with high-level autonomous driving systems generate driving behaviors in a black box using data-driven models. These fundamentally different mechanisms for generating behaviors may produce distinct kinematic characteristics in traffic. More importantly, these time-domain metrics cannot reflect frequency-related traffic dynamics across different driving scenarios. Thus, this study adapted a real-world PAV dataset with four PAV platforms and developed a frequency-domain framework to quantify kinematic differences between PAVs and HVs across diverse driving scenarios, including varying driving states, lighting, weather, and vehicle densities. The framework transforms kinematic signals into the frequency domain and extracts spectral features, and then compares these features between PAVs and HVs based on kernel density estimation and Wasserstein distance. The results reveal clear scenario-dependent PAV-HV spectral differences. Specifically, speed-related differences were consistently smaller during car-following than cruising, while rainy conditions consistently enlarged acceleration-related differences compared with clear conditions. These findings highlight the necessity of multi-scenario evaluations and demonstrate the value of frequency-domain analysis for characterizing PAV-HV kinematic differences under real-world conditions.

</details>


### [275] [ProClosure: Hierarchical Room-Object Assignment using Progressive Boundary Closure from Monocular Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12614)
*Vinoth Kumar Muthuraj,Soumyadeep Banik,Kushal Sharma,Hardik Jain*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Progressive Boundary Closure recovers room-level structure from monocular video to improve room segmentation and object-to-room assignments for robotic fetch tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monocular maps have incomplete boundaries where walls/doors are unobserved, causing misassignment of objects to rooms and unreliable navigation for tasks like fetching items from specific rooms.

Method: Fuse SLAM front end with an open-vocabulary segmenter to build a structural point cloud and camera trajectory. Rasterize to a top-down map. Progressively thicken boundaries inward, freezing each free-space region once enclosed, so openings seal at multiple scales. Seeds from camera poses initialize the segmentation, making it deterministic. Assign each object to the room that holds most of its extent.

Result: On 10 floors of 6 HM3D-Semantics scenes, compared against HOV-SG on identical top-down maps: 74 rooms recovered for 72 annotated regions (HOV-SG: 44). Room F1 improved from 0.741 to 0.890 at IoU 0.25 (with some precision loss). Object-to-room ARI improved from 0.488 to 0.696 (p=0.002), consistent across floors.

Conclusion: Progressive Boundary Closure yields more accurate room segmentation and robust object-to-room assignments from monocular observations. By closing openings at appropriate scales and seeding with camera poses, the method reduces cross-room leakage and improves retrieval-oriented representations, at some precision cost.

Abstract: A 3D scene graph groups objects into rooms. When a robot is asked to fetch an object from the kitchen, that grouping is what tells it where to look. An object recorded in the wrong room is not retrievable by a query naming the correct room. We introduce Progressive Boundary Closure, which recovers room layer from a monocular RGB video. A SLAM front end and an open-vocabulary segmenter supply a structural point cloud, camera trajectory and object tracks. The cloud is rasterised into a top-down map, rooms are recovered from it, and each object takes the room holding most of its extent. The difficulty lies in the map itself. Walls are recorded only where the camera looked, so a gap in the boundary may be a doorway or a stretch of wall that was never observed; nothing distinguishes the two. Prior methods treat both as passages, merging rooms that should remain separate. We observe that both require the same treatment: a room should not extend across either, so both are closed and need not be distinguished. Such an opening closes under a small amount of boundary growth, and few sightlines cross it, so points in different rooms rarely see one another. We use the first to recover rooms and the second to assign objects to them. Rooms are obtained by Progressively thickening the boundary inward and freezing each free-space region once it becomes enclosed, so every opening seals at its own scale rather than at a radius fixed in advance. Camera poses are used as seeds, which removes the sampling heuristic and makes the segmentation deterministic. Over 10 floors of 6 HM3D-Semantics scenes, scored against HOV-SG on identical top-down maps, we recover 74 rooms for 72 annotated regions (HOV-SG: 44), raising room F_1 from 0.741 to 0.890 at IoU 0.25 at some cost in precision, and object-to-room ARI from 0.488 to 0.696 (p=0.002, ahead on every floor).

</details>


### [276] [Online Material Estimation for Conditioned Diffusion Policy in Shaping Deformable Linear Objects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12634)
*Ryunosuke Yamada,Tomohiro Motoda,Yukiyasu Domae,Tokuo Tsuji*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A diffusion policy for deformable linear object manipulation conditioned on online-estimated material labels; achieves near-oracle performance using online material estimation, tested on 4 materials and 3 groove tasks with 480 real-robot demonstrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DLO manipulation is highly sensitive to material properties (stiffness, elasticity). A single policy must adapt to different objects even for the same goal shape, making generalization difficult without material priors.

Method: A recurrent estimator predicts the material label of the grasped object from time-series of multi-view images and robot joint states. The predicted label conditions a diffusion policy at every inference step. Evaluation uses 480 real-robot demonstrations across four DLO materials and three groove-placement tasks, comparing per-material specialist policies, a task-conditioned policy without material labels, a policy conditioned on ground-truth labels, and the proposed online-conditioned diffusion policy.

Result: Ground-truth material labels improve success rate from 45.8% (task-only) to 60.0%. The proposed online-label diffusion policy achieves 60.8%, matching the ground-truth-label policy without access to ground-truth labels. Post-hoc analysis indicates the estimator captures material-related information and the diffusion policy responds to the conditioning; a key failure case is persistent confusion between two similar materials.

Conclusion: The proposed online-material conditioning via a recurrent estimator and diffusion policy enables robust DLO shape control across materials, achieving near-oracle performance without material priors. The main limitation is material confusion between similar materials; future work could address reducing ambiguity and extending to more materials and tasks.

Abstract: Shape control of deformable linear objects (DLOs) is challenging for imitation learning because deformation behavior varies with material properties such as stiffness and elasticity, so a single policy must generate different action sequences for different objects even when the goal shape is identical. We propose a diffusion policy conditioned on material labels that are estimated online during manipulation. A recurrent estimation network predicts the material label of the grasped object from the time series of multi-view images and robot joint states, and the predicted label conditions the diffusion policy at every inference step. We collected 480 real-robot demonstrations covering four DLO materials and three groove-placement tasks, and compared per-material specialist policies, a task-conditioned policy without material labels, a policy conditioned on ground-truth material labels, and the proposed policy. Conditioning on ground-truth material labels improved the average success rate from 45.8% to 60.0% over the task-only policy, and the proposed policy reached 60.8% without any prior material information, matching the policy given ground-truth labels. A post-hoc analysis shows that the estimator extracts material-related information from the manipulation observations and that the diffusion policy responds to the resulting conditioning signal, while the one pronounced failure case is associated with persistent confusion between two similar materials.

</details>


### [277] [Breaking the Vision-Action Shortcut: Latent Interface Training for Generalizable Robotics Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12641)
*Jianman Lin,Shailesh Shailesh,Zhongyi Luo,Jiafei Duan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Latent Interface Training (LIT) is a two-stage, framework-agnostic approach that decouples spatial goal generation from visual conditioning via a pose-supervised latent interface, improving robustness to visual distribution shifts across multiple architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language-action models risk exploiting task-irrelevant visual cues that correlate with demonstrated actions within the training distribution, harming generalization under distribution shifts. Preserving spatial information while constraining visual usage is essential.

Method: Stage 1: train an action expert to generate action chunks conditioned on language, robot state, and each chunk's terminal SE(3) pose without using images. Stage 2: build a pose-supervised latent interface that combines visual and semantic representations and serves as the only visual conditioning pathway for the expert; train the interface to reconstruct the terminal pose used in Stage 1. Framework-agnostic implementation evaluated on Pi0.5, MolmoAct2, FAST-WAM, and ImageWAM.

Result: LIT yields LIBERO-Plus success gains of 3.87–10.70 percentage points while maintaining or increasing average LIBERO success; real-world evaluations show 13.30–16.70 percentage-point gains across three tasks under unseen camera configurations, lighting, and distractors.

Conclusion: The proposed two-stage latent-interface approach effectively mitigates vision-action shortcuts by preserving goal-relevant spatial information while restricting visual conditioning, and it generalizes across diverse robot-action architectures.

Abstract: Robot foundation models achieve strong in-distribution performance but often degrade under visual distribution shifts. When learning to generate actions from pretrained visual representations, models may exploit task-irrelevant visual cues that correlate with demonstrated actions within the training distribution. Such vision-action shortcuts can undermine generalization when these correlations change under distribution shifts. Mitigating these shortcuts requires constraining how visual information is used for action generation while preserving task-relevant spatial information. We propose Latent Interface Training (LIT), a framework-agnostic two-stage strategy that first establishes a spatial-goal-conditioned action prior without images, then constrains visual conditioning through a pose-supervised latent interface. Stage 1 trains the action expert to generate action chunks conditioned on language, robot state, and each demonstrated chunk's terminal SE(3) end-effector pose, learning goal-directed action generation independently of visual cues. Stage 2 introduces a latent interface that aggregates visual and semantic representations and serves as the pretrained action expert's only visual conditioning pathway. The interface is supervised to reconstruct the terminal pose previously used to condition Stage 1, encouraging it to retain the goal-relevant spatial information needed for action generation. Across four vision-language-action and world-action architectures (Pi0.5, MolmoAct2, FAST-WAM, and ImageWAM), LIT improves overall LIBERO-Plus success by 3.87-10.70 percentage points while preserving or improving average LIBERO success. Real-world evaluations show 13.30-16.70 percentage-point gains in success aggregated across three tasks under unseen camera configurations, lighting variations, and distractors.

</details>


### [278] [Driving Context-guided Model Predictive Planning and Control for Autonomous Car Racing at the Limit and Beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12660)
*Ayoub Raji,Federico Sacco,Nicola Musiu,Marko Bertogna*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MPC-based motion planning and control for autonomous racing using a context-aware cost blending state machine, with two optimization variants differing in horizon and open-/closed-loop configuration; validated on the Superformula EAV-25 achieving lap times within 2% of top human drivers and robust overtaking and oversteer recovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable high-performance autonomous racing by adapting planning and control objectives to different driving contexts (overtaking, nominal, countersteering) via a unified MPC framework with context-aware weighting.

Method: A single problem formulation and vehicle model are used for two optimization-based solutions that differ in horizon length, update rate, tuning, and open-loop vs closed-loop execution. A cost blending state machine detects driving contexts and assigns predefined weights to the Model Predictive Planning (MPP) and MPC modules. Validation conducted on the fully autonomous Superformula EAV-25 racecar.

Result: The approach achieves lap times within 2% of the best human driver reference, demonstrating capability at the limit of handling, smooth overtaking maneuvers, and rapid stabilization after high oversteering.

Conclusion: Context-driven cost blending in an MPC-based planning/control pipeline enables effective high-performance autonomous racing, including overtaking and stability recovery. The paper suggests strong interaction between planning and control and potential applicability to varying driving contexts; further details on generalization and robustness would strengthen the work.

Abstract: This paper presents a Model Predictive Control-based motion planning and control pipeline for autonomous car racing capable of adapting to different driving contexts, such as overtaking, nominal driving, and countersteering. A Cost Blending state machine manages the identification of different driving contexts and the selection of their predefined weights to be applied to the Model Predictive Planning (MPP) and Control (MPC) modules. The two optimization-based solutions share the same problem formulation and model, differing only in horizon length, rate, tuning, and in their open-loop versus closed-loop approach to maximize the effectiveness of their interaction. The work is validated on the fully autonomous open-wheel racecar Superformula EAV-25, with a lap time achieved that is within 2% of the best human driver reference. The results demonstrate the capability of the solution in driving at the limit of handling, smoothly executing overtaking maneuvers, and quickly reacting to high oversteering conditions to recover the vehicle stability.

</details>


### [279] [Size Doesn't Matter: Material-State Reinforcement Learning for Excavator Transferable Soil Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12677)
*Lennart Werner,Pol Eyschen,Sean Costello,Pierluigi Micarelli,Andrei Cramariuc,Marco Hutter*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RL-controlled soil manipulation using a GPU-parallelized MPM simulator learns state-aware policies transferable across machines, enabling autonomous embankment construction with performance on par or better than an expert.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current autonomous earthmoving methods rely on heuristic models and lack integration of soil mechanics; autonomy is limited to excavation/dumping and often uses single-face actions. The work aims to enable multi-face manipulation, material-state awareness, and cross-machine transfer to improve autonomy.

Method: Train reinforcement learning controllers in a GPU-accelerated Material Point Method particle simulation. Controllers are conditioned on material state (shape, compactness) to enable use of multiple shovel faces and material displacement inside/outside the shovel. Policies are normalized in end-effector space for cross-machine transfer and deployed via a calibrated machine interface. Validation includes transferring learned policies to an 11.5t hydraulic excavator and a 500 g tabletop robot.

Result: Autonomous construction of a 42 m long, 2.1 m high embankment in 45 minutes, using 201 policy strokes with no failure, retry, or operator intervention. In direct comparison, the autonomous controller matched expert progression speed and produced a higher, more consistent embankment. Qualitative backfilling and compaction experiments demonstrate material-state awareness and transfer across machines.

Conclusion: Integrating soil-mechanics-informed RL in a GPU-accelerated simulator with material-state conditioning and calibrated cross-machine deployment yields robust, transferable earthmoving policies, extending autonomy to multi-face excavation tasks and achieving reliable embankment construction.

Abstract: Earthmoving tasks such as excavation, backfilling, or embankment construction require deliberate repositioning of deformable soil. For these tasks, human operators use all shovel faces, while autonomous systems so far are limited to excavation and dumping. Current methods often rely on heuristic models but do not incorporate soil mechanics. We address this shortcoming by using Reinforcement Learning in a GPU-parallelized Material Point Method particle simulation. Our controllers are conditioned on material state such as shape and compactness, enabling skills that use multiple contact faces of the tool and displace material both inside and outside of the shovel. To use the same learned weights across machines, our policies operate in a normalized end-effector space and are deployed through a calibrated machine interface. We evaluate this calibrated transfer on an 11.5t hydraulic excavator and a 500g tabletop robot. We validate performance through autonomous construction of a 42m long, 2.1m high embankment in 45min, executing 201 individual policy strokes without failure, retry, or operator intervention. In a direct comparison, the autonomous controller matches an expert operator's progression speed and produces a higher, more consistent embankment. Additional qualitative backfilling and compaction experiments demonstrate the material-state awareness and calibrated transfer across machines.

</details>


### [280] [Improving Imitation Learning Efficiency for Manipulation through Geometric Prior Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12721)
*Shogo Iwakata,Tomohiro Motoda,Ryosuke Yamada,Koshi Makihara,Ryoichi Nakajo,Keitaro Tanaka,Masaki Murooka,Roman Mykhailyshyn,Hirokatsu Kataoka,Shigeo Morishima,Yukiyasu Domae*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Geometric visual pretraining with simple plane/object/hand scenes and a cube-hand representation provides transferable priors that improve early imitation-learning performance across multiple robots and real-world tasks with limited task demonstrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imitation learning can be sample-inefficient; there is a need for a cheap, robot-agnostic prior that can be provided before task-specific data are collected, enabling cross-robot and real-world transfer.

Method: Construct a geometric pretraining dataset consisting of scenes with a plane, an object, and a hand; represent the hand as a cube; automatically generate trajectories; pretrain on this dataset; fine-tune on ACT with three simulated robots across five tasks, plus three real-world tasks using limited demonstrations.

Result: Fine-tuning from the geometric prior achieves higher early-stage success rates than training from scratch across many robot-task combinations; demonstrates transfer across robots and to real-world tasks with limited data.

Conclusion: Highly simplified geometric scenes can provide useful initialization for imitation-learning policies, enabling cross-robot and real-world transfer when task data are scarce.

Abstract: Applying an imitation learning policy to a new manipulation task usually requires collecting new demonstrations and retraining the model, which makes sample efficiency a practical concern. Pretraining on large-scale robot datasets is effective in this respect, but such datasets are costly to collect and train on, while data augmentation techniques typically require a new round of data generation and retraining for each task. A complementary question is what useful prior can be provided to a policy at negligible cost before any task-specific data are collected. In this study, we construct a geometric visual pretraining dataset in which each scene contains only a plane, an object, and a hand, and trajectories are generated automatically. The scenes contain neither textures nor backgrounds; pretraining primarily exposes the policy to the geometric relationship between the hand and the object. Furthermore, representing the hand as a cube avoids tailoring the dataset to a specific robot morphology. We evaluate this geometric prior using ACT on three simulated robots across five manipulation tasks each, as well as on three real-world robot tasks. Across many of these robot--task combinations, fine-tuning from the geometric prior achieves higher success rates in the early stages of training than training from scratch while using only a small number of task demonstrations. These results suggest that even highly simplified geometric scenes can provide a useful initialization that transfers across robots and to real-world tasks when task data are limited.

</details>


### [281] [Control Architecture for Safe Grasping of Fragile Objects Using a Coarse Position-Controlled Gripper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12737)
*Marko Pavlic,Moritz Geier,Timo Markert,Darius Burschka*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Online geometry-aware contact estimation with an adaptive admittance controller enables compliant, safe grasps of unknown objects using a coarse gripper, while minimizing contact force and preventing slip.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots operating in unstructured environments must safely grasp unknown objects without causing damage. Humans instinctively adjust grasping force; robotics requires compliant interaction to adapt to uncertain object properties and dynamic instabilities during manipulation.

Method: A geometry-aware force/torque-based contact estimation method is integrated with an adaptive admittance controller for a coarse position-controlled gripper. Online estimation of desired contact forces maintains stable contact with objects of unknown properties, enabling compliant and stable grasps with minimal force.

Result: Experimental tests across objects with varying sizes, shapes, stiffness, and weight show the algorithm prevents slippage and achieves safe grasping by applying minimal necessary force, reducing deformation.

Conclusion: The proposed approach yields compliant, stable grasps for unknown object properties and demonstrates robustness across object variations while minimizing deformation and excessive forces.

Abstract: Robots are increasingly used in unstructured environments. The need for them to safely grasp unknown objects without damaging them becomes crucial. Humans achieve this by sensing and quickly responding by adjusting their grasping force. Similarly, effective grasp acquisition in robots requires compliant interaction strategies that can adapt to uncertain object properties and adjust to any instabilities during manipulation.
  We present a geometry-aware force/torque-based contact estimation method for a coarse position-controlled gripper, combined with an adaptive admittance controller for safe grasp acquisition. The desired contact forces are estimated online to keep stable contact with objects of unknown properties. This enables compliant and stable grasps while avoiding excessive forces. Experiments with objects of different sizes, shapes, stiffnesses, and weights show that the proposed algorithm not only prevents slippage but also applies minimal force to safely grasp an object without causing excessive deformation.

</details>


### [282] [High-Fidelity Multi-Body Simulator for Autonomous Racing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12795)
*Nicola Musiu,Francesco Iacovacci,Fausto Lupo,Matteo Pini,Giovanni Scapicchi,Francesco Moretti,Eugenio Mascaro,Pietro Musso,Ayoub Raji,Marko Bertogna,Vincenzo Maria Arricale,Angelo Lo Sapio,Alessandro Piccarelli,Garron Fish*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A high-fidelity vehicle dynamics simulation environment for autonomous racing validation, using Dymola, CRG road modeling, FMU export, and a real-time SIL framework with experimental-calibrated validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a credible, high-fidelity ground-truth simulator for testing autonomous racing algorithms before real-world deployment, addressing limitations of lower-fidelity sims.

Method: Develop multi-body dynamics in Dymola using racecar libraries; model 3D road surfaces with CRG; export as FMU; integrate into a C++-based software-in-the-loop simulator; implement calibration with experimental data; perform validation; ensure real-time performance on portable hardware.

Result: A real-time capable digital twin simulator that yields reliable ground truth for algorithm validation and supports calibration/validation studies prior to real-world deployment.

Conclusion: The proposed framework offers a credible, high-fidelity testing platform for autonomous racing, enabling rigorous algorithm validation prior to deployment.

Abstract: We present a custom high-fidelity vehicle dynamics simulation environment for testing and validation of Autonomous Racing software. The digital twin of the autonomous vehicle is developed in Dymola, using racecar dynamics modeling libraries to build a complete multi-body model. A 3D road surface, including elevation profiles and curbs, is implemented using the Curved Regular Grid (CRG) standard. The model is exported from Dymola as a Functional Mock-up Unit (FMU) and integrated into a custom software-in-the-loop simulator, where communication interfaces with the autonomous racing stack were developed in C++. A calibration procedure based on experimental data is also presented, along with a validation study to further support the quality of the proposed framework. The simulator runs in real time on a portable computer and provides reliable ground truth for algorithms validation prior to real-world deployment.

</details>


### [283] [VertexCBF: Improving Neural Control Barrier Functions via Vertex-Restricted Control Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12831)
*Bojan Derajić,Sebastian Bernhard,Wolfgang Hönig*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VertexCBF learns neural control barrier functions by approximating the stationary Hamilton–Jacobi value function with physics-informed and sparsely supervised training. It exploits control-affine dynamics and a convex polytope control set, using vertex-based supervision generated by a GPU-accelerated vertex-restricted tree search and a residual architecture to enforce safety constraints. It achieves larger, less conservative safe sets and is validated on 15 systems and in hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety is critical as autonomous robot fleets grow; existing CBF design methods struggle with effectiveness, scalability, or interpretability and can yield overly conservative safe sets. A scalable, explainable approach to learning CBFs is needed.

Method: Train a neural network to approximate the stationary Hamilton–Jacobi value function using physics-informed and sparsely supervised signals. Leverage control-affine dynamics and a convex polytope control set, with the Hamiltonian maximized at control vertices. Generate supervision points via a GPU-parallel vertex-restricted tree search. Use a residual architecture to guarantee the learned CBF never exceeds the user-specified constraint function.

Result: Empirical evaluation on 15 systems shows VertexCBF reliably recovers large safe sets where baselines are conservative or fail. A hardware mobile-robot experiment demonstrates safe pedestrian avoidance using a neural CBF trained by VertexCBF.

Conclusion: VertexCBF provides a scalable, explainable framework for neural CBFs, combining physics-informed learning with vertex-based supervision to achieve less conservative, verifiable safety guarantees, validated in simulation and hardware.

Abstract: As the number of autonomous robots continues to grow, safety becomes increasingly important. Control barrier functions (CBFs) provide a theoretically grounded framework for ensuring safety, but existing design methods often face limitations in effectiveness, scalability, or interpretability, and may result in overly conservative safe sets. In this paper, we propose \emph{VertexCBF}, a framework for learning neural CBFs in a scalable, systematic, and explainable way. We approximate the stationary Hamilton--Jacobi value function using a neural network trained via a combination of physics-informed and sparsely supervised learning. By exploiting control-affine dynamics and a convex polytope control set, under which the Hamiltonian is maximized at the control vertices, we efficiently generate supervision points via GPU-parallel vertex-restricted tree search, while a residual architecture guarantees that the learned CBF is never larger than the specified constraint function. We evaluate the method on 15 systems and compare it against relevant baselines, showing that it reliably recovers large safe sets where the baselines are conservative or fail completely. In addition, we perform a hardware experiment in which a mobile robot safely avoids pedestrians using a neural CBF trained with our method.

</details>


### [284] [Parameter Sensitivity Analysis for Aerial LiDAR-Inertial Odometries in low-altitude flights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12837)
*Robert Milijas,Jose Ramiro Martinez-de Dios,Stjepan Bogdan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Systematic parameter influence analysis for EKF-based FAST-LIO2 and Cartographer LIO on aerial LiDAR data; demonstrates near-optimal ATE with tuned parameters and a simplified tuning workflow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SLAM performance depends heavily on sensor, motion, and algorithmic parameters. Tuning is labor-intensive and domain-specific, especially for aerial platforms with diverse LiDARs. The study seeks actionable guidance to predict parameter impact and streamline tuning across environments.

Method: Exhaustive grid search over parameter combinations for FAST-LIO2 EKF-based LIO and Cartographer LIO; datasets from low-to-moderate altitude aerial flights using different LiDARs; evaluation via absolute trajectory error (ATE). Relationships between parameters and ATE assessed with Pearson correlation; parameter importance estimated via random forest permutation importance using RF models trained to predict ATE from parameter settings.

Result: Identified parameters with the strongest influence on performance for both FAST-LIO2 and Cartographer; developed simplified tuning procedure and concrete recommendations. With the proposed tuning, ATE is within 5 cm of the grid-search optimum in 94% of analyzed cases across the datasets.

Conclusion: The framework provides practical, transferable tuning guidance for aerial LiDAR SLAM that substantially reduces tuning effort while maintaining near-optimal accuracy. It demonstrates generalizability across LiDAR types and environments and offers a foundation for more efficient, data-driven SLAM parameter tuning.

Abstract: LiDAR-based SLAM (Simultaneous Localization and Mapping) and LIO (LiDAR-inertial odometry) algorithms are often used for precise navigation of unmanned aerial vehicles, especially during interactions with the aerial robot's environment. However, the performance of these algorithms is greatly dependent on the scenario, LiDAR, and robot motion characteristics, often requiring an intensive tuning process to achieve the desired performance. To aid these tuning efforts, this paper analyzes the influence on performance of the parameters of an EKF-based LIO algorithm (FAST-LIO2) and the LIO module of a graph-based SLAM algorithm (Cartographer) on aerial LiDAR SLAM datasets recorded using different LiDARs in low-to-moderate-altitude flights in diverse environments. The analysis is conducted on the absolute trajectory error (ATE) resulting from processing the datasets with the LIO algorithms configured with each combination of parameters obtained in an exhaustive grid search. The relationship between individual parameters and the ATE results is assessed using Pearson's correlation, while the influence of each parameter is assessed using random forest permutation importance analyses with random forest models trained to predict the resulting ATE values based on the choice of parameters. The performed analysis obtains for Cartographer and FAST-LIO2: i) the identification of parameters with stronger influence in performance, ii) a simplified tuning procedure, and iii) tuning recommendations. Using the proposed tuning recommendations, both algorithms obtain on the analyzed datasets ATE values within 5 cm to the optimal performance found in the grid search procedure in 94% of the analyzed cases.

</details>


### [285] [A Multi-Vehicle Dataset with Camera, LiDAR, and Radar Sensors and Scanned 3D Models for Custom Auto-Annotation using RTK-GNSS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12871)
*Philipp Berthold,Bianca Forkel,Mirko Maehlisch*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes a new autonomous driving dataset with scanned 3D vehicle models, RTK-GNSS pose and continuous kinematics to enable full dynamic surroundings and customizable reference formats; supports single- and multi-object scenarios with seven vehicles and evaluation of occlusion and reflections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current datasets provide semantic segmentation, point-wise associations, or bounding boxes but lack precise, time-continuous ground truth of the full dynamic scene for evaluating measurement principles; a richer reference is needed for robust perception evaluation.

Method: Assemble scanned 3D models of all vehicles; obtain pose and continuous kinematics via RTK-GNSS; record single- and multi-object scenarios involving seven target vehicles; enable computation of reference formats at user-defined granularity; leverage known vehicle normals to study occlusion and reflection effects; describe dataset and technical development background; present exemplar evaluations.

Result: Description of the dataset and its development background, plus initial exemplar evaluations demonstrating the dataset’s utility for evaluating measurement principles, particularly under occlusion and reflection scenarios.

Conclusion: The dataset enables deeper evaluation of sensing and perception pipelines in autonomous driving by providing precise, time-resolved, full-scene ground truth and configurable reference formats.

Abstract: Datasets are a crucial element in the development of perception algorithms. They relate sensor measurement data to annotated reference information and allow for the deduction of sensor and object characteristics. In autonomous driving, the reference data commonly consist of semantic image segmentation, point-wise associations, or bounding box annotations. The dataset proposed in this work, however, aims to dig deeper into the evaluation of measurement principles and provides scanned 3D models of all vehicles together with a pose and continuous kinematics reference obtained by RTK-GNSS. Combined, the state of the complete dynamic surrounding of the sensor vehicle is known for any point in time. Subsequent reference formats can be easily computed in user-defined granularity. This dataset involves single-object and multi-object recordings with seven target vehicles. In particular, measurement effects such as occlusion, as well as reflections, can be evaluated, as the normals of the shape of the target vehicles are known. We describe the dataset, discuss the technical background of its development, and briefly present exemplary evaluations.

</details>


### [286] [From Transportation to Manipulation: Enabling Grasping in Magnetic Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12883)
*Lara Bergmann,Noah Greis,Cedric Grothues,Lisa-Marie Weigelt,Klaus Neumann*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A low-cost Gripper MagBot integrates three MagLev movers with a 1-DoF gripper to form a 6-DoF parallel manipulator for in-machine grasping, featuring two configurations, autonomous docking, and demonstration in simulation and on real hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable manipulation and grasping directly on MagLev systems to expand beyond transport, enabling flexible, reconfigurable workflows in high-mix low-volume manufacturing without costly robot arms.

Method: A parallel 6-DoF manipulator built from three MagLev movers with a coupled 1-DoF gripper; two operating configurations (default and single-track) for stability and workspace tradeoffs; autonomous reconfiguration via a docking station; inverse kinematics controller; demonstrations in simulation and with a real Gripper MagBot; CAD files, assembly instructions, BOM, and videos provided.

Result: Successful pick-and-place demonstrations in both simulation and real hardware using the inverse kinematics controller; autonomous docking/reconfiguration capability demonstrated; documentation and multimedia resources publicly available.

Conclusion: The Gripper MagBot presents a compact, scalable approach to integrating manipulation on MagLev platforms, enabling cost-effective, reconfigurable in-machine grasping without external robots; further work could address quantitative performance metrics, payload limits, and robustness under disturbances.

Abstract: Magnetic levitation (MagLev) systems have great potential for application in high-mix, low-volume manufacturing due to their scalability and flexibility, enabling highly reconfigurable in-machine material flow. However, their manipulation capabilities remain largely unexploited, as current applications almost exclusively focus on transportation. To enable grasping and manipulation directly on MagLev systems without requiring additional costly handling equipment, such as industrial robot arms, we present the Gripper MagBot, a low-cost parallel 6-DoF manipulator with an integrated 1-DoF gripper that mechanically couples three MagLev movers. The Gripper MagBot supports two operating configurations: a default mode and a single-track mode, selectable depending on the required stability and workspace footprint. To reconfigure a machine, the MagBot can be autonomously dropped off and picked up using a docking station. We showcase pick-and-place examples in simulation, as well as with the real Gripper MagBot using our inverse kinematics controller. CAD files, assembly instructions, a component list, and videos are available at https://sites.google.com/view/gripper-magbot.

</details>


### [287] [Before the Tipping Point: Force-Guided Active Perception for Shape-Agnostic Estimation of 3D Centers of Mass](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12894)
*Steven M. Hyland,Jing Xiao,Cagdas D. Onal*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A force-based method estimates an unknown object's 3D center of mass height, mass, and tipping behavior from a single sub-critical tipping experiment using a push-retract, force-angle sensing cycle with a robot, enabling estimation without prior shape models and informing non-prehensile manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimating 3D inertial parameters of unknown, irregular, or non-graspable objects is difficult; prior shape or mass distribution information is often unavailable, hindering safe and reliable manipulation.

Method: Perform a quasistatic elevated push–retract tipping sequence with a robot manipulator equipped with a 6-DOF force/torque sensor. Record force–angle data during tipping; fit object parameters from its trajectory. The push–retract cycle reduces frictional bias, enabling generalized parameter fitting. A safety margin maintains the object in a sub-critical tipping regime to prevent toppling.

Result: Experimental validation on diverse unknown objects without prior models or shapes using a 6-axis force–torque sensor. The method recovers mass, CoM height, and toppling angle with relative errors below 5.0% across objects. Demonstrates reliable 3D inertial parameter estimation under safety thresholds, supporting non-prehensile manipulation and challenging grasping scenarios.

Conclusion: The approach provides a practical, model-free way to estimate 3D inertial parameters from a single tipping experiment, enabling safer and more reliable manipulation of difficult objects and expanding capabilities for non-prehensile grasping.

Abstract: Estimating the 3D center of mass of unknown objects is challenging when grasping is infeasible, geometry is irregular, or mass distribution is uneven. We present a force-based method that estimates CoM height and mass from a single sub-critical tipping experiment by a robot manipulator. The robot applies a quasistatic elevated push and retract motion, using force-angle measurements recorded during tipping to identify parameters from the object trajectory. Our proposed push-retract cycle mitigates frictional bias, enabling generalized fitting. We experimentally validate our method using a robot manipulator with a six-axis force torque sensor on varying types of objects without prior shape information and without specific models. We also propose a method to prevent toppling, keeping the object in a sub-critical tipping regime by leveraging a safety margin. In experimental studies, our method recovers mass, CoM height, and toppling angle with relative errors below 5.0 percent across all unknown objects. This work demonstrates reliable 3D inertial parameter estimation under proper safety thresholds in tipping. Our proposed method informs and enables reliable non-prehensile manipulation and robotic grasping of challenging objects that were previously infeasible.

</details>


### [288] [Robust Underwater Grasping of Sloped Objects with a Waterproof Passive Adaptive Gripper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12927)
*Jooyoung Hong,Daewon Hong,Joohyung Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A waterproof passive adaptive gripper enables robust underwater grasping by combining a passive rotational joint for global self-alignment on sloped surfaces and passive variable-stiffness pads for local adaptation, demonstrated in both dry and underwater tests with improved holding and robustness over rigid-pad baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Underwater grasping in domestic environments is challenging due to reduced friction, torque-induced rolling on slopes, and slip on wet/submerged objects; a robust, adaptable gripper is needed for daily-life underwater manipulation.

Method: Develop a fully waterproof parallel-jaw gripper featuring a passive rotational joint for global self-alignment on sloped surfaces and passive variable-stiffness pads for local surface adaptation; evaluate experimentally on cylindrical and conical objects in dry and underwater conditions, comparing against rigid-pad and fixed-joint baselines.

Result: The proposed gripper achieves superior holding capability and grasp robustness underwater compared to rigid-pad and fixed-joint baselines, demonstrating stable underwater manipulation of diverse everyday objects.

Conclusion: The design provides a practical, robust solution for underwater manipulation of everyday objects, addressing a gap in current underwater robotic grasping for daily-life applications.

Abstract: Robust grasping of everyday objects remains challenging for parallel-jaw grippers, particularly when handling sloped or asymmetric items that induce torque-driven rolling and shear slip. These challenges become even more severe in domestic environments such as kitchens, where objects are often wet or submerged, drastically reducing friction between the gripper and the object. To address these issues, we present a waterproof passive adaptive gripper that combines local and global adaptability for high-performance grasping in the submerged environments. The proposed gripper features a fully waterproof design that integrates a passive rotational joint for global self-alignment on sloped surfaces and passive variable-stiffness pads for local surface adaptation. Experiments in both dry and underwater conditions on various cylindrical and conical objects demonstrate superior holding capability and grasp robustness compared to rigid-pad and fixed-joint baselines. The proposed design offers a practical and robust solution that successfully enables stable underwater manipulation of diverse everyday objects, effectively addressing a critical gap in current underwater robotic grasping for daily-life applications.

</details>


### [289] [ARC: Autonomous Robotics Compliance A Three-Layer Governance Architecture for Deployed Autonomous Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12932)
*Tord Eide,Einar Holt*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A conceptual governance framework for autonomous robots featuring ARC's three-layer compliance architecture implemented via model safety validation, cognitive certification benchmarks, and operational authorization standards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address safety, accountability, and regulatory compliance in autonomous robotic systems by establishing structured, layered compliance and traceability.

Method: Proposes an architectural framework (ARC) with three layers and instantiates it through three mechanisms: model safety validation, cognitive certification benchmarks, and operational authorization standards.

Result: Delivers a conceptual blueprint for governance; defines layers and instantiation mechanisms; potential for improved safety assurance and regulatory alignment; no empirical evaluation presented.

Conclusion: If adopted, ARC could enhance governance, transparency, and accountability for autonomous robots; further work needed on implementation, metrics, and interoperability.

Abstract: Proposed governance framework for autonomous robotic systems, introducing a three-layer compliance architecture (ARC) instantiated through model safety validation, cognitive certification benchmarks, and operational authorization standards.

</details>


### [290] [A Robot Among People:From Social Imitation to the Social Becoming of Human Groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12937)
*Victor Tuan Vu Pham,Judith Dörrenbächer,Thomas H. Weisswange,Marc Hassenzahl*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Robots in groups should be understood as environmental catalysts for collective sense-making, not autonomous problem-solvers or imitations of human sociability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To escape the solutionist trap in social robotics and recast robots as group-facing elements that shape shared experience.

Method: Conceptual analysis building on prior work on robotic street furniture; argument about how meaning emerges through how people position and interact with robots in public spaces.

Result: Robots function as constrained, group-facing entities that enable care, norms, and identity by enabling sense-making, rather than repairing dysfunction or imitating humans.

Conclusion: Design robots grounded in our needs for being and becoming together, focusing on relational capacity rather than autonomy or intelligence.

Abstract: Robots designed to mediate human groups often fall into the solutionist trap: they are framed as sociable agents that fix problems such as conflict, disengagement, or lack of coordination. We suggest a different way of thinking. Rather than discrete agents, robots can be understood as situated elements of shared environments; catalysts and carriers of group experience whose meaning emerges through how people position, interpret, and interact with them. From this perspective, robots are not there to repair some ostensible dysfunctionality, but to enable group-level sense-making around care, norms, and identity. Our prior work on robotic street furniture suggests that this does not happen by imitating human sociality but by taking the shape of deliberately constrained, group-facing entities that happen and act for \textit{us} without being socially entangled as one of us. We thus understand robots in public spaces not in terms of autonomy or intelligence, but as a relational capacity. This implies designing robots not in our image or for our utility, but grounded in our needs in being and becoming together.

</details>


### [291] [Distributed Stochastic Optimal Control for Pattern-Oriented Swarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12959)
*Qingrui Zhang,Chenghao Yu,Feng Xue,Xintong Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A GRF-based stochastic optimal control framework for swarm robotics that unifies geometric pattern control, uncertainty-aware navigation, and safe collision avoidance by extending GRF to the temporal domain, using unscented transforms for uncertainty propagation, and density-field representations for patterns; validated by simulations and real-world quadrotor experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address multifaceted challenges in pattern-oriented swarms: geometric control, self-organization, and safe navigation under environmental uncertainty and heterogeneous platform dynamics; provide a unified probabilistic framework capable of handling non-convex constraints and dynamic obstacles.

Method: Extend Grf into the temporal domain to cast coordination as Bayesian inference. Develop an uncertainty- and safety-aware collision avoidance module using the unscented transform to propagate state uncertainty for dynamic obstacles and neighbors. Introduce density-guided pattern control by encoding geometric patterns as implicit density fields, enabling distributed, self-healing, elastic reconfiguration without explicit target assignments. Demonstrate model-agnostic applicability across quadrotor and fixed-wing swarms and validate through Monte Carlo simulations and real-world experiments (15-quadrotor indoor, 4-quadrotor outdoor).

Result: Extensive Monte Carlo simulations demonstrate robustness across diverse scenarios. The framework is shown to be model-agnostic, performing on both quadrotor and fixed-wing swarms. Indoor experiments with 15 quadrotors and outdoor deployments with 4 autonomous quadrotors validate reliable geometric pattern transitions and safety-aware navigation.

Conclusion: The proposed GRF-based temporal probabilistic framework provides a unified approach for uncertainty-aware pattern formation and safe navigation in swarms, with cross-platform generalizability and demonstrated real-world viability. It enables intrinsic self-healing and elastic reconfiguration through density-encoded patterns and principled collision avoidance under stochastic obstacle motion.

Abstract: While offering significant promise for diverse applications, pattern-oriented swarms encounter multifaceted challenges in geometric control, self-organization, and safe navigation through dynamic environments. In this paper, we present a GRF-based stochastic optimal control framework to address these challenges within a unified probabilistic architecture. By extending the GRF into the temporal domain, the proposed framework casts collective coordination as a Bayesian inference task, enabling swarms to accommodate environmental uncertainty, satisfy non-convex constraints, and reconcile heterogeneous dynamics across diverse platforms. We develop an uncertainty- and safety-aware collision avoidance module for navigation in the presence of stochastic obstacle motion. The unscented transform is employed to propagate state uncertainty for both dynamic obstacles and neighboring agents, yielding principled confidence bounds for collision avoidance. In addition, density-guided pattern control is introduced, which encodes geometric patterns as implicit density fields. This representation decouples pattern specification from explicit agent-to-target assignments, thereby facilitating intrinsic self-healing and elastic reconfiguration in a distributed manner. The proposed framework is extensively evaluated through Monte Carlo simulations across diverse scenarios. Its model-agnostic nature is demonstrated on both quadrotor and fixed-wing UAV swarms, highlighting its generalizability across platforms with heterogeneous dynamics. Finally, the efficacy and robustness of the proposed method are validated through indoor experiments with a 15-quadrotor swarm and outdoor deployments involving 4 custom-built autonomous quadrotors. These experiments substantiate the proposed framework's capacity to maintain reliable geometric pattern transitions and safety-aware navigation within real-world environments.

</details>


### [292] [Tuning ROS 2 for Energy-Efficient Navigation: Empirical Insights from Costmap 2D Configurations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12971)
*Michel Albonico,Andreas Wortmann,Ivano Malavolta*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ROS 2 package reconfigurations materially impact energy efficiency in mobile robot navigation; energy-aware, environment-specific configuration is essential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve energy efficiency and operational longevity of autonomous robots by understanding how Nav2/Costmap 2D configurations affect energy use in realistic scenarios.

Method: Controlled experiments in two warehouse-like environments (small and large) with varying obstacle layouts and Costmap 2D parameterizations; repeated trials measuring energy usage, power profile, CPU load, memory, and navigation performance.

Result: Identified critical configuration settings that separate good from poor energy/performance outcomes; energy efficiency is highly environment-dependent, necessitating tailored configurations.

Conclusion: Careful, environment-aware ROS 2/Nav2 configuration can enhance energy efficiency; practitioners should develop guidelines for selecting configurations based on workspace characteristics.

Abstract: Robots are increasingly used in diverse application areas, where autonomous navigation plays a central role. As these systems become more widespread, improving their energy efficiency is critical to extending operational time and reducing environmental impact. The Robot Operating System (ROS) is a widely adopted middleware for robotics, offering a rich set of configurable packages. However, this flexibility can result in suboptimal software configurations in dynamic environments, negatively affecting both performance and energy consumption. This paper investigates the impact of ROS 2 package reconfigurations on the energy efficiency of mobile robot navigation. We conduct a controlled experiment in two warehouse-like scenarios (small and large) with varying obstacle layouts and Costmap 2D configurations (essential to the Nav2 stack). Through repeated trials, we measure energy usage, power profile, CPU load, memory consumption, and navigation performance. Results show that configurations must be carefully chosen for the specific robotic environment, and we were able to identify critical settings that lead to good and poor performance and energy consumption.

</details>


### [293] [Comfort by Construction: Adaptive, Comfort-Bounded Action Spaces for Learned Driving Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13011)
*Anna Rothenhäusler,Daniel Jost,Raghu Rajan,Faris Janjos,Oliver Scheel,Andreas Look,Joschka Boedecker*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: An adaptive action parameterization rediscretizes the action grid at each step to exactly cover the feasible control set, addressing grid-induced safety metric distortions in data-driven driving simulators; it is complemented by PufferDrive-Editor for auditing and achieves sub-1% comfort violations with better navigability than baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed-grid data-driven driving simulators enforce accelerations and steering rates but ignore realized dynamics, causing RL policies to exploit abrupt, last-second maneuvers that are unsafe and outside human norms; naive comfort enforcement via clamping leads to grid collapse and loss of control fidelity.

Method: Introduce an adaptive action parameterization that closed-form inverts the lateral-jerk constraint to rediscretize the action grid at every step so the per-step feasible set is fully spanned. Develop PufferDrive-Editor (browser tool) to audit realized kinematics and create challenging scenes.

Result: Empirical evaluation on the Waymo Open Motion Dataset and a hand-authored slalom shows the adaptive model keeps comfort violations below 1% and outperforms clipped-grid and direct-jerk baselines in navigability.

Conclusion: Adaptive, per-step feasibility-aligned action parameterization mitigates artificial safety metric inflation and preserves fine-grained control, yielding more realistic and navigable simulations; accompanying editor aids auditing of kinematics and scene authoring.

Abstract: Data-driven driving simulators command accelerations and steering rates from a fixed grid without constraining the realized accelerations and jerks. As a result, reinforcement-learning policies inflate safety metrics through abrupt, last-second maneuvers that lie far outside the range of human driving and would be unacceptable to occupants of a real vehicle, so the metrics measure simulator permissiveness rather than policy quality. Enforcing comfort bounds naively is not enough: lateral limits shrink quadratically with speed, so clamping a static grid saturates it and destroys fine-grained control ("grid collapse"). We propose an adaptive action parameterization that rediscretizes the grid at every step to span exactly the per-step feasible control set, via closed-form inversion of the lateral-jerk constraint. We further present PufferDrive-Editor, a browser-based tool to audit realized kinematics and author kinematically challenging scenes. On the Waymo Open Motion Dataset and a hand-authored slalom, our adaptive model holds comfort violations below 1% while outperforming clipped-grid and direct-jerk baselines in navigability.

</details>


### [294] [Global Path Planner with Multi-Model Switching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13015)
*Pietro Gori,Francesco Iotti,Eduard Zelenay,Rastislav Marko,Michele Pierallini,Franco Angelini,Gabriele Pannocchia,Manolo Garabini*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces a terrain-aware global planner with adaptive multi-model Pure Pursuit and Heading-Aware A*, validated in simulation on a quadruped and quadrotor, achieving improved path fidelity, efficiency, and robustness over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To maintain path fidelity and energy efficiency across heterogeneous terrains by real-time switching between kinematic models, addressing integrated planning and tracking.

Method: Components: traversability graph for terrain analysis; Heading-Aware A* for path generation; multi-model Pure Pursuit with real-time adaptive kinematic switching based on terrain features and robot states; simulation validation on Artaban quadruped and X3 quadrotor.

Result: Simulation validation demonstrates improved performance, robustness, and adaptability over standard baselines across diverse platforms.

Conclusion: Adaptive kinematic modeling with terrain-aware planning enhances fidelity and efficiency and provides robustness across terrains and platforms; this approach can generalize to other robots.

Abstract: This work enhances global path planning via a pure-pursuit controller with multi-model kinematic switching that sustains plan fidelity across diverse terrains. The system includes a traversability graph for terrain analysis, a Heading-Aware A* algorithm for generating feasible paths, and a multi-model Pure Pursuit controller for dynamic tracking. A core innovation is adaptive kinematic modeling, enabling real-time switching between kinematic models based on terrain features and robot states. This adaptability optimizes path efficiency and energy use in challenging scenarios. We validate the approach in simulation on different platforms, namely the Artaban quadruped and the X3 quadrotor drone, showcasing improved performance, robustness, and adaptability over standard baselines.

</details>


### [295] [Dynin-Robotics: Omnimodal Unified Diffusion Vision-Language-Action Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13053)
*Hoeun Lee,Jaeik Kim,Jusang Oh,Jinhyeok Kim,Geon Choi,Hyeonggeun Kim,Jaeyoung Do*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A diffusion-based, multi-modal trajectory model (Dynin-Omni) enables unified language-conditioned control by predicting actions, next states, terminal goals, and instructions from language, vision, goals, and actions tokens; pretrained on 1.33M trajectories, achieving strong cross-task transfer, efficiency, and zero-shot results on robotics benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To unify diverse control objectives (action prediction, next-state prediction, goal completion, and instruction reconstruction) under a shared trajectory model that can be conditioned in flexible ways and scaled at test time, enabling language-conditioned robot policies with explicit state and goal representations.

Method: Introduce Dynin-Robotics with an omnimodal masked-diffusion backbone (Dynin-Omni) that encodes language, visual observations, goals, and actions as discrete tokens. By varying conditioning and target spans, the model learns multiple interfaces: action prediction, action-conditioned next-state prediction, terminal goal-state prediction, and trajectory-to-instruction reconstruction. Test-time interfaces include goal prediction, action-candidate evaluation, and joint refinement of action and future-state predictions. Pretrain on ~1.33M trajectories from 48 Open X-Embodiment datasets; adapt to downstream domains. Evaluate on VLABench, LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and a Franka Research 3 robot; optimize inference with block-parallel action decoding.

Result: On VLABench tasks, pretraining improves adaptation within a fixed Stage-2 budget; the full objective mixture improves shifted-instruction success versus policy-only post-training with the same coupled decoder. Combining goal guidance with joint action-next-state denoising further improves shifted-instruction success compared to action-only decoding; benefits depend on prediction composition. Achieves competitive performance on LIBERO and zero-shot LIBERO-Plus, plus a 78.4% average success across four manipulation conditions on Franka Research 3. Block-parallel implementation accelerates model-side action decoding by up to 29.2x under the reported profiling setup.

Conclusion: Shared trajectory modeling provides a versatile, common interface for learning and composing complementary robot objectives (action, state, goal, and instruction) within a single model, enabling improved performance and inference efficiency across tasks and domains.

Abstract: Visual goal and dynamics prediction can provide language-conditioned robot policies with both a target outcome and a representation of action-dependent scene changes. We bring these predictions into action generation and selection through a shared trajectory model. Dynin-Robotics implements this formulation on Dynin-Omni, an omnimodal masked-diffusion backbone, representing language, visual observations, goals, and actions as discrete tokens. By varying conditioning and target spans, the same model learns action prediction, action-conditioned next-observation prediction, terminal goal-state prediction, and trajectory-to-instruction reconstruction. These interfaces support test-time scaling through goal prediction, action-candidate evaluation, and joint refinement of action and future-state predictions. We continually pretrain the model on approximately 1.33 million trajectories from 48 Open X-Embodiment datasets and adapt it separately to downstream domains. On two VLABench tasks, robot pretraining improves adaptation within a fixed Stage-2 step budget, and the full objective mixture improves shifted-instruction success over Policy-only post-training under the same coupled decoder. Combining goal guidance with joint action-next-state denoising further improves shifted-instruction success over action-only decoding; the benefit depends on how the predictions are composed. Dynin-Robotics achieves competitive performance on LIBERO and zero-shot LIBERO-Plus, together with a 78.4% average success rate across four manipulation conditions on a Franka Research 3 robot. An optimized block-parallel implementation accelerates model-side action decoding by up to 29.2x relative to the base implementation under the reported profiling setup. These results support shared trajectory modeling as a common interface for learning complementary robot objectives and composing their predictions during control.

</details>


### [296] [ASTRIL-MPC: Autonomous Traversal Framework of Articulated Tracked Robots with Language-Guided Neural-Kinematic MPC](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13083)
*Zhenfeng Gan,Yanbo Chen,Lirong Che,Junbo Tan,Xueqian Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ASTRIL-MPC fuses learned kinematics, NMPC, and an LLM-guided retuning loop to enable fast, robust autonomous traversal for articulated tracked robots in cluttered, contact-rich environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RTI is hybrid and discontinuous; traditional analytic models struggle; need data-efficient autonomous control with fast planning.

Method: Learned kinematics model predicting short-horizon state increments from height sequences and recent trajectories; NMPC with multi-objective costs and strict feasibility constraints; LLM proposes bounded updates to selected weights and bounds via safety-checked interface (range clipping, rate limiting, consistency checks).

Result: Predictor enables ~100 ms control cycle; improvements in traversal-quality score up to 71% vs non-adaptive NMPC and 67% vs PPO across three tasks; eliminates measurable collision impacts during descent; multi-height generalization.

Conclusion: Combining learned kinematics, optimization-based planning, and language-guided retuning yields data-efficient, robust autonomous control for ATRs in structured yet contact-rich environments.

Abstract: In urban search and rescue, articulated tracked robots (ATRs) must traverse structured but contact-rich environments such as stairwells and cluttered building interiors. Reliable autonomy remains challenging because robot-terrain interaction (RTI) is hybrid and discontinuous, and effective flipper-track coordination is difficult to model analytically. We present ASTRIL-MPC, a language-guided neural kinematics model predictive control (MPC) framework for autonomous traversal. A learned kinematics model predicts short-horizon task-state increments from a height sequence and recent trajectories; NMPC plans with multi-objective costs and strict feasibility constraints; and a large language model (LLM) proposes bounded updates to selected weights and bounds through a safety-checked interface with range clipping, rate limiting, and consistency checks. The compiled predictor enables a full control cycle within 100 ms. Across three traversal tasks and a multi-height generalization setting, ASTRIL-MPC improves an aggregate traversal-quality score by up to 71% over a non-adaptive NMPC and by 67% over a PPO baseline, while eliminating measurable collision impacts during descent. These results indicate that combining learned kinematics, optimization-based planning, and language-guided retuning yields data-efficient and robust autonomy for articulated tracked robots.

</details>


<div id='cs.CG'></div>

# cs.CG [[Back]](#toc)

### [297] [Direct Topology Tracking in Continuous Implicit Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12157)
*Guanqun Ma,David Lenz,Kaiyuan Tang,Hanqi Guo,Chaoli Wang,Tom Peterka,Bei Wang*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: A grid-free framework for tracking topological features directly on continuous implicit representations (INRs, MFAs, analytic functions) by querying the model and its derivatives to follow critical points without resampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discretization on grids introduces aliasing and resolution limits in topological feature tracking; continuous implicit models offer smooth, differentiable representations that can store and reconstruct data without grid constraints.

Method: Identify and track critical points by querying the implicit model and its derivatives, propagating their trajectories as the model changes, without converting to discrete grids; applicable to analytic functions, MFAs, and INRs.

Result: Demonstrates the framework across a range of implicit representations, producing smooth, coherent trajectories of critical points and enabling feature-driven visualization directly on continuous models.

Conclusion: Enables topology-aware feature tracking directly on continuous representations, facilitating high-fidelity visualization and analysis workflows for INRs and MFAs with reduced discretization artifacts.

Abstract: We present a framework for tracking topological features directly within continuous implicit models. Such models, including implicit neural representations (INRs) and multivariate functional approximations (MFAs), are increasingly adopted to represent scientific data without the resolution constraints of discrete grids. They offer compact, smooth, and differentiable representations of complex fields, enabling new opportunities for high-performance data storage, reconstruction, and analysis. Given a continuous implicit model, our method tracks the evolution of critical points by querying the model and its derivatives, thereby eliminating the need to resample onto a grid. This approach enables faithful feature tracking while avoiding discretization-induced artifacts such as aliasing. We demonstrate the generality of our framework across a range of implicit representations, including analytic functions, MFAs, and INRs, and show that it produces smooth, coherent critical point trajectories. By enabling feature tracking directly on continuous representations, our method supports a new class of feature-driven visualization workflows centered on implicit models.

</details>
